# Detailed outline (gate G1)

The section-level plan for every chapter. `/plan-book` built it in October 2026 from the seed outline (`docs/OUTLINE.md`) and a light scan of each chapter's current sources (`research/chNN/scan.md`). After gate G1 the chapter numbers are frozen.

**How to read it.** Each chapter lists its sections (numbered `NN.SS`), each with a purpose and the concepts it teaches; then its running-example hooks, must-cite sources, dependencies on earlier chapters and an estimated length. Sources named here are leads from the light scans: every name, date and number is re-verified at research time.

## Changes from seed

**The chapter list is unchanged.** All 52 chapters keep their seed numbers, titles and Parts. No chapter was added, removed, split, merged or moved, and no Part proposed such a change. Every change below is inside a chapter. Each Part ends with its own "Changes from seed (Pn)" list, which gives every change and its reason; this summary gathers the main ones.

**In numbers.** 540 sections (5–12 per chapter). Estimated length about 588,000 words; every chapter falls between 7,000 and 14,000 words, so none needs a split.

**1. One owner for every concept.** The seed taught several things in two places. Each now has one owner, and other chapters recap it:
- *Body:* screw drives move to ch06 (from ch05); tactile-sensor physics to ch08 (from ch07); design for repair to ch10 (from ch04); actuator networks and system identification to ch29 (previewed in ch05).
- *Foundations:* the vision transformer moves to ch12 (from ch11), because it needs attention; how RLHF works moves to ch14 (from ch12), which owns preference learning; ch13 owns the robot policy and imitation learning at a basic level; ch15 adds brief motion planning, which no chapter owned.
- *Data and simulation:* ch20 owns simulation data, data multiplication and teleoperation in simulation as data sources, and ch28 owns them as training practice; wearable grippers move to ch19 (from ch18); ch29 owns domain randomisation and online adaptation, and ch28 owns curricula and teacher–student training (the seed listed them in both).
- *Training and world models:* ch30 owns catastrophic forgetting and ch39 owns uncertainty (no earlier chapter owned either); ch39 owns RL inside world models as a use (ch34 and ch38 preview it); ch41 owns force- and touch-aware WAMs (ch40 previews them).
- *Evaluation:* ch29 owns sim–real correlation metrics and ch39 owns world-model evaluation, so ch43's two seed sections on them become one recap section (43.05).

**2. Boundaries recorded between neighbouring chapters.** ch09 and ch16 (the hardware vs which layer runs where); ch10, ch44 and ch45 (safety hardware vs safety engineering vs standards); ch18 and ch25 (labelling human video vs crossing between bodies); ch20, ch34 and ch48 (deployment data as a source vs RL from it vs the closed loop); ch24, ch44 and ch45 (data law vs privacy by design vs product regulation); ch26, ch29 and ch31–ch33 (mixture design vs co-training to close the gap vs stage recipes); ch46 and ch47 (planning vs operating remote assistance and maintenance); ch47 and ch48 (operational records vs logging for learning); ch47 and ch49 (how an update installs vs what is released, and to whom); ch42 and ch52 (WAM-versus-VLA scenarios vs field-wide open problems); ch02, ch46, ch50 and ch51 (who owns which deployment evidence).

**3. One map of lab recipes.** The brief asks how each major lab's recipe maps onto the training stages. Section 30.08 holds one master table. ch31–ch34 each end with a "recipes compared" section that fills in their stage (31.11, 32.09, 33.11, 34.10). ch38 uses the same four stage names for world models in one table (38.10), with "not disclosed" where a lab publishes no recipe. ch50 recaps the map lab by lab.

**4. Structure added across the book.**
- Opening sections that say what the thing is and why it matters (for example 05.01, 27.01, 30.01, 46.01, 48.01, 49.01).
- Closing sections that apply the chapter to the four running examples (for example 25.09, 26.10, 30.09, 35.09, 36.10, 38.11, 39.10, 40.11, 44.12, 46.11, 47.10, 48.11, 52.12).
- Tables built for the appendices: the data dictionary (21.10, for Appendix A); the metric catalogue (43.11) and fleet KPIs (47.09), for Appendix C; and a dated status table of standards and law (45.11).
- Long seed sections split into several: ch40's architectures into four; ch41's root causes and remedies into six; ch34's RL fine-tuning into three styles; ch24's privacy, consent, licensing and law into four.
- ch50 places the 19 labs in six group sections, each lab with an identical teardown template, because 19 sections would exceed the limit of 12.

**5. Honest gaps planned in.** Where the scans found no primary evidence, the plan says so rather than implying a number exists: humanoid reliability, MTBF and uptime (10.07); cost per hour of data and operator throughput (17.08, 19.06); the effect of calibration or sync error on policy success (22.10); how labs run their data pipelines (ch23); operator-to-robot ratios (47.05 teaches how to derive one as a queueing problem); robot release practice (49.06 uses labelled analogues); home readiness (ch46's home checklists rest largely on `[inference]`). Open question 2 asks how to treat these.

**6. Leads updated from the scans.** All are re-verified at research time.
- Skild's "S1" replaces the seed's "Skild Brain"; π0.7 remains the newest π model.
- The WAM surveys are dated June and September 2026 (a May 2026 survey is broader than WAMs). The NVIDIA WAM explainer found is dated 15 June 2026; no July or August post was found.
- DreamZero (arXiv 2602.15922, 17 February 2026) is confirmed as the paper that introduced the term "World Action Model"; it also labels earlier work as WAMs after the fact.
- GR00T N2, and its reported DreamZero basis, appear only on aggregator sites, so they are excluded until traced to NVIDIA.
- ISO 25785-1 is still a committee draft. ISO's listing suggests the 2025 edition of ISO 10218 folds in ISO/TS 15066 content (unconfirmed).
- The ch45 plan records a scan finding that Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (the Digital Omnibus) moves the AI Act's high-risk dates (to verify).
- India's DPDP Rules carry two notification dates (13 November 2025 in the Gazette, 14 November in the government press release); ch24 shows both.

## Open questions for the human

Each question has a recommendation, which the build will follow unless you say otherwise in `FEEDBACK.md`.

1. **The reference humanoid (ch04).** Should it be a composite spec with ranges, drawn from several leading robots, or anchored to one real robot? Several makers' spec pages are undated, and Tesla's newest generation is not unveiled. *Recommendation:* a composite with ranges, each row sourced and dated. It stays neutral and survives product changes.
2. **Where public evidence is missing.** No primary sources were found for humanoid reliability and uptime, cost per hour of data, operator-to-robot ratios, a cost breakdown of a humanoid (ch02), or robot release practice. Options: (a) state the gap, teach the method and use made-up illustrative numbers marked as such; (b) as (a), plus clearly labelled analogues from neighbouring fields (industrial arms and mobile robots for reliability; car and software release practice for staged rollouts); (c) cut those sections. *Recommendation:* (b).
3. **Paywalled standards.** ISO and UNECE pages blocked the scouts (HTTP 403), and the standards themselves are paid documents: ISO 10218-1/-2:2025, ISO 13482, ISO 24089, the ISO 25785-1 draft and UN R156. Can you supply the texts? If not, may the book cite the issuing body's catalogue entry or official summary, and say that the full text was not read? *Recommendation:* yes to the second if you cannot get them.
4. **Labs that disclose little.** OpenAI, Tesla, Meta's humanoid effort and Neura publish little or no technical detail, and Genie 3 and 1X publish no world-model recipe. Tesla's world-model work exists only as conference keynote talks. Should these labs appear as rows marked "not disclosed" in the comparison tables (30.08, 38.10, ch50), and may a keynote talk be cited, attributed as such? *Recommendation:* yes to both. A visible "not disclosed" is itself information.
5. **Labels that clash with the book's.** DreamZero calls earlier systems WAMs after the fact, and Meta calls V-JEPA 2's action conditioning "post-training" where the book's stages call it mid-training. *Recommendation:* use the book's single definitions everywhere. A system is a WAM if it meets the definition, whatever its date. Each lab's own label is also given, attributed, so nothing is silently renamed.
6. **Confidence levels in ch42's scenarios.** Words (low, medium, high) or percentages? *Recommendation:* words, with the criteria for each level stated. Percentages would imply more precision than the evidence has.
7. **ch01's previews.** ch01 points to 31 concepts that later chapters teach. The style contract gives every concept heading the full five layers. Should ch01's previews be short asides instead? *Recommendation:* asides, so ch01 stays a map of about 7,000 words and never pre-empts the chapter that owns a concept.
8. **Regulatory scope (ch24, ch45).** The scans covered the EU, India and parts of the US and China. Japan and Korea were not scanned, and ch24 has no US or Chinese data law yet. *Recommendation:* full treatment for the EU, US, China and India, where most deployments and data collection happen; a short dated status table for Japan and Korea.
9. **Organisations outside the agreed lab list.** Hexagon (its AEON is central to the battery-station example) and specialists such as eternal.ag matter to specific topics. Should Hexagon get its own ch50 teardown? *Recommendation:* no. Cite them where they matter, as the brief allows, and keep ch50 to the agreed 19 labs.
10. **Smaller calls the Parts made on their own.** These are listed so you can veto any of them: the vision transformer moves to ch12; ch15 treats motion planning only briefly; ch16 uses a made-up (marked) torque-tool timing example because no public timing data exists; the pilot (ch29) recaps concepts from chapters not yet written, as `checks/ch29-known.md` will record; ch33's "making models fast" becomes its own chapter only if ch33 passes 18,000 words; ch50 groups the Chinese makers by region while the other groups are by kind of lab. *Recommendation:* keep all six.

## P0 · Orientation

### ch01 · The lifecycle on one page
- **Purpose:** One map of how an intelligent humanoid goes from parts to a deployed, self-improving worker, and where each step is taught in the book. It previews and points; it teaches no concept that a later chapter owns. Written last.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** no.
- **Depends on:** none (first in book order; previews every later chapter).
- **Estimated length:** about 7,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **01.01 · The lifecycle map.** Lay out the whole journey as one loop and show which Part covers each stage. *Concepts:* the humanoid lifecycle as eight stages (body → data → simulation → training → world models → evaluation → deployment → flywheel; owned here); what each stage hands to the next (owned here); how the 13 Parts map onto the stages; preview: deployment readiness (taught in ch46); preview: the data flywheel (taught in ch48).
  - **01.02 · The body and the foundations at a glance.** Point to where the hardware and the from-zero foundations are taught, and say why a product reader needs both. *Concepts:* preview: anatomy, degrees of freedom and the reference humanoid (taught in ch04); preview: actuators, transmissions, hands and senses (taught in ch05–ch08); preview: compute, power and safety hardware (taught in ch09–ch10); preview: neural networks, transformers, generative models, RL and control (taught in ch11–ch15).
  - **01.03 · The layered brain in one picture.** Show the stack from millisecond motor loops to slow reasoning, using more than one published architecture so no lab is the template. *Concepts:* preview: layered brain, clock rates and latency (taught in ch16); preview: fast-slow designs, "System 1/2" and "System 0/1/2" (taught in ch16; Helix 02 and GR00T N1 as attributed examples); preview: whole-body control (taught in ch35); preview: vision-language-action model (taught in ch31).
  - **01.04 · Where the data comes from.** Sketch the sources of robot data, from web video to fleet logs, and why data is the bottleneck. *Concepts:* preview: the data pyramid (taught in ch17); preview: human and egocentric video (taught in ch18); preview: teleoperation (taught in ch19); preview: synthetic, autonomous and deployment data (taught in ch20); preview: simulation as a data source (taught in ch20; simulators, training and the gap in ch27–ch29).
  - **01.05 · Training stages in one picture: policies and world models.** Contrast the two families of learned models and the stages each passes through. *Concepts:* preview: pre-, mid- and post-training and RL (taught in ch30); preview: training robot policies (taught in ch31–ch35); preview: world models (taught in ch36–ch39); preview: world action models, with the book's single glossary definition (taught in ch40); preview: convergence of WAMs and VLAs (taught in ch42); preview: steerable prompting with subgoals and metadata (π0.7 as an attributed example; taught in ch42).
  - **01.06 · The gate before deployment: evaluation and safety.** Show what has to be proven before a robot meets a real site. *Concepts:* preview: evaluating robot policies (taught in ch43); preview: safety engineering for learned robots (taught in ch44); preview: standards, certification and regulation (taught in ch45); preview: failure modes of world models and WAMs (taught in ch41).
  - **01.07 · Deployment and the improvement loop.** Close the loop from launch to retraining, and point to what deployments have reported so far. *Concepts:* preview: readiness criteria and acceptance tests (taught in ch46); preview: running a fleet and its KPIs (taught in ch47); preview: the data flywheel (taught in ch48); preview: continual improvement and release engineering (taught in ch49); preview: published deployments, attributed as company claims (taught in ch02, ch46 and ch51); preview: landscape, teardowns and open problems (taught in ch50–ch52).
  - **01.08 · The four running examples and the reference humanoid.** Introduce the four tasks and the one body they share, with its variants. *Concepts:* the four running examples (introduced here: dinner for five, kitchen reset, battery-pack station, greenhouse harvest); the reference humanoid and its variants (wheeled base, rail trolley, soft home exterior; introduced here, specified in ch04); "same brain, three deployments" as a book-long thread.
  - **01.09 · How to read this book.** Explain the reading aids so the reader can navigate alone. *Concepts:* (asides, not five-layer concepts) the five-layer template; evidence tags and claim badges; running-example callouts; research prompts and "Try it" pointers; reading paths (Appendix F); the "current as of" date.
- **Running-example hooks:** all four in 01.08; dinner for five in 01.03 (a plan days ahead vs sub-second control); dinner and battery station in 01.04 (abundant cooking video vs scarce battery-station video); each example's "later twist" in 01.07; battery station's live high voltage and the dinner's allergens in 01.06.
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - World Action Models: A Survey — Shen et al. — 2026-06-18 — arXiv 2606.20781 — preprint
  - World-Action Models for Robot Learning and Control: A Survey — Lu et al. — 2026-09-13 — arXiv 2609.16074 — preprint
  - World Model for Robot Learning: A Comprehensive Survey — Hou et al. — 2026-04-30 — arXiv 2605.00080 — preprint
  - World Models for Embodied Intelligence: Plausible to Controllable to Actionable — Yao et al. — 2026-09-15 — arXiv 2609.16697 — preprint
  - π0.7 — Physical Intelligence — 2026-04-16 — arXiv 2604.15483 — preprint
  - Gemini Robotics 2 — Google DeepMind — 2026-07-30 — https://deepmind.google/blog/gemini-robotics-2-brings-whole-body-intelligence-to-robots/ — company-claim
  - Helix 02 — Figure — 2026-01-27 — https://www.figure.ai/news/helix-02 — company-claim
  - F.02 Contributed to Production of 30,000 Cars — Figure — 2025-11-19 — https://www.figure.ai/news/production-at-bmw — company-claim
  - VLM-based VLA survey — authors not recorded — 2025-08 — arXiv 2508.13073 — preprint (unconfirmed)
  - World Action Models are Zero-shot Policies (DreamZero) — Ye et al., NVIDIA — 2026-02-17 — https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.15922 — preprint (from the ch40 scan)
- **Notes:** The 2026 surveys disagree on where world models end and WAMs begin: use the BRIEF's one glossary definition and point to ch40 for the dispute. No confirmed 2026 VLA survey yet (gap). Every concept here is a preview; the writer should keep each preview to a short five-layer block or an `.aside` and never pre-empt the owning chapter.

### ch02 · Why a humanoid?
- **Purpose:** The first-principles case for and against the humanoid form, and when legs, hands, wheels, rails or specialist machines win. Critics are presented as fairly as proponents.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** no.
- **Depends on:** ch01 (lifecycle map; running examples; reference humanoid).
- **Estimated length:** about 8,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **02.01 · The humanoid bet.** State plainly what building a robot in human shape is a bet on. *Concepts:* general-purpose vs special-purpose robots; form factor as a design choice; preview: anatomy and degrees of freedom (taught in ch04).
  - **02.02 · A world built for human bodies.** Make the environment-fit argument from first principles. *Concepts:* environment fit (stairs, doors, shelves and benches sized for people); deploying into existing sites without redesign; tool use with tools made for hands; preview: hands and end-effectors (taught in ch07).
  - **02.03 · Human data as the bootstrap.** Explain why a human-shaped body may learn more from human demonstrations, and the limits of that claim. *Concepts:* body match between demonstrator and robot; preview: learning from human video (taught in ch18); preview: teleoperation (taught in ch19); preview: the embodiment gap and retargeting (taught in ch25); the counter-argument that video carries no touch or force (Brooks).
  - **02.04 · The costs of the form: balance, energy, safety and complexity.** Weigh what the human shape costs. *Concepts:* dynamic stability and falls (preview: balance, taught in ch15; falling safely, taught in ch10); energy and runtime (preview: taught in ch09); safety of a heavy legged machine near people (preview: taught in ch44); part count, cost and reliability (preview: taught in ch04 and ch10).
  - **02.05 · Wheels, rails and specialist machines.** Show the alternatives that keep some human traits and drop others. *Concepts:* wheeled humanoids (Hexagon AEON at BMW Leipzig as an attributed example); rail-mounted trolleys in greenhouses; specialist machines (eternal.ag Harvester as an attributed example); fixed automation.
  - **02.06 · When a general-purpose robot beats a specialist.** Give a decision frame for choosing a form, from first principles. *Concepts:* breadth of tasks vs efficiency per task; spreading one body's cost across many jobs; changeover and redeployment; a legs/wheels/rails/specialist decision frame (`[inference]`, with illustrative numbers marked `nofact`).
  - **02.07 · Evidence from deployments so far.** Report what deployments actually show, attributed, and what they do not yet show. *Concepts:* Figure at BMW Spartanburg (company claim, with BMW's own confirmation); Agility Digit at GXO (company claim); the AEON pilot at BMW Leipzig (press, primary pending); pilots vs scaled deployment; preview: deployment readiness (taught in ch46); preview: sector-by-sector deployment (taught in ch51).
  - **02.08 · Where the debate stands now.** Lay out both sides and what would settle the question. *Concepts:* the proponents' case; the critics' case (Brooks on dexterity, wheels and safety); the legs-vs-wheels debate among companies; signals to watch; preview: open problems (taught in ch52).
- **Running-example hooks:** battery station in 02.05 (wheeled AEON), 02.06 (against fixed automation) and 02.07; greenhouse in 02.05 (rail trolley; specialist harvester as the bar) and 02.06 (the toughest business case: many jobs in one body); home tasks in 02.02 (stairs, doors, human-sized kitchens), 02.04 (safety near a toddler) and 02.06 (why a home needs a generalist).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - BMW piloting Hexagon's wheeled humanoid — The Robot Report — 2026-03-02 — https://www.therobotreport.com/bmw-piloting-hexagons-wheeled-humanoid-in-germany/ — press
  - F.02 Contributed to the Production of 30,000 Cars at BMW — Figure — 2025-11-19 — https://www.figure.ai/news/production-at-bmw — company-claim
  - BMW Spartanburg "Home of X" release — BMW Group — 2026-06-30 — https://www.press.bmwgroup.com/global/article/detail/T0458864EN/ — company-claim
  - Why Today's Humanoids Won't Learn Dexterity — Rodney Brooks — 2025-09-26 — https://rodneybrooks.com/why-todays-humanoids-wont-learn-dexterity/ — press
  - eternal.ag launches autonomous harvesting robot — FruitToday — 2026-03-19 — https://fruittoday.com/en/eternal-ag-launches-fully-autonomous-harvesting-robot-to-tackle-greenhouse-labor-shortage/ — press
  - Digit deployed at GXO — Agility Robotics — 2024-10-03 — https://www.agilityrobotics.com/content/digit-deployed-at-gxo-in-historic-humanoid-raas-agreement — company-claim
  - Humanoid companies debate wheels vs legs — Bloomberg — 2026-06-12 — https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-06-12/humanoid-robotics-companies-debate-whether-wheels-make-more-sense-than-legs — press (unconfirmed: headline only, page blocked)
- **Notes:** Gaps: no primary BMW or Hexagon release on Leipzig; no primary eternal.ag release; no non-aggregator cost-curve or bill-of-materials analysis (the BofA figures need a primary source); no sourced argument yet on how people respond to working beside human-shaped robots. Present Brooks and the companies with equal care, and say which evidence is stronger. Company deployment figures stay attributed.

### ch03 · A short history: from programmed arms to robot foundation models
- **Purpose:** The dated arc, from original sources, that explains why 2023–2026 changed robotics. Each milestone is dated and previews the chapter that teaches its mechanism.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** no.
- **Depends on:** ch01 (lifecycle map); ch02 (the humanoid bet; general-purpose vs special-purpose robots).
- **Estimated length:** about 9,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **03.01 · Industrial robots and teach pendants.** Show how robots first worked: programmed motions in fenced, structured cells. *Concepts:* the programmed industrial arm (Unimate as the first, date unconfirmed); teach-pendant programming; why fixed automation needs structured cells.
  - **03.02 · Model-based control and the DARPA Robotics Challenge.** Show the era of hand-built physical models and where it hit limits. *Concepts:* model-based control (preview: taught in ch15); legged robots of the model-based era; the DARPA Robotics Challenge Finals and their lessons on brittleness.
  - **03.03 · Deep learning reaches robots: end-to-end policies, deep RL and sim-to-real (2015–21).** Show the first learned visuomotor policies and the move to training in simulation. *Concepts:* end-to-end visuomotor policies (Levine et al.); preview: neural networks (taught in ch11); preview: deep reinforcement learning (taught in ch14); preview: sim-to-real transfer and domain randomisation (taught in ch29), with the Rubik's Cube hand as the milestone.
  - **03.04 · The imitation-learning revival (2023).** Show how cheap hardware and new generative methods made copying demonstrations work. *Concepts:* low-cost bimanual teleoperation (ALOHA; preview: taught in ch19); action chunking (ACT) and Diffusion Policy (preview: taught in ch13).
  - **03.05 · Transformers meet robots: RT-1 and RT-2.** Show the step from a transformer robot policy to the vision-language-action idea. *Concepts:* the transformer robot policy (RT-1; preview: transformers, taught in ch12); the vision-language-action model as an idea (RT-2; preview: taught in ch31).
  - **03.06 · Cross-embodiment data: Open X-Embodiment.** Show the first large pooling of data across many robot bodies. *Concepts:* cross-embodiment datasets (preview: taught in ch17 and ch25); pooling data across labs.
  - **03.07 · Generalist robot foundation models and dual-system humanoid brains (2024–26).** Trace the flow-matching VLAs and the split into fast and slow systems on humanoids. *Concepts:* flow-matching VLAs (π0, through π0.7; preview: flow matching, taught in ch13); dual-system and three-system humanoid brains (GR00T N1, Helix 02; preview: taught in ch16); lab-built generalist models (Gemini Robotics, through Gemini Robotics 2); open vs closed models (preview: taught in ch31).
  - **03.08 · World models and WAMs (2018–2026).** Trace prediction-based models from early world models to the 2026 WAM surveys. *Concepts:* world models (preview: taught in ch36); latent predictive planning (V-JEPA 2; preview: taught in ch36 and ch39); world action models and the naming of the term (preview: taught in ch40).
  - **03.09 · The first commercial deployments.** Date the first paid deployments, with every "first" attributed. *Concepts:* robots-as-a-service humanoid deployment (Agility at GXO; company claim); production-line deployment (Figure at BMW; company claim); preview: deployment readiness (taught in ch46).
  - **03.10 · Why 2023–2026 changed robotics.** Draw the arc together in one timeline and name the shifts. *Concepts:* from programming to learning; from one task to generalist models; from one robot to many bodies; from prediction to action (all `[inference]` drawn from the dated milestones, reasoning shown).
- **Running-example hooks:** battery station in 03.01 (why rigid, repeated cells suit programmed arms but cables and connectors do not, `[inference]`) and 03.09 (first deployments are in car plants); kitchen reset in 03.04 and 03.07 (bimanual imitation and generalist models as the route to home tasks); dinner for five in 03.10 (no published system yet does the whole task, per the running-examples file, to be re-verified).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - End-to-End Training of Deep Visuomotor Policies — Levine et al. — 2015-04-02 — arXiv 1504.00702 — preprint
  - Solving Rubik's Cube with a Robot Hand — OpenAI — 2019-10-16 — arXiv 1910.07113 — preprint
  - Diffusion Policy — Chi et al. — 2023-03-07 — arXiv 2303.04137 — preprint
  - ALOHA / ACT — Zhao et al. — 2023-04-23 — arXiv 2304.13705 — preprint
  - RT-1 — Brohan et al. — 2022-12-13 — arXiv 2212.06817 — preprint
  - RT-2 — Brohan et al. — 2023-07-28 — arXiv 2307.15818 — preprint
  - Open X-Embodiment — Open X-Embodiment Collaboration — 2023-10-13 — arXiv 2310.08864 — preprint
  - π0 — Black et al. — 2024-10-31 — arXiv 2410.24164 — preprint
  - GR00T N1 — NVIDIA — 2025-03-18 — arXiv 2503.14734 — preprint
  - V-JEPA 2 — Assran et al. — 2025-06-11 — arXiv 2506.09985 — preprint
- **Notes:** Further leads: Gemini Robotics (arXiv 2503.20020, 2025-03-25); DreamZero (arXiv 2602.15922, 2026-02-17, from the ch40 scan); the 2026 WAM surveys and π0.7, Helix 02 and Gemini Robotics 2 (listed under ch01). Gaps: no opened primary source for Unimate (1961, unconfirmed), the DRC Finals (J. Field Robotics, 2017, unconfirmed), ANYmal, Genie or DreamerV3. "First commercial humanoid deployment" is a company claim and stays attributed.

- ch01: added section 01.02 (the body and the foundations at a glance) — the seed's sections skipped Parts P1–P3, and the chapter must point to every Part.
- ch01: split "readiness and the deployment loop" into 01.06 (evaluation and safety) and 01.07 (deployment and the improvement loop) — P9 and P10–P11 each need a clear pointer, and the scan supports a readiness-and-flywheel framing.
- ch01: dropped "(write last)" from the title — it is a production note, kept in Purpose and in `state/progress.json`.
- ch02: added 02.01 (the humanoid bet) — the chapter needs a plain statement of the general-purpose thesis before weighing it.
- ch02: added 02.07 (evidence from deployments so far) — the scan suggests it, and it keeps deployment numbers attributed and separate from argument.
- ch03: renamed seed section "deep RL and sim-to-real" to 03.03 (adding end-to-end visuomotor policies) and "flow-matching VLAs and dual-system humanoid brains" to 03.07; widened 03.07 to include Gemini Robotics and the 2026 successors (π0.7, Helix 02, Gemini Robotics 2) — the scan names Levine et al. (2015) and these models as milestones.
- ch03: added 03.10 (why 2023–2026 changed robotics) — the chapter's stated purpose is to explain the arc, which needs a synthesis.
- No PROPOSED chapter-level changes.

## P1 · The body

### ch04 · Anatomy, degrees of freedom and the reference humanoid
- **Purpose:** What a humanoid body is made of, how its design limits what it can learn and do, and the book's reference humanoid: a dated spec table built from today's leading robots, with its three variants. Hardware is explained by what it means for data, learning and deployment, not as a bill of materials.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch01 (the four running examples; the reference humanoid introduced); ch02 (form factor as a design choice; wheeled humanoids and rail trolleys; part count, cost and reliability as costs of the form).
- **Estimated length:** about 11,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **04.01 · Links, joints and degrees of freedom.** Give the vocabulary every later hardware chapter uses. *Concepts:* links and joints; joint types (rotary and sliding); degrees of freedom (owned here); actuated vs passive degrees of freedom; the floating base (a body not bolted to the ground, so balance is never free); preview: balance (taught in ch15).
  - **04.02 · Kinematic chains: legs, torso, arms, neck and hands.** Show how the joints are arranged into limbs and how makers split their degrees of freedom. *Concepts:* kinematic chain and the body as a tree of chains; how degrees of freedom are allocated per limb; redundancy (more joints than a task strictly needs, and why that helps); preview: frames, forward and inverse kinematics (taught in ch15); preview: hand degrees of freedom as the place where makers diverge (taught in ch07).
  - **04.03 · Workspace, reach and payload.** Explain what the body can reach and lift, and why spec sheets are not like-for-like. *Concepts:* workspace and reach; payload (peak vs sustained vs one-handed vs two-handed); joint torque as the root of payload (preview: actuators, taught in ch05); why published payloads cannot be compared directly.
  - **04.04 · Mass, height and centre of mass.** Show what weight and size cost, and why the centre of mass matters. *Concepts:* mass and what it costs (energy, fall energy, safety near people); height and proportion; centre of mass as a body property (owned here); preview: balance and centroidal dynamics (taught in ch15); preview: falling safely (taught in ch10).
  - **04.05 · How the body shapes data and learning.** Make the bridge from hardware to models explicit. *Concepts:* the body defines the action space a model must output (preview: actions and action spaces, taught in ch21); the body defines what demonstrations can be reused (preview: embodiment gap and retargeting, taught in ch25); changing the body invalidates data and models (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); preview: sensors as the other half of the interface (taught in ch08).
  - **04.06 · Designing for manufacture and the supply chain.** Explain why a body that cannot be built in volume cannot be deployed in volume. *Concepts:* design for manufacture (part count, shared actuator sizes across joints, cost); the humanoid supply chain and in-house vs bought components (owned here; actuator cases in ch05); public price points vs bill-of-materials estimates (only primary, dated figures; aggregator estimates rejected); preview: design for repair and maintainability (taught in ch10).
  - **04.07 · Today's leading humanoids compared.** Lay out the current robots side by side and teach how to read their spec sheets. *Concepts:* (aside) a dated spec table of current humanoids (Atlas product version, Digit 5, Figure 03, Unitree G1 and H2, NEO, with Apollo 2, UBTech Walker S2, AgiBot, Galbot, Neura 4NE1, Hexagon AEON and Tesla Optimus added only where primary data exist); how to read a humanoid spec sheet (undated pages, peak vs continuous figures, company claims, missing cells); where makers diverge (hand degrees of freedom, legs vs wheels, battery strategy).
  - **04.08 · The reference humanoid.** Define the one body the book's four tasks share, cell by cell, from the table. *Concepts:* the reference humanoid specification (owned here: every cell traced to a dated source, ranges where makers disagree, "not published" where no maker discloses); how later chapters use it; the "current as of" date on the spec.
  - **04.09 · Variants: wheeled base, rail trolley and soft home body.** Show what changes when the same upper body meets three sites. *Concepts:* the wheeled-base variant for the battery hall (recap: wheeled humanoids, ch02); the sealed, heat-tolerant rail-trolley variant for the greenhouse (recap: rail trolleys, ch02; preview: sealing and heat, taught in ch09); the soft, quiet home variant (preview: soft covers, taught in ch10); "same brain, three bodies": what a model must relearn per variant (preview: cross-embodiment, taught in ch25).
- **Running-example hooks:** dinner in 04.03 (reaching into the oven and across a hot hob) and 04.09 (home variant); reset in 04.03 (reaching into dishwasher racks and high cupboards) and 04.09 (soft, quiet body around a toddler); battery station in 04.03 (payload for busbars and the torque tool) and 04.09 (wheeled base); greenhouse in 04.03 (reach to high vines from a raising trolley; crate payloads) and 04.09 (sealed rail-trolley variant).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Atlas spec sheet — Boston Dynamics — 2025-12-23 — https://bostondynamics.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/01/atlas-spec-sheet.pdf — company-claim
  - Boston Dynamics unveils new Atlas robot — Boston Dynamics — 2026-01-05 — https://bostondynamics.com/blog/boston-dynamics-unveils-new-atlas-robot-to-revolutionize-industry/ — company-claim
  - Agility unveils Digit 5 — Agility Robotics — 2026-09-15 — https://www.agilityrobotics.com/content/agility-unveils-digit-5-humanoid-robot-built-for-cooperatively-safe-work-at-scale — company-claim
  - Introducing Figure 03 — Figure — 2025-10-09 — https://www.figure.ai/news/introducing-figure-03 — company-claim
  - Figure 03 product page — Figure — undated (© 2026) — https://www.figure.ai/figure — company-claim
  - Unitree H2 page — Unitree — undated — https://www.unitree.com/mobile/H2/ — company-claim
  - Unitree G1 page — Unitree — undated — https://www.unitree.com/g1/ — company-claim
  - NEO's Hands — 1X — 2026-07-09 — https://www.1x.tech/discover/neos-hands — company-claim
  - BMW piloting Hexagon's wheeled humanoid — The Robot Report — 2026-03-02 — https://www.therobotreport.com/bmw-piloting-hexagons-wheeled-humanoid-in-germany/ — press

- **Notes:** Tesla has not unveiled Optimus V3 (as of the scan); its row says "not published" and blog or patent figures stay out. Undated spec pages get an access date plus an archived capture date. The scan's Digit 5 mass is far above the aggregator figure for Digit 4: verify the number and its unit against Agility's primary. Gaps: UBTech, AgiBot, Galbot, Neura, Hexagon AEON and an Apollo 2 datasheet are unscanned; no primary bill-of-materials estimate.

### ch05 · Actuators: motors, drives and power electronics
- **Purpose:** How a joint is made to move, from the physics of an electric motor to the drive electronics that control it, and why actuator choices shape control, learning, simulation and field reliability.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch04 (joints and degrees of freedom; payload and its root in joint torque; design for manufacture and the supply chain).
- **Estimated length:** about 11,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **05.01 · What an actuator must do.** Set the job description before the parts. *Concepts:* the actuator as a package (motor, drive, transmission, sensor); torque and speed; mechanical power as torque times speed (equation); the torque-speed trade-off; preview: transmissions (taught in ch06).
  - **05.02 · Electric motors from zero.** Derive how electricity becomes torque. *Concepts:* force on a current in a magnetic field; brushless DC and permanent-magnet synchronous motors; the torque constant (torque proportional to current; equation); back-EMF (the voltage a spinning motor makes, which caps its speed); losses (copper heating proportional to current squared, iron and friction losses; equation).
  - **05.03 · Motor drivers, inverters and field-oriented control.** Show the electronics that turn battery power into controlled motor current. *Concepts:* the inverter and pulse-width modulation; commutation; field-oriented control; the current-torque inner loop (owned here: the physics and the driver); preview: where this loop sits among the robot's loops and its clock rate (taught in ch16); preview: position control with PD gains (taught in ch15).
  - **05.04 · Current as a sense: proprioceptive actuation.** Explain how a motor can feel the force it applies. *Concepts:* estimating joint torque from motor current; why gearing and friction blur that estimate (preview: transmissions, taught in ch06); why felt force matters for contact-rich learning; preview: dedicated joint torque sensors (taught in ch06).
  - **05.05 · Torque density, heat and the continuous-vs-peak gap.** Show why heat, not strength, usually sets the limit. *Concepts:* torque density; thermal limits and derating (owned here); continuous vs peak torque, and why spec sheets omit the continuous figure; what this means for sustained tasks and payload claims (recap: payload, ch04).
  - **05.06 · Hydraulic vs electric actuation.** Explain why most humanoids are now electric. *Concepts:* hydraulic actuation (power density, pumps, leaks, noise); electric actuation's advantages for maintenance, control and cost; Atlas's move from hydraulic to electric as the marker (re-check date); preview: hydraulic hands (taught in ch07).
  - **05.07 · Rotary, linear and integrated actuator modules.** Show the main packaging choices. *Concepts:* rotary actuators; linear actuators (preview: ball and roller screws, taught in ch06); integrated actuator modules (motor, encoder, controller and gearbox in one housing); a small family of actuator sizes reused across the body.
  - **05.08 · Who makes the actuator.** Apply the supply-chain idea to the most expensive moving parts. *Concepts:* recap: the humanoid supply chain (ch04); in-house vs supplier actuators (Hyundai Mobis for Atlas; Schaeffler's product module; Agility's proprietary cycloidal leg actuators; Figure's in-house claims, all attributed); what disclosure gaps mean for buyers (Tesla's actuator story rests on older-generation material and patents).
  - **05.09 · Real actuators vs ideal ones: what a simulator must capture.** Name the behaviours that make a real joint differ from its textbook model. *Concepts:* friction, torque and voltage saturation, delay and bandwidth limits, temperature-dependent behaviour; why these matter for learned policies; preview: actuator networks and system identification (taught in ch29); preview: physics engines (taught in ch27).
  - **05.10 · Wear and failure.** Show how actuators degrade in service and what signals warn of it. *Concepts:* actuator failure modes (overheating, bearing and gear wear, sensor faults); trending current and temperature as early warnings; preview: reliability and duty cycles (taught in ch10); preview: fleet monitoring (taught in ch47).
- **Running-example hooks:** battery station in 05.01 and 05.04 (absorbing the torque tool's reaction; sensing connector seating through current); greenhouse in 05.05 (heat above 30 °C reduces how long a motor can hold peak torque, `[inference]` until sourced); dinner in 05.05 (sustained holding of a heavy pot); reset in 05.06 (why a home body cannot leak hydraulic fluid, `[inference]`).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Proprioceptive actuator design in the MIT Cheetah — Wensing, Wang, Seok, Otten, Lang, Kim — 2017 — https://dl.acm.org/doi/abs/10.1109/TRO.2016.2640183 — peer-reviewed
  - Design principles for a family of direct-drive legged robots — Kenneally, De, Koditschek — 2016-07 — https://repository.upenn.edu/ese_papers/705 — peer-reviewed
  - Learning agile and dynamic motor skills for legged robots — Hwangbo et al. — 2019 — https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/scirobotics.aau5872 — peer-reviewed (unconfirmed: page returned 403; abstract only)
  - Atlas spec sheet — Boston Dynamics — 2025-12-23 — https://bostondynamics.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/01/atlas-spec-sheet.pdf — company-claim
  - Hyundai Mobis to supply Atlas actuators — Boston Dynamics — 2026-01 — https://bostondynamics.com/news/hyundai-mobis-forms-strategic-collaboration-framework-with-boston-dynamics/ — company-claim (unconfirmed: date)
  - Schaeffler planetary actuator for humanoids — Schaeffler — 2026-01 — https://www.schaeffler.com/en/media/press-releases/press-releases-detail.jsp?id=88156672 — company-claim
  - Introducing Figure 03 — Figure — 2025-10-09 — https://www.figure.ai/news/introducing-figure-03 — company-claim
  - Agility unveils Digit 5 — Agility Robotics — 2026-09-15 — https://www.agilityrobotics.com/content/agility-unveils-digit-5-humanoid-robot-built-for-cooperatively-safe-work-at-scale — company-claim
  - Tesla rotary and linear actuators with roller screws — Tesla — no primary yet — (seed lead, verify)
- **Notes:** No maker publishes continuous (thermal) torque: the chapter says so and uses illustrative numbers marked `nofact` for the derating example. Gaps: a motor-physics primer source, Unitree motor datasheets, Tesla actuator primaries, thermal-derating papers, a fresh primary for Atlas retiring hydraulics. Aggregator actuator tables are rejected.

### ch06 · Transmissions, joints and sensing the joint
- **Purpose:** The gears, screws, springs and tendons between motor and limb, and how a joint knows where it is and how hard it pushes. Each choice is explained by what it does to safety, control, learning and sim-to-real.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch04 (joints, degrees of freedom, payload); ch05 (torque-speed trade-off; torque constant; current as a sense; integrated actuator modules; rotary vs linear actuators).
- **Estimated length:** about 10,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **06.01 · Why gearing.** Derive what a gear ratio buys and what it costs. *Concepts:* gear ratio (torque multiplied, speed divided; equation); efficiency losses; reflected inertia (the motor's inertia felt at the joint grows with the square of the ratio; equation); why high ratios make a limb stiff and hard to push back.
  - **06.02 · Gear types: planetary, harmonic and cycloidal.** Show the three rotary reducers in humanoids and their trade-offs. *Concepts:* planetary gearboxes; harmonic (strain-wave) drives; cycloidal drives; comparison by ratio, backlash, shock tolerance, size and cost.
  - **06.03 · Screws and linkages: turning rotation into pushing.** Explain how linear actuators drive rotary joints. *Concepts:* the screw principle; ball screws; planetary roller screws; linkage-driven joints (a linear actuator moving a knee or ankle); recap: linear actuators (ch05); the roller-screw vs rotary debate and its thin peer-reviewed evidence.
  - **06.04 · Direct drive, quasi-direct drive and backdrivability.** Show the low-ratio end of the spectrum and why legged robots moved there. *Concepts:* direct drive; quasi-direct drive (owned here); backdrivability (owned here); impact mitigation; recap: current as a sense (ch05) and why it works better at low ratios.
  - **06.05 · Imperfections: backlash, friction, compliance and transparency.** Name the flaws a controller and a simulator must live with. *Concepts:* backlash; friction and stiction; unwanted compliance in the drivetrain; transparency (how faithfully the motor feels the world through the transmission); preview: modelling these for sim-to-real (taught in ch29).
  - **06.06 · Deliberate springs: series-elastic, parallel-elastic and variable-stiffness actuators.** Explain why designers sometimes add springs on purpose. *Concepts:* series-elastic actuators (Pratt and Williamson, 1995); parallel-elastic actuators (2026 humanoid-ankle work); variable-stiffness actuators; shock tolerance and energy storage.
  - **06.07 · Tendons and cables.** Show how motors can sit far from the joint they move. *Concepts:* tendon and cable drives; remote actuation (motors in the forearm, tendons through the wrist; 1X NEO as an attributed example); stretch, friction and routing; preview: tendon-driven hands (taught in ch07).
  - **06.08 · How a joint knows where it is: encoders.** Explain position sensing at the joint. *Concepts:* incremental vs absolute encoders; resolution; dual encoders (motor side and output side) and what their difference reveals; why position data is the backbone of proprioception (preview: proprioception as a data stream, taught in ch08 and ch21).
  - **06.09 · How a joint knows how hard it pushes: joint torque sensing.** Explain force sensing at the joint. *Concepts:* joint torque sensors (strain-gauge type); torque from spring deflection in series-elastic designs; recap: torque estimated from current (ch05); preview: six-axis force/torque sensors at wrist and ankle (taught in ch08).
  - **06.10 · Which maker uses which transmission.** Summarise current practice, honestly graded. *Concepts:* (aside) a table of disclosed transmission choices by robot and joint (Digit 5 cycloidal legs; Schaeffler two-stage planetary; NEO tendon hand; others only where primary sources exist), each row tagged by evidence strength.
- **Running-example hooks:** battery station in 06.04 and 06.09 (feeling a connector seat and a torque tool kick through a transparent, torque-sensed joint); reset in 06.04 (a backdrivable arm is safer around a toddler and gentler on wine glasses, `[inference]` until sourced); greenhouse in 06.06 (compliance for brushing past vines without damage, `[inference]`); dinner in 06.07 (tendon hands and knife grip, previewing ch07).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Series elastic actuators — Pratt, Williamson — 1995 — https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/Series-elastic-actuators-Pratt-Williamson/e99eda93c1090c606800064f7d904fa006e64332 — peer-reviewed
  - Proprioceptive actuator design in the MIT Cheetah — Wensing et al. — 2017 — https://dl.acm.org/doi/abs/10.1109/TRO.2016.2640183 — peer-reviewed
  - Design principles for a family of direct-drive legged robots — Kenneally, De, Koditschek — 2016-07 — https://repository.upenn.edu/ese_papers/705 — peer-reviewed
  - A Dual-Cam Parallel Elastic Actuator for Humanoid Ankles — Jiang, Zhang, Tsagarakis — 2026-08-31 — arXiv 2608.30832 — preprint
  - NEO's Hands — 1X — 2026-07-09 — https://www.1x.tech/discover/neos-hands — company-claim
  - Schaeffler planetary actuator for humanoids — Schaeffler — 2026-01 — https://www.schaeffler.com/en/media/press-releases/press-releases-detail.jsp?id=88156672 — company-claim
  - Agility unveils Digit 5 — Agility Robotics — 2026-09-15 — https://www.agilityrobotics.com/content/agility-unveils-digit-5-humanoid-robot-built-for-cooperatively-safe-work-at-scale — company-claim
  - Humanoid Locomotion with Roller Screw-Driven Knee Joints — authors not recorded — date not confirmed — no URL recorded (Springer chapter) — peer-reviewed (unconfirmed)
- **Notes:** 1X's quasi-direct-drive tendon ratios are a company claim, not independently tested. Tesla's tendon-hand claims are patent or blog only and stay out unless a primary appears. Gaps: harmonic and cycloidal drive primaries (Harmonic Drive, Nabtesco), encoder and torque-sensor datasheets, a variable-stiffness actuator review.

### ch07 · Hands and end-effectors
- **Purpose:** The hand as the hardest part of the body and the tightest data bottleneck: how hands are built, sensed and used with tools, and why makers keep changing their designs.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch02 (Brooks's argument that video lacks touch and force); ch04 (degrees of freedom; payload); ch05 (torque density; current as a sense); ch06 (tendon drives; quasi-direct drive; backdrivability; joint sensing).
- **Estimated length:** about 10,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **07.01 · Why the hand is the hardest part.** Explain why manipulation rests on the hand, and why the hand limits data. *Concepts:* manipulation as controlled contact; the hand as the largest share of a humanoid's degrees of freedom; the hand as the data bottleneck (hardest to teleoperate and to capture from people; preview: taught in ch18 and ch19).
  - **07.02 · Grippers vs dexterous hands.** Lay out the spectrum of end-effectors and when each wins. *Concepts:* parallel-jaw and simple grippers; dexterous multi-fingered hands; task-specific end-effectors and tool changers; the trade-off of simplicity and reliability vs versatility.
  - **07.03 · Degrees of freedom and actuation.** Show how fingers are moved and how designs differ. *Concepts:* hand degrees of freedom, actuated vs coupled joints; direct actuation in the hand; tendon actuation from the forearm (recap: tendon drives, ch06); linkage actuation; hydraulic hands (Sanctuary, seed lead); finger count (Atlas's four-finger choice; NEO, Sharpa Wave and others with more degrees of freedom; all attributed).
  - **07.04 · Sensing in the hand: fingertips, palm and palm cameras.** Explain where a hand's senses go and what coverage buys. *Concepts:* fingertip tactile sensing; palm sensing; palm cameras (Figure 03 as an attributed example); coverage vs resolution (owned here; whole-hand coverage with modest resolution, per Tactile Genesis); preview: how tactile sensors and cameras work (taught in ch08).
  - **07.05 · Strength vs delicacy.** Show why one hand must both grip hard and touch lightly. *Concepts:* grip force range; fingertip force and the smallest detectable force; force control in the hand (recap: backdrivability, ch06); hand payload vs arm payload (recap: payload, ch04).
  - **07.06 · Durability and repair of hands.** Explain why hands wear out first and how designs plan for it. *Concepts:* cycle life and claimed test cycles (company claims); tendon and cover wear; modular, replaceable hands; preview: reliability and maintenance (taught in ch10).
  - **07.07 · Matching human demonstrations.** Explain why hand shape decides how much human data a robot can reuse. *Concepts:* human hand vs robot hand mismatch; why near-human hands ease reuse of human data and why simpler hands can still learn; preview: gloves and hand-pose capture (taught in ch18); preview: hand retargeting (taught in ch25).
  - **07.08 · Tool use: powered tools, knives and cutters.** Show what changes when the hand holds a tool. *Concepts:* tools as extensions of the hand; seating and reacting to a powered tool; knife and cutter safety and control; hand-held tools vs tool-specific end-effectors.
  - **07.09 · Where hands stand in 2026.** Report the current designs and the reversals honestly. *Concepts:* (aside) a dated comparison table of current hands (Figure 03 and the F.04 preview, the new Atlas hand, NEO, Sharpa Wave, and Shadow, Psyonic and Inspire where primaries exist); reported retreats from tendon hands (Figure, Tesla; unconfirmed, attributed); the absence of independent dexterity benchmarks (preview: evaluation, taught in ch43); preview: dexterity as an open problem (taught in ch52).
- **Running-example hooks:** dinner in 07.05 and 07.08 (chef's knife, tongs, a hot pan); reset in 07.04 and 07.05 (wine glass, wet sponge, palm camera inside a rack); battery station in 07.08 (torque tool seating and reaction; connector push, click and tug); greenhouse in 07.05 and 07.08 (cutters; delicate trusses without bruising).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Introducing Figure 03 — Figure — 2025-10-09 — https://www.figure.ai/news/introducing-figure-03 — company-claim
  - Figure F.04 hands report — RuntimeWire — 2026-10-03 — https://runtimewire.com/article/figure-f04-hands-mechanical-complexity — press
  - Robot Hands for Modern AI and Real Work — Boston Dynamics — 2026-10-02 — no URL recorded — company-claim (unconfirmed: primary post not yet found)
  - Boston Dynamics unveils new four-finger hand for Atlas — Robotics & Automation News — 2026-10-02 — https://roboticsandautomationnews.com/2026/10/02/boston-dynamics-unveils-new-four-finger-hand-for-atlas-humanoid-robot/105440/ — press
  - Sharpa Wave product page — Sharpa — 2026 — https://www.sharpa.com/pages/wave — company-claim (unconfirmed: date)
  - NEO's Hands — 1X — 2026-07-09 — https://www.1x.tech/discover/neos-hands — company-claim
  - Tactile Genesis — Chung et al. — 2026-06-21 — arXiv 2606.22332 — preprint
  - Vision-Based Tactile Intelligence for Robotics (survey) — Zhou et al. — 2026-08-16 — arXiv 2608.15490 — preprint
  - Shadow Hand — Shadow Robot — no date — no URL — (seed lead, verify)
  - Sanctuary hydraulic hands — Sanctuary AI — no date — no URL — (seed lead, verify)
- **Notes:** Every hand claim is a company claim or press; there are no independent dexterity benchmarks, and the chapter says so. The F.04 preview has no published specs; the Tesla tendon-hand reversal rests on a reported post and needs a primary. Gaps: Shadow, Psyonic, Inspire and Sanctuary primaries; hand durability data.

### ch08 · The senses: cameras, depth, inertial, force, touch and sound
- **Purpose:** The physics of every sensor on a humanoid, what each measures, how it fails in real sites, and what each adds to learning. ch08 owns the sensor physics; data modalities (ch21) and calibration (ch22) are previewed only.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch04 (the body as the interface for learning); ch05 (current as a sense); ch06 (encoders; joint torque sensing); ch07 (sensor placement in the hand; palm cameras; coverage vs resolution).
- **Estimated length:** about 12,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **08.01 · What a sensor gives a robot.** Set the common vocabulary for every sense. *Concepts:* a sensor as a measurement with rate, resolution, range, noise and delay; sensing the world vs sensing the body; preview: modalities and the data dictionary (taught in ch21); preview: calibration and time synchronisation (taught in ch22).
  - **08.02 · Cameras.** Explain how a camera turns light into data, and why placement matters. *Concepts:* the image sensor; rolling vs global shutter; exposure, frame rate and motion blur; dynamic range and HDR; field of view and lenses; camera placement on the body (head, chest, wrist; recap: palm cameras, ch07).
  - **08.03 · Depth: stereo, time of flight and structured light.** Show three ways to measure distance with light. *Concepts:* stereo depth; time-of-flight depth; structured-light depth; why shiny, transparent and dark surfaces defeat depth sensors.
  - **08.04 · LiDAR.** Explain scanning laser ranging and where humanoids use it. *Concepts:* LiDAR; its trade-offs against cameras and depth sensors for navigation.
  - **08.05 · The body's sense of itself: IMU and proprioception.** Show how a robot knows its own motion and posture. *Concepts:* the inertial measurement unit (accelerometer and gyroscope; drift); recap: encoders and joint torque sensing (ch06); proprioception as the combined body state; state estimation by fusing inertial, joint and contact signals (owned here at an introductory level); preview: balance (taught in ch15).
  - **08.06 · Force/torque sensors.** Explain six-axis force sensing at wrists and ankles. *Concepts:* six-axis force/torque sensors; wrist and ankle placement; contact detection and force control inputs; recap: joint torque sensing (ch06).
  - **08.07 · Touch: tactile sensors and skins.** Explain the main ways to build artificial touch. *Concepts:* vision-based tactile sensors (GelSight line; DIGIT and Digit 360); capacitive sensing; magnetic sensing; electrical impedance tomography skins for whole-body coverage; recap: coverage vs resolution (ch07).
  - **08.08 · Sound.** Show what microphones add beyond speech. *Concepts:* microphones and arrays; contact and event sounds (a connector's click, sizzling); speech input (preview: language models, taught in ch12); multimodal touch that includes audio (Sparsh-X).
  - **08.09 · What each sense adds to learning.** Tie the sensors to model performance. *Concepts:* why vision alone falls short in contact-rich tasks (evidence that proprioception alone was inadequate in a simulation study); learned tactile representations (Sparsh, Sparsh-X); tactile datasets and benchmarks (TacVerse); weak transfer across different tactile sensors (owned here); preview: vision encoders (taught in ch11); preview: multimodal training data (taught in ch21 and ch41).
  - **08.10 · Sensors in sun, steam and dust.** Show how real sites degrade every sense. *Concepts:* glare and direct sunlight; steam, condensation and fogged lenses; dust and grime on lenses and skins; redundancy across senses; preview: sealing and ingress protection (taught in ch09).
  - **08.11 · Sensor suites compared.** Lay out what current humanoids carry. *Concepts:* (aside) a dated table of published sensor suites (Figure 03, the product Atlas and its hand, Digit 5, others only from primary spec sheets), each cell attributed.
- **Running-example hooks:** dinner in 08.02 (reading labels and use-by dates; judging colour), 08.10 (steam and glare over the hob) and 08.06 (probe thermometer as a tool, not a body sensor); reset in 08.02 and 08.03 (wrist and palm cameras; transparent glasses defeat depth); battery station in 08.03 (shiny busbars), 08.06 and 08.08 (confirming a connector click by force and sound); greenhouse in 08.02 (ripeness by colour), 08.10 (sunlight, humidity) and 08.04 (navigating long rows).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Introducing Figure 03 — Figure — 2025-10-09 — https://www.figure.ai/news/introducing-figure-03 — company-claim
  - Meta FAIR robotics: Sparsh, Digit 360, Digit Plexus — Meta — 2024-10-31 — https://ai.meta.com/blog/fair-robotics-open-source/ — company-claim
  - Tactile Beyond Pixels (Sparsh-X) — Higuera et al. — 2025-06-17 — arXiv 2506.14754 — preprint
  - TacVerse — Wei et al. — 2026-06-24 — arXiv 2606.25877 — preprint
  - Tactile Genesis — Chung et al. — 2026-06-21 — arXiv 2606.22332 — preprint
  - Vision-Based Tactile Intelligence for Robotics (survey) — Zhou et al. — 2026-08-16 — arXiv 2608.15490 — preprint
  - 3D-Printed Conformal EIT Skin — Chen et al. — 2026-08-03 — arXiv 2608.02080 — preprint
  - GelSight and DIGIT original papers — authors not recorded — no date — no URL — (seed lead, verify)
- **Notes:** Sensor-suite comparisons found so far come from aggregators (rejected); the 08.11 table needs primary spec sheets for Atlas, Optimus and Digit. Gaps: GelSight and DIGIT originals; magnetic-skin primaries (ReSkin, AnySkin seen in search, not opened); camera, depth and microphone basics sources; evidence on sun, steam and dust; T-Rex dataset not opened.

### ch09 · Compute, power, heat and the network
- **Purpose:** The onboard computer, the battery and charging, power distribution, heat, sealing and the network: one coupled budget that limits which models a robot can run, for how long, and where it can work.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch04 (mass; the reference humanoid and its variants); ch05 (motor losses; thermal derating); ch08 (sensor rates and how sites degrade sensors).
- **Estimated length:** about 10,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **09.01 · One body, three budgets: energy, power and heat.** Show that runtime, compute and cooling trade against each other. *Concepts:* energy budget and runtime (runtime equals stored energy divided by average power; equation); power budget (peak vs average draw); heat budget (every watt used becomes heat); why the three are coupled.
  - **09.02 · The onboard computer.** Explain what a robot's computer is and how its capacity is measured. *Concepts:* CPU, GPU and AI accelerators; memory capacity and bandwidth; measures of compute (operations per second; number precision such as FP4); Jetson Thor and IGX Thor as current reference parts (attributed); in-house chips (Tesla, gap); preview: the safety controller as a separate computer (taught in ch10).
  - **09.03 · Inference budgets: making the model fit the body.** Show why a model's size meets the robot's watts and memory. *Concepts:* inference (running a trained model to choose the next action) and its cost per decision; model size vs memory; compute per second at a given decision rate; watts per decision; preview: neural networks (taught in ch11); preview: latency budgets and clock rates (taught in ch16); preview: distillation and quantisation (taught in ch33).
  - **09.04 · Off-board compute and the network.** Explain what moves off the robot, and what happens when the link fails. *Concepts:* on-site edge servers vs the cloud; Wi-Fi and private 5G; bandwidth, latency and dropouts; offline operation; preview: splitting the brain between robot and server (taught in ch16); preview: remote assistance (taught in ch46) and over-the-air updates (taught in ch47).
  - **09.05 · Batteries.** Explain how a humanoid stores energy, and what a battery spec means. *Concepts:* cells, energy density and pack capacity; pack voltage; the battery management system; battery safety and transport certification (UN38.3, per Figure 03); runtime vs shift length.
  - **09.06 · Recharging: swapping, wireless charging and fast charging.** Compare the three ways to keep a robot working. *Concepts:* autonomous battery swapping (product Atlas, UBTech Walker S2); inductive charging through the feet (Figure 03); fast charging with a small battery (Digit 5); comparing them for uptime and site layout (`[inference]`: no neutral comparison exists).
  - **09.07 · Power distribution.** Show how energy gets from the pack to every motor, sensor and computer. *Concepts:* the power bus and voltage conversion; protection (fuses and isolation); peak current during fast motions (recap: motor current, ch05); energy returned when motors brake (gap: source needed).
  - **09.08 · Heat: thermal management in sealed bodies.** Explain how the body sheds heat, and what sealing does to that. *Concepts:* heat sources (motors, compute, battery); conduction, convection and fans; why sealing traps heat; operating temperature ranges (product Atlas as an attributed example); recap: thermal derating (ch05).
  - **09.09 · Ingress protection: sealing against water and dust.** Explain IP ratings and the trade-off with cooling. *Concepts:* the IP code and how its two digits are read (primary source needed); what a given rating does and does not promise; sealing vs cooling vs maintenance access; washdown, steam and humidity.
  - **09.10 · The reference humanoid's budget, by variant.** Pull the chapter together into one worked budget. *Concepts:* (aside) an illustrative energy, compute and heat budget for the legged reference humanoid and its three variants, with numbers marked `nofact` except where sourced; why the wheeled base can carry more battery (`[inference]`).
- **Running-example hooks:** dinner in 09.01 (runtime across an evening of cooking) and 09.08 (heat near the hob and oven); reset in 09.04 (home Wi-Fi drops; privacy of off-board processing) and 09.06 (charging in a home); battery station in 09.06 (swap vs charge against cycle time and uptime) and 09.04 (plant network); greenhouse in 09.06 (recharging without blocking the row), 09.08 and 09.09 (heat above 30 °C, humidity, sealing) and 09.04 (network coverage along long rows).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - NVIDIA Jetson Thor brings 2K teraflops of AI compute to robots — The Robot Report — 2025-08-25 — https://www.therobotreport.com/nvidia-jetson-thor-brings-2k-teraflops-of-ai-compute-to-robots/ — press
  - NVIDIA Jetson Thor product page — NVIDIA — no date — no URL — (seed lead, verify)
  - NVIDIA Announces Isaac GR00T Reference Humanoid for Academic Research — NVIDIA — 2026-06 — https://investor.nvidia.com/news/press-release-details/2026/NVIDIA-Announces-NVIDIA-Isaac-GR00T-Reference-Humanoid-Robot-for-Academic-Research/default.aspx — company-claim (unconfirmed: exact day)
  - Introducing Figure 03 — Figure — 2025-10-09 — https://www.figure.ai/news/introducing-figure-03 — company-claim
  - Boston Dynamics unveils new Atlas robot — Boston Dynamics — 2026-01-05 — https://bostondynamics.com/blog/boston-dynamics-unveils-new-atlas-robot-to-revolutionize-industry/ — company-claim
  - Agility unveils Digit 5 — Agility Robotics — 2026-09-15 — https://www.agilityrobotics.com/content/agility-unveils-digit-5-humanoid-robot-built-for-cooperatively-safe-work-at-scale — company-claim
  - UBTech Walker S2 autonomous battery swap — CnEVPost — 2025-07-17 — https://cnevpost.com/2025/07/17/ubtech-humanoid-robot-autonomous-battery-swap/ — press (find UBTech's own release)
- **Notes:** Ownership: ch09 owns the compute and network hardware; ch16 owns which brain layer runs where and the latency budget. Runtime claims vary widely and listicle figures are rejected; Atlas's IP67 is secondary only. Gaps: Tesla chips, thermal data, power-distribution sources, network evidence, Hexagon AEON, an IP-code primary, a battery-basics source.

### ch10 · Safety hardware, reliability and maintenance
- **Purpose:** How a humanoid's body is built to stop, limit itself, fall safely and keep working, and what is (and is not) publicly known about its reliability. ch10 owns the hardware; ch44 owns safety engineering of learned systems; ch45 owns standards.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch04 (mass and fall energy; design for manufacture); ch05 (current as a sense; thermal limits; actuator wear); ch06 (backdrivability; joint torque sensing); ch07 (hand durability); ch09 (batteries; the onboard computer; sealing).
- **Estimated length:** about 10,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **10.01 · What safety hardware is for.** Show where a humanoid's hazards come from and which layer handles each. *Concepts:* hazardous energy in a humanoid (moving mass, stored electrical energy, falls); hardware safety vs software safety layers; preview: hazard analysis and functional safety (taught in ch44); preview: standards and certification (taught in ch45).
  - **10.02 · Stopping: emergency stop, safe torque off and brakes.** Explain the ways a robot is made to stop, and why stopping a biped is hard. *Concepts:* emergency stop; safe torque off; holding brakes; the fail-passive gap (cutting power to a balancing robot makes it fall; Ding et al. 2026); controlled stop vs power cut; why wheeled and rail variants ease the problem (`[inference]`).
  - **10.03 · Independent safety controllers.** Explain the separate computer whose only job is to stop the robot. *Concepts:* the independent safety controller (Digit 5 on NVIDIA Halos, with FORT Robotics, attributed); separation from the AI computer (recap: onboard computer, ch09); preview: safety ratings (SIL and PL, taught in ch44).
  - **10.04 · Limiting force and speed, and detecting collisions.** Show how the body limits harm when contact happens. *Concepts:* torque, speed and power limits in drive firmware; collision detection from current and joint torque (recap: ch05 and ch06); backdrivability as passive safety (recap: ch06); preview: power-and-force limiting and speed-and-separation as certified safety functions (taught in ch44).
  - **10.05 · Falling safely.** Explain what a fall does to a humanoid and how designs reduce the harm. *Concepts:* the physics of a fall (recap: mass and centre of mass, ch04); protective materials (soft responsive materials surviving drops); learned protective falls (SafeFall's reduced peak impact); fall detection and getting back up; preview: fall management in the safety case (taught in ch44); preview: balance control (taught in ch15).
  - **10.06 · Soft covers and the home body.** Show how the outer skin changes safety and upkeep. *Concepts:* soft exteriors (foam and washable soft goods; Figure 03 as an attributed example); pinch points and covered joints; cleanable covers for kitchens; recap: the soft home variant (ch04).
  - **10.07 · Reliability: what MTBF and uptime mean, and what is published.** Teach the measures and state plainly that no public numbers exist. *Concepts:* reliability, mean time between failures and mean time to repair (equations); availability (uptime) from MTBF and MTTR (equation, with illustrative numbers marked `nofact`); the evidence gap: no public MTBF or uptime figures for any humanoid as of the build date; reading claims like "minimal failures" and fleet-hour totals (Agility's fleet hours, company claim); preview: fleet KPIs (taught in ch47).
  - **10.08 · Duty cycles and design lifetime.** Explain how use patterns set how long parts last. *Concepts:* duty cycle at the system level; design lifetime and accumulated wear; recap: actuator wear and thermal derating (ch05); claimed cycle tests (company claims).
  - **10.09 · Maintenance, spares and field repair.** Show how a body is designed to be fixed quickly. *Concepts:* design for maintainability (owned here: modular, swappable limbs, hands and batteries); preventive, predictive and corrective maintenance; field-replaceable units and spares; preview: field service operations (taught in ch47).
  - **10.10 · Lessons from deployments so far.** Report what makers have disclosed about failures and redesigns, attributed. *Concepts:* (aside) Figure's BMW write-up (hours worked, the forearm as top failure point, no MTBF); BMW's move to Figure 03 with soft components and wireless charging; Digit 5's safety controller; what a buyer should ask for that is not yet published; preview: deployment readiness (taught in ch46).
- **Running-example hooks:** battery station in 10.02 (a biped stopping or falling beside a live high-voltage pack; the wheeled variant's advantage) and 10.04 (contact limits near busbars); reset in 10.04 and 10.06 (a toddler or pet nearby; soft covers); dinner in 10.02 (emergency stop during a kitchen fire risk) and 10.06 (cleanable covers); greenhouse in 10.09 (maintenance in heat and humidity) and 10.02 (cutters held during a stop).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - F.02 Contributed to the Production of 30,000 Cars at BMW — Figure — 2025-11-19 — https://www.figure.ai/news/production-at-bmw — company-claim
  - BMW Group advances the use of physical AI with Figure 03 project in Spartanburg — BMW Group — 2026-06-25 — https://www.press.bmwgroup.com/global/article/detail/T0458778EN/bmw-group-advances-the-use-of-physical-ai-in-production-with-figure-03-project-in-spartanburg?language=en — company-claim
  - Toward Certified Functional Safety for Industrial Humanoid Robots — Ding, Cui, Wang, Wen — 2026-08-03 — arXiv 2608.02809 — preprint
  - SafeFall: Learning Protective Control — Meng et al. — 2025-11-23 — arXiv 2511.18509 — preprint
  - Soft Responsive Materials Enhance Humanoid Safety — Wang et al. — 2026-01-06 — arXiv 2601.02857 — preprint
  - Agility unveils Digit 5 — Agility Robotics — 2026-09-15 — https://www.agilityrobotics.com/content/agility-unveils-digit-5-humanoid-robot-built-for-cooperatively-safe-work-at-scale — company-claim
  - Introducing Figure 03 — Figure — 2025-10-09 — https://www.figure.ai/news/introducing-figure-03 — company-claim
  - VIGOR and stoppability papers — authors not recorded — 2026 — arXiv 2602.16511 and 2609.02358 — preprint (unconfirmed: not opened)
  - Production Atlas design notes — Boston Dynamics — no date — no URL — (seed lead, verify)
- **Notes:** No public MTBF or uptime data exist for any humanoid (per the scan): the chapter teaches the measures with `nofact` numbers and says plainly that no real values are published. Two BMW releases are in play (2026-06-25 here; 2026-06-30 under ch02): verify both and what each says. ISO 25785-1's draft status is secondary only and belongs to ch45; a reliability-engineering primer source is needed for the 10.07 equations.

- ch05: reframed "actuator models for simulation" as 05.09 (real vs ideal actuators), with actuator networks and system identification previewed to ch29 — ch29 owns them.

- ch07: added 07.01 (why the hand is the hardest part) and 07.09 (where hands stand in 2026) — the scan reports design reversals and no independent benchmarks; tactile-sensor physics moved to ch08, with ch07 owning placement and coverage vs resolution — teach once.

- ch09: added 09.01 (the coupled budgets) and 09.10 (worked budget by variant); split compute into 09.02 and 09.03; ch09 owns compute and network hardware while ch16 owns where each layer runs — the seed listed off-board inference in both chapters.
