## P2 · Foundations from zero

### ch11 · How machines learn: neural networks from scratch
- **Purpose:** How a machine learns a skill from examples, built up from one artificial neuron to the pre-trained vision encoders and scaling laws behind today's robot models. ch11 owns the generic machinery of learning that every later training chapter uses.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** no.
- **Depends on:** ch03 (end-to-end visuomotor policies as the moment learning reached robots); ch08 (sensors as the inputs a model sees; vision encoders previewed); ch09 (inference and its cost; measures of compute and number precision; GPUs and accelerators; model size vs memory).
- **Estimated length:** about 12,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **11.01 · Learning as fitting a function.** Show that a learned model is a function with adjustable settings, fitted to examples instead of written by hand. *Concepts:* a model as a function from inputs to outputs (owned here); parameters (weights) as the adjustable settings; training vs inference (recap: inference, ch09); supervised learning from labelled examples (owned here); kinds of learning signal (labels, the data itself, rewards; self-supervised learning taught in 11.09; preview: reinforcement learning, taught in ch14); why hand-written rules fail for perception (`[inference]`, reasoning shown).
  - **11.02 · Neurons, layers and deep networks.** Build a network from one neuron up, and show why depth needs special care. *Concepts:* the artificial neuron (weighted sum plus a non-linearity; equation); activation functions and why non-linearity is essential; layers and the multilayer perceptron; depth and width; parameter count as a measure of size (recap: model size vs memory, ch09); residual connections and normalisation layers as what makes very deep networks trainable (owned here).
  - **11.03 · Loss functions: saying what "good" means.** Show how a single number turns "do better" into something a computer can push on. *Concepts:* the loss function (owned here); mean squared error for continuous outputs (equation); softmax, turning scores into probabilities (owned here); cross-entropy for choices among categories (equation); the loss as a stand-in for the real goal, and how the two can diverge (preview: reward design, taught in ch14).
  - **11.04 · Gradient descent and backpropagation.** Derive how a network improves itself, one small step at a time. *Concepts:* the gradient as the direction of steepest increase; gradient descent (owned here; update rule, equation); the learning rate; stochastic gradient descent with mini-batches; backpropagation via the chain rule (owned here); why GPUs suit training (recap: GPUs and accelerators, ch09).
  - **11.05 · Optimisers and the training run.** Show what a real training run looks like beyond the basic update. *Concepts:* momentum; Adam and its adaptive step sizes (equation, in an "Under the hood" block); learning-rate schedules and warm-up; batch size; reading loss curves; checkpoints; training compute counted in floating-point operations (recap: measures of compute and number precision, ch09).
  - **11.06 · Overfitting, regularisation and data splits.** Explain why a model that aces its training data can still fail, and how that is caught before launch. *Concepts:* training, validation and test splits (owned here); underfitting and overfitting; generalisation (owned here); regularisation (weight decay, dropout, early stopping); data augmentation; distribution shift between training and deployment data (owned here); preview: generalisation axes in robot evaluation (taught in ch43); preview: the sim-to-real gap (taught in ch29).
  - **11.07 · Embeddings and learned representations.** Show how a network turns things into lists of numbers whose distances carry meaning. *Concepts:* the embedding as a learned vector (owned here); similarity as distance or angle between vectors; representations and latent spaces (owned here); pre-training and reuse of representations (transfer learning, owned here at a generic level; preview: training stages for robot models, taught in ch30); preview: text tokens (taught in ch12); preview: autoencoder latents (taught in ch13).
  - **11.08 · Convolutional networks: how machines first learned to see.** Explain the architecture that made image recognition work. *Concepts:* the image as a grid of numbers (recap: the image sensor, ch08); convolution and weight sharing; feature hierarchies from edges to objects; pooling and downsampling; inductive bias (what an architecture assumes before it sees data; owned here).
  - **11.09 · Pre-trained vision encoders: self-supervised and contrastive.** Show how today's robot models get their eyes, and why the choice is still unsettled. *Concepts:* the vision encoder as a reusable front end (owned here; previewed in ch08); self-supervised learning without labels (owned here; the DINO line from DINOv2 to DINOv3, with dense features and distilled smaller students); contrastive image-text learning (owned here; SigLIP 2); fusing two encoders for a robot model (OpenVLA's SigLIP plus DINOv2, and the small gain it reported); which encoder suits control as an open question; preview: the vision transformer architecture (taught in ch12); preview: model distillation (taught in ch33).
  - **11.10 · Scaling laws.** Explain how loss falls predictably with model size, data and compute, and where the rule breaks. *Concepts:* power-law scaling (owned here; equation); compute-optimal allocation between model size and data (the Chinchilla correction to Kaplan et al.); data-constrained scaling with repeated epochs (2026 refits); disputes over how the fits are made (attributed, unopened lead); preview: robot data scaling (taught in ch17); preview: scaling evidence for robot foundation models (taught in ch31).
- **Running-example hooks:** greenhouse in 11.01, 11.03 and 11.06 (a ripeness classifier as the first worked model; a variety unseen in training as distribution shift; seasonal data); battery station in 11.03 (a "connector seated" classifier from force and sound, where a missed failure costs far more than a false alarm, `[inference]`); dinner in 11.09 (a pre-trained encoder reading packaging and judging colour); reset in 11.06 (the first week in a new home as distribution shift).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Adam: A Method for Stochastic Optimization — Kingma, Ba — 2014-12-22 — arXiv 1412.6980 — preprint
  - Scaling Laws for Neural Language Models — Kaplan et al. — 2020-01-23 — arXiv 2001.08361 — preprint
  - Training Compute-Optimal LLMs (Chinchilla) — Hoffmann et al. — 2022-03-29 — arXiv 2203.15556 — preprint
  - Practical Scaling Laws (data-constrained) — Bryant — 2026-05-09 — arXiv 2605.09189 — preprint
  - IsoFLOP-bias critique of Chinchilla fits — authors not recorded — 2026-03 — arXiv 2603.22339 — preprint (unconfirmed: not opened)
  - DINOv2 — Oquab et al. — 2023-04-14 — arXiv 2304.07193 — preprint
  - DINOv3 — Siméoni et al. (Meta) — 2025-08-13 — arXiv 2508.10104 — preprint
  - SigLIP 2 — Tschannen et al. (Google DeepMind) — 2025-02-20 — arXiv 2502.14786 — preprint
  - OpenVLA — Kim et al. — 2024-06-13 — arXiv 2406.09246 — preprint
- **Notes:** Core equations: the neuron; mean squared error and cross-entropy; the gradient-descent update; Adam (under the hood); a power-law scaling curve. The ViT architecture moves to ch12 because it needs attention; robot data scaling (Lin et al., arXiv 2410.18647) belongs to ch17. Gaps: backpropagation (1986), ResNet, CNN and original contrastive image-text primaries not yet confirmed; a source for dropout and weight decay.

### ch12 · Transformers, language models and vision-language models
- **Purpose:** How the transformer works, how it becomes a language model and then a vision-language model, and why these models now serve as the "common sense" backbone of many robot brains. ch12 owns the generic machinery; robot-specific architectures belong to ch31.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** no.
- **Depends on:** ch09 (memory capacity and bandwidth; inference budgets); ch11 (neurons and layers; softmax and cross-entropy; gradient descent; residual connections and normalisation; embeddings and transfer learning; self-supervised learning; vision encoders; scaling laws).
- **Estimated length:** about 12,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **12.01 · From text to tokens.** Show how text becomes a sequence of numbers a network can read. *Concepts:* tokens and tokenisation (owned here; sub-word vocabularies); the vocabulary; token embeddings (recap: embeddings, ch11); sequence length as a cost driver; preview: tokenisers for images, video and actions (taught in ch13).
  - **12.02 · Attention.** Derive the mechanism that lets each token look at every other one. *Concepts:* self-attention (owned here; queries, keys and values; scaled dot-product attention, equation); attention weights as a soft look-up (recap: softmax, ch11); multi-head attention; causal masking (each token sees only the past); cross-attention, one sequence attending to another (owned here).
  - **12.03 · The transformer block.** Assemble attention into the repeating unit of every modern model. *Concepts:* the transformer block (attention plus a feed-forward network, with residual connections and normalisation; recap: ch11); positional information (owned here); encoder vs decoder stacks; why compute and memory grow with the square of sequence length.
  - **12.04 · Next-token pre-training.** Explain how predicting the next word, at scale, yields broad knowledge. *Concepts:* the language-modelling objective (equation; the chain rule of probability; recap: cross-entropy, ch11); self-supervised pre-training on text (recap: ch11); autoregressive decoding and sampling temperature (owned here); in-context and few-shot learning (GPT-3); confident errors, or hallucination (owned here at a generic level; source needed); recap: scaling laws (ch11).
  - **12.05 · Fine-tuning and instruction tuning.** Show how a raw text predictor becomes a helpful assistant. *Concepts:* fine-tuning (owned here at a generic level); supervised instruction tuning on prompt-response pairs (owned here); the InstructGPT recipe at a glance (a supervised step, then a preference step); preview: reward models, RLHF and preference optimisation (taught in ch14); preview: parameter-efficient fine-tuning (taught in ch33).
  - **12.06 · Vision transformers and vision-language models.** Show how images enter a language model. *Concepts:* the vision transformer (owned here; an image cut into patches treated as tokens; previewed in ch11); the vision-language model (owned here: a vision encoder, a connector that maps image features into token space, and a language model; recap: vision encoders, ch11); interleaved image and text inputs; current open families (Gemma 3 and Gemma 4, Qwen3-VL) as dated table rows with sizes; what VLMs still get wrong about space and physics (source needed).
  - **12.07 · Reasoning models and chain of thought.** Explain why letting a model think step by step before answering can help, and what that costs. *Concepts:* chain of thought (owned here); test-time compute, spending more computation per answer (owned here); thinking modes in current models (Gemma 4; Gemini Robotics 1.5; attributed); evidence that RL elicits reasoning (DeepSeek-R1; preview: GRPO, taught in ch14); the latency cost of thinking on a robot (preview: latency budgets, taught in ch16); preview: embodied chain of thought (taught in ch32).
  - **12.08 · Mixture of experts.** Show how a model can be huge yet cheap to run per token. *Concepts:* the mixture-of-experts layer (owned here; a router sends each token to a few expert sub-networks; top-k gate, equation); total vs active parameters (DeepSeek-V3's total and active counts, per its report); load balancing across experts; MoE vision-language models (Qwen3-VL, Gemma 4); memory still set by total parameters (recap: memory capacity, ch09).
  - **12.09 · Context, memory and long inputs.** Explain how much a model can hold in mind at once, and why that is expensive. *Concepts:* the context window (owned here; Qwen3-VL's long context as a dated example); the key-value cache and why memory grows with context (owned here; source needed); long video and long tasks as pressure on context; preview: memory and long context for robot policies (taught in ch32); preview: memory in video world models (taught in ch37).
  - **12.10 · VLMs as robot backbones.** Show why many robot models start from a VLM, and what that buys and costs. *Concepts:* reusing web knowledge for robots (π0.5 on a PaliGemma-style backbone of SigLIP plus Gemma, attributed); reasoning before acting (Gemini Robotics 1.5, attributed); the gap between a model that describes and one that acts (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); preview: the vision-language-action model (taught in ch31); preview: mid-training toward the physical world (taught in ch32); preview: fast-slow designs (taught in ch16).
- **Running-example hooks:** reset in 12.02 and 12.10 ("leave my glass": grounding a phrase to one object among many); dinner in 12.06 and 12.07 (reading labels and "may contain" warnings; reasoning about hidden allergens, where one confident error is a failure); battery station in 12.09 (work instructions and torque settings pulled into context from plant systems); greenhouse in 12.06 (describing signs of pests and disease for a scouting report).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Attention Is All You Need — Vaswani et al. — 2017-06-12 — arXiv 1706.03762 — preprint
  - Language Models are Few-Shot Learners (GPT-3) — Brown et al. — 2020-05-28 — arXiv 2005.14165 — preprint
  - Training language models to follow instructions with human feedback (InstructGPT) — Ouyang et al. — 2022-03-04 — arXiv 2203.02155 — preprint
  - An Image is Worth 16x16 Words (ViT) — Dosovitskiy et al. — 2020-10-22 — arXiv 2010.11929 — preprint
  - Gemma 4 Technical Report — Gemma Team — 2026-07-02 — arXiv 2607.02770 — tech-report
  - Qwen3-VL Technical Report — Bai et al. — 2025-11-26 — arXiv 2511.21631 — tech-report
  - DeepSeek-V3 Technical Report — DeepSeek-AI — 2024-12-27 — arXiv 2412.19437 — tech-report
  - DeepSeek-R1 (Nature) — DeepSeek-AI — 2025-09 — https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-025-09422-z — peer-reviewed (unconfirmed: day)
  - π0.5 — Physical Intelligence — 2025-04-22 — arXiv 2504.16054 — preprint
  - Gemini Robotics 1.5 — Gemini Robotics Team — 2025-10-02 — arXiv 2510.03342 — tech-report
- **Notes:** Core equations: scaled dot-product attention; the next-token objective via the chain rule of probability; the top-k MoE gate. RLHF mechanics move to ch14, which owns preferences. Further leads: Gemma 3 (arXiv 2503.19786); MoE survey (arXiv 2608.08650, unopened). Gaps: hallucination, key-value cache, VLM spatial weaknesses and PaliGemma primaries; π0.5 backbone sizes came from secondary pages; reasoning claims rest largely on company reports.

### ch13 · Generative models: autoencoders, diffusion, flow matching and autoregression
- **Purpose:** How a network learns to produce new samples (images, video or robot actions) rather than one fixed answer, taught in full depth from the basics. ch13 owns the generic generative machinery and the first robot use of it (Diffusion Policy and action chunking); action representations and VLA architectures belong to ch31, and video world-model internals to ch37.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** no.
- **Depends on:** ch03 (Diffusion Policy, action chunking and flow-matching VLAs previewed as milestones); ch09 (inference budgets); ch11 (loss functions; gradient descent; representations and latent spaces; vision encoders); ch12 (tokens; attention and cross-attention; the transformer block; autoregressive decoding).
- **Estimated length:** about 12,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **13.01 · What a generative model does.** Explain the difference between predicting one answer and sampling from a whole range of good answers. *Concepts:* a probability distribution over data (owned here); sampling (owned here); conditional generation (given a prompt, image or observation); multimodal distributions and why averaging them fails (two good ways round an obstacle average into a collision; owned here); likelihood vs sample quality.
  - **13.02 · Autoencoders and variational autoencoders.** Show how a network learns to compress data and rebuild it. *Concepts:* the autoencoder (encoder, bottleneck, decoder; owned here); the latent space (recap: representations, ch11); the variational autoencoder (owned here; the evidence lower bound, equation: reconstruction plus a KL term); the conditional VAE (owned here; used by ACT in 13.09); blurry outputs as the classic VAE weakness.
  - **13.03 · Tokenisers for images, video and actions.** Explain how continuous signals become discrete tokens a transformer can model. *Concepts:* vector quantisation and codebooks (owned here); image and video tokenisers that compress in space and time; compression ratio vs fidelity; turning actions into tokens by binning (owned here; illustrative numbers marked `nofact`); recap: text tokens (ch12); preview: action tokenisers such as FAST (taught in ch31); preview: video compression inside world models (taught in ch37).
  - **13.04 · Diffusion.** Derive how a model learns to turn noise into data by removing noise step by step. *Concepts:* the forward noising process (owned here; equation); the learned reverse (denoising) process; the noise-prediction objective (DDPM; equation); the score-based view and its unification with diffusion (Song et al.); the number of sampling steps and its cost; guidance toward a condition (source needed).
  - **13.05 · Flow matching and rectified flow.** Show the simpler cousin of diffusion that many robot models now use. *Concepts:* the velocity field and the flow from noise to data (owned here); the flow-matching objective (owned here; equation); straight paths and rectified flow; sampling by integrating an ordinary differential equation in a few steps (Euler step; equation); how flow matching relates to diffusion.
  - **13.06 · Diffusion transformers and latent diffusion.** Show how the denoiser is built at scale. *Concepts:* the denoiser network (U-Net vs transformer); the diffusion transformer, DiT (owned here; recap: the transformer block, ch12); latent diffusion, generating in an autoencoder's latent space (owned here; recap: 13.02; original source needed); conditioning by cross-attention and adaptive normalisation (recap: cross-attention, ch12); the 2025 argument that the autoencoder step may be unnecessary (lead to verify); preview: video diffusion transformers (taught in ch37).
  - **13.07 · Autoregressive generation beyond text.** Show how "predict the next token" extends to images, video and actions, and where it struggles. *Concepts:* recap: autoregressive decoding and the chain rule of probability (ch12); autoregressive image, video and action generation (owned here at a generic level); compounding errors over long sequences (owned here); one token at a time vs parallel denoising; hybrids of the two (preview: diffusion forcing, taught in ch37).
  - **13.08 · Fast sampling: distillation, consistency models and one-step flows.** Explain how many denoising steps are cut to a few, or to one. *Concepts:* why step count sets latency (recap: inference budgets, ch09); step distillation, a few-step student copying a many-step teacher (owned here at the sampler level); consistency models (owned here); one-step flows (MeanFlow, then Improved MeanFlow); the quality-speed trade-off; preview: model distillation and quantisation for deployment (taught in ch33).
  - **13.09 · Generating actions: Diffusion Policy, action chunking and flow-matching heads.** Show how generative models became the way robots output motion. *Concepts:* the robot policy, a mapping from what the robot senses to what it does (owned here); imitation learning from demonstrations at a generic level (owned here; preview: how demonstrations are collected, taught in ch19; preview: behaviour-cloning objectives, taught in ch31); the policy as a conditional generative model of actions; Diffusion Policy (owned here; previewed in ch03); action chunking with ACT's conditional VAE (owned here; previewed in ch03); flow-matching action heads (π0 as an attributed example; preview: action representations and VLA architectures, taught in ch31); one-step flow policies (2025–26 single-group preprints, treated cautiously); the debate over whether diffusion policies memorise (lead to verify); preview: real-time chunking and asynchronous inference (taught in ch16).
  - **13.10 · Choosing a generative model.** Lay the families side by side for a product reader. *Concepts:* (aside) a comparison table of VAEs, autoregressive models, diffusion and flow matching by sample quality, diversity, speed, training stability and controllability (`[inference]` where no head-to-head source exists); where each family reappears later (preview: world models, taught in ch36–ch37; WAMs, taught in ch40).
- **Running-example hooks:** dinner in 13.01 and 13.09 (two valid ways to stir or flip, so an averaged action fails); greenhouse in 13.01 (many valid cut points on one truss); reset in 13.09 (action chunks for a two-handed dishwasher-loading motion); battery station in 13.08 (sampling latency against a force-controlled insertion, and why few-step generation matters, `[inference]`).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - DDPM — Ho et al. — 2020-06-19 — arXiv 2006.11239 — preprint
  - Score-Based Generative Modeling through SDEs — Song et al. — 2020-11-26 — arXiv 2011.13456 — preprint
  - Flow Matching for Generative Modeling — Lipman et al. — 2022-10-06 — arXiv 2210.02747 — preprint
  - Rectified Flow — Liu et al. — 2022-09-07 — arXiv 2209.03003 — preprint
  - Scalable Diffusion Models with Transformers (DiT) — Peebles, Xie — 2022-12-19 — arXiv 2212.09748 — preprint
  - Consistency Models — Song et al. — 2023-03-02 — arXiv 2303.01469 — preprint
  - Mean Flows for One-step Generative Modeling — Geng et al. — 2025-05-19 — arXiv 2505.13447 — preprint
  - Diffusion Policy — Chi et al. — 2023-03 (arXiv; IJRR 2025) — arXiv 2303.04137 — peer-reviewed (unconfirmed: arXiv day)
  - Learning Fine-Grained Bimanual Manipulation (ACT) — Zhao et al. — 2023-04-23 — arXiv 2304.13705 — preprint
  - π0 — Black et al. — 2024-10-31 — arXiv 2410.24164 — preprint
- **Notes:** Core equations: the VAE evidence lower bound; DDPM forward noising and noise-prediction loss; the flow-matching loss and the Euler sampling step; the autoregressive factorisation (recap). Further leads: Improved MeanFlow (Dec 2025), One-Step Flow Policy (arXiv 2603.12480), ManiFlow (Sep 2025), FAST (Jan 2025; ch31), Demystifying Diffusion Policies (arXiv 2505.05787, unread), VAE-free latent diffusion (Oct 2025). Gaps: VAE, vector-quantisation, latent-diffusion and guidance originals unscanned; ch03 dates Diffusion Policy's arXiv 2023-03-07, the ch13 scan leaves the day unconfirmed.

### ch14 · Reinforcement learning from zero
- **Purpose:** How an agent learns from rewards rather than examples, from the basic decision problem to the RL methods that now train large models. ch14 owns RL fundamentals, learning from preferences and RL for large models (GRPO); RL in simulation (ch28), for robot policies (ch34) and for world models (ch38) are previewed only.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** no.
- **Depends on:** ch11 (loss functions and their gap from the real goal; gradient descent; distribution shift); ch12 (the InstructGPT recipe; reasoning models and chain of thought); ch13 (the robot policy; imitation learning; sampling from a distribution).
- **Estimated length:** about 13,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **14.01 · The RL problem: agents, environments and rewards.** Frame learning by trial and error from first principles. *Concepts:* agent and environment (owned here); state, action and reward; stochastic policies (recap: the robot policy, ch13); the Markov decision process (owned here); episodes; partial observability; how RL differs from supervised learning (recap: ch11); preview: the environment interface and RL gyms (taught in ch27).
  - **14.02 · Returns, discounting and value.** Define what the agent maximises and how future reward is valued. *Concepts:* the return (owned here; equation); discounting and the discount factor; the value function and the Q-function (owned here); the Bellman equation (owned here; equation); the advantage (owned here).
  - **14.03 · Learning values: temporal-difference learning and Q-learning.** Show how an agent learns values from its own experience. *Concepts:* Monte Carlo vs temporal-difference estimates; the TD error (owned here; equation); Q-learning (owned here; update equation); deep Q-learning with a neural network (source needed); the instability of combining bootstrapping, function approximation and off-policy data (Sutton and Barto).
  - **14.04 · Policy gradients, actor-critic and PPO.** Show how to improve a policy directly by nudging it toward actions that worked. *Concepts:* the policy-gradient theorem (owned here; equation); baselines and variance; actor-critic methods (owned here); on-policy learning; PPO and its clipped objective (owned here; equation); sample inefficiency and why massively parallel simulation suits PPO (preview: taught in ch28).
  - **14.05 · Off-policy RL and soft actor-critic.** Show how to reuse old experience, and why that matters when data is expensive. *Concepts:* off-policy learning and the replay buffer (owned here); maximum-entropy RL and SAC (owned here; objective in an "Under the hood" block); sample efficiency vs stability; preview: real-world RL on robots (taught in ch34).
  - **14.06 · Exploration.** Explain the trade-off between trying new things and using what already works. *Concepts:* the exploration-exploitation trade-off (owned here); random and entropy-driven exploration (recap: maximum entropy, 14.05); sparse rewards as an exploration problem; demonstrations as a way past exploration (preview: imitation then RL, taught in ch34); preview: safe exploration (taught in ch34 and ch44).
  - **14.07 · Model-based RL.** Show how a learned model of the world lets an agent practise in imagination. *Concepts:* learned dynamics models (owned here); planning vs learning in imagination; DreamerV3 as one set of settings across many domains; model error and how a policy exploits it; preview: world models (taught in ch36); preview: RL inside world models (taught in ch38 and ch39); preview: model predictive control (taught in ch15).
  - **14.08 · Offline RL.** Show how to learn from logged data without new trial and error. *Concepts:* offline (batch) RL (owned here); overestimating actions the data never tried (recap: distribution shift, ch11); conservative values (CQL); avoiding unseen actions altogether (IQL); preview: learning from deployment logs (taught in ch34 and ch48).
  - **14.09 · Reward design and reward hacking.** Show that what you reward is what you get, including what you did not mean. *Concepts:* sparse vs dense rewards; reward shaping; reward hacking and specification gaming (owned here; primary source needed); verifiable rewards checked by a program vs learned reward models (owned here); recap: the loss as a stand-in for the goal (ch11); preview: success detectors, robot reward models and VLM judges (taught in ch34).
  - **14.10 · Learning from preferences.** Show how human comparisons become a reward signal. *Concepts:* reward models from pairwise comparisons (owned here; Bradley–Terry model, equation); RLHF (owned here; recap: the InstructGPT recipe, ch12); the KL penalty that keeps a tuned model near its starting point (equation); direct preference optimisation without an explicit reward model (primary source needed); over-optimising a reward model.
  - **14.11 · RL for large models: GRPO and relatives.** Explain the RL methods that now train reasoning models, and what theory says about them. *Concepts:* GRPO (owned here; group-relative advantages with no value network; equation); RL with verifiable rewards; DeepSeekMath and DeepSeek-R1 as the dated evidence (recap: reasoning models, ch12); the 2026 analysis of GRPO's gradient as a U-statistic; preview: RL fine-tuning of VLAs (taught in ch34); preview: GRPO-style RL for video world models (taught in ch38).
  - **14.12 · Where RL fits in the robot lifecycle.** Map each RL idea to the later chapters that use it. *Concepts:* (aside) a map of RL uses: massively parallel RL in simulation for locomotion and whole-body control (preview: ch28), RL on robot policies after imitation (preview: ch34), RL for world models (preview: ch38); why robots usually imitate first and reinforce second (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); preview: the training-stage framework (taught in ch30).
- **Running-example hooks:** battery station in 14.01, 14.08 and 14.09 (success is easy to verify, a bolt in spec and a latched connector, but trial and error on live packs is unacceptable, so practice comes from simulation, dummy modules or logs); dinner in 14.09 ("tastes good" cannot be scored, and a reward that only checks the plate invites hacking, `[inference]`); reset in 14.10 ("the board never goes in the dishwasher" as a learned preference); greenhouse in 14.05 and 14.06 (slow, seasonal data rewards sample efficiency; sparse success signals).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Reinforcement Learning: An Introduction, 2nd ed. — Sutton, Barto — 2018-11-13 (per MIT Press listing) — http://incompleteideas.net/book/the-book-2nd.html — peer-reviewed (book)
  - Proximal Policy Optimization Algorithms — Schulman et al. — 2017-07-20 — arXiv 1707.06347 — preprint
  - Soft Actor-Critic — Haarnoja, Zhou, Abbeel, Levine — 2018-01-04 — arXiv 1801.01290 — preprint
  - Conservative Q-Learning — Kumar et al. — 2020-06-08 — arXiv 2006.04779 — preprint
  - Offline RL with Implicit Q-Learning — Kostrikov, Nair, Levine — 2021-10-12 — arXiv 2110.06169 — preprint
  - DreamerV3 (Mastering Diverse Domains through World Models) — Hafner et al. — 2023-01-10 — arXiv 2301.04104 — preprint
  - Training language models to follow instructions with human feedback (InstructGPT) — Ouyang et al. — 2022-03-04 — arXiv 2203.02155 — preprint
  - DeepSeekMath — Shao et al. — 2024-02-05 — arXiv 2402.03300 — preprint
  - DeepSeek-R1 — DeepSeek-AI — 2025-01-22 — arXiv 2501.12948 — peer-reviewed (Nature 2025)
  - Demystifying GRPO: Policy Gradient is a U-Statistic — Zhou et al. — 2026-03-01 — arXiv 2603.01162 — preprint
- **Notes:** Core equations: the discounted return; the Bellman equation; the TD error and Q-learning update; the policy gradient; PPO's clipped objective; the Bradley–Terry model with a KL penalty; GRPO's group-normalised advantage. Gaps (per the scan): no confirmed primaries yet for deep Q-learning, DPO, reward hacking or exploration bonuses. DeepSeek-R1 has an arXiv version (2025-01-22) and a Nature version (2025-09, day unconfirmed): cite the peer-reviewed one and check both.

### ch15 · Kinematics, dynamics and control
- **Purpose:** The classical physics and control that make a humanoid's body move as intended: where its limbs are, what forces move them, how feedback holds a target, and how balance and whole-body motion are posed as optimisation. ch15 owns classical kinematics, dynamics, balance and whole-body control as optimisation; learned whole-body control and the policy–controller interface (ch35) and the layered software stack (ch16) are previewed.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** no.
- **Depends on:** ch04 (links, joints and degrees of freedom; kinematic chains; redundancy; the floating base; centre of mass); ch05 (torque and the torque constant; the current-torque inner loop; saturation, delay and bandwidth); ch06 (backdrivability; series-elastic actuators; encoders; joint torque sensing); ch08 (IMU; force/torque sensors; introductory state estimation); ch10 (falling safely); ch11 (optimisation by gradient); ch14 (policies; model-based RL).
- **Estimated length:** about 12,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **15.01 · Frames, positions and orientations.** Give the language for saying where anything is. *Concepts:* coordinate frames (owned here); position, orientation and pose; rotation representations (rotation matrices, Euler angles and gimbal lock, quaternions; owned here); the homogeneous transform (owned here; equation); chaining transforms; preview: extrinsic calibration (taught in ch22).
  - **15.02 · Forward kinematics.** Show how joint angles determine where the hands and feet are. *Concepts:* forward kinematics (owned here; previewed in ch04; equation as a product of transforms); recap: kinematic chains and the body as a tree (ch04); joint space vs task space (owned here); robot description files as the machine-readable chain (preview: URDF and MJCF, taught in ch27).
  - **15.03 · Inverse kinematics.** Show the harder reverse question: which joint angles put the hand where it must go. *Concepts:* inverse kinematics (owned here; previewed in ch04); no solution, one or many; redundancy and null-space motion (recap: redundancy, ch04); numerical, iterative IK; joint limits; preview: retargeting human motion to robot bodies (taught in ch25).
  - **15.04 · Jacobians: velocities, forces and singularities.** Derive the matrix that links joint motion to hand motion, and hand forces to joint torques. *Concepts:* the Jacobian (owned here; velocity map, equation); force-torque duality through the Jacobian transpose (owned here; equation); singularities and lost directions of motion; manipulability.
  - **15.05 · Dynamics: how torques make motion.** Show how forces, masses and inertia determine movement. *Concepts:* Newton–Euler intuition for one link; the robot's equation of motion (owned here; mass matrix, velocity-dependent and gravity terms, joint torques and contact forces; equation); forward vs inverse dynamics (owned here); gravity compensation; the floating base and underactuation (recap: ch04); recap: state estimation (ch08).
  - **15.06 · Contact and friction.** Explain why touching the world is the hardest part of the physics. *Concepts:* contact forces that push but never pull (unilateral constraints; owned here); friction and the friction cone (owned here); impacts; hybrid dynamics as contacts make and break; preview: why contact is hard to simulate (taught in ch27 and ch29).
  - **15.07 · Feedback control: PD, PID and impedance.** Show how a controller holds a target against disturbances, and how a limb is made firm or soft on purpose. *Concepts:* feedback and error (owned here); the PD law (owned here; previewed in ch05; equation); gains as stiffness and damping; PID and the integral term; feedforward plus feedback; impedance control (owned here; equation) and admittance control; force control; delay as a limit on gains (recap: delay and bandwidth, ch05); recap: the current-torque inner loop (ch05); preview: loop clock rates (taught in ch16).
  - **15.08 · Balance: centre of mass, support polygon, ZMP and centroidal dynamics.** Explain what keeps a two-legged machine upright, from standing to walking. *Concepts:* static balance and the support polygon (owned here; recap: centre of mass, ch04); dynamic balance; the zero-moment point (owned here); the linear inverted pendulum model (owned here; equation); centroidal dynamics, the whole body's momentum about its centre of mass (owned here); stepping to recover balance; recap: falling safely (ch10).
  - **15.09 · Trajectories and model predictive control.** Show how a robot plans motion ahead and re-plans as the world changes. *Concepts:* trajectories and trajectory optimisation (owned here); collision-free motion planning in brief (owned here); model predictive control (owned here; receding-horizon cost and constraints; equation); MPC on simplified models for locomotion; sampling-based MPC (MPPI) on real legged hardware; whole-body inverse-dynamics MPC at the torque level (rate per its authors); recap: model-based RL (ch14).
  - **15.10 · Whole-body control as optimisation.** Show how one controller juggles balance, hands, contacts and limits at once. *Concepts:* tasks and constraints (owned here); quadratic-programme whole-body control (owned here; equation; primary source needed); task priorities and hierarchies; distributing contact forces (recap: friction cone, 15.06); torque limits (recap: ch05); preview: the policy–controller interface and learned whole-body control (taught in ch35); preview: the layered brain (taught in ch16).
  - **15.11 · Where learning replaces or wraps classical control.** Weigh learned against model-based control, fairly and with attribution. *Concepts:* residual learning on top of a classical controller (owned here); learned controllers replacing hand-written ones (Helix 02, company claim); RL-trained locomotion (preview: taught in ch28); MPC-guided and RL-guided hybrids (2026 leads, unopened); what classical models still give (explicit constraints, interpretability, analysis; `[inference]`); preview: learned whole-body control (taught in ch35); preview: safety filters around learned policies (taught in ch44).
- **Running-example hooks:** battery station in 15.04 and 15.07 (the Jacobian transpose and the torque tool's reaction; pushing a connector until it clicks as the force-controlled insertion loop; a worked stiff vs impedance-controlled arm, illustrative numbers marked `nofact`); dinner in 15.08 and 15.09 (balance while lifting a heavy pot from the oven; a menu planned days ahead vs a stir corrected within a fraction of a second); reset in 15.03 and 15.07 (reaching into dishwasher racks; the right wiping force; a soft arm near a toddler); greenhouse in 15.03 and 15.08 (IK from a raising trolley to high trusses; on the rail-trolley variant the balance problem largely disappears, `[inference]`).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Modern Robotics — Lynch, Park — 2017 (Cambridge UP) — http://modernrobotics.org — peer-reviewed (book)
  - Real-Time Whole-Body Control of Legged Robots with MPPI — Alvarez-Padilla et al. — 2024-09-16 — arXiv 2409.10469 — preprint
  - Whole-Body Inverse Dynamics MPC for Legged Loco-Manipulation — Molnar et al. — 2025-11-24 — arXiv 2511.19709 — preprint
  - Introducing Helix 02 — Figure AI — 2026-01-27 — https://www.figure.ai/news/helix-02 — company-claim
  - Evolution of humanoid locomotion control — Science Robotics, authors not recorded — date not recorded — https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/scirobotics.aed3973 — peer-reviewed (unconfirmed: page returned 403)
  - Impedance control — Hogan — 1985 — no URL — peer-reviewed (unconfirmed: named in scan as a gap)
  - Zero-moment-point originals — Vukobratović; Kajita — date not recorded — no URL — (unconfirmed: named in scan as a gap)
  - Centroidal dynamics — Orin — date not recorded — no URL — (unconfirmed: named in scan as a gap)
- **Notes:** Core equations: the homogeneous transform; forward kinematics; the Jacobian velocity map and its transpose for forces; the equation of motion; the PD and impedance laws; the linear inverted pendulum; the MPC and whole-body QP objectives. Thin scan (5 sources): research must find primaries for ZMP, the inverted-pendulum model, centroidal dynamics, impedance control, QP whole-body control and humanoid MPC (2024–26), plus a neutral learned-vs-model-based review. Helix 02 stays attributed, with ch35 owning its controller.

### Changes from seed (P2)
- ch11: moved the vision transformer (ViT) to ch12 (12.06); ch11 keeps CNNs (11.08) and pre-trained encoders with their self-supervised and contrastive objectives (11.09) — ViT needs attention, taught in ch12, and ch08 already previews vision encoders to ch11.
- ch11: added residual connections and normalisation (11.02), the training run (11.05), distribution shift (11.06) and transfer learning (11.07) — later chapters rely on them, and no other chapter owns them generically.
- ch11: widened scaling laws to data-constrained scaling and disputed fits, with robot data scaling left to ch17 — the scan reports 2026 refits and contested Chinchilla fits; teach once.
- ch12: "tokens and embeddings" became 12.01 (tokens), recapping ch11's embeddings — teach once.
- ch12: RLHF mechanics moved to ch14 (14.10); ch12 keeps fine-tuning and instruction tuning and previews the preference step — RL is not yet taught at ch12, and ch14 owns preferences.
- ch12: added 12.06 (vision transformers with VLMs) and 12.10 (VLMs as robot backbones); 12.04 owns autoregressive decoding — the ViT move, the scan's robot-backbone evidence, and text being where the reader first meets autoregression.
- ch13: added 13.01 (what a generative model does) and 13.10 (choosing a generative model) — multimodal actions are the first-principles reason robots use generative policies, and a product reader needs the comparison.
- ch13: renamed "autoregressive generation" to 13.07 "autoregressive generation beyond text" — ch12 teaches autoregressive decoding first.
- ch13: action tokenisers taught as a generic mechanism, with FAST previewed to ch31; sampler distillation kept here, with deployment distillation and quantisation previewed to ch33 — ownership boundaries.
- ch13: 13.09 owns the robot policy and imitation learning at a generic level — ch13 is the first chapter to need them; ch14 recaps.
- ch14: merged "returns and discounting" with value functions into 14.02 and split value learning into 14.03 (TD and Q-learning); moved exploration (14.06) before model-based RL — the Bellman equation belongs with returns, and value learning needs its own derivation.
- ch14: added verifiable rewards vs learned reward models (14.09) and 14.12 (where RL fits in the robot lifecycle); took RLHF from ch12 — scan suggestions; teach once.
- ch15: split "dynamics and contact" into 15.05 and 15.06; added trajectories and brief motion planning to 15.09; added a worked stiffness-vs-impedance torque-tool example (15.07 and hooks) — no other chapter owns motion planning, and the scan suggests the worked example.
- No PROPOSED chapter-level changes.

## P3 · The software stack

### ch16 · The layered robot brain: loops, clock rates and latency
- **Purpose:** How a humanoid's brain is split into layers that run at different speeds (millisecond motor loops, a whole-body controller, a visuomotor policy and a slow reasoner), how they hand work to each other, and how the whole stack meets its deadlines on the robot and beside it. ch16 owns the layered architecture, clock rates, latency budgets, asynchronous inference and real-time chunking, the safety layer as an architectural slot, middleware, and which layer runs where. Published architectures from several labs are described as attributed company claims, so no single lab is the template.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch03 (dual-system and three-system humanoid brains previewed as milestones); ch05 (the current-torque inner loop; delay and bandwidth limits); ch08 (a sensor as a measurement with rate and delay); ch09 (the onboard computer; inference budgets; off-board compute, network dropouts and offline operation); ch10 (emergency stop; the independent safety controller); ch12 (reasoning models and test-time compute; VLMs as robot backbones); ch13 (the robot policy; action chunking; flow-matching action heads; fast sampling); ch15 (PD and impedance control; model predictive control; whole-body control as optimisation; where learning wraps classical control).
- **Estimated length:** about 12,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **16.01 · Why layers.** Derive from first principles why one brain is split into loops that run at different speeds. *Concepts:* the control loop as sense, compute, act (owned here); clock rate, period and deadline (owned here; period equals one over rate, equation); timescale separation (balance needs answers in milliseconds, a menu can wait hours; owned here); the layered or hierarchical architecture (owned here); what each layer hands down (goals, subtasks, motion targets, torques) and passes up (state, progress, failure); the timescale ladder from motor current to multi-day plans (`[inference]` framing, rates filled from later sections); preview: the case for one end-to-end model (taught in ch31 and ch42).
  - **16.02 · Motor and joint loops.** Show the fastest layer and why it must never wait for anything above it. *Concepts:* recap: the current-torque inner loop (ch05); recap: PD and impedance control (ch15); the joint servo loop and its typical kHz rate (owned here as an architectural slot; primary rate source needed); where it runs (drive firmware and microcontrollers next to the motor); stiffness and damping targets as the interface from layers above; why a fast inner loop buys time for slow outer loops (`[inference]`, reasoning shown).
  - **16.03 · The whole-body controller layer.** Show the layer that turns "move the hand here" into coordinated torques for every joint while keeping balance. *Concepts:* recap: whole-body control as optimisation and task priorities (ch15); the whole-body controller's slot, inputs, outputs and rate (owned here); model-based vs learned whole-body controllers at this slot (Helix 02's System 0 at a stated 1 kHz, company claim; NVIDIA's decoupled whole-body controller, unconfirmed lead); preview: action interfaces and learned whole-body control (taught in ch35).
  - **16.04 · The visuomotor policy layer and action chunking as a timing device.** Show the layer that turns camera images and body state into motion, and how chunks bridge its slow rate to the fast layers below. *Concepts:* recap: the robot policy and action chunking (ch13); policy rate vs control rate (owned here); a chunk's length vs how much of it is executed before re-planning (execution horizon; owned here); interpolating between policy outputs for the controller's ticks; published policy-layer rates (Helix System 1 at a stated 200 Hz, company claim; GR00T N1's diffusion action module); recap: few-step sampling and latency (ch13); preview: VLA architectures and action representations (taught in ch31).
  - **16.05 · The reasoner and planner layer.** Show the slow layer that understands instructions, breaks a goal into steps and checks progress. *Concepts:* recap: vision-language models and reasoning models (ch12); task decomposition into subtasks (owned here at the architecture level); variable-rate reasoning (Helix 02's System 2 stated as variable rate; Helix's original System 2 at a stated 7–9 Hz; company claims); a separate embodied reasoner orchestrating an action model (Gemini Robotics 1.5's reasoner and VLA split; Gemini Robotics 2's ER 2 reasoner); tool calls and outside services (search, ordering) as reasoner actions (`[inference]` until sourced); plans that span hours or days vs sub-second control; preview: embodied reasoning and planning data (taught in ch32).
  - **16.06 · Fast-slow designs: "System 1/2" and "System 0/1/2".** Lay the published architectures side by side and show what the split buys and costs. *Concepts:* the dual-system design (owned here: a slow, large model and a fast, small model running asynchronously); where the "System 1/2" label comes from (source needed; else dropped); the interface between systems (latent vectors vs language vs subgoals; owned here at the architecture level); the three-layer "System 0/1/2" design (Helix 02, company claim); a model family split by role (Gemini Robotics 2: ER 2, a whole-body VLA and an on-device VLA; company claim); (aside) a comparison table of published architectures with each layer's stated rate and model type (Helix, Helix 02, GR00T N1 and N1.6, Gemini Robotics 1.5 and 2), "not stated" where a lab gives no rate; trade-offs of splitting (training each layer separately vs jointly; mismatched interfaces; `[inference]`); preview: single-model vs dual-system VLAs (taught in ch31); preview: layered hybrids in the convergence debate (taught in ch42).
  - **16.07 · Latency budgets.** Teach the reader to add up where the time goes between a sensor event and the motor's response. *Concepts:* latency vs rate vs throughput (owned here); end-to-end latency from sensing to actuation (owned here); the latency budget as a sum of stages (owned here; equation: capture, transfer, queueing, inference, communication and actuation); jitter and missed deadlines (owned here); stale observations (acting on an old picture of the world); why a high rate does not guarantee a fast reaction (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); (aside) a worked latency budget for the torque-tool tightening step, with illustrative numbers marked `nofact`; preview: delay modelling in simulation (taught in ch29).
  - **16.08 · Asynchronous inference and real-time chunking.** Show how a robot keeps moving smoothly while its next actions are still being computed. *Concepts:* synchronous inference and the pause-then-jerk problem at chunk boundaries (owned here); asynchronous inference with a policy server and a robot client, and the chunk threshold that triggers the next request (owned here; LeRobot as the open example); the timing condition that the remaining chunk must outlast inference delay (owned here; equation, derived); real-time chunking by freezing the actions already committed and inpainting the rest (owned here; Black, Galliker and Levine, NeurIPS 2025); training-time action conditioning that removes the inference-time inpainting cost (December 2025 preprint); preview: distillation and quantisation for speed (taught in ch33); preview: inference speed and control rates of world action models (taught in ch40).
  - **16.09 · The safety layer and fallbacks.** Show where safety sits in the stack, why it must be independent of the learned layers, and what the robot does when a layer fails. *Concepts:* the safety layer as an architectural slot with authority over every learned layer (owned here at the architecture level); recap: emergency stop, safe torque off and the independent safety controller (ch10); watchdogs and deadline monitors (what happens when a layer misses its deadline; owned here; source needed); graceful degradation and fallback behaviours (hold, slow down, controlled stop, ask a human; owned here at the architecture level; recap: the fail-passive gap for bipeds, ch10); company safety layers, attributed (Gemini Robotics 2 safety report, unopened lead); preview: runtime monitors, control barrier functions and certified fallbacks (taught in ch44); preview: remote assistance as a fallback (taught in ch46).
  - **16.10 · Middleware, real-time operating systems and logging.** Show the plumbing that moves messages between layers on time and records what happened. *Concepts:* middleware (owned here); ROS 2 nodes, topics and publish-subscribe messaging (owned here; primary source needed); DDS and quality-of-service settings (owned here; primary source needed); real-time operating systems, scheduling priority and determinism (owned here; ros2_control's SCHED_FIFO option as the documented example; real-time kernel primary needed); the controller manager's update rate and overrun warnings (ros2_control, documented); logging at the architecture level (timestamps at every layer boundary, what each layer should record, log volume vs value; owned here; `[inference]` until sourced); preview: clocks and time synchronisation (taught in ch22); preview: ROS bags, MCAP and storage formats (taught in ch23); preview: fleet monitoring and observability (taught in ch47).
  - **16.11 · On-robot vs off-board: where each layer runs.** Decide which layers can leave the robot, and pull the chapter into one picture for the reference humanoid. *Concepts:* recap: onboard compute, edge servers, the cloud and network dropouts (ch09); the placement rule (a layer can run off-board only if network delay plus its own inference fits its deadline, and the robot stays safe when the link drops; owned here; `[inference]`, reasoning shown); on-device models as a design choice (Gemini Robotics 2's on-device VLA, company claim; GR00T reference build on Jetson Thor, recap ch09); remote policy servers (LeRobot asynchronous inference across a network); offline operation and degraded modes (recap: ch09); privacy of off-board processing in homes (preview: taught in ch24 and ch44); (aside) the reference humanoid's brain layer by layer: rate, latency budget and placement, with illustrative numbers marked `nofact` except where sourced.
- **Running-example hooks:** dinner for five in 16.01 and 16.05 (a menu and grocery order planned days ahead, a hob timetable over hours, a stir corrected in under a second; the reasoner orders and waits for payment approval while lower layers sit idle) and 16.09 (a kitchen-fire fallback); battery station in 16.07 (worked latency budget: the torque tool's reaction arrives faster than the policy layer can see it, so the joint and whole-body loops must absorb it, `[inference]` until sourced), 16.03 (holding posture under the tool's kick) and 16.11 (plant network vs on-robot control); kitchen reset in 16.05 and 16.06 ("leave my glass" handled by the reasoner, grasping by the policy) and 16.08 (smooth, uninterrupted two-handed motion around a toddler); greenhouse in 16.11 (patchy network along long rows, so control stays on the robot, `[inference]`).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Helix (original announcement) — Figure AI — 2025-02-20 — https://www.figure.ai/news/helix — company-claim
  - Introducing Helix 02 — Figure AI — 2026-01-27 — https://www.figure.ai/news/helix-02 — company-claim
  - GR00T N1 — NVIDIA — 2025-03-18 — arXiv 2503.14734 — preprint
  - GR00T N1.6 — NVIDIA GEAR — 2025-12-15 — https://research.nvidia.com/labs/gear/gr00t-n1_6/ — tech-report
  - Gemini Robotics 1.5 — Gemini Robotics Team — 2025-10-02 — arXiv 2510.03342 — tech-report
  - Gemini Robotics 2 — Google DeepMind — 2026-07-30 — https://deepmind.google/blog/gemini-robotics-2-brings-whole-body-intelligence-to-robots/ — company-claim
  - Real-Time Execution of Action Chunking Flow Policies — Black, Galliker, Levine — 2025-06-09 — arXiv 2506.07339 — peer-reviewed (NeurIPS 2025)
  - Training-Time Action Conditioning for Efficient RTC — Black et al. — 2025-12-05 — arXiv 2512.05964 — preprint
  - Asynchronous Inference — Hugging Face LeRobot — undated — https://huggingface.co/docs/lerobot/en/async — tech-report
  - ros2_control Controller Manager docs — ros-controls — rolling, 2026 — https://control.ros.org/rolling/doc/ros2_control/controller_manager/doc/userdoc.html — standard
- **Notes:** Every per-layer rate is a company claim unless independently measured; none are, per the scan, so 16.06 and 16.07 say so and GR00T N1.6's unstated rates stay "not stated". Gaps: independent latency measurements; ROS 2, DDS and real-time kernel primaries; a joint-loop rate source; watchdog and fallback sources; the Gemini Robotics 2 safety report (unopened); any torque-tool timing data; an origin source for "System 1/2". Further lab leads to check at research time: π0.5's high-level and low-level inference (arXiv 2504.16054), so the comparison is not limited to three labs.

### Changes from seed (P3)
- ch16: expanded "why layers" into 16.01 with the control loop, clock rate and timescale separation as owned concepts — every later section needs them, and ch05, ch09, ch12, ch13 and ch15 all preview clock rates and latency budgets to ch16.
- ch16: split "latency budgets and asynchronous inference" into 16.07 (latency budgets, with a worked torque-tool example) and 16.08 (asynchronous inference and real-time chunking) — the scan reports real-time chunking has matured (a NeurIPS 2025 paper and a December 2025 training-time variant) and suggests the worked example.
- ch16: reframed the visuomotor section as "action chunking as a timing device" (16.04), recapping ch13's chunking instead of re-teaching it — teach once; ch13 owns chunking, ch16 owns its timing role.
- ch16: added a comparison table of published architectures with per-layer rates (16.06) and a layer-by-layer synthesis for the reference humanoid (16.11) — the scan suggests a layer-by-layer rate table, and several labs keep any one from being the template.
- ch16: merged "on-robot vs off-board inference" with the chapter synthesis in 16.11, recapping ch09's hardware — P1 recorded that ch09 owns compute and network hardware and ch16 owns placement.
- ch16: drew boundaries — safety layer as an architectural slot only (monitor design to ch44); logging at the architecture level only (formats to ch23, clocks to ch22); whole-body controller as a slot only (learned WBC and the action interface to ch35); VLA internals to ch31 — teach once.
- No PROPOSED chapter-level changes.

## P4 · Data

### ch17 · Why data is the bottleneck
- **Purpose:** Why robot learning is held back by data more than by models or compute; how the sources of robot data trade scale against relevance; what the scaling evidence does and does not show; and what data costs. ch17 owns the action label, the units of robot data, the data pyramid and the scale–alignment trade-off, pooled cross-embodiment datasets as a resource, robot data-scaling laws, diversity vs volume, and cost per hour by source. Each source is taught in ch18–ch20.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch01 (the data pyramid previewed); ch03 (Open X-Embodiment as a milestone; the imitation-learning revival); ch04 (the body defines the action space); ch07 (the hand as the data bottleneck); ch11 (supervised and self-supervised learning; generalisation and distribution shift; power-law scaling laws); ch13 (the robot policy; imitation learning); ch14 (learning from rewards; offline RL).
- **Estimated length:** about 9,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **17.01 · No internet of actions.** Derive from first principles why robots lack the web-scale data that language and vision models had. *Concepts:* the action label, a record of what the body did at each moment (owned here); why web text, images and video carry no robot actions (recap: supervised and self-supervised learning, ch11); physical time as the binding constraint (one robot gathers at most one hour of experience per hour; `[inference]`, reasoning shown); data tied to one body (recap: the body defines the action space, ch04); recap: imitation learning (ch13).
  - **17.02 · Counting robot data: hours, episodes, trajectories and frames.** Teach the units, and why company totals cannot be compared directly. *Concepts:* episodes, trajectories, hours and frames as units of robot data (owned here; the episode's internal structure is taught in ch21); what an "hour" may include (idle time, failed attempts, human video vs robot time); converting between units (illustrative numbers marked `nofact`); reading a volume claim critically.
  - **17.03 · The data pyramid.** Lay out the sources from the web down to the robot's own experience, and what each layer trades. *Concepts:* the data pyramid (owned here; GR00T N1's pyramid and the 2026 Data Pyramid survey as attributed framings); the scale–alignment trade-off (owned here: the more plentiful a source, the less it matches the robot's body and task); the layers: web data, human video, simulation and synthetic data, teleoperated demonstrations, autonomous and deployment data; preview: learning from people (taught in ch18); preview: teleoperation (taught in ch19); preview: synthetic, simulated, autonomous and deployment data (taught in ch20).
  - **17.04 · Pooled and cross-embodiment datasets.** Show how labs pool robot data across bodies and sites, and what pooling hides. *Concepts:* cross-embodiment datasets as a resource (owned here; previewed in ch03): Open X-Embodiment (22 embodiments), DROID, AgiBot World Colosseo (1M+ trajectories, 217 tasks); open vs closed datasets; what pooling buys (variety) and hides (mixed action conventions; preview: action spaces, taught in ch21); preview: learning across bodies (taught in ch25).
  - **17.05 · Scaling laws for robot data.** Show how performance grows with data, using the academic and company evidence side by side. *Concepts:* recap: power-law scaling (ch11); data-scaling laws in imitation learning (Lin et al.: a power law in the number of environments and objects; equation); company scaling curves, all attributed (GEN-0's 270,000 hours, 10,000 hours a week and a claimed model-size threshold; Xiaomi-Robotics-1 on 100,000+ hours of real trajectories; Dyna-2's 1-million-hour human-video curve); preview: scaling human data (taught in ch18); preview: scaling evidence for robot foundation models (taught in ch31).
  - **17.06 · Diversity vs volume.** Explain why more kinds of situations beat more copies of one situation. *Concepts:* diversity vs volume (owned here; Lin et al.'s finding that more environments and objects beat more demonstrations per scene); factors of variation (scenes, objects, tasks, operators, bodies; owned here); diminishing returns from repeating the same scene; preview: designing for coverage and the long tail (taught in ch26).
  - **17.07 · Limits of the scaling evidence.** Say plainly what the curves do not yet prove. *Concepts:* unreviewed company curves; inconsistent "hours" (recap: 17.02); different evaluation tasks behind each curve (preview: evaluation, taught in ch43); the new, unreplicated claims that human video beats robot data (preview: taught in ch18); what would settle the question (shared benchmarks and disclosed data; `[inference]`).
  - **17.08 · What data costs: cost per hour by source.** Teach the cost drivers from first principles and state the evidence gap. *Concepts:* cost drivers per source (hardware and its depreciation, operator or contributor time, success yield, post-processing and storage; owned here); cost per useful hour (owned here; equation, illustrative numbers marked `nofact`); the evidence gap (public per-hour figures exist only in vendor blogs, excluded by the sources policy); paid crowd capture as a cost model (Figure's Index app; unconfirmed); preview: operator throughput (taught in ch19); preview: data budgets and return on investment (taught in ch26).
  - **17.09 · The 2025–26 data race.** Lay out who claims what, and what each claim counts. *Concepts:* (aside) a dated table of claimed volumes and their units (GEN-0, Xiaomi-Robotics-1, Dyna-2, AgiBot World, EgoScale, Figure's Project Go-Big and Index, Sunday's ACT-1), each row attributed with its evidence tag; the shift from teleoperated robot data toward egocentric human video and gloves (company claims and 2026 preprints); what the race means for a buyer judging a vendor's data claims (`[inference]`).
- **Running-example hooks:** dinner in 17.01 and 17.03 (cooking video is everywhere, yet none of it records a robot hand's actions); battery station in 17.03 and 17.08 (almost no public video of the task, and demonstrations on unpowered dummy modules are slow to collect, `[inference]`); kitchen reset in 17.06 (every new home is a new environment, the diversity axis Lin et al. measured, `[inference]`); greenhouse in 17.06 (some situations come up once a year, so volume cannot buy diversity quickly).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Data Scaling Laws in Imitation Learning for Robotic Manipulation — Lin et al. — 2024-10-24 (rev. 2026-06-26) — arXiv 2410.18647 — preprint
  - GEN-0: Embodied Foundation Models That Scale with Physical Interaction — Generalist — 2025-11-04 — https://generalistai.com/blog/nov-04-2025-GEN-0 — company-claim
  - AgiBot World Colosseo — AgiBot-World-Contributors — 2025-03-09 — arXiv 2503.06669 — preprint
  - Xiaomi-Robotics-1 — Xiaomi Robotics Team — 2026-07-16 — arXiv 2607.15330 — tech-report
  - Dyna-2: A 1-Million-Hour Scaling Law for World-Action Models — Dyna Robotics — 2026-08 — https://www.dyna.co/dyna-2 — company-claim (unconfirmed: day; second half unread)
  - Data Pyramid for Embodied Manipulation: A Survey — Ye et al. — 2026-07-27 — arXiv 2607.24744 — preprint
  - GR00T N1 — NVIDIA — 2025-03-18 — arXiv 2503.14734 — preprint
  - Open X-Embodiment — Open X-Embodiment Collaboration — 2023-10-13 (v9 2025-05-14) — arXiv 2310.08864 — preprint
  - Project Go-Big — Figure — 2025-09-18 — https://www.figure.ai/news/project-go-big — company-claim
  - Sunday ACT-1 "zero robot data" — Sunday — 2025-11 — https://www.sunday.ai/blog/no-robot-data — company-claim (unconfirmed: date)
- **Notes:** Figure's Index statistics reached the scan only through an aggregator, and AgiBot World's hour count (2,976.4) only through a search snippet: both unconfirmed until primaries are found. Vendor-blog cost figures are excluded, so 17.08 teaches drivers with `nofact` numbers and states the gap. DROID (arXiv 2403.12945) is taken from the ch21 scan; HumanScale and EgoScale are taught in ch18.

### ch18 · Learning from people: web video, egocentric video and motion capture
- **Purpose:** How robots learn from people rather than from robots: web and third-person video, first-person (egocentric) capture, hand tracking and gloves, full-body motion capture, narration, and the methods that pull actions out of video that has none. ch18 owns human-data capture devices and datasets, hand-pose capture, learned inverse dynamics models, latent action models as labellers, and the evidence on scaling human data. Retargeting, the embodiment gap and latent action spaces as a cross-body interface belong to ch25.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch02 (human data as the bootstrap; Brooks's argument that video lacks touch and force); ch07 (human vs robot hand mismatch); ch08 (cameras; IMU; tactile sensing); ch11 (self-supervised learning; embeddings; transfer learning); ch13 (autoencoders; vector quantisation and codebooks; imitation learning); ch15 (frames and poses; inverse dynamics in the physics sense); ch17 (the data pyramid; the scale–alignment trade-off; data-scaling laws; units of robot data).
- **Estimated length:** about 12,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **18.01 · Why learn from people.** Set out what human data offers and what it lacks before the devices. *Concepts:* people as the largest source of manipulation behaviour (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); recap: the scale–alignment trade-off (ch17); what human data gives (scenes, objects, intent, task order) and what it lacks (robot actions, force); first-person vs third-person viewpoints (owned here).
  - **18.02 · Web and third-person video.** Show what the internet's video can and cannot teach a robot. *Concepts:* web video as a resource (how-to and cooking video); exocentric and multi-view capture (Ego-Exo4D; primary unconfirmed); what web video lacks (action labels, camera calibration, depth, a steady viewpoint); camera-motion confounds, where the camera's own movement looks like the world moving (owned here at the data level; preview: world-model failures from it, taught in ch41); preview: rights to use web video (taught in ch24).
  - **18.03 · Egocentric capture devices.** Explain the wearables that record what a person sees and does. *Concepts:* head-worn research glasses (Project Aria Gen 2's sensor suite, attributed); mixed-reality headsets as hand trackers (Apple Vision Pro in EgoDex); what a device records beyond video: eye gaze, its own pose, hand tracking, audio (owned here); device pose tracking from cameras and IMU, known as SLAM (owned here at an introductory level); preview: stream rates and sizes (taught in ch21); preview: device calibration and sync (taught in ch22).
  - **18.04 · Egocentric datasets.** Lay out the main datasets and teach how to read their cards. *Concepts:* (aside) a dated table of egocentric datasets: Ego4D (3,670 hours), EgoDex (829 hours with 3D hand tracking), Aria Gen 2 Pilot Dataset (cooking and cleaning scenarios), EgoVerse (1,362 hours, 2,087 demonstrators, pooled across labs), HumanVerse-500 (500 hours of whole-body capture), with EPIC-KITCHENS and HOT3D only if primaries are confirmed; how to read a dataset card (hours, participants, scenarios, annotations, licence); recap: units of robot data (ch17).
  - **18.05 · Hands: pose estimation, tracking and gloves.** Show how a person's hand motion becomes numbers. *Concepts:* the hand pose as data, joint angles and fingertip positions over time (owned here); estimating hand pose from video vs headset tracking vs gloves (owned here); capture gloves (Sunday's glove, company claim; DexUMI, seed lead); force-sensing gloves (2026 lead, unopened); hand-pose accuracy and its effect on co-training (What Matters When Cotraining on Everyday Human Videos?); recap: human vs robot hand mismatch (ch07); preview: handheld grippers (taught in ch19); preview: hand retargeting (taught in ch25).
  - **18.06 · Full-body motion capture.** Explain how whole-body human motion is recorded for humanoids. *Concepts:* optical marker capture vs inertial suits (owned here; sources needed); motion datasets (AMASS, seed lead); whole-body egocentric capture for humanoids (HumanVerse-500); preview: whole-body teleoperation (taught in ch19); preview: body retargeting (taught in ch25); preview: motion imitation in simulation (taught in ch28).
  - **18.07 · Wearable capture at scale.** Show how companies and labs now collect human data from many wearers. *Concepts:* crowd and contractor capture programmes (owned here; Figure's Index app, unconfirmed; Sunday's gloves, company claim; the AoE wearable-collection preprint); throughput and diversity of wearable capture vs teleoperation (`[inference]`); quality control at scale (preview: taught in ch24); consent of wearers and bystanders (preview: taught in ch24).
  - **18.08 · Narration and language.** Explain how human video comes with words, and why that matters. *Concepts:* narrations, timestamped sentences describing what the wearer does (owned here; Ego4D); scenario and activity labels; narration as weak supervision for subtasks; preview: language fields (taught in ch21); preview: language annotation (taught in ch24).
  - **18.09 · Extracting actions from human video.** Show the three ways to recover "what was done" from video. *Concepts:* hand and wrist trajectories as pseudo-actions (owned here); the learned inverse dynamics model, which infers the action between two frames (owned here; equation; distinct from inverse dynamics in physics, ch15); latent action models, which learn a compact code for "what changed" between frames without any labels (owned here; LAPA; equation; recap: vector quantisation, ch13); pseudo-labels and their noise; preview: latent action spaces across bodies (taught in ch25); preview: video-then-inverse-dynamics policies (taught in ch40).
  - **18.10 · Scaling and co-training with human data.** Report the evidence that human data helps robots, with each claim graded. *Concepts:* preview: co-training (taught in ch26), defined in one line; EgoMimic (co-training with Aria-glasses data); EgoScale (log-linear scaling over 20,854 hours; a reported 54% gain from human pre-training); EgoVerse; HumanScale and Dyna-2's claims that egocentric video beats robot data for pre-training (new and unreplicated, attributed); Figure's Project Go-Big and Sunday's ACT-1 (company claims); preview: human-video pre-training as a training stage (taught in ch31).
  - **18.11 · Limits of human data.** State what human data cannot supply, and the remedies in sight. *Concepts:* missing force, touch and proprioception (owned here at the data level; recap: Brooks, ch02; preview: modality gaps in world models, taught in ch41); human motion that a robot body cannot reproduce (preview: the embodiment gap, taught in ch25); privacy of recorded homes (preview: taught in ch24); rare and seasonal tasks absent from video.
- **Running-example hooks:** dinner in 18.02 and 18.08 (abundant cooking video and narrations, but chopping force and judging doneness leave no trace on video); kitchen reset in 18.03 and 18.04 (kitchens and cleaning are common in egocentric datasets, including the Aria Gen 2 pilot); battery station in 18.02 and 18.11 (almost no public video of busbar fitting; a connector's click and push force are invisible to a camera, `[inference]`); greenhouse in 18.07 and 18.11 (footage is seasonal and scarce; wearable capture by skilled pickers as a route, `[inference]`).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Ego4D — Grauman et al. — 2021-10-13 — arXiv 2110.07058 — peer-reviewed
  - EgoDex — Hoque et al. (Apple) — 2025-05-16 — arXiv 2505.11709 — preprint
  - EgoMimic — Kareer et al. — 2024-10-31 — arXiv 2410.24221 — preprint
  - Aria Gen 2 announcement — Meta — 2025-02-27 — https://www.meta.com/blog/project-aria-gen-2-next-generation-egocentric-research-glasses-reality-labs-ai-robotics/ — company-claim
  - Aria Gen 2 Pilot Dataset — Kong et al. — 2025-10-17 — arXiv 2510.16134 — preprint
  - LAPA — Ye et al. — 2024-10-15 — arXiv 2410.11758 — peer-reviewed
  - EgoScale — Zheng et al. (NVIDIA, Berkeley) — 2026-02-18 — arXiv 2602.16710 — preprint
  - EgoVerse — Punamiya et al. — 2026-04-08 — arXiv 2604.07607 — preprint
  - What Matters When Cotraining on Everyday Human Videos? — Li et al. — 2026-06-04 — arXiv 2606.06627 — preprint
  - HumanVerse-500 / λ₀ — Xu et al. — 2026-09-30 — arXiv 2610.00438 — preprint
- **Notes:** Further leads: HumanScale (arXiv 2606.20521, ch17 scan); AoE (arXiv 2602.23893); glove-force paper (arXiv 2609.14173, unopened). Not found or unconfirmed: DexUMI, HOT3D, AMASS, the Ego-Exo4D paper (only a Meta blog seen), EPIC-KITCHENS, 1X's data mix. Being-H0.5 and H0.7 are confirmed in the ch25 scan and are taught there.

### ch19 · Teleoperation: how robot demonstrations are collected
- **Purpose:** How people produce robot demonstrations by driving or imitating the robot: leader-follower arms, VR and haptic set-ups, exoskeletons and motion-capture suits for whole bodies, and handheld grippers; who the operators are and what limits their output; what makes a demonstration good; how data factories scale collection; and how remote expert modes and corrections become collection channels. ch19 owns collection interfaces and operator practice; using interventions as training data belongs to ch20 and ch34.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch03 (ALOHA as a milestone); ch06 (encoders; backdrivability); ch07 (hands; tool use; matching human demonstrations); ch08 (cameras and wrist cameras; force/torque sensors); ch13 (the robot policy; imitation learning; multimodal distributions; ACT and action chunking); ch15 (inverse kinematics; impedance control; whole-body control); ch16 (latency budgets; the safety layer; remote assistance previewed as a fallback); ch17 (the data pyramid; units; cost per useful hour); ch18 (hand-pose capture; gloves; full-body motion capture).
- **Estimated length:** about 12,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **19.01 · What a demonstration is.** Define the teleoperated demonstration and why it sits near the top of the pyramid. *Concepts:* the teleoperated demonstration, the robot's own sensors and actions recorded while a person drives it (owned here); recap: imitation learning (ch13); recap: the scale–alignment trade-off (ch17); embodied vs robot-free collection (owned here: driving the robot vs carrying a device that mimics it); preview: the episode's structure (taught in ch21).
  - **19.02 · Leader-follower rigs.** Show the cheapest, most direct way to drive a robot arm. *Concepts:* joint-space mapping from a leader arm to a follower arm (owned here); ALOHA and Mobile ALOHA (ACT paper confirmed; Mobile ALOHA a seed lead); GELLO (seed lead); low cost and its limits (matching kinematics, no felt force); recap: backdrivability and encoders (ch06).
  - **19.03 · VR, AR and haptic teleoperation.** Show how a headset and hand tracking drive a robot, and how touch might be fed back. *Concepts:* hand and head tracking mapped to robot targets through inverse kinematics (recap: ch15); stereo head-camera streaming and presence (Open-TeleVision, seed lead); latency, motion sickness and operator comfort (recap: latency budgets, ch16; source needed); force feedback and bilateral teleoperation (owned here; source needed); visual and sound cues standing in for touch (`[inference]`); hand retargeting in the loop (preview: taught in ch25).
  - **19.04 · Whole-body humanoid teleoperation: exoskeletons and mocap suits.** Show how one person drives a whole humanoid, legs included. *Concepts:* mapping a human body onto a humanoid in real time (owned here at the interface level); motion-capture-driven tracking controllers (TeleGate, trained on 2.5 hours of motion-capture data; HumanPlus, OmniH2O and TWIST as seed leads; Teleopit unopened); exoskeleton cockpits (source needed); the robot, not the operator, keeps balance (recap: whole-body control, ch15); preview: training tracking controllers (taught in ch28); preview: whole-body action interfaces (taught in ch35).
  - **19.05 · Handheld grippers: UMI and its successors.** Explain how demonstrations are collected with no robot at all. *Concepts:* the robot-free demonstration device (owned here; UMI); a wrist camera and gripper that match the robot's; pose tracking and its weak points (UMI-3D's LiDAR fix); scale (FastUMI-100K, unconfirmed); tactile and VR-tracked variants; preview: latency matching and calibration of handheld rigs (taught in ch22).
  - **19.06 · Operators: training, fatigue, throughput and cost.** Show the human side of collection and its arithmetic. *Concepts:* the teleoperator as a skilled role (training, fatigue, shift length; sources needed); collection throughput as attempts per hour times success rate (owned here; equation, illustrative numbers marked `nofact`); cost per useful demonstration (recap: cost per useful hour, ch17); the evidence gap (public throughput and cost figures exist only in vendor blogs, excluded).
  - **19.07 · What makes a good demonstration.** Teach collection practice that produces learnable data. *Concepts:* consistency across operators (recap: multimodal distributions, ch13); recoveries and deliberate failures as collection practice (owned here); pace and pauses (preview: idle-time checks, taught in ch24); task protocols and scene resets (owned here); preview: quality checks and filtering (taught in ch24); preview: how many demonstrations a task needs (taught in ch33).
  - **19.08 · Data factories: collection at scale.** Show how collection becomes an industrial operation. *Concepts:* the data factory, a site with many robots and operators running scripted task menus (owned here; AgiBot World Colosseo's collection platform as the documented example; Tesla and Figure as seed leads with no primary); scene variation inside a factory; scale vs diversity (recap: ch17).
  - **19.09 · Remote expert modes and corrections.** Show how a deployed robot's human helpers become a collection channel. *Concepts:* remote expert mode in homes (1X NEO Expert Mode, company claim, with claimed no-go zones); human-in-the-loop corrections with handheld devices (HIL-UMI); takeover interfaces, how a human seizes and returns control (owned here); home teleoperation and privacy (preview: taught in ch24 and ch44); preview: interventions as training data (taught in ch20); preview: remote assistance in deployment (taught in ch46).
  - **19.10 · Choosing a collection method.** Compare the methods for a product reader. *Concepts:* (aside) a comparison table of leader-follower, VR, whole-body, handheld and remote-expert collection by body match, force information, cost, scale and set-up (`[inference]` where no head-to-head study exists); preview: teleoperation in simulation as a data source (taught in ch20); which method suits each running example.
