- **Running-example hooks:** battery station in 19.03 and 19.10 (force-blind teleoperation of a connector push, click and tug; demonstrations on unpowered dummy modules); kitchen reset in 19.09 (a remote expert guiding the robot in its first week sees the family's home); dinner in 19.07 (recoveries such as a dropped utensil; hot oil limits what can safely be demonstrated, `[inference]`); greenhouse in 19.05 (a handheld gripper with a cutter carried along the rows, `[inference]`).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Universal Manipulation Interface — Chi et al. — 2024-02-15 — arXiv 2402.10329 — preprint
  - UMI-3D — authors not recorded — 2026-04-15 — arXiv 2604.14089 — preprint
  - HIL-UMI — Han et al. — 2026-09-17 — arXiv 2609.20659 — preprint
  - FastUMI-100K — authors not recorded — 2025-10 — arXiv 2510.08022 — preprint (unconfirmed: not opened)
  - TeleGate — Li, Tang, Wu — 2026-02-10 — arXiv 2602.09628 — preprint
  - Data Pyramid for Embodied Manipulation: A Survey — Ye et al. — 2026-07-27 — arXiv 2607.24744 — preprint
  - NEO Home Robot (Expert Mode) — 1X — undated — https://www.1x.tech/neo — company-claim (unconfirmed: date)
  - Learning Fine-Grained Bimanual Manipulation (ACT) — Zhao et al. — 2023-04-23 — arXiv 2304.13705 — preprint
  - AgiBot World Colosseo — AgiBot-World-Contributors — 2025-03-09 — arXiv 2503.06669 — preprint
  - Mobile ALOHA; GELLO; HumanPlus; OmniH2O; TWIST; Open-TeleVision — no date — no URL — (seed lead, verify)
- **Notes:** HumanoidUMI (arXiv 2606.27239) was withdrawn on 2026-07-07: do not cite. Expert Mode autonomy percentages are industry estimates and stay out. Gaps: haptics, operator fatigue and training sources; primaries for Tesla, Figure and AgiBot data factories; Teleopit (arXiv 2608.01834) unopened.

### ch20 · Synthetic, autonomous and deployment data
- **Purpose:** The data that does not need a person driving the robot: simulation, multiplied and generated demonstrations, the robot's own autonomous attempts, human interventions during those attempts, and logs from deployed fleets. ch20 owns each of these as a data source: what it contains, how it is made, what the evidence says, and how it fails. ch28 owns training with synthetic and simulated demonstrations, ch34 owns RL from corrections and the Recap algorithm, ch39 owns using world models to generate data, and ch48 owns the closed improvement loop.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch11 (distribution shift); ch13 (compounding errors; generative models; imitation learning); ch14 (on-policy and off-policy data; offline RL; rewards); ch15 (homogeneous transforms); ch16 (logging at the architecture level); ch17 (the data pyramid; cost per useful hour); ch18 (learned inverse dynamics models); ch19 (demonstrations; remote expert modes; takeover interfaces).
- **Estimated length:** about 11,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **20.01 · Data without a human driver.** Map the three families of machine-made and machine-gathered data. *Concepts:* synthetic data (simulated, multiplied, generated), autonomous data and deployment data as one map (owned here); who supplies the label in each case (`[inference]` framing); recap: the data pyramid (ch17).
  - **20.02 · Simulation as a data source.** Show what simulated data offers and what it cannot yet provide. *Concepts:* simulated data with perfect labels and privileged state such as exact contacts (owned here as a data source); scripted and planner-generated demonstrations in simulation; teleoperation in simulation (owned here as a data source; practice taught in ch28); randomised rendering as data variety (preview: domain randomisation, taught in ch29); what each running example's simulator cannot produce (preview: simulators, taught in ch27; sim-to-real gap, taught in ch29).
  - **20.03 · Data multiplication: the MimicGen line.** Explain how a handful of human demonstrations becomes thousands. *Concepts:* data multiplication (owned here): split demonstrations into object-centred segments and replay them on new object poses (equation: the object-relative transform; recap: transforms, ch15); MimicGen (unconfirmed); DexMimicGen (60 source demonstrations become 21,000); SkillMimicGen (seed lead); HumanoidMimicGen (humanoid loco-manipulation; reports synthetic data beating real-only data by 20% on one benchmark, attributed); limits (needs a simulator and object poses; suits rigid objects); preview: training with synthetic demonstrations (taught in ch28).
  - **20.04 · Generated video and neural trajectories.** Show how video world models are being used as data engines, and how thin the evidence still is. *Concepts:* video world models as data engines (preview: world models, taught in ch36); neural trajectories: generate videos of a robot doing new tasks, then label their actions with an inverse dynamics model (DreamGen; recap: inverse dynamics models, ch18); re-rendering real or simulated video in new appearances and predicting futures (Cosmos-Transfer2.5 and Cosmos-Predict2.5); the DreamGen benchmark; thin public evidence that generated video helps real robots at scale; preview: generating data with world models and its reliability (taught in ch39); preview: physics failures in generated video (taught in ch41).
  - **20.05 · Autonomous rollouts.** Show why the robot's own attempts are uniquely valuable data. *Concepts:* the rollout, a recorded attempt by the robot running its own policy (owned here); on-policy data (recap: ch14) and why it exposes the robot's own mistakes; success and failure outcomes as signal; reset and supervision costs; preview: real-world RL (taught in ch34).
  - **20.06 · Human interventions and corrections.** Explain why corrections target exactly the states demonstrations miss, and why they are noisy. *Concepts:* recap: compounding errors (ch13) and distribution shift (ch11); DAgger and human-gated DAgger (owned here; HG-DAgger, seed lead): gather corrections where the robot goes wrong; interventions as noisy data to be filtered and weighted (ROVE); recap: takeover interfaces (ch19); preview: learning from corrections with RL (taught in ch34).
  - **20.07 · Deployment and fleet data.** Introduce what deployed robots can contribute, without yet closing the loop. *Concepts:* fleet logs as a data source (owned here at the source level: what a deployed robot records and in what volume; recap: logging, ch16); learning while deploying (rollouts plus interventions across 16 robots reaching 95% average success, per the authors); π*0.6's mix of demonstrations, corrections and autonomous experience (Physical Intelligence technical report); preview: logging triggers, failure mining and the flywheel (taught in ch48); preview: the Recap algorithm (taught in ch34); preview: privacy of fleet logs (taught in ch24 and ch47).
  - **20.08 · Labels that come for free.** Show the labels that a task's own process produces. *Concepts:* process signals as labels (tool torque records, task-completion events, interventions marking failure; owned here; `[inference]` where unsourced); automatic success and failure labels from detectors and VLM judges (preview: taught in ch34); label noise and its cost (preview: label QA, taught in ch24); preview: hindsight relabelling in the flywheel (taught in ch48).
  - **20.09 · Mixing machine-made data with human data.** Weigh what each source adds when combined. *Concepts:* evidence on mixing ratios (HumanoidMimicGen, DexMimicGen; `[inference]` beyond them); synthetic data as coverage, not ground truth (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); (aside) a comparison table of sources by label quality, cost, scale and gap to reality; preview: co-training and mixture weights (taught in ch26); preview: sim+real co-training (taught in ch29).
- **Running-example hooks:** battery station in 20.02, 20.03 and 20.08 (rigid parts from design files multiply well in simulation while cables and connector clicks do not; the torque tool records every bolt, a free label); dinner in 20.04 (generated cooking video can look right while the food physics is wrong; preview ch41); kitchen reset in 20.06 and 20.07 (remote-expert takeovers in the first week become correction data); greenhouse in 20.07 (fleet data builds slowly and by season).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - pi*0.6: a VLA That Learns From Experience — Physical Intelligence — 2025-11-18 — arXiv 2511.14759 — tech-report
  - Learning While Deploying — Wang et al. — 2026-05-01 — arXiv 2605.00416 — preprint
  - ROVE — Xiao et al. — 2026-06-15 — arXiv 2606.17011 — preprint
  - HumanoidMimicGen — Lin, Mandlekar et al. — 2026-05-26 — arXiv 2605.27724 — preprint
  - DexMimicGen — Jiang et al. — 2024-10-31 — arXiv 2410.24185 — preprint
  - DreamGen — Jang et al. (NVIDIA GEAR) — 2025-05-19 — arXiv 2505.12705 — preprint
  - World Simulation with Video Foundation Models for Physical AI — NVIDIA — 2025-10-28 — arXiv 2511.00062 — tech-report (unconfirmed: date vs arXiv number)
  - MimicGen — Mandlekar et al. — 2023 — arXiv 2310.17596 — preprint (unconfirmed: not opened)
  - HG-DAgger — no authors recorded — no date — no URL — (seed lead, verify)
  - SkillMimicGen — no authors recorded — no date — no URL — (seed lead, verify)
- **Notes:** The 20% synthetic-over-real result is one benchmark's own claim: present it attributed and test it. No successor to π*0.6's Recap was found; check Physical Intelligence's site at research time. Public evidence that generated video helps real robots at scale is thin, and the chapter says so.

### ch21 · Modalities and the data dictionary
- **Purpose:** What a robot dataset actually holds, stream by stream: every observation, action, language and metadata field, at what rate, in what units, at what size, and how it is normalised for training; ending in the book's data dictionary, which Appendix A reproduces. ch21 owns the logical data model (episodes, streams, fields and conventions). Physical file formats belong to ch23, sensor physics to ch08, and action representations inside models to ch31.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch04 (the body defines the action space); ch06 (encoders); ch08 (every sensor; a sensor as a measurement with rate, resolution, range, noise and delay); ch13 (action chunking; turning actions into tokens by binning); ch15 (frames; rotation representations; joint space vs task space); ch16 (policy rate vs control rate; logging at the architecture level); ch17 (units of robot data); ch18 (egocentric device streams; narrations); ch19 (teleoperated demonstrations); ch20 (rollouts; interventions; fleet logs).
- **Estimated length:** about 12,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **21.01 · The episode: the unit of robot data.** Define the structure every robot dataset shares. *Concepts:* episode, step, timestep and frame (owned here; RLDS's episode-and-step model); the observation, action and language at each step; episode-level fields (task, success, length); recap: units of robot data (ch17); preview: files and formats (taught in ch23).
  - **21.02 · Images and depth as data.** Show how camera streams are described and stored. *Concepts:* image streams (resolution, frame rate, colour format, naming per camera: head, wrist, external; recap: cameras, ch08); depth streams (units, invalid pixels; recap: depth sensing, ch08); raw vs compressed size (preview: video compression, taught in ch23); DROID's three cameras as a worked schema.
  - **21.03 · Body and contact streams: proprioception, force/torque, tactile, IMU and audio.** Show the non-image streams and how they are named. *Concepts:* joint positions, velocities and efforts as data (units, joint ordering, naming; recap: proprioception, ch08); force/torque and tactile streams (rates, array shapes; recap: ch08); IMU streams (Aria Gen 2's two IMUs at 800 Hz as a dated example); audio and other wearable signals (Aria Gen 2's audio and PPG); stream rates that differ by orders of magnitude (preview: aligning streams, taught in ch22).
  - **21.04 · Actions and action spaces.** Teach exactly what an "action" field can mean. *Concepts:* the action space (owned here; previewed in ch04): joint positions, velocities or torques; end-effector poses in a named frame (recap: frames, ch15); absolute vs delta (relative) actions (owned here); gripper and hand actions; base and whole-body commands; commanded vs measured, what the policy asked for vs what the body did (owned here); action rate vs control rate (recap: ch16); action chunks in storage (recap: chunking, ch13); conventions that live in dataset cards rather than formats (scan finding); preview: action representations in models (taught in ch31); preview: action interfaces to the whole-body controller (taught in ch35); preview: cross-embodiment action spaces (taught in ch25).
  - **21.05 · Language fields.** Show where words sit in a robot dataset. *Concepts:* task instructions; narrations (recap: ch18); subtask labels; reasoning traces (preview: embodied reasoning data, taught in ch32); LeRobot's task table; where language comes from (preview: language annotation, taught in ch24).
  - **21.06 · Metadata.** Show the context that makes an episode interpretable later. *Concepts:* metadata (owned here): embodiment and robot identifiers; per-episode calibration records (DROID; preview: calibration, taught in ch22); site, operator and collection mode (teleoperated, autonomous, intervention; recap: ch19 and ch20); timestamps; GR00T's modality file mapping vector slices to named fields; privacy-relevant metadata (preview: taught in ch24).
  - **21.07 · Rates, sizes and the weight of an hour.** Teach the reader to estimate how big an hour of robot data is. *Concepts:* size per hour per stream (owned here; equation: rate times bytes per sample times 3,600, divided by a compression ratio); which streams dominate (video vs the rest; illustrative numbers marked `nofact` except where sourced); worked hours for the reference humanoid and for an egocentric headset (Aria Gen 2 rates sourced); preview: storage tiers and costs (taught in ch23).
  - **21.08 · Normalisation.** Explain how numbers on very different scales are made comparable for training. *Concepts:* data normalisation (owned here; distinct from normalisation layers, ch11): mean–standard-deviation and min–max scaling (equations), quantile scaling; dataset statistics files (LeRobot's stats file); per-embodiment statistics and why pooled statistics mislead (`[inference]`); angle wrap-around and rotation representations (recap: quaternions, ch15); undoing normalisation on the robot.
  - **21.09 · Schemas compared: RLDS, LeRobot, GR00T, DROID and Open X-Embodiment.** Show that the field has no single standard, and how the main schemas differ. *Concepts:* (aside) a dated table of how each schema defines episodes, observations, actions, language and metadata; LeRobot v2.1 vs v3.0, and GR00T's "flavour of LeRobot v2" with its modality file; Open X-Embodiment's cross-robot conventions; no single standard (scan finding); preview: physical layouts and converters (taught in ch23).
  - **21.10 · The data dictionary.** Assemble every stream into one reference table, the source for Appendix A. *Concepts:* (aside) the data dictionary (owned here): one row per stream with name, description, source sensor or device, rate, units, shape and data type, frame or convention, storage format, typical size per hour, and users (policy, world model, evaluation, safety, fleet operations); rows for every stream in 21.02–21.06, sourced where possible and `nofact` where illustrative; how to add a row for a new sensor; Appendix A reproduces this table.
- **Running-example hooks:** dinner in 21.02 and 21.05 (packaging labels readable only at enough resolution; an instruction given days before the task, `[inference]`); kitchen reset in 21.04 and 21.05 ("leave my glass" as a language field tied to one object; delta vs absolute actions while wiping); battery station in 21.03 and 21.06 (torque-tool records and force/torque streams as fields; plant work-instruction identifiers as metadata, `[inference]`); greenhouse in 21.07 (full-shift recording makes size per hour a cost question).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - LeRobotDataset v3.0 docs — Hugging Face — undated (page current 2026) — https://huggingface.co/docs/lerobot/lerobot-dataset-v3 — tech-report
  - LeRobotDataset:v3.0 blog — Capuano et al. (Hugging Face) — 2025-09-16 — https://huggingface.co/blog/lerobot-datasets-v3 — tech-report
  - Isaac-GR00T N1.7 repository — NVIDIA — undated — https://github.com/Nvidia/Isaac-GR00T — tech-report
  - GR00T N1 — NVIDIA — 2025-03-18 — arXiv 2503.14734 — preprint
  - DROID — Khazatsky et al. — 2024-03-19 (v2 2025-04-22) — arXiv 2403.12945 — preprint
  - Open X-Embodiment — Open X-Embodiment Collaboration — 2023-10-13 (v9 2025-05-14) — arXiv 2310.08864 — preprint
  - RLDS — Ramos et al. — 2021-11-04 — arXiv 2111.02767 — preprint
  - Aria Gen 2 Pilot Dataset — Kong et al. — 2025-10-17 — arXiv 2510.16134 — preprint
- **Notes:** Appendix A lifts 21.10 directly, so every rate, unit, format and size in it needs a claim tag or a `nofact` marker. Gaps: the full Open X-Embodiment field list; published sizes per hour from any dataset card; tactile and audio stream conventions. ch21 owns the logical schema; RLDS and LeRobot file layouts are taught in ch23.

### ch22 · Capture rigs, calibration and time synchronisation
- **Purpose:** How recorded data is tied to the true geometry and timing of the world: calibrating cameras, sensors and the robot's own body, synchronising clocks, aligning streams that arrive late, validating rigs in the field, and what errors in any of these may do to learning. ch22 owns calibration, time synchronisation and stream alignment. ch15 owns frames and transforms, and ch16 owns latency budgets inside the running robot.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch06 (encoders); ch08 (cameras, depth and IMU; a sensor's delay); ch15 (frames, poses and homogeneous transforms; forward kinematics); ch16 (latency budgets; middleware); ch18 (egocentric devices; motion capture); ch19 (teleoperation rigs; handheld grippers); ch21 (streams, rates, timestamps and calibration metadata).
- **Estimated length:** about 10,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **22.01 · Where and when: why calibration and sync matter.** Show that a data point is useless unless we know where and when it was taken. *Concepts:* calibration, measuring the numbers that relate sensor readings to the world and to each other (owned here); synchronisation, putting every reading on one clock (owned here); intrinsic, extrinsic and temporal calibration as three kinds; recap: frames and transforms (ch15).
  - **22.02 · Camera intrinsics and lens distortion.** Derive how a camera maps the world onto pixels, and how that mapping is measured. *Concepts:* the pinhole camera model (owned here; equation: projection with focal length and principal point); lens distortion, radial and tangential; calibration with a known pattern; reprojection error as the quality score (owned here); wide and fisheye lenses on wrists and headsets; OpenCV's procedure (source unconfirmed).
  - **22.03 · Extrinsics and hand-eye calibration.** Show how a camera's position on or around the robot is found. *Concepts:* extrinsic calibration (owned here; previewed in ch15); eye-in-hand vs eye-to-hand cameras; the hand-eye problem (owned here; equation: AX = XB, explained in plain words); camera-to-base calibration for external cameras (DROID's per-scene calibration); calibration-tolerant policies that predict their own hand-eye transform (CamVLA, preprint).
  - **22.04 · Multi-camera rigs and IMU–camera calibration.** Show how many sensors are calibrated together in space and time. *Concepts:* multi-camera rigs (shared targets, overlapping views); joint spatial and temporal calibration of cameras and IMUs (the Kalibr method of Furgale et al.); the IMU–camera time offset as a calibrated quantity (owned here); which Aria sensors carry intrinsics and extrinsics (Aria calibration documentation); factory vs field calibration.
  - **22.05 · Calibrating the robot's own body.** Show that the body is a sensor too, and drifts. *Concepts:* robot kinematic calibration (owned here: joint zero offsets, link lengths, encoder zeroing; recap: encoders, ch06; forward kinematics, ch15); force/torque sensor bias and drift (recap: ch08); re-calibration after repairs (`[inference]`; recap: maintenance, ch10); sources needed.
  - **22.06 · Clocks: timestamps, triggers, PTP and drift.** Teach how separate devices agree on the time. *Concepts:* the timestamp, capture time vs arrival time (owned here); clock offset and drift (owned here; equation: a linear clock model); hardware triggers; the Precision Time Protocol, IEEE 1588-2019 (owned here; equation: offset and path delay from four timestamps); a shared nanosecond clock across devices (Aria Gen 2; claim to trace); recap: middleware (ch16).
  - **22.07 · Latency and aligning streams.** Show how streams recorded at different rates and delays are lined up for training. *Concepts:* sensor latency in recorded data, the delay between an event and its timestamp (owned here); latency matching between camera, pose and gripper streams (UMI); resampling and interpolating streams onto one timeline (owned here); recap: latency budgets on the running robot (ch16); misaligned labels as a silent error (`[inference]`).
  - **22.08 · Capture rigs in practice.** Walk through the calibration and sync routine for each kind of rig. *Concepts:* (aside) routines per rig type: robot cells, egocentric glasses, handheld grippers, motion-capture studios and gloves (recap: devices, ch18 and ch19); what each must log as metadata (recap: ch21); calibration as a recurring operations task (`[inference]`).
  - **22.09 · Field validation and drift monitoring.** Show how a team knows a rig is still calibrated. *Concepts:* checks before and during collection (reprojection-error thresholds, known-object checks, sync events such as a flash or a tap; sources needed, `[inference]`); detecting a bumped camera from the data alone; re-calibration triggers; preview: automated quality checks (taught in ch24).
  - **22.10 · What bad calibration and sync do to learning.** State what is known, what is not, and what first principles suggest. *Concepts:* the evidence gap (no controlled study yet links calibration or sync error to policy success); indirect evidence (CamVLA's reported tolerance of extrinsic error in closed-loop policies, preprint; UMI's latency matching); first-principles expectations (`[inference]`, reasoning shown: errors should matter most for open-loop, precise and multi-view tasks); preview: data quality as a root cause of world-model failures (taught in ch41).
- **Running-example hooks:** battery station in 22.03 and 22.10 (lining up a connector depends on hand-eye accuracy, `[inference]` until sourced); kitchen reset in 22.07 (a wrist camera lagging the arm during a fast grasp of a glass, `[inference]`); dinner in 22.09 (a camera knocked during cooking, and how drift is detected); greenhouse in 22.05 and 22.09 (heat and vibration on a rail trolley shifting calibration over a season, `[inference]`).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Unified temporal and spatial calibration for multi-sensor systems — Furgale, Rehder, Siegwart — 2013 (IROS) — http://vigir.missouri.edu/~gdesouza/Research/Conference_CDs/IEEE_IROS_2013/media/files/0240.pdf — peer-reviewed (unconfirmed: exact date)
  - IEEE 1588-2019 PTP — IEEE — 2020-06-16 — https://standards.ieee.org/ieee/1588/6825/ — standard
  - Aria Gen 1 Device Calibration docs — Meta — undated — https://facebookresearch.github.io/projectaria_tools/docs/tech_spec/device_calibration — tech-report
  - Aria Gen 2 Pilot Dataset — Kong et al. — 2025-10-17 — arXiv 2510.16134 — preprint
  - UMI — Chi et al. — 2024-02-15 — arXiv 2402.10329 — preprint (RSS 2024 version: https://www.roboticsproceedings.org/rss20/p045.pdf)
  - From Fixed to Free Cameras (CamVLA) — Li et al. — 2026-07-06 — arXiv 2607.05396 — preprint
  - DROID — Khazatsky et al. — 2024-03-19 (v2 2025-04-22) — arXiv 2403.12945 — preprint
  - OpenCV calibration tutorial — OpenCV — undated — https://docs.opencv.org/4.x/dc/dbb/tutorial_py_calibration.html — tech-report (unconfirmed: HTTP 403)
- **Notes:** Aria Gen 2's sub-millisecond multi-device sync came from a search result: trace it to Aria's documentation before use. 22.10 states the evidence gap plainly and labels its reasoning `[inference]`. Gaps: robot kinematic calibration, force/torque bias, field-validation practice and a direct sync-error ablation.

### ch23 · Formats, storage and data pipelines
- **Purpose:** How robot data travels from the robot's disk to a training GPU: raw logs, training formats, video compression, loaders that stream data fast enough, indexes for finding the right episodes, storage tiers and their costs, and the lineage that traces a model back to its data. ch23 owns physical formats and pipelines. ch21 owns the logical schema, and ch24 owns quality and curation decisions.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch11 (embeddings; training runs and checkpoints); ch13 (action chunking); ch16 (middleware; ROS 2 topics; logging at the architecture level); ch21 (episodes; streams; size per hour; schemas compared); ch22 (timestamps; calibration metadata).
- **Estimated length:** about 10,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **23.01 · The data path from robot to model.** Draw the whole pipeline before its parts. *Concepts:* the data pipeline (owned here: log, upload, ingest, convert, validate, index, store, load); batch vs streaming pipelines; where quality checks and the flywheel plug in (preview: taught in ch24 and ch48).
  - **23.02 · Raw logging: ROS 2 bags and MCAP.** Show how a robot records everything it publishes. *Concepts:* the log file as a timestamped message record (recap: ROS 2 topics, ch16); MCAP's structure: chunks, indexes and compression (owned here); ROS 2 bags with MCAP as the default storage; on-robot limits on disk and upload bandwidth (`[inference]`); Foxglove adoption claims (company claims).
  - **23.03 · Training formats.** Compare the layouts used to feed training, and why they differ from logs. *Concepts:* why training needs random access and shuffling that logs do not offer (`[inference]`); RLDS on TensorFlow Datasets; LeRobotDataset v2.1 and v3.0 (many episodes per Parquet and MP4 file, relational metadata, a Lance option); HDF5 (seed lead); Zarr v3.1 chunked arrays; WebDataset tar shards; Robo-DM's single-file container; converters between formats; (aside) a comparison table.
  - **23.04 · Video compression and learning.** Explain how video is shrunk and what that may cost a policy. *Concepts:* how video codecs work: keyframes, predicted frames and groups of pictures (owned here; source needed); encoder settings and size vs quality (Li, Xu and Schwertfeger); lossy compression vs policy performance (Robo-DM's claims of storage up to 70 times smaller than RLDS and no policy degradation at 75 times compression; one lab, preprint; verify both figures); decoding cost during training; preview: compression artefacts as a world-model data issue (taught in ch41).
  - **23.05 · Streaming data loaders.** Show how data reaches the GPU fast enough. *Concepts:* the data loader (owned here); sharding and shuffling across shards; streaming from a remote hub (LeRobot's streaming class); keeping GPUs fed when decoding is slow (`[inference]`); time windows and action chunks as sample units (recap: ch13 and ch21).
  - **23.06 · Indexing, search and curation infrastructure.** Show how a team finds the episodes it needs among millions. *Concepts:* metadata indexes and queries (recap: metadata, ch21); embedding search over episodes and frames (recap: embeddings, ch11; source needed); finding rare events (`[inference]`); preview: quality checks and filtering decisions (taught in ch24); preview: failure mining (taught in ch48).
  - **23.07 · Storage tiers and costs.** Teach the arithmetic of keeping robot data. *Concepts:* hot, warm and cold storage tiers (owned here; source needed); object storage vs local disks; volume arithmetic (recap: size per hour, ch21; equation: monthly storage cost, illustrative numbers marked `nofact`); retention and deletion policies (preview: data-protection duties, taught in ch24).
  - **23.08 · Lineage, versioning and reproducibility.** Show how a team can say exactly which data trained which model. *Concepts:* dataset and format versions (LeRobot's codebase-version field); content hashes and immutable snapshots (source needed); lineage from a model checkpoint back to its episodes (owned here); why lineage matters for audits and deletion requests (preview: taught in ch24 and ch49).
  - **23.09 · A pipeline for the reference fleet.** Pull the chapter together for one fleet. *Concepts:* (aside) an end-to-end pipeline for a fleet of reference humanoids, stage by stage, with sizes and volumes marked `nofact` except where sourced; what published lab data engines disclose (little: the scan found only vendor pages).
- **Running-example hooks:** greenhouse in 23.02 and 23.07 (patchy network along rows forces store-and-forward uploads of full-shift logs, `[inference]`); battery station in 23.08 (tracing a torque-critical skill back to its episodes for an audit, `[inference]`); kitchen reset in 23.07 and 23.08 (deleting one household's data on request; preview ch24); dinner in 23.04 (heavy compression blurring label text, `[inference]`).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - LeRobotDataset v3.0 docs — Hugging Face — undated — https://huggingface.co/docs/lerobot/lerobot-dataset-v3 — tech-report
  - LeRobotDataset:v3.0 blog — Capuano et al. — 2025-09-16 — https://huggingface.co/blog/lerobot-datasets-v3 — tech-report
  - MCAP specification — Foxglove — undated (spec version 0) — https://mcap.dev/spec — tech-report
  - Robo-DM — Chen et al. — 2025-05-21 — arXiv 2505.15558 — preprint
  - High-Quality, ROS Compatible Video Encoding — Li, Xu, Schwertfeger — 2024-08-01 — arXiv 2408.00538 — preprint
  - RLDS — Ramos et al. — 2021-11-04 — arXiv 2111.02767 — preprint
  - Zarr core specification v3.1 — Miles et al. — undated — https://zarr-specs.readthedocs.io/en/latest/v3/core/index.html — standard
  - WebDataset — repository — undated — https://github.com/webdataset/webdataset — tech-report
  - HDF5 — no authors recorded — no date — no URL — (seed lead, verify)
- **Notes:** Robo-DM is the only direct compression-vs-policy evidence (one lab, preprint), and its two headline ratios need checking against the paper. Gaps: an independent compression study, primary lab data-engine descriptions, cloud storage prices, a video-codec basics source, versioning tools.

### ch24 · Quality, annotation, privacy and consent
- **Purpose:** How raw robot data becomes trustworthy, labelled and lawful training data: what quality means, automated checks, filtering and policy-aware curation, segmentation and language labels from people and VLMs, success labels, annotation QA, and the privacy, consent, licensing and data-protection rules that govern collection and use. ch24 owns data quality, annotation, and data-protection law as it applies to datasets. ch44 owns privacy by design in the deployed product; ch45 owns product regulation, the AI Act's risk classes and certification.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch11 (validation sets; generalisation; embeddings); ch12 (VLMs; hallucination); ch13 (multimodal demonstrations); ch14 (rewards; verifiable rewards vs learned reward models); ch16 (privacy of off-board processing, previewed); ch17 (diversity vs volume; open vs closed datasets); ch18 (wearable and crowd capture; narrations; web video); ch19 (good demonstrations; recoveries; home teleoperation); ch20 (interventions as noisy data; free labels); ch21 (fields; metadata); ch22 (calibration and sync checks); ch23 (indexing; lineage; retention).
- **Estimated length:** about 13,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **24.01 · What quality means for robot data.** Define quality by what it does to the policy, not by how tidy the data looks. *Concepts:* data quality as fitness for the policy (owned here); quality as contribution to validation loss (QoQ); human-judged quality vs policy-useful data (DataMIL's finding; preview: taught in ch26); quality vs quantity when adapting a pretrained policy (REAL-I challenge report, unconfirmed); preview: quality over quantity in fine-tuning (taught in ch33).
  - **24.02 · Automated quality checks.** Show the checks every episode should pass. *Concepts:* checks for sync and calibration (recap: ch22), dropped frames, idle time, coverage and success (owned here); per-episode quality reports; thresholds and their false alarms (`[inference]`); preview: fleet observability (taught in ch47).
  - **24.03 · Filtering and deduplication.** Show how bad and repeated data is removed, and what can be lost. *Concepts:* episode filtering and trimming (owned here); near-duplicate detection with embeddings (recap: ch11; source needed); the risk of discarding useful recovery behaviour (REAL-I); recap: filtering interventions (ROVE, ch20).
  - **24.04 · Curation by influence.** Explain the newest way to judge a demonstration: by its measured effect on the policy. *Concepts:* influence functions, estimating how much one training example changes the model's behaviour (owned here; equation in plain words); CUPID (demonstrations ranked by effect on closed-loop success); QoQ; ATHENA (influence scaled to multi-task data); dependence on the chosen checkpoint and validation set; preview: datamodels and mixture-level selection (taught in ch26).
  - **24.05 · Segmentation and subtask labels.** Show how long episodes are cut into meaningful steps. *Concepts:* temporal segmentation into subtasks (owned here); subtask boundaries as labels for hierarchical policies (preview: taught in ch31 and ch32); automatic segmentation from gripper and contact events (`[inference]`); recap: narrations (ch18).
  - **24.06 · Language annotation: people and VLM auto-labelling.** Show how instructions and descriptions are written, by hand and by machine. *Concepts:* human instruction writing; VLM auto-labelling and relabelling (owned here: dense, multi-aspect labels with measured policy gains on RoboCasa, per How to Instruct Your Robot); labeller errors (recap: hallucination, ch12; accuracy audits are a gap); preview: hindsight relabelling in the flywheel (taught in ch48); preview: embodied reasoning traces (taught in ch32).
  - **24.07 · Success, failure and reward labels.** Show how outcomes are recorded and checked. *Concepts:* episode outcome labels: success, failure, partial credit (owned here); human outcome labelling vs process signals (recap: free labels, ch20); automatic success detectors and VLM judges (preview: taught in ch34); noise in outcome labels (`[inference]`).
  - **24.08 · Annotation tooling, guidelines and QA.** Show how labelling is run as a quality-controlled operation. *Concepts:* annotation guidelines (owned here); gold sets and audits; agreement between annotators (owned here; source needed); annotation tools (sources needed); cost and throughput of annotation (gap).
  - **24.09 · Privacy in robot data.** Show what robot and wearable data reveal about people, and how it is protected. *Concepts:* what robot data captures about people: faces, bodies, voices, screens, documents and home layouts (owned here); redaction such as blurring and removing audio (source needed); where redaction runs, on the robot before upload or later in the pipeline (owned here at the data level; previewed in ch16; preview: privacy by design, taught in ch44); claimed no-go zones and safeguards in home teleoperation (1X, company claim; recap: ch19); bystanders in egocentric capture (recap: ch18).
  - **24.10 · Consent and contributor agreements.** Show who must agree to what. *Concepts:* data subjects vs contributors (owned here); informed consent for wearable capture, teleoperators, crowd contributors and households (owned here); withdrawal and deletion (recap: lineage, ch23); consent duties under India's DPDP Rules (detail in 24.12).
  - **24.11 · Licensing and data rights.** Explain who may use which data for what. *Concepts:* dataset licences and terms of use (owned here; source needed); rights to web video (recap: ch18); licence terms carried through mixtures (`[inference]`); recap: open vs closed datasets (ch17).
  - **24.12 · Data-protection and AI-data rules.** Lay out the rules that apply to collecting and training on personal data, dated. *Concepts:* GDPR basics: lawful basis, special categories, data-subject rights (official text not yet rechecked); India's DPDP Act and Rules (notified November 2025, most duties phasing in after 18 months; the notification date differs by source: Gazette 13 November, PIB 14 November); EU AI Act data-governance duties for high-risk systems, and the Digital Omnibus on AI, Regulation (EU) 2026/1744, deferring Annex III duties (per law-firm summaries; confirm in EUR-Lex); preview: AI Act risk classes, product regulation and certification (taught in ch45); preview: what to log, and with what privacy, in a fleet (taught in ch47).
- **Running-example hooks:** kitchen reset in 24.09 and 24.10 (a remote expert seeing a family's kitchen; children and guests as bystanders who never consented); dinner in 24.06 and 24.12 (annotating packaging and "may contain" text; guests' allergies as health information, `[inference]` until the legal category is sourced; preview ch44 and ch48); battery station in 24.07 (bolt torque records as clear-cut outcome labels); greenhouse in 24.03 (rare seasonal events filtered out as outliers by mistake, `[inference]`).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - CUPID: Curating Data your Robot Loves with Influence Functions — Agia et al. — 2025-06-23 — arXiv 2506.19121 — peer-reviewed
  - Quality over Quantity: Demonstration Curation via Influence Functions — Lee, Min et al. — 2026-03-10 — arXiv 2603.09056 — preprint
  - How to Instruct Your Robot: Dense Language Annotations Power Robot Policy Learning — Kim, Wang et al. — 2026-05-16 — arXiv 2605.17077 — preprint
  - ATHENA: Heterogeneous Influence Functions for Robot Data Curation — Xu, Wang et al. — 2026-06-15 — arXiv 2606.16208 — preprint
  - How to Better Train VLAs: Lessons from REAL-I Challenge at ICRA 2026 — authors not recorded — date not confirmed — arXiv 2609.13679 — preprint (unconfirmed)
  - Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) — EU Parliament and Council — 2026-07-08 — https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/reg/2026/1744/oj/eng — standard
  - Digital Personal Data Protection Rules, 2025 (G.S.R. 846(E)) — MeitY — 2025-11-13 — https://www.meity.gov.in/static/uploads/2025/11/53450e6e5dc0bfa85ebd78686cadad39.pdf — standard
  - DPDP Rules notified — PIB — 2025-11 — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=2190014&reg=3&lang=2 — press
  - GDPR — EU — no date — no URL — (seed lead, verify)
- **Notes:** Present both DPDP dates with attribution and say which record is stronger and why (the Gazette is the official legal text; the PIB release is a summary). Gaps: GDPR and EDPB texts, VLM-labeller accuracy audits, annotation-agreement, redaction and dataset-licensing sources. If the draft passes 18,000 words, propose moving 24.09–24.12 into their own chapter.

### ch25 · Bridging bodies: retargeting and cross-embodiment action spaces
- **Purpose:** How data from one body is made usable by another: the embodiment gap, retargeting human or robot motion onto a humanoid's body and hands, keeping retargeted motion physically possible, and the shared, latent and prompted action spaces that let one model learn across many bodies. ch25 owns the embodiment gap, retargeting and cross-embodiment action spaces. ch28 owns training tracking controllers in simulation, and ch31 owns cross-embodiment pre-training recipes.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch04 (degrees of freedom; the body defines the action space; the reference humanoid's variants); ch07 (human vs robot hand mismatch); ch13 (VAEs; vector quantisation); ch15 (forward and inverse kinematics; frames; dynamics and contact); ch17 (cross-embodiment datasets; the scale–alignment trade-off); ch18 (hand pose; full-body motion capture; learned inverse dynamics; latent action models); ch19 (whole-body teleoperation); ch21 (action spaces and conventions; normalisation; metadata).
- **Estimated length:** about 10,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **25.01 · The embodiment gap.** Define what differs between bodies and why it blocks reuse of data. *Concepts:* the embodiment gap (owned here; previewed in ch02, ch04 and ch18): differences in size and proportion, joints and degrees of freedom, hands, dynamics, sensors and appearance; human-to-robot vs robot-to-robot gaps; recap: the scale–alignment trade-off (ch17); four bridges as a map: retarget, share a space, learn a latent, tell the model which body.
  - **25.02 · Kinematic body retargeting.** Show how a person's motion is mapped onto a humanoid's joints. *Concepts:* retargeting (owned here; previewed in ch15); scaling and matching key points; retargeting as optimisation (equation: a weighted pose-matching cost subject to joint limits; recap: inverse kinematics, ch15); GMR (General Motion Retargeting; real-time on a CPU, per its repository); artefacts such as foot sliding, ground penetration and self-collision; "retargeting matters": retargeting quality drives tracking-policy quality (GMR report).
  - **25.03 · Hand retargeting.** Show how a human hand pose becomes robot finger commands. *Concepts:* hand retargeting by fingertip-position vs joint-angle matching (owned here); recap: hand-pose capture and gloves (ch18); recap: human vs robot hand mismatch (ch07); grasps that do not transfer (`[inference]`; source needed).
  - **25.04 · Physical feasibility and physics-based tracking.** Explain why a motion that looks right may be impossible for the robot. *Concepts:* kinematically valid vs dynamically feasible motion (owned here); physics-based tracking as a filter that keeps only motion a simulated body can perform (PHC and OmniH2O, seed leads not rescanned); preview: training tracking controllers with RL (taught in ch28); preview: whole-body action interfaces (taught in ch35).
  - **25.05 · Shared and unified action spaces.** Show how bodies can be made to speak one action language. *Concepts:* end-effector space as a common denominator (recap: action spaces, ch21); Open X-Embodiment's action conventions; a unified human–robot action space (Being-H0.5, with its Mixture-of-Flow design; owned here as a concept); what a shared space loses (`[inference]`).
  - **25.06 · Latent action spaces.** Show how a learned code can stand in for actions across bodies. *Concepts:* recap: latent action models (ch18); latent actions as the policy's action space, decoded per body (owned here); task-centric latent actions (UniVLA); VLM-guided latent actions (Nikulin, Zisman et al., 2026); compute-saving claims against OpenVLA from the authors' own benchmarks; Being-H0.7 as a latent world-action model (preview: WAMs, taught in ch40).
  - **25.07 · Embodiment tokens and soft prompts.** Show how one model is told which body it is driving. *Concepts:* embodiment conditioning (owned here); per-source soft prompts (X-VLA); embodiment metadata as conditioning (recap: metadata, ch21); preview: VLA architectures (taught in ch31).
  - **25.08 · Learning across bodies: what the results show.** Grade the evidence for cross-embodiment transfer. *Concepts:* positive transfer across embodiments (Open X-Embodiment); human-to-robot transfer at scale (EgoScale; recap: ch18); X-VLA and Being-H0.5 results on the authors' own benchmarks; the missing independent cross-embodiment benchmark; preview: cross-embodiment pre-training (taught in ch31).
  - **25.09 · Bridging bodies for the running examples.** Apply the bridges to "same brain, three bodies". *Concepts:* (aside) which bridge suits each task and variant: legged, wheeled base, rail trolley (recap: variants, ch04); what must be relearned per variant (`[inference]`, reasoning shown).
- **Running-example hooks:** battery station and greenhouse in 25.09 (wheeled and rail-trolley bases change the lower body but keep the arms, so arm data should transfer more readily than locomotion data, `[inference]`); dinner in 25.03 (a knife grip from human cooking video mapped onto a robot hand); kitchen reset in 25.02 (retargeted reaching into a low dishwasher rack; foot sliding near a toddler as a safety concern, `[inference]`).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Retargeting Matters: General Motion Retargeting for Humanoid Motion Tracking — Araujo, Ze, Xu, Wu, Liu — 2025-10-02 — arXiv 2510.02252 — preprint
  - GMR repository — Ze et al. — undated — https://github.com/YanjieZe/GMR — tech-report
  - X-VLA: Soft-Prompted Transformer for Cross-Embodiment VLA — Zheng, Li et al. — 2025-10-11 — arXiv 2510.10274 — preprint (ICLR 2026 per repo)
  - UniVLA: Learning to Act Anywhere with Task-centric Latent Actions — Bu, Yang et al. — 2025-05-09 — arXiv 2505.06111 — peer-reviewed (RSS 2025 per repo)
  - Vision-Language Models Unlock Task-Centric Latent Actions — Nikulin, Zisman et al. — 2026-01-30 — arXiv 2601.22714 — preprint
  - Being-H0.5 — Luo, Wang et al. — 2026-01-19 — arXiv 2601.12993 — preprint
  - Being-H0.7: Latent World-Action Model from Egocentric Videos — Luo, Zhang et al. — 2026-04-30 — arXiv 2605.00078 — preprint
  - EgoScale — Zheng, Niu et al. — 2026-02-18 — arXiv 2602.16710 — preprint
  - Open X-Embodiment — Open X-Embodiment Collaboration — 2023-10-13 (v9 2025-05-14) — arXiv 2310.08864 — preprint
  - PHC; OmniH2O — no authors recorded — no date — no URL — (seed lead, verify)
- **Notes:** Several 2026 results are unreviewed preprints, and latent-action efficiency claims come from the authors' own benchmarks: present them as such. No independent cross-embodiment benchmark was found. ch18 teaches the latent action model's mechanism; 25.06 teaches its use as a cross-body action space.

### ch26 · Designing datasets and data mixtures
- **Purpose:** How to design a dataset and a data mixture on purpose: specify what the robot must handle, cover it, organise tasks, weight and order sources, co-train across web, human, simulated and robot data, measure what each slice is worth, spend a data budget well, and document the result. ch26 owns dataset design, mixture weights, co-training as a concept, datamodel-based selection, data ablations, data budgets and dataset documentation. Stage-specific recipes belong to ch30–ch33, and sim+real co-training as a sim-to-real method to ch29.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch11 (data splits; distribution shift; scaling laws); ch17 (diversity vs volume; factors of variation; cost per useful hour; the data pyramid; cross-embodiment datasets); ch18 (human data and its scaling; EgoMimic); ch19 (collection methods and their costs); ch20 (synthetic, autonomous and deployment data; mixing evidence); ch21 (metadata; the data dictionary); ch24 (quality; influence functions; consent and licensing); ch25 (cross-embodiment action spaces; embodiment prompts).
- **Estimated length:** about 11,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **26.01 · A dataset as a designed product.** Show that a dataset is specified from the deployment backwards. *Concepts:* the target distribution, the situations the robot will meet at its sites (owned here); a dataset specification derived from deployment requirements (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); recap: distribution shift (ch11).
  - **26.02 · Coverage, diversity and the long tail.** Teach how to plan coverage rather than hope for it. *Concepts:* coverage matrices over factors of variation (owned here; recap: factors of variation and diversity vs volume, ch17); the long tail and rare events at the data level (owned here); sampling for coverage vs sampling for frequency; preview: rare-event failures (taught in ch41); preview: failure mining (taught in ch48).
  - **26.03 · Task taxonomies.** Show how tasks are organised so data can be planned and reused. *Concepts:* task taxonomies (owned here: tasks as verbs, objects and scenes; skills and subtasks); AgiBot World's 217 tasks as a documented example; reusing skills across tasks (`[inference]`); sources thin.
  - **26.04 · Mixture weights and curricula.** Explain how much of each source to show the model, and in what order. *Concepts:* the data mixture and its weights (owned here; equation: sampling probability per source); uniform vs tuned weights; Re-Mix's learned domain weights via distributionally robust optimisation (equation in an "Under the hood" block; its reported gains, 38% over uniform weights and 75% less data, are the authors' own, on RT-X data); curricula as ordering over time (owned here at the data level); preview: why stage order matters (taught in ch30).
  - **26.05 · Co-training: web + robot, human + robot, sim + real.** Teach the core recipe of training one model on several sources at once. *Concepts:* co-training (owned here; previewed in ch18 and ch20); π0.5's mixture of multi-robot, web and subtask-prediction data (technical report); GR00T N1's data pyramid as a mixture (recap: ch17); human + robot co-training (recap: EgoMimic, ch18); sim + real co-training (TUCO, unopened lead; preview: as a sim-to-real method, taught in ch29); preview: adding actions without erasing knowledge (taught in ch32); preview: pre-training recipes (taught in ch31).
  - **26.06 · Selecting data for a target task: datamodels and attribution.** Show how to choose which samples help a given task. *Concepts:* data attribution at the mixture level (owned here; recap: influence functions, ch24); datamodels, which predict a model's behaviour from which samples were included (owned here; DataMIL); sample-level vs domain-level selection; human-judged quality vs policy usefulness (DataMIL's finding).
  - **26.07 · Ablations: what is each slice worth?** Teach the experiment that measures a slice's value. *Concepts:* data ablations, retraining without a slice and measuring the change (owned here); a fair ablation (same compute, same evaluation); the cost and noise of real-robot evaluation (preview: statistics of evaluation, taught in ch43); `[inference]` where unsourced.
  - **26.08 · Data budgets and return on investment.** Show how to spend a data budget across sources. *Concepts:* recap: cost per useful hour (ch17); the marginal value of data (owned here; equation, illustrative numbers marked `nofact`); allocating a budget across sources (`[inference]`); the evidence gap (public ROI and budget sources are thin).
  - **26.09 · Dataset documentation.** Show what a dataset must say about itself. *Concepts:* dataset cards (owned here: collection method, embodiment, coverage, action conventions, licence, consent basis, known gaps); LeRobot dataset cards and DROID's documentation as examples; recap: conventions that live in cards (ch21); recap: consent and licensing (ch24).
  - **26.10 · A data plan for each running example.** Pull Part P4 together into four data plans. *Concepts:* (aside) one data plan per task, with sources, mixture, coverage priorities and budget drivers, numbers marked `nofact` (dinner: abundant cooking video plus label-reading data; kitchen reset: home teleoperation and household-specific data; battery station: scarce video, dummy-module demonstrations and synthetic multiplication; greenhouse: seasonal, scarce footage); preview: the data flywheel (taught in ch48).
- **Running-example hooks:** all four in 26.10; dinner in 26.02 (packaging in many languages and fonts as a coverage axis for label reading, `[inference]`); battery station in 26.05 and 26.08 (sim + real co-training on rigid parts; a revised connector in the later twist as a new coverage cell); greenhouse in 26.02 (once-a-year situations as the long tail); kitchen reset in 26.01 (a new home as a shift in the target distribution).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Re-Mix: Optimizing Data Mixtures for Large Scale Imitation Learning — Hejna, Bhateja et al. — 2024-08-26 — arXiv 2408.14037 — peer-reviewed (unconfirmed: CoRL 2024 venue)
  - DataMIL: Selecting Data for Robot Imitation Learning with Datamodels — Dass, Khaddaj et al. — 2025-05-14 — arXiv 2505.09603 — preprint (ICLR 2026 per a secondary note)
  - π0.5: a VLA with Open-World Generalization — Physical Intelligence — 2025-04-22 — arXiv 2504.16054 — tech-report
  - GR00T N1 — NVIDIA — 2025-03-18 — arXiv 2503.14734 — preprint
  - CUPID — Agia et al. — 2025-06-23 — arXiv 2506.19121 — peer-reviewed
  - ATHENA — Xu, Wang et al. — 2026-06-15 — arXiv 2606.16208 — preprint
  - AgiBot World Colosseo — AgiBot-World-Contributors — 2025-03-09 — arXiv 2503.06669 — preprint
  - Open X-Embodiment — Open X-Embodiment Collaboration — 2023-10-13 (v9 2025-05-14) — arXiv 2310.08864 — preprint
- **Notes:** Influence-based curation (CUPID, QoQ, ATHENA) is taught in ch24 and only recapped here. Gaps: Open X-Embodiment's mixture lessons, the GR00T N1.6 technical report, independent mixture benchmarks, ROI and budget sources, a documentation-standard source (none in the scans). TUCO was seen only in search results.

### Changes from seed (P4)
- ch17: added 17.02 (counting robot data) and 17.04 (pooled and cross-embodiment datasets) — the scan finds "hours" used inconsistently across claims, and ch03 previews cross-embodiment datasets to ch17.
- ch17: split "scaling evidence and its limits" into 17.05 (scaling laws) and 17.07 (limits of the evidence); recast "cost per hour by source" as 17.08 (cost drivers plus an explicit evidence gap); made "the data race" a dated table of attributed claims (17.09) — company curves are unreviewed, and per-hour costs exist only in vendor blogs, which are excluded.
- ch18: merged "web video" and "exocentric and multi-view capture" into 18.02; split egocentric devices (18.03) from datasets (18.04); added 18.07 (wearable capture at scale) and 18.10 (scaling and co-training with human data); moved wearable grippers to ch19 (handheld grippers, 19.05) — the scan suggests the additions, and teach once.
- ch18 vs ch25: ch18 owns hand-pose capture, learned inverse dynamics models and latent action models as a way to label action-less video; ch25 owns the embodiment gap, body and hand retargeting, and latent and unified action spaces as cross-body interfaces. ch18's "limits" section previews the embodiment gap to ch25 instead of teaching it — ch02 and ch04 already preview the gap to ch25.
- ch19: merged haptics into 19.03 (VR, AR and haptic teleoperation); added 19.08 (data factories), 19.09 (remote expert modes and corrections, including home teleoperation and privacy) and 19.10 (choosing a collection method); made cost and throughput (19.06) carry an explicit evidence caveat — scan suggestions; public figures are vendor-only.
- ch20 vs ch28: ch20 owns simulation data, data multiplication (the MimicGen line) and teleoperation in simulation as data sources; ch28 owns them as training practice — per the Part brief.
- ch20 vs ch48 and ch34: ch20 introduces fleet logs, interventions and free or automatic labels as sources, and owns DAgger as the reason corrections are collected; ch48 owns logging triggers, failure mining and the closed loop; ch34 owns RL from corrections and the Recap algorithm. Fleet logs and automatic labels were folded into 20.07 and 20.08, and 20.04 (video world models as data engines) was added, with ch39 owning world-model generation in depth — scan suggestions; teach once.
- ch20: took ownership of simulation as a data source from the ch01 preview (which pointed to ch27–ch29) — per the Part brief; ch27–ch29 keep simulators, training and the gap. ch01's 01.04 preview now points to ch20 as well (updated during consolidation).
- ch21: added 21.01 (the episode), 21.09 (schemas compared) and 21.10 (the data dictionary, built to be lifted into Appendix A); added commanded vs measured actions to 21.04; drew the line that ch21 owns the logical schema and ch23 the physical formats — the scan finds no single standard, and Appendix A needs one table.
- ch22: added 22.01 (why calibration and sync matter), 22.07 (latency and aligning streams), 22.08 (capture rigs in practice) and calibration-tolerant policies in 22.03; renamed "what bad calibration does to learning" to 22.10 and widened it to sync, stating the evidence gap — the scan finds no controlled study and suggests the latency section.
- ch23: added 23.01 (the whole pipeline), 23.08's model-to-data lineage and 23.09 (a pipeline for the reference fleet), with lab data engines marked as a gap — the scan found only vendor pages for lab data engines.
- ch24: added 24.01 (what quality means) and 24.04 (curation by influence); split privacy (24.09), consent (24.10), licensing (24.11) and law (24.12) — the scan reports policy-aware curation as the main 2025–26 shift, and each legal topic needs its own dates.
- ch24 vs ch44 and ch45: ch24 owns data-protection law as it applies to collecting and training on data (GDPR, DPDP, AI Act data governance) and where redaction runs; ch44 owns privacy by design in the deployed robot; ch45 owns the AI Act's risk classes, product regulation and certification — per the Part brief.
- ch25: added 25.03 (hand retargeting), 25.04 (physical feasibility), 25.05 (shared and unified action spaces) and 25.09 (bridges for the running examples); physics-based tracking kept as a concept, with tracking-controller training previewed to ch28 — the scan reports retargeting artefacts that harm policies, and ch28 owns motion imitation.
- ch26: added 26.01 (a dataset as a designed product), 26.06 (datamodels and attribution) and 26.10 (a data plan per running example); split "ablations and data attribution" into 26.06 and 26.07 — the scan reports sample-level selection as a new layer, and the Part needs a synthesis.
- ch26 vs ch29, ch31 and ch32: ch26 owns co-training as a concept and mixture design; ch29 owns sim+real co-training as a sim-to-real method; ch31 owns pre-training recipes; ch32 owns curating by proximity to the target domain and adding actions without erasing knowledge; ch33 owns how many demonstrations and quality over quantity in fine-tuning — per the Part brief; teach once.
- No PROPOSED chapter-level changes.
