## P5 · Simulation

### ch27 · Simulators and RL gyms
- **Purpose:** What a robot simulator and an RL environment are, how physics engines, renderers, assets and GPU parallelism work, which tools are in use in 2026, and where today's tools break for each running example. ch27 owns the simulator, the environment interface, physics engines (including why contact is hard to simulate), rendering and simulated sensors, assets and scene formats, GPU-parallel simulation, frameworks, digital-twin and real-to-sim tools, and simulated benchmarks. ch20 owns simulation as a data source; ch28 owns training in simulation; ch29 owns the gap and the use of real-to-sim tools to close it and to evaluate; ch36 owns world models vs simulators.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch04 (links, joints and degrees of freedom; the reference humanoid and its variants); ch05 (real vs ideal actuators: friction, saturation, delay); ch06 (backlash, friction and compliance); ch08 (cameras, depth, IMU, force/torque and tactile sensors; a sensor as a measurement with noise and delay); ch09 (GPUs and accelerators); ch11 (why GPUs suit training); ch14 (agent and environment; the MDP; episodes; rewards; verifiable rewards; learned dynamics models); ch15 (frames and transforms; forward kinematics; the equation of motion; contact and friction; the friction cone; PD control); ch16 (clock rate and the control loop); ch20 (simulation as a data source); ch22 (the pinhole camera model); ch25 (physics-based tracking).
- **Estimated length:** about 12,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **27.01 · What a simulator is, and why robots practise in one.** Define the simulator from first principles and say what it buys. *Concepts:* the robot simulator (owned here: a program that steps a model of the robot and its world forward in time); simulated time vs wall-clock time and the real-time factor (owned here; equation); why simulation is attractive: safety, speed, parallel copies, perfect labels and free resets (recap: simulation as a data source, ch20); recap: learned dynamics models (ch14); preview: world models vs simulators (taught in ch36); preview: the sim-to-real gap (taught in ch29).
  - **27.02 · Anatomy of an environment and the Gym API.** Show the parts every RL environment has and the interface that ties them together. *Concepts:* the environment as code (owned here; previewed in ch14): state, physics step, observation, reward, reset, termination and truncation; recap: the MDP and episodes (ch14); the Gym/Gymnasium reset-and-step interface (owned here; primary source needed); task vs scene vs environment (owned here); the RL gym, an environment packaged for training and benchmarking (owned here); vectorised environments (preview: GPU parallelism, 27.07).
  - **27.03 · Physics engines I: rigid bodies, joints and contact.** Derive what a physics step does and why contact makes it hard. *Concepts:* the physics step (owned here: integrate the equation of motion over a small time step; recap: equation of motion, ch15; equation: semi-implicit Euler step); time-step size, stability and cost; robot description files, URDF and MJCF (owned here; previewed in ch15); collision detection with meshes vs simple shapes; contact models, hard constraints vs soft penalty contact (owned here); solver iterations and tolerances; why contact is hard to simulate (owned here; previewed in ch15; recap: friction cone, ch15); actuator models inside the simulator (PD drives, torque limits; recap: real vs ideal actuators, ch05; preview: actuator networks, taught in ch29); engines in use (MuJoCo, PhysX in Isaac Sim and Isaac Lab, Newton; Genesis as an unconfirmed lead).
  - **27.04 · Physics engines II: deformables, cloth, fluids and cutting.** Show why soft and changing matter is the frontier of simulation. *Concepts:* deformable bodies (owned here: mesh-based and particle-based approaches; sources needed); cloth, cables and hoses as thin deformables; fluids and granular food (sources needed); vertex block descent (VBD) for deformables in Isaac Lab 3.0 (company claim); cutting, tearing, breaking and cooking as largely unsupported (`[inference]` until sourced); the accuracy-speed trade-off for soft matter; preview: deformables as a gap source (taught in ch29).
  - **27.05 · Rendering and simulated sensors.** Show how a simulator produces what the robot's sensors would see and feel. *Concepts:* rendering (owned here: rasterisation vs ray tracing; sources needed); simulated cameras and depth (recap: pinhole model, ch22; recap: depth sensors, ch08); state vs pixel observations and their cost (ManiSkill3's GPU-parallel state and visual simulation); simulated IMU, force/torque and tactile signals (sources needed); sensor noise models (owned here); transparent and shiny surfaces as hard cases (`[inference]` until sourced); preview: the visual gap (taught in ch29).
  - **27.06 · Assets and scenes.** Show what a simulated world is built from and how scenes are made at scale. *Concepts:* the asset (owned here: geometry, materials, physical properties, articulations); OpenUSD as a scene format (owned here; primary source needed); importing CAD models; asset libraries and procedurally generated scenes (RoboCasa365's 2,500 kitchens); LLM-generated tasks and scenes (owned here; sources needed); generative 3D worlds as scene sources (Choi et al.); physical properties such as mass and friction as the hidden half of an asset (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); preview: asset quality and evaluation accuracy (taught in ch29).
  - **27.07 · GPU-parallel simulation.** Explain how thousands of simulated robots run at once, and why that changed robot RL. *Concepts:* many environments on one GPU (owned here; recap: GPUs, ch09 and ch11); throughput in simulated steps per second (owned here; equation: environments times steps per second per environment); keeping physics, observations, rewards and the policy on the GPU to avoid copies (`[inference]` unless sourced); MJX and MuJoCo Playground (including its sim-to-real results); MuJoCo Warp; ManiSkill3; Isaac Lab; preview: massively parallel RL (taught in ch28).
  - **27.08 · Frameworks over engines: one task API, many backends.** Show how the tools are converging, and what that means for a team. *Concepts:* the learning framework vs the physics engine (owned here); Isaac Lab (paper) and Isaac Lab 3.0's swappable physics backends (beta 2, June 2026; company claim); Newton as an open engine under the Linux Foundation with NVIDIA, Google DeepMind and Disney (company claim); mjlab, an Isaac Lab-style framework on MuJoCo Warp; convergence on one task API over many engines (scan finding); cross-engine checks as a cheap robustness test (`[inference]`; preview: ch29).
  - **27.09 · Digital twins and real-to-sim reconstruction.** Show how a real place or object is turned into a simulation. *Concepts:* the digital twin (owned here); real-to-sim reconstruction (owned here: building a simulation from real scans); meshes, photogrammetry and Gaussian splatting (owned here as representations; primary sources needed); Gaussian-splat soft bodies (scan lead); appearance vs physics: a reconstruction can look right but carry no mass or friction (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); preview: real-to-sim for closing the gap and for evaluation (taught in ch29).
  - **27.10 · Task suites and benchmarks.** Show how simulated benchmarks are built and what their scores mean. *Concepts:* the simulated benchmark (owned here: fixed tasks, scenes and success checks); success checks written in code (owned here; recap: verifiable rewards, ch14); RoboCasa365 (365 tasks, 2,500 kitchens); BEHAVIOR-1K and the 2025 BEHAVIOR Challenge (winner about 26%, secondary report; first-place paper unconfirmed); ManiSkill3 task suites; LIBERO, RoboTwin 2.0 and RoboVerse (seed leads, unconfirmed); benchmark saturation and overfitting to a suite (`[inference]`); preview: simulation for evaluation (taught in ch28); preview: whether simulated scores predict real ones (taught in ch29); preview: evaluation methodology (taught in ch43).
  - **27.11 · A gym for each running example, and where the tools break.** Give each task the simulator, gym and world model it would need, and name the break points. *Concepts:* (aside) per task: engine, assets, gym, success check and the world model it would need (preview: world models, taught in ch36–ch41); dinner for five (food, liquids, heat and cooking change: no engine in the scans models them, `[inference]` until sourced); kitchen reset (rigid dishes and racks in simulated kitchens vs cloth napkins, water, a wet sponge, breakage and transparent glass); battery station (CAD-accurate rigid parts simulate well; cables, hoses, connector clicks and torque-tool reaction do not); greenhouse (Agri-Sim's Unity and ROS 2 tomato greenhouse, simulation-only with no real-transfer tests; plant deformation, occlusion and changing light); (aside) a break-point table by physics, rendering, sensing and assets; preview: the gap for each example (taught in ch29).
- **Running-example hooks:** all four in 27.11; battery station in 27.06 (CAD import of modules and busbars) and 27.04 (cables and coolant hoses as deformables); dinner in 27.04 (liquids, dough and heat); kitchen reset in 27.05 (rendering transparent wine glasses) and 27.10 (RoboCasa365's kitchens as the nearest benchmark, `[inference]`); greenhouse in 27.09 (scanning real rows into a twin, `[inference]`) and 27.11 (Agri-Sim).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Isaac Lab: A GPU-Accelerated Simulation Framework for Multi-Modal Robot Learning — Mittal et al. (NVIDIA) — 2025-11-06 — arXiv 2511.04831 — preprint
  - Isaac Lab 3.0 Beta 2 Release — NVIDIA/Isaac Lab team — 2026-06-23 — https://github.com/isaac-sim/IsaacLab/discussions/6249 — company-claim
  - Newton's Integration with Isaac Sim and Isaac Lab — NVIDIA — 2026-05-15 (updated) — https://perspectives.nvidia.com/isaac-sim/newtons-integration-isaac-sim-and-isaac-lab/ — company-claim
  - MuJoCo Playground — Zakka et al. — 2025-02-12 — arXiv 2502.08844 — preprint
  - mjlab — Zakka et al. — 2026-01-29 — arXiv 2601.22074 — preprint
  - ManiSkill3 — Tao et al. — 2024-10-01 (rev. 2025-05-30) — arXiv 2410.00425 — preprint
  - RoboCasa365 — Nasiriany et al. — 2026-03-04 — arXiv 2603.04356 — preprint
  - Agri-Sim — Shi et al. — 2026-08-29 — arXiv 2608.29100 — preprint
  - Task adaptation of VLA: 1st Place, 2025 BEHAVIOR Challenge — Larchenko et al. — date not confirmed — arXiv 2512.06951 — preprint (unconfirmed)
  - MuJoCo, MJX and MuJoCo Warp originals; Genesis; BEHAVIOR-1K; LIBERO; RoboTwin 2.0; RoboVerse — no date — no URL — (seed lead, verify)
- **Notes:** An Isaac Lab 3.0 general-availability date was seen only in aggregator text and stays out. BEHAVIOR's "about 26%" is secondary until the challenge's own results or the winner's paper confirm it. Gaps: the Gym/Gymnasium API reference, PhysX and OpenUSD primaries, and deformable, fluid, rendering and simulated-tactile sources.

### ch28 · Training in simulation
- **Purpose:** How robots are trained in simulation today: massively parallel RL for locomotion, curricula, privileged teacher–student training, motion tracking and whole-body controllers trained as foundation models, dexterity, synthetic demonstrations and teleoperation in simulation, sim+real co-training, simulation for evaluation, and what it all costs in compute. ch28 owns these as training practice. ch29 owns domain randomisation, adaptation and why co-training closes the gap; ch34 owns RL for VLAs (in simulation and on real robots); ch36–ch39 own world models, which appear here only as a bridge.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch03 (deep RL and sim-to-real; the Rubik's Cube hand as a milestone); ch05 (real vs ideal actuators); ch10 (learned protective falls); ch11 (training compute; checkpoints); ch12 (mixture of experts); ch13 (the robot policy; imitation learning; Diffusion Policy; guidance); ch14 (the MDP; PPO and sample inefficiency; SAC and off-policy learning; reward shaping and reward hacking; exploration); ch15 (PD control; balance; whole-body control as optimisation; residual learning); ch16 (the whole-body controller slot); ch18 (full-body motion capture); ch19 (whole-body teleoperation; VR teleoperation); ch20 (simulation as a data source; data multiplication; teleoperation in simulation as a data source; DAgger); ch25 (retargeting; physical feasibility and physics-based tracking); ch26 (co-training as mixture design; curricula as data ordering); ch27 (environments and the Gym API; physics engines and contact; rendering; generated scenes; GPU-parallel simulation; frameworks; benchmarks).
- **Estimated length:** about 13,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **28.01 · Why train in simulation, and what it cannot give.** Make the first-principles case and state its limits up front. *Concepts:* real trial and error is slow, costly and unsafe (recap: PPO's sample inefficiency, ch14; GPU-parallel simulation, ch27); which skills are trained mainly in simulation today (locomotion, whole-body control, in-hand dexterity) vs mainly from real data (contact-rich and deformable manipulation; `[inference]` until sourced); the dispute over real-world vs simulated RL (Choi et al. argue scene diversity favours simulation; attributed; preview: real-world RL, taught in ch34); preview: the sim-to-real gap (taught in ch29).
  - **28.02 · Massively parallel RL for locomotion.** Show the recipe that made learned walking routine. *Concepts:* locomotion as an RL problem (owned here: body-state and command observations; joint position targets for PD drives as actions, recap: PD control, ch15; falls as termination); the locomotion reward as a weighted sum of terms (equation; recap: reward shaping and hacking, ch14); learning to walk in minutes on one GPU (Rudin et al. 2022, seed lead); open locomotion gyms (legged_gym, Unitree RL Gym; seed leads); fast off-policy RL for humanoids (FlashSAC, RSS 2026; recap: SAC, ch14); wall-clock time vs samples as the real budget; recap: learned protective falls (ch10); preview: domain randomisation (taught in ch29).
  - **28.03 · Curricula.** Show how training is ordered from easy to hard so a policy can learn at all. *Concepts:* curriculum learning in RL training (owned here; distinct from data ordering, recap: ch26); terrain curricula that harden as the policy improves (seed lead); adaptive sampling of hard motion clips (GMT); recap: exploration and sparse rewards (ch14); preview: automatic domain randomisation as a curriculum over simulator settings (taught in ch29).
  - **28.04 · Teacher–student training with privileged information.** Show how a simulator's perfect knowledge is turned into a policy that needs only the robot's own senses. *Concepts:* privileged information (owned here: true friction, terrain shape and contact states known only to the simulator); the teacher policy trained with RL on privileged state (owned here); distilling the teacher into a student that sees only onboard sensors (owned here; equation: student imitation loss; recap: DAgger, ch20); history encoders, giving the student a window of recent observations (owned here at the mechanism level); why the student is the deployable policy; preview: online adaptation as a gap-closing method (taught in ch29). Original teacher–student source needed.
  - **28.05 · Motion imitation and tracking.** Show how human motion clips replace hand-written rewards. *Concepts:* the motion-tracking task (owned here: an RL policy rewarded for following a reference clip in physics; equation: tracking reward as a decaying function of pose error); DeepMimic (seed lead); recap: motion capture (ch18), retargeting and physics-based tracking (ch25); BeyondMimic (tracking plus guided diffusion to compose skills; recap: guidance, ch13); GMT (general motion tracking with adaptive sampling and a motion mixture of experts; recap: mixture of experts, ch12); mjlab's motion-imitation reference tasks; tracking as a substitute for reward engineering (scan finding); ASAP (seed lead, not confirmed).
  - **28.06 · Whole-body controllers as foundation models.** Show how one tracking controller is scaled up to serve many tasks and commanders. *Concepts:* the general-purpose tracking controller as a reusable lower layer (owned here at the training level; recap: the whole-body controller slot, ch16; whole-body control as optimisation, ch15); scaling motion tracking (SONIC: 42M parameters, 700 hours of motion capture, 21k GPU hours, per its authors); command interfaces from teleoperation, VR and higher-level policies (HOVER, OmniH2O, ExBody2 as seed leads; recap: whole-body teleoperation, ch19); what scale does and does not buy (SONIC's evidence, attributed); preview: action interfaces and learned whole-body control (taught in ch35).
  - **28.07 · Dexterous manipulation in simulation.** Show why hands strain simulators and how sim-trained dexterity reaches hardware. *Concepts:* many simultaneous contacts as the core difficulty (recap: contact, ch27); in-hand RL with a simulated hand (recap: the Rubik's Cube milestone, ch03); sim-to-real dexterity on humanoids (Lin et al. 2025, seed lead); DextrAH (seed lead); tactile and force feedback in sim-trained dexterity (two arXiv IDs share one title, unconfirmed); simulated touch (recap: tactile sensors, ch08; simulated sensors, ch27); RL experts distilled into vision-based policies (recap: 28.04).
  - **28.08 · Synthetic demonstrations and teleoperation in simulation.** Show how demonstrations made in simulation are used to train imitation policies. *Concepts:* recap: data multiplication and the MimicGen line as a data source (ch20); training on generated demonstrations as practice (owned here: how many, how mixed with real ones, what fails; MimicGen unconfirmed; DexMimicGen and HumanoidMimicGen results recapped from ch20); scripted, motion-planner and RL-expert demonstrators (owned here); teleoperation in simulation as practice (owned here: VR operators driving a simulated robot; recap: as a data source, ch20; recap: VR teleoperation, ch19; sources needed); when synthetic demonstrations mislead (preview: ch29).
  - **28.09 · Sim+real co-training.** Teach the recipe for training one policy on simulated and real data together. *Concepts:* recap: co-training as mixture design (ch26); sim+real co-training as a training recipe (owned here: mixing ratios, task-aligned vs task-agnostic simulated data, matched cameras and action spaces); Maddukuri et al. (reported gain of 38% from simulated data; attributed, preprint); TUCO (unopened lead); preview: why co-training closes the gap, with the 2026 mechanistic analysis (taught in ch29).
  - **28.10 · Generated worlds and the bridge to world models.** Show where learned and generated worlds are starting to stand in for hand-built simulators. *Concepts:* generative 3D worlds as RL training grounds for VLAs (Choi et al.: reported real success rising from 21.7% to 75%; attributed, preprint; recap: generated scenes, ch27); preview: RL in simulation for VLAs (taught in ch34); learned simulators, world models as an alternative training ground (preview: taught in ch36, ch38 and ch39); what physics engines still give that generated worlds do not (`[inference]`, reasoning shown).
  - **28.11 · Simulation for evaluation.** Show how simulation serves as a cheap test bench before hardware. *Concepts:* simulated evaluation as a first gate (owned here as practice: regression checks on every checkpoint; recap: benchmarks and coded success checks, ch27); training simulators vs evaluation-only simulators; what a simulated score can and cannot certify (`[inference]`); preview: sim–real correlation and the reverse gap (taught in ch29); preview: world-model-based evaluation (taught in ch39); preview: evaluation methodology (taught in ch43).
  - **28.12 · What training in simulation costs.** Teach the compute arithmetic and its drivers. *Concepts:* GPU-hours as the unit of simulation training cost (owned here; recap: training compute, ch11); reported budgets (SONIC's 21k GPU hours; FlashSAC's minutes vs hours; both attributed); cost drivers: number of environments, rendering vs state-only, model size and hyperparameter sweeps (`[inference]`); converting GPU-hours to money (equation; illustrative prices marked `nofact`).
- **Running-example hooks:** battery station in 28.07 and 28.08 (insertion skills and synthetic demonstrations from CAD-accurate rigid parts, while connector clicks and cables still need real dummy-module data, `[inference]`); kitchen reset in 28.08 and 28.09 (dishwasher loading in simulated kitchens co-trained with home demonstrations; napkins and water out of reach, `[inference]`); dinner in 28.01 (food cannot be simulated, so simulation trains balance and arm motion but not cooking, `[inference]`); greenhouse in 28.02 and 28.06 (the rail-trolley variant needs no walking policy, but whole-body reaching from a raising trolley does, `[inference]`).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - SONIC: Supersizing Motion Tracking for Natural Humanoid Whole-Body Control — Luo et al. (NVIDIA) — 2025-11-11 (v. 2026-08-13) — arXiv 2511.07820 — peer-reviewed
  - BeyondMimic — Liao et al. — 2025-08-11 — arXiv 2508.08241 — preprint
  - GMT: General Motion Tracking — Chen et al. — 2025-06-17 — arXiv 2506.14770 — preprint
  - FlashSAC — Kim et al. — 2026-04-06 — arXiv 2604.04539 — preprint
  - Sim-and-Real Co-Training — Maddukuri et al. — 2025-03-31 — arXiv 2503.24361 — preprint
  - Scaling Sim-to-Real VLA RL with Generative 3D Worlds — Choi et al. — 2026-03-19 — arXiv 2603.18532 — preprint
  - mjlab — Zakka et al. — 2026-01-29 — arXiv 2601.22074 — preprint
  - MimicGen — Mandlekar et al. — date not confirmed — arXiv 2310.17596 — preprint (unconfirmed)
  - Rudin et al. 2022 (learning to walk in minutes); DeepMimic; PHC; HOVER; OmniH2O; ExBody2; ASAP (2025); Lin et al. 2025 (sim-to-real dexterity on humanoids); DextrAH; legged_gym; Unitree RL Gym — no date — no URL — (seed lead, verify)
- **Notes:** SONIC's and FlashSAC's figures are the authors' own; the tactile-dexterity paper appears under two arXiv IDs (2601.02778 and 2607.04940) with one title, so confirm which is current. Gaps: the teacher–student original, Rudin et al., teleoperation-in-simulation sources, and any public price per GPU-hour from a primary source.

### ch29 · The sim-to-real gap
- **Purpose:** Why skills learned in simulation stumble on real hardware, where the gap comes from, how it is measured, and the four families of methods that close it; then the reverse problem of trusting simulated and world-model evaluations, the gap between world models and reality, and the gap for each running example. ch29 is the pilot: it is built first, so every recap of ch27 and ch28 must stand alone in one line with a chapter-level cross-reference. ch29 owns the gap, its measurement (including sim–real correlation metrics), domain randomisation, system identification, actuator networks, delay modelling, online adaptation, residual policies, real-to-sim and co-training as gap-closing methods, and the reverse gap. ch43 owns evaluation methodology, ch39 world-model-based evaluation, and ch41 the world-model failure taxonomy.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch01 (the world model in one line; the four running examples); ch03 (sim-to-real and domain randomisation as a milestone; the Rubik's Cube hand); ch05 (real vs ideal actuators: friction, saturation, delay, temperature; actuator wear); ch06 (backlash, friction, compliance and transparency); ch08 (sensor noise and delay; sensors in sun, steam and dust; tactile sensing); ch11 (generalisation; distribution shift); ch13 (the robot policy; imitation learning); ch14 (the MDP; PPO; a policy exploiting model error); ch15 (the equation of motion; contact and friction; PD control; residual learning); ch16 (latency budgets; stale observations; policy rate vs control rate); ch20 (simulation as a data source; data multiplication); ch22 (the pinhole camera model; latency and aligning streams); ch25 (the embodiment gap); ch26 (co-training as mixture design); ch27 (the simulator; physics engines and why contact is hard to simulate; deformables; rendering and simulated sensors; assets; swappable backends; digital twins, real-to-sim reconstruction and Gaussian splatting; benchmarks); ch28 (massively parallel RL; curricula; teacher–student training and history encoders; synthetic demonstrations; the sim+real co-training recipe; simulation for evaluation).
- **Estimated length:** about 13,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **29.01 · What the sim-to-real gap is.** Define the gap and its three faces before any cause or cure. *Concepts:* the sim-to-real gap, or reality gap (owned here; previewed in ch03 and ch11; recap: distribution shift, ch11); its three faces (owned here, framed by the brief): forward (a policy trained in simulation underperforms on hardware), reverse (a simulated or world-model evaluation mispredicts real results) and the gap between world models and reality; how it differs from the embodiment gap (recap: ch25); policies that exploit simulator quirks (recap: exploiting model error, ch14); zero-shot transfer vs transfer with some real data (owned here); The Reality Gap in Robotics survey as the chapter's map.
  - **29.02 · Where the gap comes from.** Trace the gap to its physical and computational causes. *Concepts:* a taxonomy of causes (owned here; per Aljalbout et al.): dynamics and contact (recap: why contact is hard to simulate, ch27; friction, ch15); actuation (recap: real vs ideal actuators, ch05; backlash, friction and compliance, ch06); sensing and rendering (recap: rendering and simulated sensors, ch27; sensors in sun, steam and dust, ch08); latency and timing (recap: latency budgets and stale observations, ch16); environment and assets (recap: deformables and assets, ch27); variation between units of one robot model and drift with wear (recap: actuator wear, ch05; `[inference]` until sourced); (aside) a table mapping each cause to its symptom on hardware.
  - **29.03 · Measuring the gap.** Teach the measures that turn "it works worse on the robot" into numbers. *Concepts:* paired sim and real tests (owned here: one policy, one task, both worlds); the success-rate gap (owned here; equation); system-identification error, the mismatch between simulated and real trajectories under the same commands (owned here; equation); sim–real correlation of policy scores (owned here; ch43 recaps): Pearson correlation (equation), rank correlation, and mean maximum rank violation, MMRV (equation; as used in SIMPLER, verify); why a high correlation can hide poor absolute accuracy (scan finding; worked example with numbers marked `nofact`); preview: sample sizes and confidence intervals (taught in ch43).
  - **29.04 · Closing it I: domain randomisation.** Show how training across many slightly wrong worlds makes reality one more variation. *Concepts:* domain randomisation (owned here; previewed in ch03 and ch20; the style guide's worked example); visual randomisation (Tobin et al. 2017, seed lead) and dynamics randomisation (original source needed); the objective as average performance over a distribution of simulators (equation); choosing ranges (too narrow misses reality, too wide yields timid policies); automatic domain randomisation that widens ranges as the policy improves (OpenAI 2019; recap: the Rubik's Cube milestone, ch03; recap: curricula, ch28); the price of robustness.
  - **29.05 · Closing it II: system identification, actuator networks and delay modelling.** Show how the simulator is made more like the real robot. *Concepts:* system identification (owned here; previewed in ch05; equation: choose simulator parameters that minimise trajectory error, recap 29.03); identifying friction, mass, payload and motor parameters (recap: ch05 and ch06); actuator networks, a learned model of the real actuator placed inside the simulator (owned here; previewed in ch05; Hwangbo et al. 2019); delay modelling (owned here; previewed in ch16; equation: the applied action is the command issued a delay earlier); differentiable simulation for system identification (HALO, payload-carrying humanoids); identification and randomisation as complements (`[inference]`, reasoning shown).
  - **29.06 · Closing it III: adaptation, residual policies and real-world fine-tuning.** Show how the policy, rather than the simulator, absorbs the difference. *Concepts:* recap: teacher–student training and history encoders (ch28); online adaptation, inferring hidden environment properties at run time from recent history (owned here; RMA, seed lead); residual policies that learn a correction on top of a sim-trained policy (owned here as a gap-closing method; recap: residual learning, ch15); learning the simulator's error from real data, delta-action models (ASAP, seed lead, not confirmed); real-world fine-tuning after simulation (owned here at the gap level; preview: real-world RL, taught in ch34); preview: safe exploration on hardware (taught in ch34 and ch44).
  - **29.07 · Closing it IV: real-to-sim digital twins and sim+real co-training.** Show how real data is brought into simulation, and simulation into real training. *Concepts:* recap: digital twins, real-to-sim reconstruction and Gaussian splatting (ch27); real-to-sim for training, rebuilding the deployment scene to train or fine-tune in it (owned here as a gap-closing method); recap: the sim+real co-training recipe (ch28) and co-training as mixture design (ch26); why co-training bridges the gap (owned here; Lei et al.'s mechanistic analysis); what each family costs and when it fails (`[inference]` until sourced).
  - **29.08 · The reverse gap I: when simulated evaluations mislead.** Show when a simulator's verdict on a policy can be trusted. *Concepts:* the reverse gap (owned here: a simulated score that ranks or predicts real policies wrongly); SIMPLER's real-to-sim evaluation of manipulation policies (owned here); a practical recipe for sim–real correlation in VLA evaluation (Wang et al. 2026: ranking, correlation and failure patterns); real-to-sim evaluation with Gaussian splats (PolaRiS; Gaussian-splat soft bodies as a lead; Real-to-Sim Policy Evaluation with Gaussian Splatting, unconfirmed); asset and scene reconstruction quality as a driver (Verma et al.: r 0.90 with authored assets vs 0.51 with defaults; attributed, preprint); a catalogue of reverse-gap failure modes (owned here: visual mismatch, missing physics, coded success checks that disagree with real judgement, ranking flips; `[inference]` where unsourced); preview: evaluation methodology (taught in ch43).
  - **29.09 · The reverse gap II: world models as evaluators, and the gap between world models and reality.** Show what changes when a learned world model, not a physics engine, judges the policy. *Concepts:* recap: the world model in one line (previewed in ch01; taught in ch36); world-model-based policy evaluation (preview: taught in ch39); the Interactive World Simulator's correlation claim (preprint, attributed); evidence that world-model evaluators ignore actions unlike those in their training data (Chen et al., "Do Robotic World Models Really Follow Actions?"); the gap between world models and reality (owned here at the level of trusting results: physics violations, optimistic or hallucinated success, action non-compliance; preview: the failure taxonomy, taught in ch41); how to read a correlation claim (how many policies, which tasks, which metric; `[inference]`, reasoning shown).
  - **29.10 · Choosing a gap-closing strategy and reading transfer claims.** Turn the chapter into a decision aid for a product reader. *Concepts:* (aside) a decision table mapping each cause (29.02) to the closing families (29.04–29.07) and to the measure that shows the fix worked (29.03); combining families in practice (per the survey; `[inference]` where unsourced); how to read a "zero-shot sim-to-real" claim (what was randomised or identified, how many real trials, which hardware, what failed); the gap after launch, where field logs reveal conditions outside the simulated ranges (preview: the data flywheel, taught in ch48).
  - **29.11 · The gap for each running example.** Apply causes, cures and trust in evaluation to the four tasks. *Concepts:* (aside) per task: dominant causes, applicable families, what simulated or world-model evaluation can be trusted for, and what must be tested on hardware; dinner for five (liquids and food, heat, and doneness that can be neither simulated nor scored; `[inference]` until sourced); kitchen reset (glass transparency and breakage, a wet sponge, cloth napkins); battery station (CAD-accurate rigid parts transfer, while connector clicks, cable stiffness and torque-tool reaction do not; system identification of the tool's reaction; unpowered dummy modules as the real-side test); greenhouse (plant deformation and lighting; Agri-Sim is simulation-only with no transfer test; real-to-sim reconstruction of rows, `[inference]`).
- **Running-example hooks:** all four in 29.11; battery station in 29.05 (identifying the torque tool's reaction and the delay of the force signal) and 29.08 (a coded "latched" check vs the real tug test); kitchen reset in 29.04 (randomising glass appearance and dish masses, `[inference]`); dinner in 29.09 (a world model predicting a sauce that looks plausible but behaves wrongly; preview ch41); greenhouse in 29.07 (a reconstructed row as a training and test scene, `[inference]`).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - The Reality Gap in Robotics — Aljalbout et al. — 2025-10-23 — arXiv 2510.20808 — peer-reviewed
  - Solving Rubik's Cube with a Robot Hand — OpenAI — 2019-10-16 — arXiv 1910.07113 — preprint
  - Learning agile and dynamic motor skills for legged robots — Hwangbo et al. — 2019 — https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/scirobotics.aau5872 — peer-reviewed (unconfirmed: page returned 403; abstract only)
  - Evaluating Real-World Robot Manipulation Policies in Simulation (SIMPLER) — Li et al. — 2024-05-09 — arXiv 2405.05941 — preprint
  - A Practical Recipe Towards Improving Sim-and-Real Correlation for VLA Evaluation — Wang et al. — 2026-06-09 — arXiv 2606.10366 — preprint
  - PolaRiS — Jain et al. — 2025-12-18 — arXiv 2512.16881 — preprint
  - Measuring Asset and Scene Reconstruction Effects in Real-to-Sim Evaluation — Verma et al. — 2026-09-30 — arXiv 2610.00731 — preprint
  - Do Robotic World Models Really Follow Actions? — Chen et al. — 2026-08-25 — arXiv 2608.24885 — preprint
  - HALO — Wang et al. — 2026-03-16 — arXiv 2603.15084 — preprint
  - Tobin et al. 2017 (domain randomisation); RMA (2021); ASAP (2025); Lin et al. 2025 — no date — no URL — (seed lead, verify)

The Runway world-model correlation figure (0.95) has no primary source and stays out unless research finds one. Gaps: a dynamics-randomisation original, a delay-modelling source, and the RMA and ASAP primaries.

### Changes from seed (P5)
- ch27: added 27.01 (what a simulator is) and 27.08 (frameworks over engines: one task API, many backends) — the scan finds tools converging on one task API over swappable engines (Isaac Lab 3.0, Newton, mjlab).
- ch27: split "physics engines" into 27.03 (rigid bodies and contact, with URDF and MJCF) and 27.04 (deformables, cloth, fluids and cutting) — ch15 previews contact simulation and robot description files to ch27, and soft matter is where every running example breaks.
- ch27: merged "LLM-generated tasks and scenes" into 27.06 (assets and scenes) — sources are thin, and generation is one way of making scenes; 27.11 adds the world model each example would need, as a preview — BRIEF §3 asks for each example's simulator, world model and gym.
- ch27 vs ch29: ch27 owns digital-twin and real-to-sim tools (including Gaussian splatting); ch29 owns their use to close the gap and to evaluate — teach once.
- ch28: added 28.01 (why train in simulation, with the real-vs-simulated RL dispute) and 28.10 (generated worlds and the bridge to world models); merged "teleoperation in simulation" into 28.08 — the scan reports generative 3D worlds feeding VLA RL, BRIEF §3 allows world models in ch28 only as a bridge, and the chapter needed room within 12 sections.
- ch28 vs ch29: ch28 owns curricula in RL training, teacher–student privileged training with history encoders, and the sim+real co-training recipe; ch29 owns domain randomisation (including automatic domain randomisation), online adaptation (RMA) and why co-training closes the gap — the seed listed curricula and teacher–student in both, and ch03, ch20 and the style guide already point domain randomisation to ch29.
- ch28 vs ch34: RL in simulation for VLAs is previewed to ch34; Choi et al. appears in ch28 only as a bridge — ch34's seed owns RL for VLAs.
- ch29: added 29.02 (where the gap comes from) as its own section; widened measurement (29.03) to Pearson and rank correlation, MMRV and system-identification error; split the reverse gap into 29.08 (simulated evaluations) and 29.09 (world-model evaluators and the gap between world models and reality); added 29.10 (choosing a strategy and reading transfer claims) — scan suggestions, BRIEF §3's three faces of the gap, and the product reader's need for a decision aid.
- ch29 vs ch39, ch41 and ch43: ch29 owns sim–real correlation metrics and the reverse-gap failure catalogue; ch43 should recap them (its seed section "simulation benchmarks and how well they correlate with reality" becomes a recap when P9 is planned); ch39 owns world-model-based evaluation; ch41 owns the world-model failure taxonomy — per the Part brief.

## P6 · Training robot policies

### ch30 · A primer on training stages: pre-, mid- and post-training and RL
- **Purpose:** The shared vocabulary for Part P6: what each training stage is for, why stages come in this order, what each costs, and how every major lab's published policy recipe maps onto the stages. ch30 owns the training stage, the foundation model, the four stages as generic concepts, catastrophic forgetting, stage-level budgets and the master lab-recipe map. ch31–ch34 teach each stage in depth and add a per-stage comparison; ch38 reuses the same four stage names for world models; ch50 recaps the map lab by lab.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch03 (generalist robot foundation models and flow-matching VLAs as milestones); ch11 (transfer learning; learning-rate schedules and the training run; training compute; scaling laws; distribution shift); ch12 (next-token pre-training; fine-tuning and instruction tuning; the InstructGPT recipe; VLMs as robot backbones); ch13 (the robot policy; imitation learning; flow-matching action heads; sampler distillation); ch14 (RL fundamentals; RLHF and the KL penalty; GRPO; where RL fits in the robot lifecycle); ch16 (fast-slow designs); ch17 (the data pyramid; the scale–alignment trade-off; cost per useful hour); ch18 (human data and its scaling); ch20 (autonomous and deployment data); ch26 (mixture weights; co-training; curricula as data ordering); ch28 (RL in simulation; GPU-hours as the cost unit).
- **Estimated length:** about 9,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **30.01 · Why robot models are trained in stages.** Derive from first principles why one training run becomes a sequence, each stage narrower than the last. *Concepts:* the training stage (owned here: one phase with its own data, objective and settings, starting from the previous phase's weights); the foundation model (owned here: a broad pre-trained model meant to be adapted to many tasks; previewed in ch03); broad-to-narrow ordering, each stage using less but more relevant data (`[inference]` framing; recap: the scale–alignment trade-off, ch17); recap: transfer learning (ch11); recap: fine-tuning, instruction tuning and the InstructGPT recipe (ch12); the language-model pipeline as the template robotics borrowed; preview: the same stages for world models (taught in ch38).
  - **30.02 · Pre-training: a broad prior.** Show what the first and largest stage gives a robot model. *Concepts:* pre-training as a stage (owned here at a generic level; recap: next-token pre-training, ch12); the prior, what a model already "expects" before it sees task data (owned here); the kinds of pre-training data for robot policies (web and VLM data, cross-embodiment robot data, human video; recap: the data pyramid, ch17); starting from a VLM vs starting from scratch (π0.5's VLM-initialised recipe vs Helix 2.5's claimed pre-training from random initialisation on human data alone, company claim); preview: pre-training the robot brain (taught in ch31).
  - **30.03 · Mid-training: shifting the domain before task work.** Define the newest stage name and show that it means different things to different communities. *Concepts:* mid-training (owned here: an intermediate stage that shifts a pre-trained model toward a target domain before task fine-tuning); the language-model meaning (data, learning-rate schedules and long context, per the two October 2025 surveys); the robotics meaning (adding embodied data, and sometimes actions, to a VLM; DM0 and EmbodiedMidtrain); no shared definition yet (scan finding); preview: mid-training toward the physical world (taught in ch32).
  - **30.04 · Post-training: supervised fine-tuning, adaptation and distillation.** Show the stage that turns a general model into a deployable one. *Concepts:* post-training as a stage (owned here at a generic level; recap: fine-tuning, ch12); supervised fine-tuning on task demonstrations (preview: taught in ch33); adaptation to a new body, site or task (preview: taught in ch33); compression for speed (distillation and quantisation) as part of post-training (preview: taught in ch33); "task adaptation" as one lab's name for this stage (Helix 2.5, company claim); other labels to be collected at research time.
  - **30.05 · RL: learning from outcomes.** Show why reward-driven training usually comes last for robot policies. *Concepts:* the RL stage (owned here: training on the outcomes of the model's own attempts rather than on copied actions); why it needs a competent starting policy (recap: imitate first, reinforce second, ch14; exploration and sparse rewards, ch14); where the attempts happen: on real robots, in simulation or in a learned world model (recap: RL in simulation, ch28; preview: taught in ch34 and ch39); RL from deployment experience (π*0.6, Physical Intelligence technical report, results company-reported; preview: Recap, taught in ch34); an RL stage in a company recipe (GEN-1, company claim).
  - **30.06 · Why stage order matters: forgetting and interference.** Explain what goes wrong when a later stage undoes an earlier one. *Concepts:* catastrophic forgetting (owned here: new training overwrites shared weights that held earlier skills; equation: forgetting as the drop in an earlier task's score after a later stage); interference between objectives, where action-learning gradients damage a VLM's knowledge (owned here; DM0's blocked action gradients as one remedy; preview: knowledge insulation, taught in ch32); learning-rate schedules across stages (recap: ch11; Mo et al.'s taxonomy); replaying earlier-stage data in later stages (recap: co-training and mixture weights, ch26); preview: avoiding forgetting during fine-tuning (taught in ch33); preview: continual learning across releases (taught in ch49).
  - **30.07 · Compute and data budgets by stage.** Teach how volume, cost per hour and compute shift from stage to stage. *Concepts:* the budget shape, where data volume and compute shrink from pre- to post-training while relevance and cost per hour rise (`[inference]`, reasoning shown; recap: cost per useful hour, ch17); reported volumes per stage as dated, attributed rows (GEN-0's 270,000 hours and GEN-1's 500,000+ hours, company claims; GR00T N1.7's roughly 20,900 hours of human video; GE-Act 2.0's 300 to 30,000 hours); the compute-disclosure gap (few labs report GPU-hours; recap: GPU-hours, ch28); a worked stage budget with illustrative numbers marked `nofact`.
  - **30.08 · How the labs label their stages.** Lay every major lab's published policy recipe on one map, with evidence tags and honest gaps. *Concepts:* (aside) the master lab-recipe map (owned here): one row per lab, columns for pre-, mid- and post-training and RL, plus data emphasis, evidence tag and date, with "not disclosed" cells: Physical Intelligence (π0.5, π0.7, π*0.6); NVIDIA (GR00T N1, N1.6, N1.7; N2 announced, unconfirmed); Google DeepMind (Gemini Robotics 1.5 report; Gemini Robotics 2 blog only); Figure (Helix, Helix 02, Helix 2.5; company claims); Generalist (GEN-0, GEN-1); Dyna (Dyna-2); Skild (S1, company blog); Toyota Research Institute with Boston Dynamics (LBM paper; Atlas press release); AgiBot (GE-Act 2.0); Agility (whole-body control foundation model, company claim); Apptronik (Robot Park feeding Gemini Robotics, unconfirmed); Galbot (press only); UBTech (Thinker VLM); Unitree (UnifoLM-WMA-0); Meta (V-JEPA 2, a world model, so mapped in ch38); 1X, Tesla, OpenAI and Neura (no technical disclosure of a policy recipe found); academic recipes as reference rows (DM0, EmbodiedMidtrain, Being-H0.5); synonyms across labs ("co-training" in π0.5, "task adaptation" in Helix 2.5, others as found); how to read a recipe claim (which stages are disclosed, which data, which evaluation; `[inference]`); preview: per-stage comparisons (taught in ch31–ch34) and per-lab teardowns (taught in ch50).
  - **30.09 · Training stages for the running examples.** Show which stage supplies which capability for each task. *Concepts:* (aside) per task, what each stage must contribute (`[inference]`, reasoning shown, numbers marked `nofact`): dinner for five (reasoning and tool use from pre- and mid-training; multitasking skills from post-training); kitchen reset (few-shot adaptation to one household in post-training); battery station (precision post-training on dummy-module demonstrations; RL on dummy modules, never on live packs); greenhouse (few-shot post-training per variety; little data per season).
- **Running-example hooks:** all four in 30.09; battery station in 30.05 (outcome rewards are easy to verify, but trial and error on live packs is forbidden, so the RL stage runs on dummy modules or in simulation, `[inference]`); kitchen reset in 30.06 (a household fine-tune that erases general tidying skills as forgetting, `[inference]`); dinner in 30.03 (reasoning about hidden allergens as a capability that mid-training targets, `[inference]`); greenhouse in 30.07 (seasonal data keeps post-training budgets small, `[inference]`).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Mid-Training of Large Language Models: A Survey — Mo et al. — 2025-10 — arXiv 2510.06826 — preprint
  - A Survey on LLM Mid-Training — authors not recorded — 2025-10 — arXiv 2510.23081 — preprint
  - DM0: An Embodied-Native VLA towards Physical AI — En Yu et al. — 2026-02 — arXiv 2602.14974 — preprint
  - EmbodiedMidtrain — Du, Guo, Ye, Ren, Xiong — 2026-04-21 — arXiv 2604.20012 — preprint
  - π0.5: a VLA with Open-World Generalization — Physical Intelligence — 2025-04-22 — arXiv 2504.16054 — preprint
  - π*0.6: a VLA That Learns From Experience — Physical Intelligence — 2025-11-18 — arXiv 2511.14759 — tech-report (results company-reported)
  - Gemini Robotics 1.5 — Gemini Robotics Team — 2025-10-02 — arXiv 2510.03342 — tech-report
  - GR00T N1.7 blog — NVIDIA — 2026-04-17 — https://huggingface.co/blog/nvidia/gr00t-n1-7 — tech-report
  - Helix 2.5: Zero-Shot 30-Home Generalization — Figure — 2026-09-17 — https://www.figure.ai/news/helix-2-5-zero-shot-30-home-generalization — company-claim
  - GEN-1: Scaling Embodied Foundation Models to Mastery — Generalist AI — 2026-04-02 — https://generalistai.com/blog/gen-1 — company-claim
- **Notes:** Keep the four stage names identical to ch38's so the policy and world-model maps line up. The map's other rows draw on sources listed under ch31–ch35 and the ch50 scan (Dyna-2, Skild S1, Atlas, Agility, Apptronik, Galbot, UBTech Thinker, Unitree UnifoLM-WMA-0); GR00T N1.6/N1.7 stage detail, Gemini Robotics 2 and GR00T N2 have no technical report yet, so those cells say "not disclosed". The DM0 title differs between the ch30 and ch32 scans: confirm it.

### ch31 · Pre-training the robot brain
- **Purpose:** How a robot foundation model is built and pre-trained: from a VLM to a vision-language-action model, its objectives, how actions are represented, the architectures in use, the robot and human data it learns from, the scaling evidence, what it costs, and which models are open. ch31 owns the VLA, behaviour cloning as the pre-training objective, action representations inside models (including FAST), VLA architectures (single model, action expert, dual system, hierarchical inference), cross-embodiment and human-video pre-training as recipes, scaling evidence for robot foundation models, and open vs closed models. ch13 owns generic action generation; ch25 owns action spaces across bodies; ch40 owns world action models.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch03 (RT-2 and the VLA idea; Open X-Embodiment; flow-matching VLAs); ch09 (model size vs memory; inference budgets); ch11 (embeddings; vision encoders; scaling laws; training compute); ch12 (tokens; the transformer block; next-token pre-training; VLMs as robot backbones; mixture of experts); ch13 (tokenisers and binning; flow matching; Diffusion Policy; action chunking; flow-matching action heads; the robot policy); ch16 (fast-slow designs; policy rate vs control rate); ch17 (cross-embodiment datasets; robot data-scaling laws; limits of the scaling evidence); ch18 (human video; extracting actions from video; scaling with human data); ch21 (action spaces; normalisation; metadata); ch24 (subtask labels; quality labels); ch25 (unified and latent action spaces; embodiment prompts); ch26 (co-training; mixture weights); ch30 (the training stage; the foundation model; pre-training as a stage; the lab-recipe map).
- **Estimated length:** about 13,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **31.01 · From VLM to VLA: what a robot foundation model is.** Define the model this Part trains. *Concepts:* the vision-language-action model, or VLA (owned here; previewed in ch01, ch03 and ch12): camera images, body state and an instruction in, action chunks out; recap: VLMs as robot backbones (ch12); the first generation (RT-2 and OpenVLA, actions as tokens; recap: ch03); the robot foundation model as a VLA pre-trained to be adapted (recap: foundation model, ch30).
  - **31.02 · Starting from a VLM, or not.** Show what a robot model inherits from web pre-training, and the counter-recipe. *Concepts:* inheriting web knowledge by initialising from a VLM (π0.5; recap: ch12); what is kept, frozen or retrained (`[inference]` until each lab's recipe is read); recap: the vision encoder (ch11) and the open question of which encoder suits control; pre-training from random initialisation on human data alone (Helix 2.5, company claim); preview: whether VLM skill predicts VLA skill (VLM4VLA, taught in ch32).
  - **31.03 · Pre-training objectives: behaviour cloning at scale.** Derive the loss that teaches a model to act by copying. *Concepts:* behaviour cloning (owned here; previewed in ch13; equation: negative log-likelihood of demonstrated actions, as in the style guide's example); cross-entropy on action tokens vs the flow-matching loss on continuous actions (recap: equations, ch13); co-training objectives that keep language and vision skills alive (π0.5's mixture of multi-robot, web and subtask-prediction data; recap: co-training, ch26); conditioning on data-quality metadata so mixed-quality and failed episodes become usable (π0.7; owned here at the objective level); preview: steerable prompting with subgoal images (taught in ch42).
  - **31.04 · Action representations.** Show the ways a model can say "move like this", and what each costs. *Concepts:* recap: binning actions into tokens (ch13); FAST, compressing an action chunk with the discrete cosine transform before tokenising (owned here; previewed in ch13; equation in plain words: a chunk rewritten as a few frequency coefficients); continuous action heads: regression, diffusion and flow matching (recap: flow-matching action heads, ch13); action chunks inside a VLA (recap: ch13 and ch16); joint vs end-effector outputs at pre-training (recap: action spaces, ch21; unified spaces, ch25); discrete vs continuous trade-offs in training speed, precision and inference cost (`[inference]` where no head-to-head source exists); preview: action interfaces to the whole-body controller (taught in ch35).
  - **31.05 · Architectures: single model, action expert, dual system and hierarchy.** Lay out the designs that turn a VLM into a policy. *Concepts:* the single-stream VLA, actions as tokens in the language model (recap: RT-2 and OpenVLA); the action expert, a separate, smaller set of weights for actions that reads the VLM's internal features (owned here; π0 as an attributed example, to verify); mixture-of-transformers designs with separate weights per modality and shared attention (owned here; seed lead to verify; recap: mixture of experts, ch12); dual-system VLAs, a slow VLM and a fast action module (GR00T N1; recap: fast-slow designs, ch16); hierarchical inference inside one model, predicting the next subtask in words and then the actions (π0.5; recap: subtask labels, ch24); single model vs dual system as a design choice (owned here; previewed in ch16); model sizes as dated rows (π0.7 at 5B parameters; GR00T N1.7 at 3B); preview: the case for one end-to-end model (taught in ch42).
  - **31.06 · Cross-embodiment robot data in pre-training.** Show how one model learns from many bodies at once. *Concepts:* recap: cross-embodiment datasets (ch17) and embodiment prompts and unified action spaces (ch25); cross-embodiment pre-training as a recipe (owned here; previewed in ch25): π0.5's multi-robot data, Being-H0.5's 30 embodiments, X-VLA (recap: ch25); Gemini Robotics 1.5's Motion Transfer (stage detail to read in the report); positive vs negative transfer between bodies (recap: Open X-Embodiment, ch25; `[inference]` beyond it).
  - **31.07 · Human-video pre-training.** Show human data entering the pre-training stage, and what is claimed for it. *Concepts:* recap: egocentric video, hand trajectories, inverse dynamics and latent actions (ch18); human-video pre-training as a recipe (owned here; previewed in ch18): GR00T N1.7 (about 20,900 hours of human video, NVIDIA), Being-H0.5 (35,000 hours), Helix 2.5's Index data (company claim), GEN-0 and GEN-1 wearable data (company claims); recap: EgoScale's scaling result (ch18); recap: the unreplicated claims that human video beats robot data (ch17 and ch18); what a model must still learn from robot data (`[inference]`, reasoning shown).
  - **31.08 · Scaling evidence for robot foundation models.** Grade every scaling claim by who measured it and how. *Concepts:* recap: power-law scaling (ch11) and robot data-scaling laws (ch17); model and data scaling for robot foundation models (owned here; previewed in ch11 and ch17): GEN-0 (270,000 hours and a claimed model-size threshold near 7B parameters, company claim), GE-Act 2.0 (300 to 30,000 hours, preprint), TRI's blind-tested study of what pre-training buys (A Careful Examination of Large Behavior Models), EgoScale (recap); the absence of independent replication (scan finding; recap: limits of the evidence, ch17); preview: evaluation methods behind the curves (taught in ch43).
  - **31.09 · What pre-training costs.** Teach the compute arithmetic and how little of it is public. *Concepts:* recap: training compute in floating-point operations (ch11) and GPU-hours (ch28); drivers of pre-training cost (parameters, tokens or hours seen, video decoding, action-head steps; `[inference]`); what labs disclose about pre-training compute (largely nothing in the scans: an explicit gap); model size as a deployment cost fixed at pre-training (recap: model size vs memory, ch09; preview: compression, taught in ch33); a worked estimate with illustrative numbers marked `nofact`.
  - **31.10 · Open vs closed models.** Show what "open" can mean and what a product team can verify. *Concepts:* open weights, open code, open data and open training recipes as separate levels (owned here; previewed in ch03); open families as dated rows (OpenVLA; π0 and π0.5 through openpi; GR00T N1.x with its repository; X-VLA; SmolVLA as a seed lead, unconfirmed); closed families (Gemini Robotics, Helix, GEN, Skild S1, Dyna-2); licences on weights (recap: licensing, ch24); what an outsider can and cannot check (`[inference]`, reasoning shown).
  - **31.11 · Pre-training recipes compared, and the turn toward world-action models.** Fill in the pre-training column of the lab map, and show where the next recipes are heading. *Concepts:* (aside) pre-training recipes compared: data mix, initialisation, objective, action representation, architecture, size and evidence tag per model (π0.5, π0.7, GR00T N1 and N1.7, Gemini Robotics 1.5, Helix 2.5, GEN-0, Being-H0.5, GE-Act 2.0, Skild S1, TRI LBM), "not disclosed" where so; recap: the master lab-recipe map (ch30); pre-training on video prediction as an alternative prior (DreamZero; Dyna-2's world-action model, company claim; GR00T N2, announced but unconfirmed; preview: world action models, taught in ch40; preview: convergence, taught in ch42).
- **Running-example hooks:** dinner in 31.03 and 31.07 (abundant cooking video as pre-training data, yet no record of chopping force; preview ch41); kitchen reset in 31.05 ("leave my glass" handled by hierarchical inference: a subtask in words, then actions, `[inference]`); battery station in 31.04 (the precision a connector insertion needs as a test of discrete vs continuous action outputs, `[inference]`); greenhouse in 31.06 (rail-trolley data as one more embodiment in the mix, `[inference]`).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - π0.7: a Steerable Generalist Robotic Foundation Model — Physical Intelligence — 2026-04-16 — arXiv 2604.15483 — preprint
  - FAST: Efficient Action Tokenization — Pertsch et al. — 2025-01-16 — arXiv 2501.09747 — preprint
