Further leads: π0 (arXiv 2410.24164), RT-2 (arXiv 2307.15818), OpenVLA (arXiv 2406.09246), Helix 2.5, Gemini Robotics 2 (blog only), DreamZero (arXiv 2602.15922). Skild S1 is dated 2026-08-31 in press but about 2026-08-18 on Skild's own blog: cite the blog; S1 supersedes "Skild Brain".

### ch32 · Mid-training: steering a general model toward the physical world
- **Purpose:** How a general vision-language model is moved toward the physical world before it learns tasks: embodied reasoning data, embodied reasoning models, chain-of-thought for robots, adding actions without erasing knowledge, choosing data close to the target domain, memory, and an honest reading of the evidence, including the sceptical case. ch32 owns embodied reasoning, embodied-reasoning models, embodied chain of thought, knowledge insulation, proximity-based data curation and memory for robot policies. ch30 owns the definition of mid-training; ch26 owns mixture weights; ch38 owns mid-training for world models.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch11 (vision encoders; distribution shift); ch12 (VLMs; reasoning models and chain of thought; the context window and key-value cache; VLMs as robot backbones); ch13 (action tokenisers); ch16 (the reasoner layer; latency budgets); ch21 (language fields and reasoning traces); ch24 (subtask labels; VLM auto-labelling; influence functions); ch26 (co-training; mixture weights; datamodels); ch30 (mid-training as a stage; catastrophic forgetting; interference between objectives); ch31 (the VLA; behaviour cloning; action experts; FAST).
- **Estimated length:** about 10,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **32.01 · Why mid-train: the gap between a VLM and a VLA.** Show what a web-trained VLM is missing for a robot, and where the extra stage fits. *Concepts:* recap: mid-training as a stage (ch30); what web VLMs lack for robots: metric space, depth, affordances, time and task progress (`[inference]`, with RoboBrain 2.5 as the sourced example); the measured distance between VLM data and robot data (EmbodiedMidtrain); mid-training vs co-training vs fine-tuning (owned here as a distinction; recap: co-training, ch26).
  - **32.02 · Embodied reasoning data: spatial questions, pointing, trajectories and affordances.** Show the data types that teach a model about bodies in space. *Concepts:* embodied reasoning (owned here; previewed in ch16 and ch21); spatial question answering; pointing and 2D or 3D grounding (owned here); trajectory prediction as a model output; affordances, where and how an object can be acted on (owned here); dense task-progress estimation (RoboBrain 2.5); depth-aware 3D grounding (RoboBrain 2.5); recap: reasoning traces as language fields (ch21).
  - **32.03 · Embodied reasoning models.** Show the VLMs built for the physical world and how fast their versions turn over. *Concepts:* the embodied-reasoning VLM (owned here): Gemini Robotics-ER (the 1.5 report; ER 2 in preview from July 2026 with ER 1.6 being retired, per Google's documentation), Cosmos-Reason (Reason1 paper; Reason2 model card in 2B, 8B and 32B sizes, company claim), RoboBrain 2.0 and 2.5, UBTech's Thinker; their role as the reasoner layer (recap: ch16); version churn, so every row carries a date (scan finding); self-reported benchmarks (`[inference]` on what they test).
  - **32.04 · Embodied chain of thought and planning traces.** Explain why a robot policy may reason step by step before acting, and what it costs. *Concepts:* recap: chain of thought (ch12); embodied chain of thought (owned here; previewed in ch12; ECoT: reasoning in words before each action, with the content of the reasoning to be read from the paper); planning traces as training data (owned here; recap: subtask labels, ch24); thinking before acting in Gemini Robotics 1.5 (recap: ch12); the latency cost of reasoning on a robot (recap: latency budgets, ch16).
  - **32.05 · Adding actions without erasing knowledge.** Show how labs teach a VLM to act while keeping what it knew. *Concepts:* recap: catastrophic forgetting and interference between objectives (ch30); knowledge insulation (owned here; previewed in ch26: shielding the VLM's weights from the action head's gradients; Driess et al., reported to train faster and protect knowledge; equation: a combined loss with a stop-gradient, to verify against the paper); DM0's insulated action expert in a three-stage recipe; co-training with web data as protection (recap: ch26); recap: action experts and FAST (ch31).
  - **32.06 · Curating mixtures by proximity to the target domain.** Show how to choose the VLM data that looks most like robot data. *Concepts:* proximity-based data selection (owned here; previewed in ch26): a small classifier scores how "robot-like" each VLM sample is, and the closest samples are kept (EmbodiedMidtrain; equation: selection by classifier score above a threshold); reported result: a 1.1B mid-trained model competitive with VLAs on backbones 3 to 8 times larger (authors' own benchmarks); recap: mixture weights and datamodels (ch26); proximity vs influence (recap: influence functions, ch24).
  - **32.07 · Memory and long context for robot policies.** Explain why most policies forget what they just did, and how memory is added. *Concepts:* recap: the context window and key-value cache (ch12); memoryless policies, which see only the present moment (owned here; `[inference]`, reasoning shown); a memory bank of past observations (MemoryVLA; successor MemoryVLA++ unconfirmed); multi-scale video and text memory for tasks of about 15 minutes (MEM); the cost of memory in latency and compute (`[inference]`); preview: memory in video world models (taught in ch37).
  - **32.08 · Evidence for the gains, and the sceptical view.** Weigh what mid-training has shown against the main counter-evidence. *Concepts:* what the papers show and on what (mostly small models on simulated benchmarks, self-reported; scan finding); VLM4VLA's finding that general VLM ability predicts VLA success poorly, with the visual encoder as a possible bottleneck (independent, preprint); how to present the disagreement (both positions attributed, with the stronger evidence named); how to read a mid-training claim (model size, benchmark, real or simulated, who measured; `[inference]`).
  - **32.09 · Mid-training recipes compared, and the running examples.** Fill in the mid-training column of the lab map and apply it to the four tasks. *Concepts:* (aside) recipes compared: DM0 (pre-, mid- and post-training), EmbodiedMidtrain, π0.5's co-training, Gemini Robotics 1.5's ER and VLA split, Cosmos-Reason as a reasoning backbone, and labs that disclose no mid-training ("not disclosed"); recap: the master lab-recipe map (ch30); (aside) what each running example needs from mid-training (`[inference]`).
- **Running-example hooks:** dinner in 32.02, 32.04 and 32.09 (tool use, timing several dishes, and reasoning about hidden allergens as embodied reasoning; reasoning latency against a hob that cannot wait, `[inference]`); kitchen reset in 32.02 and 32.07 ("leave my glass" as pointing and grounding; remembering which items have already been put away, `[inference]`); battery station in 32.02 (affordances of connectors and bolts; progress estimation across a several-minute cycle, `[inference]`); greenhouse in 32.08 (whether general visual skill transfers to ripeness judgement, the encoder question, `[inference]`).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - EmbodiedMidtrain: Bridging the Gap between VLMs and VLAs via Mid-training — Du, Guo, Ye, Ren, Xiong — 2026-04-21 — arXiv 2604.20012 — preprint
  - DM0: An Embodied-Native VLA Model towards Physical AI — En Yu et al. — 2026-02-16 — arXiv 2602.14974 — preprint
  - Knowledge Insulating VLA Models — Driess et al. (Physical Intelligence) — 2025-05-29 — arXiv 2505.23705 — preprint (NeurIPS 2025 spotlight per OpenReview listing)
  - VLM4VLA: Revisiting VLMs in VLA Models — Zhang et al. — 2026-01-06 — arXiv 2601.03309 — preprint
  - RoboBrain 2.5: Depth in Sight, Time in Mind — BAAI — 2026-01-20 — arXiv 2601.14352 — preprint
  - Gemini Robotics-ER API documentation — Google — July 2026 update — https://ai.google.dev/gemini-api/docs/robotics-overview — company-claim
  - Robotic Control via Embodied Chain-of-Thought Reasoning — Zawalski et al. — 2024-07 (day unconfirmed) — arXiv 2407.08693 — preprint
  - MemoryVLA — Shi et al. — 2025-08-26 — arXiv 2508.19236 — preprint (ICLR 2026)
  - MEM: Multi-Scale Embodied Memory for VLAs — Torne, Pertsch et al. — 2026-03-04 — arXiv 2603.03596 — preprint
  - Cosmos-Reason2 model card — NVIDIA — undated — https://huggingface.co/nvidia/Cosmos-Reason2-8B — company-claim
- **Notes:** Core equations: the knowledge-insulation loss with a stop-gradient; proximity-based selection. Further leads: RoboBrain 2.0 (arXiv 2507.02029); Cosmos-Reason1 (arXiv 2503.15558); UBTech Thinker (arXiv 2601.21199, ch50 scan); Gemini Robotics 1.5 for the ER 1.5 model. Present VLM4VLA as an independent counter-voice beside the method papers, and date every Gemini ER version.

### ch33 · Post-training: fine-tuning, adaptation and making models fast
- **Purpose:** How a pre-trained robot model becomes a deployable one: supervised fine-tuning on task demonstrations, how many and how good those demonstrations must be, adapting to a new body or site, parameter-efficient fine-tuning, avoiding forgetting, and then making the model small and fast enough to run on the robot through distillation, quantisation, latency engineering and packaging. ch33 owns supervised fine-tuning of VLAs, demonstration-count evidence, few-shot and new-embodiment adaptation, LoRA, model distillation, quantisation, the VLA latency model and packaging runtimes. ch16 owns latency budgets and real-time chunking; ch49 owns continual learning across releases.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch09 (number precision; memory capacity and bandwidth; onboard compute); ch11 (overfitting; the training run; checkpoints); ch12 (fine-tuning; in-context and few-shot learning); ch13 (sampler distillation; compounding errors); ch16 (latency budgets; asynchronous inference and real-time chunking; on-robot vs off-board placement); ch17 (diversity vs volume); ch19 (what makes a good demonstration); ch21 (action spaces; normalisation and statistics files; GR00T's modality file); ch23 (LeRobot formats); ch24 (data quality; influence-based curation); ch25 (embodiment conditioning); ch26 (co-training); ch28 (teacher–student distillation); ch30 (post-training as a stage; catastrophic forgetting); ch31 (behaviour cloning; action representations; open models); ch32 (knowledge insulation).
- **Estimated length:** about 13,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **33.01 · What post-training must deliver.** Set the targets before the methods. *Concepts:* recap: post-training as a stage (ch30); specialist vs generalist, fine-tuning one task vs keeping a model broad (owned here; `[inference]`, reasoning shown); the three targets: task success, robustness at the site, and speed on the robot's own hardware (owned here as a frame); preview: readiness criteria (taught in ch46).
  - **33.02 · Supervised fine-tuning on task demonstrations.** Show the basic recipe and its best-documented variants. *Concepts:* supervised fine-tuning of a VLA (owned here; recap: behaviour cloning, ch31); full fine-tuning vs a frozen backbone; OpenVLA-OFT's recipe for speed and success (LIBERO success from 76.5% to 97.1% and 26 times the action throughput, per its authors); GR00T N1.7 post-training on teleoperated demonstrations in LeRobot format (recap: formats, ch23; NVIDIA repository); π0.5 fine-tuning results (primary source not yet located: gap); overfitting a small task set (recap: ch11).
  - **33.03 · How many demonstrations, and how good.** Report what the evidence says about the size and quality of a fine-tuning set. *Concepts:* demonstration-count evidence (owned here; previewed in ch19): GR00T N1's results at 30, 100 and 300 demonstrations per task; pre-training as what lowers the count (TRI LBM study; recap: ch31); quality over quantity in fine-tuning (owned here; previewed in ch24; recap: influence-based curation, ch24; REAL-I lessons, unconfirmed); consistent demonstrations (recap: ch19); vendor demonstration-count advice excluded by the sources policy (scan finding).
  - **33.04 · Adapting to a new body, site or task.** Show the routes from a general model to a specific robot in a specific place. *Concepts:* new-embodiment fine-tuning (owned here; GR00T's modality configuration, recap: ch21; recap: embodiment conditioning, ch25); site adaptation vs task adaptation (owned here; `[inference]`); few-shot adaptation from a handful of demonstrations (owned here); adaptation without weight updates, from a video prompt (Skild S1, company claim; recap: in-context learning, ch12); preview: steerable prompting (taught in ch42).
  - **33.05 · Parameter-efficient fine-tuning.** Explain how to change a big model by training a small add-on. *Concepts:* LoRA, low-rank adaptation (owned here; previewed in ch12; equation: a frozen weight matrix plus the product of two thin trainable matrices); adapters and partially frozen models; memory and storage savings (`[inference]`, numbers marked `nofact`); one base model with many small task adapters as a deployment pattern (`[inference]`); LoRA original source needed.
  - **33.06 · Avoiding forgetting during fine-tuning.** Show how to keep general skills while learning a new one. *Concepts:* recap: catastrophic forgetting (ch30) and knowledge insulation (ch32); co-fine-tuning with earlier data (recap: co-training, ch26); evidence that plain sequential LoRA with RL forgets little ("Simple Recipe Works", preprint); measuring what was kept, with a retained-skills test set (`[inference]`); preview: continual learning and replay across releases (taught in ch49).
  - **33.07 · Distillation.** Show how a big model's behaviour is copied into a smaller or faster one. *Concepts:* model distillation (owned here; previewed in ch11 and ch13: a student trained to match a teacher's outputs; equation: the distillation loss); recap: sampler-step distillation (ch13) and teacher–student training in simulation (ch28); distilling a generalist into a task specialist vs into a smaller generalist (`[inference]`); VLA-specific distillation sources not yet found (gap).
  - **33.08 · Quantisation.** Show how numbers inside the model are made shorter, and why robots make this fragile. *Concepts:* quantisation (owned here; previewed in ch09 and ch16; equation: scale, round and clip to a small integer range); post-training quantisation vs quantisation-aware training; weight and activation bit widths (W4A4 notation); QuantVLA, described by its authors as the first post-training quantisation with a diffusion action head; sub-4-bit weights (ActQuant, date unconfirmed); VLAQuantBench's closed-loop finding that 4-bit π0.5 ranged from 7.0% to 70.5% success depending on which layers were protected; CHASE-VLA's 97.3% on π0.5 at W4A4 (self-reported, 2 October 2026); why closed-loop tests matter (recap: compounding errors, ch13).
  - **33.09 · Real-time inference: what sets a VLA's latency.** Teach the reader to predict how fast a model will run where it is placed. *Concepts:* recap: latency budgets, asynchronous inference and real-time chunking (ch16); the VLA latency model (owned here: VLA-Perf's analytical model of on-device, edge and cloud inference, and its stated takeaways); drivers: parameters, memory bandwidth, denoising steps and chunk length (recap: memory bandwidth, ch09; few-step sampling, ch13); placement trade-offs (recap: on-robot vs off-board, ch16).
  - **33.10 · Packaging for the robot.** Show what has to ship with the weights for a model to run safely. *Concepts:* inference runtimes and model export (owned here; vla.cpp, date unconfirmed; NVIDIA's Isaac-GR00T deployment tooling); the artefact bundle: weights, normalisation statistics, action-space configuration and version (recap: normalisation, ch21; `[inference]` for the bundle as a whole); recap: LeRobot's policy server (ch16); preview: over-the-air updates (taught in ch47) and release engineering (taught in ch49).
  - **33.11 · Post-training recipes compared, and the running examples.** Fill in the post-training column of the lab map and apply it to the four tasks. *Concepts:* (aside) recipes compared: OpenVLA-OFT, GR00T N1 and N1.7, π0.5 and π0.7, Gemini Robotics 2's on-device VLA (company claim), Helix 2.5's task adaptation (company claim), Skild S1's in-context adaptation (company claim), with "not disclosed" where so; recap: the master lab-recipe map (ch30); (aside) each running example's post-training and compression plan (`[inference]`, numbers marked `nofact`).
- **Running-example hooks:** battery station in 33.03, 33.06 and 33.11 (precision post-training on dummy-module demonstrations; the later twist, a revised connector and torque spec, as a fine-tune that must not forget the old variant; a quantised model that fails only on the tightest insertion, `[inference]`); kitchen reset in 33.04 (first-week adaptation to one household with few demonstrations; preview: preference learning over weeks, taught in ch48 and ch49); greenhouse in 33.04 and 33.05 (few-shot adaptation per variety, with one small adapter per season, `[inference]`); dinner in 33.09 (latency for stirring vs the reasoner's slow menu planning, `[inference]`).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - How Fast Can I Run My VLA? (VLA-Perf) — Jiang, Clemons, Sankaralingam, Kozyrakis (NVIDIA) — 2026-02-20 — arXiv 2602.18397 — preprint
  - Fine-Tuning VLAs: Optimizing Speed and Success (OpenVLA-OFT) — Kim, Finn, Liang — 2025-02 (day unconfirmed) — arXiv 2502.19645 — preprint
  - VLAQuantBench — authors not recorded — 2026-09-21 — arXiv 2609.25376 — preprint
  - CHASE-VLA — authors not recorded — 2026-10-02 — arXiv 2610.02666 — preprint
  - QuantVLA — authors not recorded — 2026-02-23 — arXiv 2602.20309 — preprint
  - Simple Recipe Works: VLAs are Natural Continual Learners with RL — Hu, ..., Stone, Martin-Martin — 2026-03-12 — arXiv 2603.11653 — preprint
  - GR00T N1 — NVIDIA — 2025-03-18 — arXiv 2503.14734 — preprint
  - Isaac-GR00T repository — NVIDIA — undated — https://github.com/NVIDIA/Isaac-GR00T — tech-report
  - ActQuant — authors not recorded — date not confirmed — arXiv 2605.24011 — preprint (unconfirmed: date)
  - vla.cpp runtime — authors not recorded — date not confirmed — arXiv 2606.08094 — preprint (unconfirmed: date)
- **Notes:** Core equations: the LoRA update; the distillation loss; the quantisation map. Gaps: the LoRA original, a π0.5 fine-tuning primary, VLA distillation sources, independent demonstration-count studies. If the draft passes 18,000 words, propose moving 33.07–33.10 ("making models fast") into their own chapter.

### ch34 · Reinforcement learning for robot policies
- **Purpose:** How robot policies learn from the outcomes of their own attempts after imitation runs out: why imitation plateaus, real-world RL and human corrections, the three styles of RL fine-tuning for large VLAs, where rewards come from and how reliable judges are, RL in simulation and inside learned world models, safe exploration and resets, and what the results do and do not show. ch34 owns real-world RL for robot policies, RL fine-tuning of VLAs, the Recap algorithm, success detectors, progress reward models and VLM judges, and safe exploration at the training level. ch14 owns RL fundamentals and GRPO; ch28 owns RL in simulation for locomotion and whole-body control; ch39 owns RL inside world models; ch48 owns the deployment flywheel.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch11 (distribution shift); ch13 (compounding errors; flow-matching action heads); ch14 (the MDP; the advantage; PPO; SAC and off-policy learning; exploration; model-based RL; offline RL; reward design, verifiable rewards and reward hacking; GRPO; where RL fits); ch16 (real-time chunking); ch19 (takeover interfaces); ch20 (autonomous rollouts; DAgger and interventions; deployment data; free labels); ch24 (success and failure labels); ch28 (massively parallel RL; generated 3D worlds as training grounds); ch29 (residual policies; real-world fine-tuning after simulation); ch30 (the RL stage); ch31 (the VLA; action representations); ch32 (dense progress estimation); ch33 (supervised fine-tuning; LoRA).
- **Estimated length:** about 12,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **34.01 · Why imitation plateaus.** Derive why copying demonstrations has a ceiling. *Concepts:* recap: compounding errors (ch13) and DAgger (ch20); the imitation ceiling, a copier is at best as good, as fast and as varied as its demonstrators (owned here); errors the demonstrations never show (recap: distribution shift, ch11); recap: imitate first, reinforce second (ch14); what RL can add: speed, reliability and recovery (`[inference]` framing, sourced in 34.10).
  - **34.02 · Real-world RL on robots.** Show RL running on physical hardware in hours, not months. *Concepts:* real-world RL (owned here; previewed in ch14, ch20, ch28 and ch29); recap: off-policy RL and replay (ch14); demonstrations plus RL on real robots (HIL-SERL: training in about 1 to 2.5 hours, per its authors; SERL as a seed lead); RL for real-time VLA policies (Dong et al.: 42% to 97% with 10 minutes of online data, preprint); the cost of robot time, resets and supervision (recap: autonomous rollouts, ch20).
  - **34.03 · Human-in-the-loop corrections.** Show how a person's interventions speed up and steer RL. *Concepts:* recap: interventions as data and takeover interfaces (ch19 and ch20); corrections inside the RL loop (owned here; HIL-SERL); RL from humanoid interventions (ROVE; recap: filtering interventions, ch20); noisy, inconsistent corrections (`[inference]` beyond ROVE); preview: the deployment flywheel (taught in ch48).
  - **34.04 · RL fine-tuning of VLAs I: on-policy PPO and GRPO.** Show RL applied directly to a large policy's weights. *Concepts:* RL fine-tuning of VLAs (owned here; previewed in ch14); recap: PPO and GRPO (ch14); outcome-only rewards of 0 or 1 (SimpleVLA-RL, in simulation, and the new "pushcut" behaviour it reports); why flow-matching policies are awkward for PPO-style updates (`[inference]` until read from πRL) and πRL's answer (PPO and GRPO for flow-matching VLAs on the RLinf framework; recap: flow-matching action heads, ch13); evidence concentrated on simulated benchmarks such as LIBERO (scan finding).
  - **34.05 · RL fine-tuning of VLAs II: advantage-conditioned learning and Recap.** Show the style that learns from mixed experience without on-policy gradients. *Concepts:* recap: the advantage and offline RL (ch14); advantage-conditioned policies, which are told whether an action was better or worse than usual and learn to produce the "better" kind (owned here; equation in plain words); Recap (owned here; previewed in ch20): advantage-conditioned learning from demonstrations, corrections and autonomous experience (π*0.6 technical report; mechanism detail to be read from it); π*0.6's reported results (an 18-hour espresso run and laundry; company-reported); no open Recap training code (scan finding); preview: learning from deployment logs in the flywheel (taught in ch48).
  - **34.06 · RL fine-tuning of VLAs III: a small RL module on a frozen VLA.** Show the cheapest way to add RL to a big model. *Concepts:* a frozen VLA with a small trainable RL module (owned here; RL Token: online RL on a VLA within hours, preprint); why it is cheap and stable to update (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); recap: residual policies (ch29) and LoRA (ch33); (aside) the three styles compared by data, compute, stability and evidence (`[inference]` where no head-to-head study exists).
  - **34.07 · Rewards: hand-designed, learned and judged.** Show where a robot's reward signal comes from and how far to trust it. *Concepts:* recap: reward design, verifiable vs learned rewards and reward hacking (ch14); success detectors (owned here; previewed in ch14, ch20 and ch24); progress reward models that score how far through a task the robot is (owned here; the 2026 survey, unconfirmed; recap: dense progress estimation, ch32); VLM judges (owned here; previewed in ch14, ch20 and ch24); FailBench (best judge at 0.77 balanced accuracy; judges lean toward "success" when unsure; equation: balanced accuracy); unreliable judges on contact-rich tasks (scan finding); Dyna's reward-model claims (seed lead, not found).
  - **34.08 · RL in simulation and in learned world models.** Show the two places where a VLA can practise without a physical robot. *Concepts:* recap: massively parallel RL (ch28) and generated 3D worlds as VLA training grounds (Choi et al., ch28); RL for VLAs in simulation (owned here; previewed in ch28; SimpleVLA-RL and πRL in simulated suites); RL inside a learned world model for manipulation (RISE: a compositional world model, with real-robot gains reported; preview: taught in ch39); free resets vs a model the policy can exploit (recap: model error, ch14; the sim-to-real gap, ch29).
  - **34.09 · Safe exploration and resets.** Show how RL is kept from damaging the robot, the task or people, and how episodes restart. *Concepts:* safe exploration at the training level (owned here; previewed in ch14 and ch29; jointly with ch44): bounded actions, supervised sessions, workspace limits and human stop authority (sources needed; `[inference]` until sourced); resets as a cost of real-world RL: human resets, scripted resets and reset-free learning (owned here; sources needed); where trial and error is forbidden and only simulation, dummy hardware or logs remain (recap: battery station, ch14); preview: safety filters and runtime monitors (taught in ch44).
  - **34.10 · Results, limits and RL recipes compared.** Say what RL has demonstrably added and fill in the RL column of the lab map. *Concepts:* what RL has added, attributed (speed and success gains reported by HIL-SERL, π*0.6 and Dong et al.); limits: simulation-heavy evidence, company-reported headline results, unreliable rewards and the cost of robot time (scan findings); (aside) RL recipes compared: π*0.6 Recap, GEN-1's RL stage (company claim), SimpleVLA-RL, πRL, RL Token, HIL-SERL, RISE, with "not disclosed" for labs with no RL detail; recap: the master lab-recipe map (ch30).
- **Running-example hooks:** battery station in 34.07 and 34.09 (success is easy to verify from torque records and a latch check, yet RL may only run on dummy modules or in simulation; a judge must not call a half-seated connector a success, `[inference]`); kitchen reset in 34.03 (remote-expert takeovers in the first week as corrections inside RL, `[inference]`); dinner in 34.07 ("tastes good" has no reward and no judge; RL limited to sub-skills with checkable outcomes, `[inference]`); greenhouse in 34.02 (slow, seasonal real-world RL rewards the fastest methods, `[inference]`).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Precise and Dexterous Robotic Manipulation via HIL-RL (HIL-SERL) — Luo, Xu, Wu, Levine — 2024-10-29 — arXiv 2410.21845 — preprint (Science Robotics version unconfirmed)
  - π*0.6: a VLA That Learns From Experience — Physical Intelligence — 2025-11-18 — arXiv 2511.14759 — tech-report (results company-reported)
  - SimpleVLA-RL — Li et al. — 2025-09-11 — arXiv 2509.09674 — preprint
  - πRL — Chen et al. (RLinf) — 2025-10-29 (latest version 2026-01-29) — arXiv 2510.25889 — preprint
  - RISE: Self-Improving Robot Policy with Compositional World Model — Yang et al. — 2026-02-11 — arXiv 2602.11075 — preprint
  - RL Token — Xu, Springenberg, Equi, Levine, Ke et al. — 2026-04-24 — arXiv 2604.23073 — preprint
  - RL for Real-Time VLA Policies — Dong, Hung, Sadigh, Finn — 2026-09-16 — arXiv 2609.18207 — preprint
  - FailBench — Navasardyan et al. — 2026-09-03 — arXiv 2609.03611 — preprint
  - Progress Reward Modeling for Robotic Learning: survey — authors not recorded — 2026 — arXiv 2607.21655 — preprint (unconfirmed: search result only)
  - SERL; ConRFT; VLA-RL; RL4VLA — no date — no URL — (seed lead, verify)
- **Notes:** Core equations: recap of PPO's clipped objective and GRPO's group advantage (ch14); the advantage-conditioned objective; balanced accuracy. ROVE (arXiv 2606.17011) is owned as a data source by ch20 and recapped here. Gaps: safe-exploration and reset sources, independent replications of Recap, Dyna's reward claims.

### ch35 · Connecting brain and body: from policy outputs to whole-body motion
- **Purpose:** How a policy's outputs become coordinated motion of a whole humanoid: the interfaces between the policy and the whole-body controller, the controller as a general "cerebellum", moving and manipulating at once, learned vs model-based control at this boundary, training the layers jointly or apart, and the failures that live at the seam. ch35 owns the policy–controller interface, learned whole-body control as a deployed layer, loco-manipulation, joint vs separate training of brain and body layers, and whole-body failure modes. ch15 owns whole-body control as optimisation; ch16 owns the whole-body controller's slot and timing; ch28 owns training tracking controllers in simulation.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch04 (degrees of freedom; the reference humanoid and its variants); ch10 (falling safely); ch15 (inverse kinematics; balance and centroidal dynamics; MPC; whole-body control as optimisation; where learning replaces or wraps classical control); ch16 (the whole-body controller slot; policy rate vs control rate; fast-slow designs; the safety layer); ch19 (whole-body teleoperation); ch21 (action spaces; commanded vs measured actions); ch25 (retargeting; physical feasibility; latent action spaces); ch28 (motion tracking; whole-body controllers as foundation models; SONIC); ch29 (the sim-to-real gap); ch31 (the VLA; action representations; dual-system architectures).
- **Estimated length:** about 10,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **35.01 · The handoff from brain to body.** Show the seam between a policy and the motors, and why its design matters. *Concepts:* recap: the layered brain and the whole-body controller slot (ch16); the policy–controller interface (owned here; previewed in ch15 and ch16): what the policy hands down and what the controller promises; why the interface shapes data needs, transfer across bodies and safety (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); recap: policy rate vs control rate (ch16).
  - **35.02 · Action interfaces I: joint and end-effector targets.** Show the two classic ways a policy commands a body. *Concepts:* recap: action spaces (ch21); joint-position targets tracked by PD loops (recap: ch15); end-effector targets solved by inverse kinematics or a whole-body controller (recap: ch15); a split design with an upper-body policy over a separate lower-body controller (NVIDIA's decoupled whole-body controller for GR00T N1.5 and N1.6, unconfirmed lead); what each interface leaves to the controller (`[inference]`).
  - **35.03 · Action interfaces II: keypoints, motion commands and latents.** Show the newer interfaces that hand the body more freedom. *Concepts:* keypoint and root-velocity commands (owned here; HOVER, seed lead); human-motion targets as commands (TWIST); unified motion latents as the interface (owned here; recap: latent action spaces, ch25; MotionWAM on the Unitree G1; OpenHLM's whole-body VLA recipe); language-conditioned motion commands (LeVERB, unconfirmed); trade-offs of expressiveness, data needs and checkability (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); (aside) a table of interfaces by what the policy outputs, what the controller must solve, and published examples.
  - **35.04 · The whole-body controller as cerebellum.** Show the controller as a general-purpose motion executor that many policies can drive. *Concepts:* recap: tracking controllers trained in simulation and SONIC's scale (ch28); the learned whole-body controller as a deployed layer (owned here; previewed in ch15 and ch16); one RL-plus-imitation controller that tracks human motion (TWIST); RL combined with trajectory optimisation on a 29-degree-of-freedom Unitree G1 (AMO); Agility's whole-body control foundation model (simulation RL, an LSTM under 1M parameters, zero-shot transfer; company claim); Figure's learned System 0 replacing hand-written control (Helix 02, company claim; the "109,504 lines of C++" figure only after tracing it to Figure's own post).
  - **35.05 · Loco-manipulation.** Explain the hardest combined skill: moving the body while the hands work. *Concepts:* loco-manipulation (owned here: walking, stepping or shifting weight while manipulating); balance under load and hand contact (recap: centroidal dynamics, ch15); reaching beyond the support polygon (recap: ch15); whole-body VLAs (Gemini Robotics 2, company claim, including its reported success rates on Apollo 2); one model driving hands and feet alike (Boston Dynamics and TRI's Atlas model, company claim, no paper found).
  - **35.06 · Learned vs model-based whole-body control.** Weigh the two approaches at this layer, fairly. *Concepts:* recap: QP whole-body control and MPC (ch15) and where learning wraps classical control (ch15); hybrids that combine RL with optimisation (AMO); what each offers: explicit constraints and analysability vs robustness and natural motion (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); the evidence base, mostly Unitree G1 preprints with thin failure reporting (scan finding).
  - **35.07 · Joint vs separate training of brain and body.** Show the choice between training layers apart with a fixed interface and training them together. *Concepts:* separate training with a frozen controller and a fixed command interface (owned here; recap: decoupled designs, 35.02); joint or end-to-end whole-body policies (owned here; Atlas model and Helix 02 as company claims; OpenHLM's recipe); interface mismatch when layers are trained apart (`[inference]`); recap: trade-offs of splitting layers (ch16); preview: the case for one end-to-end model (taught in ch42).
  - **35.08 · Failure modes: falls, self-collision and drift.** Show what goes wrong at the brain–body seam and how it is caught. *Concepts:* falls and fall recovery (recap: falling safely, ch10); self-collision (owned here at the policy level; sources needed); drift, slow accumulation of pose or command error over a long task (owned here; `[inference]` until sourced); infeasible commands, a policy asking for motion the body cannot make (owned here; recap: physical feasibility, ch25); detection and recovery at the controller (`[inference]`); thinly reported failure rates (scan finding); preview: fall management and safety layers (taught in ch44).
  - **35.09 · Brain to body for the running examples.** Choose an interface and a controller for each task and body variant. *Concepts:* (aside) per task and variant (`[inference]`, reasoning shown): battery station (absorbing the torque tool's reaction in stance; the wheeled variant removes stepping but not balance under load); kitchen reset (loco-manipulation around a toddler; reaching into low racks); dinner for five (carrying a hot pot while walking; preview: safety, ch44); greenhouse (whole-body reaching from a raising rail trolley, with no walking policy needed).
- **Running-example hooks:** all four in 35.09; battery station in 35.05 and 35.08 (the torque tool's kick as a whole-body disturbance; a stumble near live high voltage as the failure that matters most, `[inference]`); kitchen reset in 35.03 and 35.05 (stepping and crouching to load a dishwasher while holding a glass, `[inference]`); dinner in 35.05 (balance while lifting a heavy pot from the oven, recap ch15); greenhouse in 35.02 (an arm-only interface may suffice on the rail trolley, `[inference]`).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - TWIST: Teleoperated Whole-Body Imitation System — Ze, Chen, Araújo, Cao, Peng, Wu, Liu — 2025-05-05 — arXiv 2505.02833 — preprint
  - AMO: Adaptive Motion Optimization — Li, Cheng, Huang, Yang, Qiu, Wang — 2025-05-06 — arXiv 2505.03738 — preprint
  - SONIC: Supersizing Motion Tracking for Natural Humanoid Whole-Body Control — Luo et al. (NVIDIA) — 2025-11-11 (v. 2026-08-13) — arXiv 2511.07820 — peer-reviewed
  - MotionWAM — Zheng, Ma, Fan, Wang, Yang, Liang — 2026-06-08 — arXiv 2606.09215 — preprint
  - OpenHLM — Hu et al. (Yang Gao) — 2026-06-20 — arXiv 2606.22174 — preprint
  - Introducing Helix 02 — Figure AI — 2026-01-27 — https://www.figure.ai/news/helix-02 — company-claim
  - Atlas LBM, Boston Dynamics and TRI announcement — Boston Dynamics, TRI — 2025-08-20 — https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/ai-powered-robot-by-boston-dynamics-and-toyota-research-institute-takes-a-key-step-towards-general-purpose-humanoids-302534045.html — company-claim
  - Training a Whole-Body Control Foundation Model — Agility Robotics — 2025-08-28 — https://www.agilityrobotics.com/content/training-a-whole-body-control-foundation-model — company-claim
  - GR00T-WholeBodyControl repository — NVlabs — undated — https://github.com/NVlabs/GR00T-WholeBodyControl — tech-report (unconfirmed: page not opened)
  - HOVER; TWIST2; LeVERB (arXiv 2506.13751) — no date — no URL — (unconfirmed: search results only)
- **Notes:** The Atlas model has only a press release (and an unopened Boston Dynamics blog post, "Large Behavior Models and Atlas Find New Footing", 2025-08-20): attribute it and say no paper exists. Further lead: Gemini Robotics 2 blog (2026-07-30) for the whole-body VLA. Gaps: self-collision and drift sources, independent failure-rate data, a primary for the "109,504 lines" figure.

### Changes from seed (P6)
- ch30: added 30.01 (why train in stages, owning the training stage and the foundation model), 30.08 (the master lab-recipe map) and 30.09 (stages for the running examples); "how different labs label their stages" became the map — BRIEF §3 requires each lab's recipe mapped onto the stages, and ch14 and ch26 preview the stage framework and stage order to ch30.
- ch30: placed catastrophic forgetting in 30.06 as an owned concept, with ch33 owning avoidance during fine-tuning and ch49 continual learning across releases — no earlier chapter owns forgetting, and three later chapters need it.
- ch30–ch34: each stage chapter ends with a "recipes compared" aside that fills in its column of the ch30 map (31.11, 32.09, 33.11, 34.10); ch50 recaps by lab and ch38 keeps the same four stage names for world models — one map, filled in stage by stage, taught once.
- ch31: added 31.01 (from VLM to VLA), 31.02 (starting from a VLM, or not), 31.07 (human-video pre-training) and 31.11 (recipes compared and the turn toward world-action models); folded hierarchical inference and model sizes into 31.05 — scan suggestions; Helix 2.5's from-scratch claim and human-video pre-training are the main 2026 shifts, and ch16, ch18 and ch24 preview these concepts to ch31.
- ch31: updated research targets from "Skild Brain" to Skild S1 and kept π0.7 as the newest π model — the scan finds S1 supersedes Skild Brain and no π1.
- ch32: split embodied reasoning into data (32.02) and models (32.03); added 32.08 (evidence and the sceptical view, with VLM4VLA) and 32.09 (recipes compared) — scan suggestions; an independent counter-voice must sit beside the method papers.
- ch33: merged "how many demonstrations" with "quality over quantity" (33.03); split "distillation and quantisation" into 33.07 and 33.08; recast "real-time inference" as the VLA latency model (33.09), recapping ch16's budgets — quantisation is now a field of its own (VLAQuantBench, CHASE-VLA), and ch16 owns latency budgets and real-time chunking.
- ch34: split "RL fine-tuning of VLAs" into three styles (34.04 on-policy, 34.05 advantage-conditioned with Recap, 34.06 a small RL module on a frozen VLA); merged "RL in simulation for VLAs" with "RL inside world models" (34.08), the latter as a preview of ch39 — the scan reports three distinct 2026 styles, and ch39 owns RL inside world models.
- ch35: split "action interfaces" into 35.02 (joint and end-effector) and 35.03 (keypoints, motion commands and latents); added 35.09 (brain to body for the running examples) — the scan reports the shift to latent and keypoint commands, and the wheeled and rail variants change the interface.
- No PROPOSED chapter-level changes.

## P7 · World models

### ch36 · What a world model is
- **Purpose:** What a world model is, why prediction counts as understanding, why the fields that use the term define it differently, the families of world models by representation, passive vs action-conditioned models, what world models are used for, what self-driving world models teach, and how a world model differs from a physics simulator. ch36 owns the world model as a concept, the definitions and taxonomies, the families, passive vs action-conditioned prediction, the map of uses, self-driving world models as a side lesson, and world models vs simulators. ch37 owns video world-model internals; ch38 owns training; ch39 owns each use in depth; ch40 owns WAMs; ch41 owns failure modes.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** no.
- **Depends on:** ch01 (the world model in one line; the four running examples); ch03 (world models and V-JEPA 2 as milestones); ch11 (self-supervised learning; representations and latent spaces); ch12 (the transformer; VLMs); ch13 (probability distributions and sampling; conditional generation; autoencoders and latent spaces; diffusion; autoregressive generation; compounding errors); ch14 (agent, environment and the MDP; learned dynamics models; model-based RL and DreamerV3; a policy exploiting model error); ch15 (model predictive control); ch18 (camera-motion confounds; latent action models); ch20 (video world models as data engines; neural trajectories); ch27 (the simulator; physics engines and why contact is hard; Gaussian splatting and digital twins); ch28 (generated worlds and the bridge to world models); ch29 (the reverse gap; the gap between world models and reality; "Do Robotic World Models Really Follow Actions?"); ch30 (the training stage).
- **Estimated length:** about 10,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **36.01 · Prediction as understanding.** Derive from first principles why a model that can predict what happens next has captured something real about the world. *Concepts:* prediction as a test of understanding (owned here; `[inference]` framing anchored in the sources); the world model (owned here; previewed in ch01 and ch03): a learned model that predicts how the state of the world changes over time, often in response to actions (equation: the transition model, next state given current state and action; recap: the MDP, ch14); state vs observation, and why a world model often predicts observations it never fully explains (recap: partial observability, ch14); Ha and Schmidhuber's "World Models" (2018) as the modern origin (seed lead, unfetched); recap: learned dynamics models, model-based RL and DreamerV3 (ch14).
  - **36.02 · Definitions across fields, and why they clash.** Show that "world model" means different things in RL, video generation, self-driving and agent research, and give the book one working definition. *Concepts:* the RL meaning (a dynamics model used to plan or train; recap: ch14); the video-generation meaning (a "world simulator" that renders plausible futures); the agent-research meaning (a model an agent uses to reason about consequences); no single definition (scan finding across the 2026 surveys); capability levels, L1 predictor, L2 simulator, L3 evolver (owned here; Chu et al., preprint); the three-axis taxonomy of Li et al. (owned here; axes to be read from the paper, the scan reports function, time and space); the plausible-to-controllable-to-actionable ladder (Yao et al., preprint; to be read); whether video generators are true simulators (a 2026 survey, arXiv 2608.23070, unopened lead); the book's working definition and where it stops (preview: world action models, taught in ch40).
  - **36.03 · Video world models: predicting pixels.** Introduce the family that generates future camera frames, and what that costs. *Concepts:* the video (pixel-space) world model (owned here as a family): predicts future frames, usually in a compressed latent space and decoded back to pixels (preview: internals, taught in ch37); Cosmos-Predict2.5 (2B and 14B, NVIDIA technical report) and GAIA-3 (15B latent diffusion, Wayve, company claim) as dated examples; why pixels are attractive (human-checkable, trainable on internet video) and costly (compute spent on detail control may not need; `[inference]`, reasoning shown); recap: video world models as data engines (ch20).
  - **36.04 · Latent world models: the JEPA line and the Dreamer line.** Show the families that predict in an abstract representation instead of pixels. *Concepts:* latent prediction, forecasting a learned representation rather than an image (owned here); the joint-embedding predictive architecture, JEPA (owned here; recap: self-supervised learning, ch11): V-JEPA 2 pre-trained on video, then given actions, and used for robot planning from 62 hours of robot data (per its authors, preprint; preview: planning, taught in ch39); the Dreamer line, a latent dynamics model learned together with an agent (recap: DreamerV3, ch14); Dreamer 4, which trains agents purely inside the model (preprint; preview: RL inside world models, taught in ch39); predicting semantic masks as a middle path (preview: Mask World Model, taught in ch37); what a latent model cannot show a human reviewer (`[inference]`).
  - **36.05 · Interactive, 3D/4D, hybrid and omnimodal world models.** Complete the family tree. *Concepts:* interactive, game-style world models that respond to controls in real time (owned here): Genie 3 (DeepMind blog, company claim, not opened; productised as Project Genie in January 2026 per the scan); 3D/4D scene models that build an explorable space rather than a video (owned here; World Labs Marble, unconfirmed; recap: Gaussian splatting, ch27); physics-informed hybrids that pair a learned model with physics equations or an engine (owned here; seed family, sources needed); omnimodal models that reason, generate video and predict actions in one system (Cosmos 3's "mixture-of-transformers", NVIDIA claim; preview: WAMs, taught in ch40; convergence, taught in ch42); (aside) a family table: what each predicts, its action input, speed, examples and evidence tag (`[inference]` where cells are unsourced).
  - **36.06 · Passive vs action-conditioned world models.** Show why only a model that takes actions as input can answer "what happens if I do this?". *Concepts:* passive prediction, conditioned only on past frames, text or an image (owned here); action-conditioned (interactive, controllable) prediction (owned here; equation: next observation given past observations, with and without the action); the counterfactual argument, two different actions from the same moment must yield two different futures (first principles); kinds of control input: robot actions, camera motion, game-style controls and language (owned here as a concept; preview: conditioning mechanisms, taught in ch37; preview: adding conditioning in training, taught in ch38); latent actions for video with no action labels (recap: latent action models, ch18); action following as the property robotics depends on (recap: Chen et al., ch29).
  - **36.07 · What world models are for.** Map every use before ch39 teaches each one. *Concepts:* the map of uses (owned here): generating training data, evaluating policies, serving as an RL environment, planning, verifying actions before they run, and serving as a policy backbone; which family suits which use (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); recap: generated video as a data source (ch20); recap: world models as evaluators and the reverse gap (ch29); preview: each use in depth (taught in ch39); preview: world models as policy backbones (taught in ch40).
  - **36.08 · Lessons from self-driving world models.** Use driving, where world models met deployment first, as a side lesson. *Concepts:* driving world models as evaluation and simulation tools (owned here as a side lesson); Wayve's GAIA-3 (15B, framed as an evaluation tool; company claim; GAIA-2 as a seed lead); the Waymo World Model, built on Genie 3, with no sizes or data disclosed (company claim); Tesla's world-model work, known only from conference keynotes (ICCV 2025) with no paper found (demo, attributed); what transfers to humanoids (closed-loop testing, rare-event generation) and what does not (contact, hands, a changing body) (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); the disclosure gap (scan finding).
  - **36.09 · World models vs simulators.** Weigh a learned world model against a hand-built physics simulator, fairly. *Concepts:* world models vs physics simulators (owned here; previewed in ch27 and ch28): hand-written laws vs patterns learned from data; what a simulator gives (exact contact rules, privileged state, repeatability, coded success checks; recap: ch27) and what a world model gives (real appearance, matter that is hard to model by hand, learning from video; `[inference]` where unsourced); what neither guarantees (recap: the reverse gap and the gap between world models and reality, ch29); hybrids and the "true simulator" question (survey lead, unopened); (aside) a comparison table by fidelity, controllability, speed, cost, guarantees and failure style; preview: failure modes (taught in ch41).
  - **36.10 · A world model for each running example.** Say what each task would ask of a world model, and where it would break. *Concepts:* (aside) per task, the family, inputs and use it would need, and the likely break point (all `[inference]`, reasoning shown): dinner for five (predicting dough and sauce, a world-model failure case); kitchen reset (predicting spills and a glass that might tip); battery station (predicting contact and the connector click, which video barely shows); greenhouse (predicting how a vine moves when touched); preview: failures and data remedies (taught in ch41).
- **Running-example hooks:** all four in 36.10; dinner in 36.06 ("what if I stir now?" needs an action-conditioned model, `[inference]`); battery station in 36.09 (a CAD-accurate simulator beats a learned model for rigid parts, yet neither predicts the click, `[inference]`); greenhouse in 36.05 (a 3D scene model of rows, `[inference]`); kitchen reset in 36.07 (verifying a grasp on a wine glass before acting; preview ch39).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Agentic World Modeling: Foundations, Capabilities, Laws, and Beyond — Chu et al. — 2026-04-24 — arXiv 2604.22748 — preprint
  - A Comprehensive Survey on World Models for Embodied AI — Li et al. — 2025-10-19 (rev. 2026-06-25) — arXiv 2510.16732 — preprint
  - World Models for Embodied Intelligence: Plausible to Controllable to Actionable — Yao et al. — 2026-09-15 — arXiv 2609.16697 — preprint
  - V-JEPA 2 — Assran et al. (Meta FAIR) — 2025-06-11 — arXiv 2506.09985 — preprint
  - Training Agents Inside of Scalable World Models (Dreamer 4) — Hafner, Yan, Lillicrap — 2025-09 — arXiv 2509.24527 — preprint
  - World Simulation with Video Foundation Models for Physical AI (Cosmos-Predict2.5) — NVIDIA — 2025-10-28 — arXiv 2511.00062 — tech-report
  - Genie 3: A new frontier for world models — Google DeepMind — 2025-08-05 — https://deepmind.google/blog/genie-3-a-new-frontier-for-world-models/ — company-claim (unconfirmed: not opened; date from search)
  - GAIA-3 — Wayve — 2025-12-02 — https://wayve.ai/thinking/gaia-3/ — company-claim
  - The Waymo World Model — Waymo — 2026-02-06 — https://waymo.com/blog/2026/02/the-waymo-world-model-a-new-frontier-for-autonomous-driving-simulation/ — company-claim
  - World Models — Ha, Schmidhuber — 2018 — no URL — (seed lead, verify)
- **Notes:** Core equations: the transition model; passive vs action-conditioned prediction. Further leads: NVIDIA Launches Cosmos 3 (2026-05-31, company claim, self-reported ranks); 1X World Model (world-model-self-learning page, date unconfirmed, not opened); World Labs Marble (unconfirmed; public release 2025-11-12 per press); World Model for Robot Learning: A Comprehensive Survey (Hou et al., arXiv 2605.00080, ch01 scan). Gaps: Ha and Schmidhuber, Genie 2 and GAIA-2 primaries; any source for physics-informed hybrids; Tesla stays keynote-only and attributed.

### ch37 · Inside a video world model
- **Purpose:** How a video world model works inside, taught in full depth from ch13's basics: compressing video into latents, video diffusion and flow transformers, autoregressive and streaming generation, conditioning on actions, camera motion and language, memory and long-horizon consistency, multi-view and 3D consistency, real-time generation, what to predict, and how quality is measured. ch37 owns the video-specific forms of ch13's machinery: spatio-temporal video compression, the video diffusion transformer, diffusion forcing, Self Forcing, Causal Forcing and shortcut forcing, conditioning mechanisms, world-model memory, 3D consistency, streaming distillation and caching, and the three quality axes. ch13 owns the generic machinery; ch38 owns training stages; ch41 owns failure modes and physics benchmarks.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** no.
- **Depends on:** ch09 (inference budgets; memory capacity and bandwidth); ch11 (representations and latent spaces); ch12 (the transformer block; attention and cross-attention; the vision transformer's patches; the context window and key-value cache); ch13 (autoencoders and VAEs; vector quantisation and video tokenisers; diffusion; flow matching; the diffusion transformer and latent diffusion; conditioning by cross-attention and adaptive normalisation; guidance; autoregressive generation and compounding errors; step distillation and consistency models); ch15 (frames and poses); ch16 (latency budgets; clock rates); ch18 (camera-motion confounds; latent action models; SLAM); ch22 (the pinhole camera model; extrinsics); ch27 (Gaussian splatting); ch29 (action non-compliance in world-model evaluators); ch32 (memory for robot policies); ch36 (the world model; the families; passive vs action-conditioned prediction).
- **Estimated length:** about 13,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **37.01 · The anatomy of a video world model.** Give the whole pipeline in one picture before the parts. *Concepts:* the pipeline, encoder to latent to generator to decoder, with conditioning inputs (owned here as an overview); why generation runs in a compressed latent space (recap: latent diffusion, ch13); two generator styles, a whole clip at once vs frame by frame (preview: 37.03 and 37.04); (aside) a block diagram with Wan, HunyuanVideo and Cosmos-Predict2.5 placed on it (from their technical reports).
  - **37.02 · Compressing video: video VAEs, tokenisers and latents.** Show how a clip shrinks to something a transformer can handle. *Concepts:* recap: autoencoders, VAEs and vector-quantised tokenisers (ch13); spatio-temporal compression, squeezing space and time together (owned here; equation: compression factor as raw values over latent values); causal video VAEs that encode each frame from the past only, so streaming works (owned here; source needed; Wan's VAE as the candidate example, to be read from its report); continuous vs discrete video tokenisers (the Cosmos platform paper's tokenisers); patches over the latent (recap: vision transformer patches, ch12); what compression throws away: small objects, fine motion and text (`[inference]` until sourced).
  - **37.03 · Diffusion and flow-matching transformers for video.** Show the generator most video world models use today. *Concepts:* recap: the diffusion transformer and flow matching (ch13); the video diffusion transformer (owned here): space-time patches as tokens, with the attention design read from the Wan and HunyuanVideo reports; text and image conditioning (recap: cross-attention, ch12); model sizes as dated rows (Wan 1.3B and 14B; HunyuanVideo 13B; Cosmos-Predict2.5 2B and 14B); the cost of attention over many frames (equation: tokens equal frames times patches per frame, and cost grows with the square of tokens; recap: ch12); bidirectional clip generation and why it cannot stream (owned here).
  - **37.04 · Autoregressive and streaming generation.** Show how a model generates the future a piece at a time, as a robot needs. *Concepts:* recap: autoregressive generation and compounding errors (ch13); causal, chunk-by-chunk video generation (owned here); diffusion forcing, giving each frame its own noise level so the past can be clean while the future is noisy (owned here; previewed in ch13; original paper not yet located); the train–test gap, where a model trained on real past frames must run on its own imperfect ones (owned here); Self Forcing, training on its own rollouts with a rolling key-value cache (peer-reviewed, NeurIPS 2025); Causal Forcing, fixing the distillation of a bidirectional teacher into a causal student (peer-reviewed, ICML 2026); Dreamer 4's shortcut forcing (preprint); 2026 follow-ups as unopened leads.
  - **37.05 · Conditioning on actions, camera motion and language.** Show how control inputs enter the network. *Concepts:* recap: passive vs action-conditioned prediction (ch36) and conditional generation (ch13); conditioning mechanisms for video world models (owned here): actions or poses as extra tokens, through adaptive normalisation or through cross-attention (recap: ch13; mechanisms to be read from Cosmos-Predict2.5 and Dreamer 4); camera-pose conditioning (recap: frames and poses, ch15; extrinsics, ch22); first-frame (image-to-world) and text conditioning; action granularity, from joint commands to game-style or latent actions (recap: latent action models, ch18); guidance strength for conditions (recap: guidance, ch13); action following as the test (recap: ch29); preview: how conditioning is trained in (taught in ch38).
  - **37.06 · Memory and long-horizon consistency.** Explain why generated worlds forget, and how memory is added. *Concepts:* recap: the context window and key-value cache (ch12) and memory for policies (ch32); long-horizon drift (owned here at the mechanism level; preview: as a failure, taught in ch41); consistency on revisiting a place, object permanence (owned here; Genie 3's "minutes of consistency", company claim, not opened); pose-free hierarchical memory past 1,000 frames (Infinite-World, preprint); implicit memory in the cache vs explicit memory stores (`[inference]`); key-value cache compression and long-horizon benchmarks (WorldRoamBench, WBench; unopened leads).
  - **37.07 · Multi-view and 3D consistency.** Show why a world must keep its shape across cameras and viewpoints. *Concepts:* multi-view consistency, several cameras showing one coherent scene (owned here; sources needed); 3D consistency, geometry that holds as the camera moves (owned here; sources needed); implicit 3D from camera conditioning vs explicit 3D representations (recap: Gaussian splatting, ch27; recap: 3D/4D scene models, ch36); why a robot's head and wrist cameras raise the stakes (`[inference]`); preview: multi-view inconsistency as a failure (taught in ch41).
  - **37.08 · Real time: distillation, caching and the latency budget.** Show what it takes to generate the world as fast as it happens. *Concepts:* recap: step distillation and consistency models (ch13) and latency budgets (ch16); few-step streaming distillation as the video form (owned here; Self Forcing and Causal Forcing as dated examples); the rolling key-value cache as a speed tool (owned here; Self Forcing); real-time claims as dated, attributed rows (Genie 3 at 720p and 24 frames per second, company claim, not opened; Dreamer 4 in real time on one GPU, preprint); frame budget (equation: time per frame equals one over frames per second); frame rate vs a robot's control rate (`[inference]`, reasoning shown).
  - **37.09 · What to predict: pixels, latents or masks.** Lay out the argument over the right prediction target. *Concepts:* the prediction-target choice (owned here); pixels (recap: 37.03); latents (recap: V-JEPA 2, ch36); semantic masks instead of pixels (Mask World Model, preprint, which argues pixels are the wrong target for control); action output alongside video (Cosmos 3's two-tower design, NVIDIA claim; preview: WAMs, taught in ch40); what a human reviewer needs vs what a controller needs (`[inference]`, reasoning shown).
  - **37.10 · Measuring quality: fidelity, physics and controllability.** Teach the three questions to ask of any generated world. *Concepts:* the three quality axes (owned here): visual fidelity (frame and video quality metrics; sources needed), physical plausibility (physical-plausibility metrics in the Li et al. survey; Physics-IQ and PAI-Bench as vendor-cited leads; preview: physics benchmarks and probes, taught in ch41) and controllability (recap: action following, ch29); why a beautiful video can be useless for control (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); preview: whether world-model scores predict real policy results (taught in ch39).
  - **37.11 · Open video world models side by side.** Put the chapter's parts together on real systems. *Concepts:* (aside) a comparison table of Wan 2.1, HunyuanVideo, Cosmos-Predict2.5, Dreamer 4 and Genie 3 by compression, generator, size, conditioning, streaming, speed and licence, with "not disclosed" cells (Genie 3 mostly not disclosed); newer Wan versions excluded until a primary report exists (scan finding); "Try it" pointer to the open models' repositories.
- **Running-example hooks:** dinner in 37.10 (a sauce that looks right but flows wrongly: high fidelity, poor physics, `[inference]`); kitchen reset in 37.06 (remembering where the "leave my glass" glass stands after the camera turns away, `[inference]`); battery station in 37.02 and 37.05 (small connectors lost in compression; actions at the precision of a click, `[inference]`); greenhouse in 37.07 (dense leaves and occlusion across head and wrist cameras, `[inference]`).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Wan: Open and Advanced Large-Scale Video Generative Models — Wan Team (Alibaba) — 2025-03-26 — arXiv 2503.20314 — tech-report
  - HunyuanVideo — Tencent — 2024-12 (day unconfirmed) — arXiv 2412.03603 — tech-report
  - World Simulation with Video Foundation Models for Physical AI (Cosmos-Predict2.5 and Transfer2.5) — NVIDIA — 2025-10-28 (v2 2026-02-24) — arXiv 2511.00062 — tech-report
  - Cosmos World Foundation Model Platform for Physical AI — NVIDIA — 2025-01 (day unconfirmed) — arXiv 2501.03575 — preprint
  - Self Forcing — Huang et al. — 2025-06-09 — arXiv 2506.08009 — peer-reviewed (NeurIPS 2025 spotlight)
  - Causal Forcing — Zhu et al. — 2026-02-02 — arXiv 2602.02214 — peer-reviewed (ICML 2026)
  - Infinite-World — Wu et al. — 2026-02-02 — arXiv 2602.02393 — preprint
  - Mask World Model — Lou et al. — 2026-04-21 — arXiv 2604.19683 — preprint
  - Training Agents Inside of Scalable World Models (Dreamer 4) — Hafner, Yan, Lillicrap — 2025-09 — arXiv 2509.24527 — preprint
  - Diffusion forcing — no authors recorded — no date — no URL — (seed lead, verify)
- **Notes:** Core equations: the compression factor; tokens per clip and quadratic attention cost; diffusion forcing's per-frame noise levels in plain words; the frame budget. Genie 3 numbers (720p, 24 fps, minutes of consistency) come from an unopened page: attribute and verify. Gaps: the diffusion forcing original; multi-view and 3D-consistency sources; fidelity-metric primaries; independent evaluations of Physics-IQ and PAI-Bench results.

### ch38 · Training world models: pre-, mid- and post-training and RL
- **Purpose:** How world models are trained, using the same four stage names ch30 uses for policies: pre-training on internet video; mid-training on physics- and embodiment-heavy data while adding action and camera conditioning; post-training for a target robot (control, long-horizon consistency, real-time speed); and RL, both as reward-driven post-training of the world model and as the loop in which a world model and a policy improve together. ch38 also owns video data curation for world models, world-model training compute, and the one world-model recipe table that maps each major model onto the stages, saying plainly where a lab discloses no recipe. ch30 owns the stage concepts and the policy map; ch37 owns the mechanisms being trained; ch39 owns RL inside world models as a use; ch40 owns WAM training recipes.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch11 (the training run; scaling laws; training compute); ch13 (diffusion; flow matching; guidance; step distillation); ch14 (model-based RL and model error; reward design and reward hacking; the KL penalty; GRPO); ch17 (the data pyramid; the scale–alignment trade-off); ch18 (web and egocentric video; camera-motion confounds; SLAM; latent action models; inverse dynamics models); ch20 (simulation data; generated video); ch23 (data pipelines); ch24 (quality filtering; VLM auto-labelling; rights to use web video); ch26 (mixture weights); ch28 (GPU-hours as the cost unit); ch30 (the training stage; the four stages; catastrophic forgetting; budget shape; the master lab-recipe map); ch32 (proximity-based data selection); ch33 (new-embodiment fine-tuning; distillation); ch34 (VLM judges; progress reward models); ch36 (families; passive vs action-conditioned); ch37 (video VAEs; video diffusion transformers; Self Forcing and Causal Forcing; conditioning mechanisms; memory; the quality axes).
- **Estimated length:** about 12,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **38.01 · The four stages, applied to world models.** Reuse ch30's stage names and show what each means for a model that predicts the world. *Concepts:* recap: the training stage and the four stages (ch30); the world-model stage map (owned here): pre-training on internet video, mid-training on physics- and embodiment-heavy data while adding action and camera conditioning, post-training for a target robot, and RL (framing from the brief); why the order is forced by data (abundant passive video, scarce action-labelled robot video; first principles; recap: the scale–alignment trade-off, ch17); what carries over from policies (forgetting, budget shape; recap: ch30) and what does not (`[inference]`).
  - **38.02 · Pre-training on internet-scale video.** Show what a world model learns from watching, and what watching cannot teach. *Concepts:* world-model pre-training (owned here): a denoising or prediction objective on video (recap: diffusion and flow matching, ch13; recap: JEPA, ch36); what passive video teaches (appearance, motion, rough dynamics) and what it lacks (actions, forces, touch; recap: limits of human data, ch18); data scale as dated rows (Cosmos-Predict2.5's 200M curated clips, NVIDIA; V-JEPA 2's more than 1M hours, per its authors); captions as conditioning data (recap: VLM auto-labelling, ch24); recap: rights to use web video (ch24).
  - **38.03 · Curating video for world models.** Show how raw footage becomes training clips. *Concepts:* the video curation pipeline (owned here; the Cosmos platform paper's pipeline, steps to be read from it): splitting into shots, filtering for quality and motion, captioning, deduplication; selecting physics-rich and embodiment-relevant clips (`[inference]` until sourced; recap: proximity-based selection, ch32); recap: data pipelines (ch23), quality filtering (ch24) and mixture weights (ch26); what fraction of raw video survives (gap: not yet sourced).
  - **38.04 · Mid-training on physics- and embodiment-heavy data.** Show the stage that moves a general video model toward bodies, hands and contact. *Concepts:* world-model mid-training (owned here; recap: mid-training as a stage, ch30; for policies, ch32): shifting the mix toward egocentric human video, robot video, simulation renders and driving (recap: ch18 and ch20); camera-motion confounds in egocentric video as a data hazard (recap: ch18; preview: as a failure, taught in ch41); keeping general knowledge while specialising (recap: catastrophic forgetting, ch30); what each lab discloses about this stage (Cosmos-Predict2.5's physical-AI focus, to read; 1X's data scale, company claim; others "not disclosed").
  - **38.05 · Adding action and camera conditioning.** Show how a passive video model is taught to respond to controls. *Concepts:* recap: conditioning mechanisms (ch37) and passive vs action-conditioned prediction (ch36); action-conditioning by fine-tuning on action-labelled video (owned here): V-JEPA 2-AC, trained with 62 hours of robot data (per its authors); camera-pose labels for camera conditioning (recap: SLAM, ch18); latent actions when no labels exist (recap: ch18); how much action-labelled data is needed (`[inference]` beyond the reported figures); the risk that conditioning is learned weakly, so predictions ignore the action (recap: ch29; preview: action non-compliance, taught in ch41); a stage-label mismatch (Meta calls V-JEPA 2-AC post-training, while the book's map files action conditioning under mid-training: show both).
  - **38.06 · Post-training for a target robot: control, consistency and speed.** Show the stage that makes a world model usable for one robot. *Concepts:* world-model post-training for a target robot (owned here): fine-tuning on that robot's cameras and action space (recap: new-embodiment fine-tuning, ch33); long-horizon consistency training (recap: Self Forcing and memory, ch37); real-time distillation as a post-training step (owned here as a stage; recap: streaming distillation, ch37); post-training examples in the Cosmos platform paper (domains to be read from it); checking the result before use (preview: evaluation, taught in ch39).
  - **38.07 · RL post-training: rewards for physics and instruction following.** Show reward-driven training applied to the world model itself. *Concepts:* recap: GRPO, reward models, the KL penalty and reward hacking (ch14); why a denoising loss leaves physics and instruction errors uncorrected (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); online RL for flow-matching generators (owned here; Flow-GRPO, preprint, mechanism to be read; recap: flow matching, ch13); DanceGRPO (seed lead); RL post-training in Cosmos-Predict2.5 (NVIDIA technical report); physics, geometry and instruction rewards from checkers and VLM judges (recap: VLM judges, ch34; WorldCompass, VGGRPO, WorldCycle, WorldReward and PhysRVG as search-only leads); hacking a physics reward (`[inference]` until sourced).
  - **38.08 · The world model and the policy improving together.** Show the training loop in which policy experience improves the world model. *Concepts:* why a world model must be refreshed where the policy goes (first principles: a policy visits states the model never saw and exploits its errors; recap: model error, ch14); the co-improvement loop (owned here at the training level): World-VLA-Loop, where policy failures feed back into world-model training (preprint); Dreamer 4's sequence, a world model from offline data, then an agent trained inside it (preprint); preview: RL inside world models as a use (taught in ch39); preview: the data flywheel (taught in ch48).
  - **38.09 · What training a world model costs.** Teach the compute arithmetic and how little of it is public. *Concepts:* recap: training compute and GPU-hours (ch11 and ch28); cost drivers: video tokens per clip (recap: ch37), model size, denoising steps and RL rollouts (`[inference]`); disclosed compute figures (none confirmed in the scans: an explicit gap); generation cost as an ongoing cost of every later use (preview: ch39); a worked estimate with illustrative numbers marked `nofact`.
  - **38.10 · World-model recipes mapped onto the four stages.** Put every major model's recipe in one table, gaps included. *Concepts:* (aside) the world-model recipe table (owned here): rows for Cosmos (the 2025 platform, Predict2.5, and Cosmos 3 once its report is confirmed), V-JEPA 2 and V-JEPA 2-AC, Dreamer 4, Genie 3 ("not disclosed" for every stage), the 1X World Model ("not disclosed" for stages; data scale only, company claim), GAIA-3 and the Waymo World Model as driving rows (mostly "not disclosed"), World-VLA-Loop as an academic loop, and DreamZero as the handover from a pre-trained video backbone to a policy (preview: WAM recipes, taught in ch40); columns for pre-, mid- and post-training and RL, plus data, the lab's own stage labels, evidence tag and date; recap: the master lab-recipe map for policies (ch30); how to read a world-model recipe claim (`[inference]`).
  - **38.11 · Training world models for the running examples.** Say which stage would supply what each task needs. *Concepts:* (aside) per task (`[inference]`, reasoning shown, numbers marked `nofact`): dinner for five (cooking video is abundant for pre- and mid-training; dough and sauce physics needs reward-driven post-training and real robot footage); kitchen reset (household egocentric video for mid-training; spills and glass); battery station (action-labelled close-up video from dummy modules for conditioning and post-training; the later twist, a revised connector, as a post-training job); greenhouse (scarce, seasonal footage limits mid-training).
- **Running-example hooks:** all four in 38.11; battery station in 38.05 (action labels at the precision of a connector push, `[inference]`); dinner in 38.07 (a physics reward can check that liquid stays in the pan, but nothing scores "tastes good", `[inference]`); kitchen reset in 38.04 (kitchens are common in egocentric video; recap ch18); greenhouse in 38.03 (curating rare plant-handling footage, `[inference]`).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - World Simulation with Video Foundation Models for Physical AI (Cosmos-Predict2.5) — NVIDIA — 2025-10-28 (v2 2026-02-24) — arXiv 2511.00062 — tech-report
  - Cosmos World Foundation Model Platform for Physical AI — NVIDIA — 2025-01 (day unconfirmed) — arXiv 2501.03575 — preprint
  - Cosmos 3: Omnimodal World Models for Physical AI — NVIDIA — 2026-06-22 — https://research.nvidia.com/labs/cosmos-lab/cosmos3/technical-report.pdf — tech-report (unconfirmed: PDF not opened)
  - V-JEPA 2 — Assran et al. (Meta) — 2025-06-11 — arXiv 2506.09985 — preprint
  - Training Agents Inside of Scalable World Models (Dreamer 4) — Hafner, Yan, Lillicrap — 2025-09 — arXiv 2509.24527 — preprint
  - Flow-GRPO — Liu et al. — 2025-05-08 — arXiv 2505.05470 — preprint
  - World-VLA-Loop — Liu et al. — 2026-02-06 — arXiv 2602.06508 — preprint
  - World Action Models are Zero-shot Policies (DreamZero) — Ye et al. (NVIDIA GEAR) — 2026-02-17 — arXiv 2602.15922 — preprint
  - Genie 3: A new frontier for world models — Google DeepMind — 2025-08-05 — https://deepmind.google/discover/blog/genie-3-a-new-frontier-for-world-models/ — company-claim (no training details)
