Cosmos 3's date conflicts across scans (2026-05-31 launch news; arXiv 2606.02800 dated 2026-06-01 in the ch40 scan; report PDF dated 2026-06-22): resolve before use; its benchmark ranks are NVIDIA's own. Genie 3 has two URLs across scans (/blog/ and /discover/blog/), and 1X has two world-model pages (2024 blog; the later "world-model-self-learning" page): confirm which says what.

### ch39 · Using world models: data, evaluation, RL and planning
- **Purpose:** How world models are put to work across the lifecycle: generating training data, evaluating policies in imagination and checking whether those scores predict reality, training policies with RL inside a world model, planning, verifying actions and predicting failures at run time, knowing when the model does not know, and an honest reading of the evidence. ch39 owns world-model data generation in depth, world-model-based policy evaluation, RL inside world models, planning with world models, test-time verification and failure prediction, and uncertainty in world models. ch20 owns generated video as a data source; ch29 owns correlation metrics and the reverse gap; ch41 owns the failure taxonomy; ch43 owns evaluation methodology; ch44 owns runtime safety layers; ch48 owns the flywheel.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch13 (sampling; compounding errors); ch14 (model-based RL; a policy exploiting model error; PPO and GRPO; rewards and reward hacking); ch15 (model predictive control; sampling-based MPC); ch16 (latency budgets); ch18 (learned inverse dynamics models); ch20 (neural trajectories and DreamGen; Cosmos-Transfer2.5 re-rendering; thin evidence at scale); ch28 (simulation for evaluation; generated worlds as training grounds); ch29 (paired tests; Pearson and rank correlation and MMRV; the reverse gap; world models as evaluators; action non-compliance); ch31 (the VLA); ch34 (RL fine-tuning of VLAs; success detectors and VLM judges; RISE; safe exploration); ch36 (the map of uses; passive vs action-conditioned; latent world models); ch37 (streaming generation; the three quality axes); ch38 (the stage map; the co-improvement loop; generation cost).
- **Estimated length:** about 12,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **39.01 · From model to tool: what each use demands.** Turn ch36's map of uses into requirements. *Concepts:* recap: the map of uses (ch36); requirements by use (owned here; `[inference]`, reasoning shown): data generation needs fidelity and diversity, evaluation needs action following and calibrated success judgement, RL needs speed and robustness to exploitation, planning and verification need speed within the control loop; (aside) a requirements table by use, with the quality axes (recap: ch37).
  - **39.02 · Generating training data with world models.** Go beyond ch20's introduction to the practice and the evidence. *Concepts:* recap: neural trajectories, DreamGen and re-rendering with Cosmos-Transfer2.5 (ch20); world-model data generation in practice (owned here): what to condition on, how many variations, filtering bad generations, labelling actions with an inverse dynamics model (recap: ch18); DreamGen's results on its own benchmark (attributed, preprint); when generated data hurts, physically wrong samples taught as truth (`[inference]`; preview: ch41); recap: thin public evidence at scale (ch20).
  - **39.03 · Evaluating policies in imagination.** Show how a policy is tested inside a world model instead of on a robot. *Concepts:* world-model-based policy evaluation (owned here; previewed in ch28 and ch29): roll the policy forward in an action-conditioned model and judge each episode (recap: VLM judges and success detectors, ch34); WorldEval (policy ranking and safety screening, preprint); Ctrl-World (evaluation and improvement in imagination; preprint, accepted at ICLR 2026 per the scan); the Gemini Robotics team's Veo-based world simulator (preprint); dWorldEval (beats WorldEval and Ctrl-World on its own benchmarks, preprint); 1X's evaluation framing (company claim; "Evaluating Bits, not Atoms" unconfirmed); recap: the Interactive World Simulator (ch29); cost per evaluated episode vs a real trial (`[inference]`, numbers marked `nofact`).
  - **39.04 · Do imagined scores predict real ones?** Show when world-model evaluation can be trusted, by condition. *Concepts:* recap: Pearson and rank correlation, MMRV and the reverse gap (ch29); reported correlations as dated, attributed rows (the Veo study's 0.88 Pearson over more than 1,600 real trials and 0.56 for novel objects, both from a search summary and unverified until read in the paper); strong for ranking, weak for novel objects and precise contact (scan finding); small policy sets and self-reporting (scan finding); recap: action non-compliance (ch29); when imagined scores mislead (owned here, a catalogue by condition; `[inference]` where unsourced); how to read an evaluation-correlation claim (how many policies, tasks and real trials; who measured).
  - **39.05 · RL inside world models.** Show how a policy practises inside a learned model, and how it cheats. *Concepts:* RL inside world models (owned here; previewed in ch14, ch28, ch30 and ch34): imagined rollouts with a learned reward or judge; recap: model-based RL and exploiting model error (ch14); Dreamer 4 (agents trained purely in imagination, preprint); World-VLA-Loop (RL with failure feedback; recap: the co-improvement loop, ch38); RISE (a compositional world model with real-robot gains reported; recap: ch34); Ctrl-World's imagined improvement (38.7% to 83.4%, per an abstract summary; verify); WMPO (ICLR 2026 lead, not yet located); free resets and parallel imagined rollouts vs a model the policy can fool (`[inference]`, reasoning shown).
  - **39.06 · Planning with world models.** Show how a robot can imagine several futures and pick the best. *Concepts:* recap: model predictive control and sampling-based MPC (ch15); world-model planning (owned here): propose action sequences, imagine their outcomes, score them against a goal, act, and re-plan (equation: choose the action sequence whose predicted outcome is closest to the goal, in plain words); planning in latent space toward an image goal (V-JEPA 2-AC, zero-shot on Franka arms, per its authors; optimiser to be read from the paper); compute at decision time vs at training time (`[inference]`); recap: latency budgets (ch16); preview: models that fold imagination into the policy (taught in ch40).
  - **39.07 · Verifying actions and predicting failure at run time.** Show how a world model can check a proposed action before the robot commits. *Concepts:* test-time verification with a world model (owned here): imagine the outcome of a candidate action chunk, then accept, veto or re-plan; CheckVLA (action-conditioned verification; recall 77.9% at a 5% false-alarm rate, preprint; equation: recall and false-alarm rate); failure prediction from imagined futures (owned here; DreamAvoid and Pre-VLA as search-only leads); recap: success detectors (ch34); the latency cost of imagining before acting (`[inference]`); self-correcting VLAs (seed lead, not found); preview: runtime monitors and safety layers (taught in ch44).
  - **39.08 · Uncertainty: knowing when the world model does not know.** Explain why every use needs the model to flag its own ignorance. *Concepts:* uncertainty in world models (owned here; no earlier chapter owns it): uncertainty from missing knowledge vs from a genuinely random world (epistemic vs aleatoric; sources needed); ways to estimate it, such as disagreement across several models or samples (sources needed; `[inference]` until sourced); using uncertainty to abstain from evaluation, to keep planning and RL away from unfamiliar states, and to trigger data collection (`[inference]`, reasoning shown; recap: model error, ch14); preview: uncertainty as a remedy (taught in ch41); preview: active data collection in the flywheel (taught in ch48).
  - **39.09 · Evidence on reliability, and what to trust today.** Grade the evidence across every use. *Concepts:* (aside) an evidence table by use: the claim, who measured it, how many policies and tasks, whether a real-world check exists, and its evidence tag (data from 39.02–39.07); self-reported vs independent results (scan finding); what a product team can lean on a world model for in 2026 and what still needs hardware (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); preview: the failure taxonomy (taught in ch41); preview: evaluation methodology and readiness gates (taught in ch43 and ch46).
  - **39.10 · World models at work in the running examples.** Apply each use to the four tasks. *Concepts:* (aside) per task, which uses help and where the evidence stops (`[inference]`, reasoning shown): battery station (screening the revised-connector update in a world model before it touches live hardware, while contact and clicks are exactly where imagined scores mislead); kitchen reset (verifying a grasp on a wine glass and predicting spills); dinner for five (imagined evaluation cannot judge doneness or taste); greenhouse (generating rare, once-a-year situations as data).
- **Running-example hooks:** all four in 39.10; battery station in 39.04 (a world model that ranks insertion policies well on rigid parts may still miss a half-seated connector, `[inference]`); kitchen reset in 39.07 (vetoing a grasp that would tip a half-full glass, `[inference]`); dinner in 39.03 (a judge cannot score "tastes good" in imagination either, `[inference]`); greenhouse in 39.02 and 39.08 (generated seasonal scenes, and the model's uncertainty on a variety it has never seen, `[inference]`).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Evaluating Gemini Robotics Policies in a Veo World Simulator — Gemini Robotics Team (Google) — 2025-12-11 — arXiv 2512.10675 — preprint (correlation figures unconfirmed: from a search summary)
  - WorldEval — Li et al. — 2025-05-25 — arXiv 2505.19017 — preprint
  - Ctrl-World — Guo, Shi, Chen, Finn — 2025 (day unconfirmed) — arXiv 2510.10125 — preprint
  - dWorldEval — Li et al. — 2026-04-24 — arXiv 2604.22152 — preprint
  - DreamGen — Jang et al. (NVIDIA) — 2025-05-19 — arXiv 2505.12705 — preprint
  - V-JEPA 2 (V-JEPA 2-AC planning) — Assran et al. (Meta) — 2025-06-11 — arXiv 2506.09985 — preprint
  - World-VLA-Loop — Liu et al. — 2026-02-06 — arXiv 2602.06508 — preprint
  - CheckVLA — Liu et al. — 2026-07-29 — arXiv 2607.26789 — preprint
  - RISE: Self-Improving Robot Policy with Compositional World Model — Yang et al. — 2026-02-11 — arXiv 2602.11075 — preprint
  - 1X World Model: Evaluating Bits, not Atoms — 1X — date unknown — https://www.1x.tech/1x-world-model.pdf — company-claim (unconfirmed: PDF unreadable)
- **Notes:** Core equations: the planning objective in plain words; recall and false-alarm rate; recap of Pearson correlation (ch29). Further leads: Interactive World Simulator (arXiv 2603.08546, ch29 scan); Dreamer 4; WMPO; GigaWorld-1, RoboWorld and PiL-World (search only). Gaps: uncertainty sources (epistemic vs aleatoric, ensembles); a readable 1X evaluation primary; independent, multi-lab correlation studies.

## P8 · World action models and convergence

### ch40 · World action models (WAMs)
- **Purpose:** What a world action model is, where the idea and the name came from, how the main designs work inside, how they are trained, how fast they run, what they are claimed to buy, and how today's systems compare. ch40 gives the book's single WAM definition, owns joint world-action models in depth, video-then-inverse-dynamics policies, latent WAMs and WAMs that drop imagination at run time, WAM training recipes, WAM inference speed and control rates, and the WAM family map and comparison table. ch36–ch39 own world models, their internals, training and uses; ch31 owns VLAs and mixture-of-transformers designs; ch41 owns failures and force- and touch-aware WAMs; ch42 owns the WAM-vs-VLA comparison and convergence.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch01 (the WAM previewed with the book's single glossary definition); ch03 (world models and WAMs as milestones; the naming of the term); ch09 (model size vs memory; inference budgets); ch12 (the transformer; attention; mixture of experts); ch13 (diffusion; flow matching; the diffusion transformer; action chunking; flow-matching action heads; step distillation); ch16 (policy rate vs control rate; latency budgets; real-time chunking; fast-slow designs); ch17 (the action label; the data pyramid; robot data-scaling laws); ch18 (learned inverse dynamics models; latent action models; egocentric video); ch25 (latent action spaces; Being-H0.7 previewed as a latent world-action model); ch30 (the training stage; the four stages; the master lab-recipe map); ch31 (the VLA; behaviour cloning; action experts; mixture-of-transformers designs; action representations); ch33 (distillation; the VLA latency model); ch35 (action interfaces; MotionWAM); ch36 (the world model; families; passive vs action-conditioned prediction; latent world models; omnimodal models); ch37 (video diffusion transformers; streaming generation; conditioning mechanisms; real-time distillation; the prediction-target choice; Cosmos 3's two-tower design); ch38 (the world-model stage map; the recipe table; DreamZero as the handover); ch39 (evaluation, RL, planning and verification with world models).
- **Estimated length:** about 12,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **40.01 · From predicting the world to acting in it.** Derive from first principles why a model that can imagine the next few seconds of video might also know what to do, and trace the idea's lineage. *Concepts:* recap: the world model and action-conditioned prediction (ch36); recap: the VLA (ch31); prediction as a route to action, if you can picture the outcome you want, the moves that lead there are constrained (owned here; `[inference]` framing, reasoning shown); video prediction as pre-training for a policy (owned here); the lineage from text-conditioned video planning to video-pre-trained policies (UniPi, GR-1, GR-2 and Genie Envisioner as seed leads to verify; order and dates read from primaries); recap: world models as policy backbones in the map of uses (ch36).
  - **40.02 · What a WAM is: the definition, its origin and its boundaries.** Give the book's one definition, say honestly who named it and when, and draw the borders with neighbouring model types. *Concepts:* the world action model, or WAM (owned here; previewed in ch01, ch03, ch13, ch25 and ch36): a policy built on a pre-trained video or world-model backbone that predicts future world states and actions together (BRIEF definition); the origin of the term in DreamZero (17 February 2026: "We term this architecture a World Action Model"; at most one short quote); why "world" rather than "video" (DreamZero leaves room for tactile, force or latent futures; preview: force- and touch-aware WAMs, taught in ch41); retroactive labelling, DreamZero and the surveys calling earlier systems WAMs after the fact (owned here: "introduced the term" is accurate, "invented the idea" is not; the cited works Kim 2026, Liao 2025 and Pai 2025 still to be resolved); the boundaries (owned here): a world model predicts but does not act (recap: ch36), a VLA acts but does not predict the world (recap: ch31), a world model wrapped around a policy sits outside it (recap: evaluation, RL and verification, ch39); equation: three conditional distributions side by side (world model: future observations given observations and actions; VLA: actions given observations and instruction; WAM: future observations and actions together given observations and instruction); the surveys' disagreement on scope (Shen et al., June 2026: rendered, latent and no-video futures; Lu et al., September 2026: joint vs video-then-inverse-dynamics; the broader May 2026 survey by Hou et al.), presented side by side.
  - **40.03 · Joint WAMs I: denoising the future and the actions together.** Teach in depth the core design, one network that generates future video and an action chunk in the same pass. *Concepts:* recap: diffusion, flow matching and the diffusion transformer (ch13); recap: the video diffusion transformer (ch37); joint denoising of video latents and actions (owned here; equation: one training loss summing a video-denoising term and an action-denoising term, with every symbol walked through); shared attention between video and action tokens, so each can inform the other (owned here; mechanism to be read from DreamZero); DreamZero as the worked example (architecture, backbone and the "zero-shot policy" claim, preprint, attributed); actions written as latent frames, so a video model can generate them like images (owned here; Cosmos Policy's single-stage design, preprint); recap: action chunking (ch13).
  - **40.04 · Joint WAMs II: two-tower and omnimodal designs.** Show the designs that give video and action their own weights while letting them talk. *Concepts:* recap: mixture-of-transformers designs with separate weights per modality and shared attention (ch31); recap: Cosmos 3's two-tower design (ch37); the video tower and the action tower (owned here as the WAM form; `[inference]` where the reports are silent); omnimodal world-action models that also reason and generate (Cosmos 3, NVIDIA technical report; its RoboArena standing is NVIDIA's own claim; preview: convergence, taught in ch42); FLUX 3 Action (7B parameters and 15 Hz, Black Forest Labs, company claim; its page carries partner claims that need tracing); what joint designs cost in parameters and memory (recap: model size vs memory, ch09; `[inference]`).
  - **40.05 · Video then inverse dynamics: imagine first, then read off the action.** Show the two-stage family that keeps the video model and the action extractor apart. *Concepts:* recap: the learned inverse dynamics model (ch18); video-then-inverse-dynamics policies (owned here; previewed in ch18): generate a short future, then infer the action that connects now to the imagined next frame (equation: the two-step factorisation in plain words); the distinction from DreamGen, which uses an inverse dynamics model to label data offline (recap: ch20); UniPi as an early form (seed lead, verify); Rhoda AI's DVA as a recent company system (press report; Rhoda's own primary to be found); Lu et al.'s joint vs video-then-inverse-dynamics split (recap: 40.02); trade-offs: a swappable video model and a small action head vs errors that pass from the imagined frame into the action (`[inference]`, reasoning shown).
  - **40.06 · Latent WAMs and WAMs that stop imagining at run time.** Show the designs that predict the future in an abstract code, or only during training. *Concepts:* recap: latent prediction and the JEPA line (ch36); recap: the prediction-target choice, pixels vs latents vs masks (ch37); latent WAMs, which predict future features rather than frames (owned here; Shen et al.'s "latent future" class); recap: latent action spaces and Being-H0.7 (ch25); MotionWAM as a whole-body design (recap: ch35; whether it counts as a latent WAM to be checked); WAMs without test-time imagination (owned here): Fast-WAM's finding that imagining the future may matter only in training, with a reported 190 ms (what the figure measures to be read from the paper; preprint, attributed); why this blurs the line between a WAM and a VLA with an extra training loss (`[inference]`; preview: taught in ch42); the controlled design study "What Matters in Designing WAMs" (causal structure, latents and objectives, preprint).
  - **40.07 · Training a WAM: recipes and data.** Show how a video model becomes a policy, stage by stage, using the stage names of ch30 and ch38. *Concepts:* recap: the four stages (ch30) and the world-model stage map (ch38); the WAM recipe (owned here): a video backbone pre-trained to imagine, then trained to act on action-labelled robot data (NVIDIA's "pretrained to imagine, fine-tuned to act" framing, company blog); DreamZero's recipe (recap: the handover in ch38's table; detail read from the paper); open modular pre-training on about 6,400 hours (OpenWAM, preprint); 1X's world-model policy (14B parameters; about 900 hours of egocentric video and 70 hours of robot data; unconfirmed, page not opened); Dyna-2's claimed scaling law for world-action models (recap: ch17; company claim); which data a WAM can use that a VLA cannot, passive video without actions (`[inference]`, reasoning shown; recap: the data pyramid, ch17); training compute (no confirmed disclosure: an explicit gap; preview: compute as a decider, taught in ch42); (aside) WAM recipes mapped onto the four stages, with "not disclosed" cells.
  - **40.08 · Inference speed and control rates.** Teach why generating video inside the control loop is expensive, and the ways WAMs get fast enough. *Concepts:* recap: latency budgets, policy rate vs control rate and real-time chunking (ch16); recap: the frame budget and streaming distillation (ch37); recap: the VLA latency model (ch33); the WAM latency problem (owned here; previewed in ch16; equation: time per decision as denoising steps times time per step, plus decoding, compared with the chunk's execution time); speed-ups (owned here): fewer denoising steps, skipping pixel decoding, predicting the future in latents, and dropping imagination at run time (recap: Fast-WAM, 40.06); (aside) a dated table of reported decision times and control rates (Fast-WAM's 190 ms, FLUX 3 Action's 15 Hz as a company claim, others from primaries), each with its hardware where stated; what a rate claim hides (hardware, chunk length, what runs off-board; `[inference]`).
  - **40.09 · What WAMs are claimed to buy.** Lay out the case for WAMs, each claim attributed and graded. *Concepts:* a physics prior inherited from video pre-training (owned here as a claim; `[inference]` framing anchored in DreamZero and the NVIDIA blog); generalisation to new tasks and scenes (DreamZero's zero-shot claims, preprint); imagined futures as an inspectable plan a person or a checker can look at (`[inference]`, reasoning shown; recap: verification, ch39); robustness under perturbation (the March 2026 robustness study's pro-WAM result, attributed; preview: weighing the head-to-head evidence, taught in ch42); counter-evidence held for later chapters (preview: failures, taught in ch41; preview: when diverse data closes the gap, taught in ch41 and ch42).
  - **40.10 · The WAM landscape: a family map and current systems compared.** Put every family and named system on one page. *Concepts:* (aside) the family map (owned here): joint, two-tower and omnimodal, video-then-inverse-dynamics, latent, no-imagination, and the adjacent family of world models wrapped around policies (recap: ch39); (aside) a dated comparison table of systems by family, backbone, size, training data, decision time or control rate, openness and evidence tag: DreamZero, Cosmos Policy, Cosmos 3, Fast-WAM, OpenWAM, FLUX 3 Action, Rhoda AI's DVA, 1X's world-model policy, Being-H0.7 and MotionWAM, with seed-lead systems (GR-1, GR-2, UVA, UWM, WorldVLA, LingBot-VA, GigaWorld-Policy, ProWAM, OA-WAM, Motus) added only after their primaries are read; GR00T N2's reported DreamZero basis (aggregators only: excluded until NVIDIA's own release is found); how to read a WAM claim (backbone, data, robot, benchmark, who measured; `[inference]`); "Try it" pointer to open WAM code and weights where a primary confirms them.
  - **40.11 · WAMs for the running examples.** Say what a WAM would have to imagine for each task, and where that imagination runs out. *Concepts:* (aside) per task (`[inference]`, reasoning shown): battery station (imagining a connector's seating and click, which video barely shows, so a force-aware WAM is the candidate; preview: taught in ch41); kitchen reset (imagining a spill before tipping a half-full glass); dinner for five (imagining dough and sauce, a world-model failure case); greenhouse (imagining how a vine moves when touched, with scarce footage).
- **Running-example hooks:** all four in 40.11; battery station in 40.08 (a decision time measured against a connector push that cannot wait, `[inference]`) and 40.02 (why "world" leaves room for force futures); kitchen reset in 40.09 (an imagined spill as an inspectable plan, `[inference]`); dinner in 40.07 (abundant cooking video is exactly the passive data a WAM can use and a VLA cannot, `[inference]`); greenhouse in 40.05 (an imagined frame of a hidden truss that the action extractor must trust, `[inference]`).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - World Action Models are Zero-shot Policies (DreamZero) — Ye et al. (NVIDIA) — 2026-02-17 — arXiv 2602.15922 — preprint
  - Cosmos Policy — NVIDIA, Stanford — 2026-01 (day unconfirmed) — arXiv 2601.16163 — preprint
  - Cosmos 3 — NVIDIA — 2026-06-01 — arXiv 2606.02800 — tech-report (date conflicts across scans; see ch38 notes)

  - World-Action Models for Robot Learning and Control: A Survey — Lu et al. — 2026-09-13 — arXiv 2609.16074 — preprint
  - Pretrained to Imagine, Fine-Tuned to Act — Moritz Reuss (NVIDIA) — 2026-06-15 — https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/pretrained-to-imagine-fine-tuned-to-act-the-rise-of-world-action-models/ — company-claim

### ch41 · Where world models and WAMs fail, and what data fixes it
- **Purpose:** A failure taxonomy for world models and WAMs, traced from symptom to root cause, with special attention to training data (volume, diversity, modality, richness of labels, embodiment and quality), then the remedies (data first, then models, training, architecture and system), how to measure progress, and what each running example needs. ch41 owns the failure taxonomy, physics and robot-specific diagnostic benchmarks for world models, data root causes of world-model failure, action-diverse and counterfactual data, force- and touch-aware WAMs, the failure-to-remedy map and the failure scorecard. ch29 owns the gap between world models and reality at the level of trusting results; ch37 owns drift, object permanence and multi-view consistency as mechanisms; ch39 owns when imagined scores mislead and uncertainty; ch17–ch26 own the data concepts recapped here; ch43 owns evaluation methodology.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch02 (Brooks's argument that video lacks touch and force); ch08 (force/torque, touch and sound; depth; weak transfer across tactile sensors); ch13 (compounding errors); ch14 (a policy exploiting model error); ch16 (latency budgets); ch17 (the action label; factors of variation; diversity vs volume; the data pyramid; data-scaling laws); ch18 (camera-motion confounds; missing force, touch and proprioception; egocentric datasets; narrations; first-person vs third-person viewpoints); ch19 (recoveries and deliberate failures as collection practice); ch20 (autonomous rollouts; failures as signal; generated video as data); ch21 (streams and modalities); ch22 (calibration and sync; what bad calibration does to learning); ch23 (video compression and learning); ch24 (subtask labels; outcome labels; quality checks); ch25 (the embodiment gap); ch26 (coverage; the long tail and rare events at the data level); ch27 (deformables, cloth, fluids and cutting in simulation); ch29 (the gap between world models and reality; action non-compliance introduced; correlation metrics); ch36 (families; physics-informed hybrids; world models vs simulators); ch37 (long-horizon drift; object permanence; multi-view consistency; the three quality axes; the prediction-target choice); ch38 (action conditioning; RL with physics rewards; video curation); ch39 (when imagined scores mislead; verification; uncertainty); ch40 (the WAM; its families; speed; claimed strengths).
- **Estimated length:** about 13,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **41.01 · How to read a failure: symptom, mechanism, root cause, remedy.** Set the method the whole chapter follows. *Concepts:* recap: the gap between world models and reality (ch29) and the three quality axes (ch37); the failure chain from symptom to mechanism to root cause to remedy to measure (owned here); why a WAM's prediction failure becomes an action failure, while a world model's failure becomes a wrong score or bad data (`[inference]`, reasoning shown; recap: WAM boundaries, ch40); "Robots Need More than VLA and World Models" as the framing that data, embodiment, world and reward interfaces are missing (preprint, attributed).
  - **41.02 · Failures of physics and perception.** Catalogue the failures where the imagined world breaks the rules of the real one. *Concepts:* physics violations: objects that pass through each other, appear, vanish or move without cause (owned here as a failure); recap: object permanence (ch37) and its failure when an object leaves the view; contact and force blindness, predicting what touching looks like but not what it does (owned here); deformables, liquids and plants (owned here as a failure; recap: why they are hard to simulate, ch27); multi-view inconsistency between head and wrist cameras (recap: ch37; failure owned here); camera-motion confounds, where the wearer's head motion is learned as motion of the world (recap: owned at the data level in ch18; failure owned here); RoboTrustBench's finding of coherent video with weak constraint reasoning, counterfactual grounding and physical interaction (preprint).
  - **41.03 · Failures of control, time and coverage.** Catalogue the failures that matter most when a model drives or judges a robot. *Concepts:* action non-compliance, a prediction that ignores the action it was given (owned here; previewed in ch29 and ch38; Chen et al.: models trained on expert data ignore off-expert actions); hallucinated success and optimistic futures (owned here as a failure; Chen et al.; recap: when imagined scores mislead, ch39); embodiment mismatch, futures that show a human hand or another robot's reach (owned here as a failure; recap: the embodiment gap, ch25); long-horizon drift (recap: the mechanism, ch37; failure owned here); rare events that the model has never seen (owned here as a failure; recap: the long tail, ch26); latency, a correct prediction that arrives too late (recap: WAM speed, ch40; latency budgets, ch16); (aside) the full taxonomy as one table: failure, symptom, who it hurts (data generation, evaluation, RL, planning, WAM control) and the root causes it traces to.
  - **41.04 · Diagnosis: benchmarks and probes.** Show how each failure is detected, and what today's tests miss. *Concepts:* physics benchmarks for generated video (owned here; previewed in ch37): VideoPhy-2's action-centric physical commonsense and WorldModelBench's physics-law and robotics checks (both preprints, not yet opened), Physics-IQ, PhyGenBench, WorldScore and "How far is video generation from world model" (seed leads, verify); robot-specific diagnostics (owned here): RoboTrustBench, and Chen et al.'s action-following probe; the counterfactual action probe, give the same moment two different actions and check that the futures differ (owned here; equation: action sensitivity as the difference between predicted futures, in plain words; metric to be read from Chen et al.); robustness suites for WAMs and VLAs (RoboTwin 2.0-Plus and LIBERO-Plus, as used by the March 2026 robustness study; preview: robustness suites, taught in ch43); what benchmarks miss: force, touch, sound and long tasks (`[inference]` until sourced).
  - **41.05 · Root causes in data I: volume and diversity.** Trace failures to how much data a model saw and how varied it was. *Concepts:* recap: diversity vs volume and factors of variation (ch17); recap: coverage and the long tail (ch26); the two volumes of a world model, abundant passive video vs scarce action-labelled robot video (owned here; recap: the world-model stage map, ch38); the robustness study's finding that π0.5 can match WAMs when trained on diverse data (preprint, revised 30 July 2026; preview: its weight in the convergence debate, taught in ch42); recap: Dyna-2's claimed scaling curve for world-action models (ch17; company claim); expert-only data as a diversity failure in action space, not scene space (owned here; Chen et al.).
  - **41.06 · Root causes in data II: missing modalities.** Show what a model cannot learn because its data never recorded it. *Concepts:* recap: missing force, touch and proprioception in human data (ch18; Brooks, ch02); the modality gap in world models (owned here; previewed in ch08 and ch18): no force or touch in video, no proprioception, no depth, no sound; recap: the connector's click as a sound event (ch08); recap: weak transfer across different tactile sensors (ch08); tactile-rich data (T-Rex, 100 hours, preprint); egocentric datasets with extra streams (recap: EgoDex and EgoVerse, ch18; EgoTouch and EgoTac surfaced by search, not opened); recap: the data pyramid survey (ch17).
  - **41.07 · Root causes in data III: richness of labels, embodiment and quality.** Trace failures to what the data says about itself, whose body it shows and how cleanly it was captured. *Concepts:* label richness for world models (owned here): action labels (recap: ch17), narration (recap: ch18), subtask boundaries and outcome labels (recap: ch24), counterfactuals and recoveries; counterfactual and off-expert action data, which shows what happens after a wrong or unusual move (owned here; Chen et al.); recap: recoveries and deliberate failures as collection practice (ch19) and failures as signal (ch20); embodiment: human vs robot bodies and first-person vs third-person viewpoints (recap: ch18 and ch25); quality: calibration and sync errors (recap: ch22, which previewed this) and compression artefacts (recap: ch23, which previewed this); the evidence gap, no controlled study yet ties a capture error to a world-model failure (`[inference]` until sourced).
  - **41.08 · Remedies I: data, what to collect, how much and in which modalities.** Turn root causes into a collection plan. *Concepts:* the failure-to-data map (owned here: each failure in 41.02–41.03 paired with the data that addresses it; `[inference]` where unsourced); action-diverse collection, including play, deliberately varied actions and failed attempts (owned here as a remedy; Chen et al.'s implication; `[inference]` beyond it); multimodal capture with force, touch and sound (T-Rex; recap: capture rigs and sync, ch22); recovery-rich and failure-rich data (recap: ch19 and ch20); how much is enough (no published dose–response study for world models: an explicit gap); recap: dataset design and data budgets (ch26).
  - **41.09 · Remedies II: models that sense force and touch.** Show the WAMs that predict or use contact signals, not only pixels. *Concepts:* force- and touch-aware WAMs (owned here; previewed in ch40): FAWAM, which uses force as both a prediction target and a correction signal (preprint); Tactile-WAM and its named failure, "tactile pollution" (preprint, not yet opened; term to be read from the paper); TacWAM, TacDyn-WAM and TacPAC (search results only, unconfirmed); recap: why "world" leaves room for touch (DreamZero, ch40); what these models still need: synchronised tactile data at scale (recap: 41.06; `[inference]`).
  - **41.10 · Remedies III: training, architecture and system.** Show the fixes that do not need new data, and their limits. *Concepts:* training: stronger action conditioning (recap: ch38), RL with physics and instruction rewards (recap: ch38), 3D and physics losses (owned here as a remedy; sources needed); predicting masks or latents instead of pixels (recap: Mask World Model, ch37); architecture: memory for long horizons and object permanence (recap: ch37), uncertainty so the model can say it does not know (recap: ch39); system: hybrids that pair a world model with a physics simulator (recap: ch36), verification before acting (recap: ch39) and guardrails outside the model (preview: safety layers, taught in ch44); DreamWAM, OA-WAM and World Action Verifier as seed leads to verify; (aside) a remedies table: failure, data remedy, model remedy, system remedy and the evidence tag for each.
  - **41.11 · Measuring progress.** Show how a team tracks whether its world model or WAM is failing less. *Concepts:* the failure scorecard (owned here): a rate per failure category from 41.03's table, measured on fixed probes and real checks, release over release (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); which benchmark covers which failure, and which have none (owned here; recap: 41.04); real-world checks that imagined results must be paired with (recap: correlation metrics, ch29; imagined vs real scores, ch39); preview: evaluation statistics and protocols (taught in ch43); preview: regression suites (taught in ch49); preview: mining field failures (taught in ch48).
  - **41.12 · What each running example needs.** Apply the taxonomy and the remedies to the four tasks. *Concepts:* (aside) per task: dominant failures, data root causes, data to collect and modalities to add (`[inference]`, reasoning shown, numbers marked `nofact`): dinner for five (dough and sauce physics; cooking video is abundant but records neither force nor heat); kitchen reset (spills, a glass that leaves the view, household-specific rare events); battery station (contact and click prediction; force- and sound-aware data from dummy modules; recent multisensor work on connector mating as a lead, re-verify); greenhouse (plant motion when touched; once-a-year situations; scarce, seasonal footage).
- **Running-example hooks:** all four in 41.12; battery station in 41.06 and 41.09 (the click and the push force are invisible to video; force-aware WAMs as the remedy, `[inference]`); dinner in 41.02 (sauce that looks right but flows wrongly; dough that does not deform like dough, `[inference]`); kitchen reset in 41.03 (a model that predicts a clean table whatever the robot does, hallucinated success, `[inference]`); greenhouse in 41.05 (some varieties and pests appear once a year, so volume cannot buy coverage, `[inference]`).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Do WAMs Generalize Better than VLAs? A Robustness Study — Zhang et al. — 2026-03-23 (rev. 2026-07-30) — arXiv 2603.22078 — preprint
  - Do Robotic World Models Really Follow Actions? — Chen et al. — 2026-08-25 — arXiv 2608.24885 — preprint
  - RoboTrustBench — Li et al. — 2026-06-01 — arXiv 2606.01600 — preprint
  - VideoPhy-2 — authors not recorded — 2025-03 — arXiv 2503.06800 — preprint (unconfirmed: not opened)
  - WorldModelBench — authors not recorded — 2025-02 — arXiv 2502.20694 — preprint (unconfirmed: not opened)
  - FAWAM — He et al. — 2026-06-07 — arXiv 2606.08555 — preprint
  - Tactile-WAM — authors not recorded — 2026-06 — arXiv 2606.26663 — preprint (unconfirmed: not opened)
  - T-Rex — Niu et al. — 2026-06-15 — arXiv 2606.17055 — preprint
  - Robots Need More than VLA and World Models — Karcini et al. — 2026-06-04 — arXiv 2606.06556 — preprint
  - Physics-IQ; PhyGenBench; WorldScore; How far is video generation from world model (2024); DreamWAM; OA-WAM; World Action Verifier — no date — no URL — (seed lead, verify)
- **Notes:** Core equations: action sensitivity as the difference between predicted futures under two actions (to be matched to Chen et al.'s metric); a per-category failure rate. The robustness study is pro-WAM yet concedes that diverse data closes the gap: present both halves, and leave the verdict to ch42. Gaps: 3D and physics-loss sources; any dose–response study of data volume for world models; a controlled capture-error study; primaries for the touch-WAM search results. If the draft passes 18,000 words, propose moving 41.08–41.10 into their own chapter.

### ch42 · How WAMs and VLAs converge on physical AI
- **Purpose:** How the two main bets for a robot's brain, a language-first VLA and a physics-first WAM, are borrowing from each other: the five routes by which they converge, the head-to-head evidence and its limits, what will decide the outcome (data, compute, latency and evaluation), several competing scenarios each with a confidence level and signals to watch, and what this means for deployment readiness. The chapter maps the debate and commits to no single thesis. ch42 owns the two-bets framing, the five convergence routes, steerable prompting with subgoal images, the embodied-native model, the case for one end-to-end model, the head-to-head comparison, the deciders and the scenarios. ch31 and ch40 own the VLA and the WAM; ch39 owns each use of a wrapped world model; ch43 owns evaluation methodology; ch52 owns open problems across the whole field.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** no.
- **Depends on:** ch12 (VLMs; reasoning models and test-time compute); ch16 (the layered brain; fast-slow designs; trade-offs of splitting layers; latency budgets); ch17 (the data pyramid; the scale–alignment trade-off; limits of the scaling evidence); ch18 (human video without actions); ch30 (the stages; the master lab-recipe map); ch31 (the VLA; architectures including single model vs dual system; conditioning on data-quality metadata in π0.7; scaling evidence); ch32 (embodied reasoning; DM0's recipe; knowledge insulation); ch33 (in-context and few-shot adaptation; the VLA latency model; the artefact bundle); ch35 (the policy–controller interface; joint vs separate training); ch36 (omnimodal world models); ch38 (the world-model recipe table; training cost); ch39 (evaluation, RL, planning and verification with world models; evidence on reliability); ch40 (the WAM definition; joint, latent and no-imagination families; speed; claimed strengths); ch41 (the failure taxonomy; the diverse-data finding of the robustness study).
- **Estimated length:** about 10,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **42.01 · Two bets: a language-first prior and a physics-first prior.** Set out what each approach inherits and why the field is borrowing across the line. *Concepts:* recap: the VLA (ch31) and the WAM (ch40); the two priors (owned here): knowledge of words, objects and instructions inherited from web-trained VLMs vs knowledge of motion and dynamics inherited from video models (`[inference]` framing, anchored in the WAM surveys' WAM-vs-VLA framing); DreamZero as the public statement of the WAM bet (recap: ch40); convergence as the borrowing of each bet's strength by the other (owned here); (aside) a map of the five convergence routes taught in 42.02–42.06.
  - **42.02 · Route 1: VLAs that predict the future.** Show VLAs that add imagination as a training signal or an output. *Concepts:* future-predicting VLAs (owned here): VLA-JEPA, DreamVLA and UniVLA (seed leads, verify; check whether this UniVLA is the latent-action UniVLA recapped in ch25); prediction as an auxiliary training loss vs as an output used at run time (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); distilling a WAM's imagination into a VLA ("Think Like a World Model, Act Like a VLA", unconfirmed, not opened); recap: Fast-WAM's imagination-only-in-training result (ch40), seen from the other side.
  - **42.03 · Route 2: WAMs that gain language and reasoning.** Show WAMs that add the instruction-following and reasoning a VLA inherits. *Concepts:* world-language-action models (owned here; the seed's world-language-action model paper, seed lead, verify); recap: omnimodal world models (ch36) and Cosmos 3 as one family that reasons, generates video and acts (ch40; NVIDIA technical report); FLUX 3 Action's claim to VLA speed with WAM quality (company claim; recap: ch40); recap: embodied reasoning (ch32), what a WAM must add to read "leave my glass" (`[inference]`).
  - **42.04 · Route 3: layered hybrids, subgoal images and test-time compute.** Show designs that keep separate models and connect them with pictures of the goal or with extra thinking time. *Concepts:* recap: the layered brain and fast-slow designs (ch16); steerable prompting with subgoal images (owned here; previewed in ch01, ch31 and ch33): a small world model proposes an image of the next goal, and the VLA acts toward it (π0.7, Physical Intelligence; the BAGEL-based subgoal model reached the scan only through search snippets: read it from the π0.7 paper before use); recap: conditioning on data-quality metadata (ch31); test-time compute in a VLA (τ0-VLA, seed lead, verify; recap: reasoning models and test-time compute, ch12); the interface between layers as an image, a latent or words (recap: ch16; `[inference]`).
  - **42.05 · Route 4: world models wrapped around policies.** Show convergence at the system level, where the world model never sits inside the policy. *Concepts:* recap: evaluation in imagination, RL inside world models, planning and verification (ch39); recap: the world model and policy improving together (World-VLA-Loop, ch38); the wrapper as a convergence route (owned here): any policy, VLA or WAM, gains imagination from outside (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); the position that VLAs and world models are both layers of a bigger stack that also needs data, embodiment, world and reward interfaces ("Robots Need More than VLA and World Models", preprint).
  - **42.06 · Route 5: natively unified, "embodied-native" models.** Show models built from the start to see, speak, imagine and act in one network. *Concepts:* the embodied-native model (owned here; DM0 recapped from ch30 and ch32): DM0 as an embodied-native VLA built from pre-training (recap: ch32); Kairos as a 4B native world-action stack designed for efficiency first (preprint); the case for one end-to-end model (owned here; previewed in ch16, ch31 and ch35): fewer interfaces to mismatch vs less inspectable layers and a single point of failure (`[inference]`, reasoning shown; recap: joint vs separate training, ch35).
  - **42.07 · Head-to-head evidence and its limits.** Weigh every direct comparison, and say plainly how thin it is. *Concepts:* the March 2026 robustness study (WAMs more robust on RoboTwin 2.0-Plus and LIBERO-Plus; π0.5 able to match with diverse data; recap: ch41); DreamZero ranked above π0.5 on RoboArena (aggregator-reported, unconfirmed: trace to RoboArena's own board); Cosmos 3's RoboArena claim (NVIDIA's own); a benchmark comparison's reported 1.58-point clean-score gap and finding that no model wins every axis (unconfirmed; the scan links it only loosely to IndustrialVLA-Bench, not opened); confounds that make a head-to-head unfair: different data, compute, model size, robots and benchmarks (owned here; `[inference]`, reasoning shown); what a fair test would hold fixed (`[inference]`; preview: evaluation methodology, arena evaluation and robustness suites, taught in ch43); (aside) a graded evidence table: comparison, who ran it, what was held fixed, evidence tag, date.
  - **42.08 · What decides the outcome: data, compute, latency and evaluation.** Name the forces that will settle the debate, whichever way it goes. *Concepts:* data, which bet can use the most abundant data, passive video vs action-labelled robot data (recap: the scale–alignment trade-off, ch17; human video without actions, ch18; Dyna-2's claim, ch17); compute, training and inference cost (the claim that WAMs need about seven times more training compute is aggregated and unconfirmed: trace or drop; recap: training cost, ch38); latency, decision time on the robot's own computer (recap: WAM speed, ch40; the VLA latency model, ch33); evaluation, whether anyone can measure the difference fairly (recap: ch39 and 42.07); (aside) a deciders table: what would favour each bet on each decider (`[inference]`, reasoning shown).
  - **42.09 · Scenarios: competing futures, confidence levels and signals to watch.** End the debate map with several futures, none chosen. *Concepts:* the scenario method (owned here: each scenario stated, given a confidence level of low, medium or high justified from 42.07–42.08's graded evidence, and paired with observable signals; all `[inference]`, reasoning shown, and no scenario declared the winner); scenarios to develop: (A) WAMs become the default policy backbone; (B) VLAs absorb future prediction and remain the default; (C) layered hybrids persist, with a reasoner, a world model and an action model kept apart; (D) one native omnimodal model wins; (E) neither suffices, and progress comes from the data, embodiment, world and reward interfaces around them (Karcini et al.'s position); signals to watch per scenario (for example: independent head-to-heads at matched data and compute; disclosed training compute; WAM decision times on onboard computers; adoption in shipped systems traced to primaries, with GR00T N2 as the pending test case); which scenarios can co-exist and which exclude each other (`[inference]`); preview: open problems across the field (taught in ch52).
  - **42.10 · What convergence means for deployment readiness.** Turn the debate into questions a product team can ask now, and apply them to the four tasks. *Concepts:* questions to ask of any robot brain: which route, which evidence, what speed on the target hardware, what it was never shown (owned here; `[inference]`); interfaces that survive a model swap (recap: the policy–controller interface, ch35; the artefact bundle, ch33); evaluating whichever approach wins (preview: readiness evaluation, taught in ch43 and ch46); (aside) per running example, which route's strengths matter most (`[inference]`, reasoning shown): dinner for five (language and reasoning for hidden allergens, a language-first strength); kitchen reset (language grounding plus spill prediction, a hybrid case); battery station (decision time and force, where neither bet is yet proven); greenhouse (data scarcity, which favours whichever bet uses unlabelled video best).
- **Running-example hooks:** all four in 42.10; dinner in 42.01 and 42.03 (an allergen rule is a language-first strength that a WAM must gain, `[inference]`); kitchen reset in 42.04 (a subgoal image of the cleared table with the glass left in place, `[inference]`); battery station in 42.08 (latency and force as deciders, `[inference]`); greenhouse in 42.08 (scarce, seasonal data as the data decider, `[inference]`).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Robots Need More than VLA and World Models — Karcini et al. — 2026-06-04 — arXiv 2606.06556 — preprint
  - Do WAMs Generalize Better than VLAs? A Robustness Study — Zhang et al. — 2026-03-23 (rev. 2026-07-30) — arXiv 2603.22078 — preprint
  - Kairos — Kairos Team — 2026-06-15 — arXiv 2606.16533 — preprint
  - DM0 — Yu et al. — 2026-02-16 — arXiv 2602.14974 — preprint
  - Cosmos 3 — NVIDIA — 2026-06-01 — arXiv 2606.02800 — tech-report
  - World Action Models are Zero-shot Policies (DreamZero) — Ye et al. (NVIDIA) — 2026-02-17 — arXiv 2602.15922 — preprint
  - π0.7: a Steerable Generalist Robotic Foundation Model — Physical Intelligence — 2026-04-16 — arXiv 2604.15483 — preprint (confirmed in the ch31 scan; the blog page https://www.pi.website/blog/pi07 was not opened, unconfirmed)
  - IndustrialVLA-Bench — authors not recorded — 2026-09 — arXiv 2609.25562 — preprint (unconfirmed: not opened)
  - Think Like a World Model, Act Like a VLA — authors not recorded — 2026-09 — arXiv 2609.24682 — preprint (unconfirmed: not opened)
  - VLA-JEPA; DreamVLA; UniVLA; τ0-VLA; the world-language-action model paper (2026) — no date — no URL — (seed lead, verify)
- **Notes:** No equation is planned; the chapter's core artefacts are the routes map, the graded evidence table, the deciders table and the scenarios table. Present every head-to-head with who ran it and what was held fixed; the RoboArena ranks and the seven-times compute figure stay out unless traced to primaries. Further lead: World Action Models: A Survey (Shen et al., arXiv 2606.20781) for the WAM-vs-VLA framing.

### Changes from seed (P8)
- ch40: added 40.01 (from predicting the world to acting in it, with the lineage) and expanded "the definition and its scope" into 40.02 (the definition, the origin of the term and the retroactive labelling, the boundaries with world models, VLAs and wrapped world models, and the surveys' disagreement) — the scan confirms DreamZero introduced the term and labels earlier work as WAMs after the fact; ch01 and ch03 point the definition and the dispute to ch40.
- ch40: split "architectures" into 40.03 (joint denoising, including action-as-latent-frames), 40.04 (two-tower and omnimodal), 40.05 (video then inverse dynamics) and 40.06 (latent WAMs and WAMs that stop imagining at run time); added 40.10 (family map and comparison table, with wrapped world models as the adjacent family) and 40.11 (the running examples) — BRIEF §3 asks for joint models in depth and a map of the adjacent families; the scan adds Fast-WAM's no-imagination design; ch31 owns mixture-of-transformers, so 40.04 recaps it.
- ch40: research targets corrected: the WAM surveys are June and September 2026 (the May 2026 survey is broader), the NVIDIA explainer found is dated 15 June 2026 (no July or August post found), and GR00T N2's DreamZero basis is aggregator-only and excluded until traced — per the scan.
- ch40 vs ch41: force- and touch-aware WAMs are previewed in ch40 and owned in ch41 as a remedy — the seed lists FAWAM under ch41, and the running-examples thread pairs force-aware WAMs with the battery station's failure case.
- ch41: grouped the seed's twelve-item failure list into 41.02 (physics and perception) and 41.03 (control, time and coverage) after a method section (41.01); split "root causes in data" into 41.05 (volume and diversity), 41.06 (modalities) and 41.07 (richness, embodiment and quality); split "remedies" into 41.08 (data), 41.09 (force- and touch-aware models) and 41.10 (training, architecture and system) — BRIEF §3 asks for special attention to data, and the scan reports a cluster of touch-aware WAMs.
- ch41 vs ch18, ch22, ch23, ch26, ch29, ch37 and ch39: ch41 owns each item as a failure and recaps its earlier owner (camera-motion confounds from ch18; calibration, sync and compression from ch22 and ch23; the long tail from ch26; the gap between world models and reality and the first mention of action non-compliance from ch29; drift, object permanence and multi-view consistency from ch37; when imagined scores mislead and uncertainty from ch39) — teach once; those chapters already preview ch41.
- ch41 vs ch43: physics benchmarks and robot-specific diagnostics for world models are owned in ch41; robustness suites (LIBERO-Plus, RoboTwin 2.0-Plus) and arena evaluation appear only as the venues of reported results and are previewed to ch43 — ch43's seed lists LIBERO-Plus among its research targets.
- ch42: gave each of the five convergence routes its own section (42.02–42.06); added a confounds-and-fairness analysis to the head-to-head section (42.07) and folded the running examples into 42.10 — the scan finds head-to-head evidence thin and mixed, and BRIEF §3 asks the chapter to weigh it.
- ch42: the scenarios section (42.09) develops five competing scenarios with confidence levels set from the graded evidence and signals to watch, and declares no winner; its scope is limited to the WAM–VLA question, with field-wide open problems previewed to ch52 — per BRIEF §3 and the Part brief.
- ch42: π0.7 is sourced from its arXiv paper (confirmed in the ch31 scan) rather than its blog (not opened in the ch42 scan), and its subgoal-image detail is marked unconfirmed until read in the paper; ch42 takes ownership of steerable prompting with subgoal images, the embodied-native model and the case for one end-to-end model, which ch01, ch16, ch30–ch33 and ch35 already preview to it.
- No PROPOSED chapter-level changes.
