## P9 · Evaluation and safety

### ch43 · Evaluating robot policies
- **Purpose:** How to measure whether a robot policy works well enough to trust: why evaluation is expensive and noisy, how to report a success rate with its uncertainty, how many trials a claim needs, how to compare two policies fairly, which cheap tiers (simulation, robustness suites, world models) may decide what, how to write a real-world protocol, how shared arenas work, and how to score generalisation, long tasks, partial credit and human interventions. It ends with a checklist for reading evaluation claims, a metric catalogue for Appendix C, and an evaluation plan for each running example. ch43 owns evaluation statistics, real-world protocols, arenas, robustness suites, generalisation axes, long-horizon and intervention metrics, and the metric catalogue. ch29 owns sim–real correlation metrics and the reverse gap; ch39 owns world-model-based evaluation; ch34 owns success detectors and VLM judges; ch46 owns acceptance tests at a site; ch47 owns fleet KPIs; ch49 owns regression suites and re-validation after updates.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch07 (no independent dexterity benchmarks); ch11 (the loss as a stand-in for the goal; data splits; generalisation; distribution shift); ch14 (reward models from pairwise comparisons and the Bradley–Terry model); ch16 (logging at the architecture level); ch17 (factors of variation; limits of the scaling evidence); ch19 (takeover interfaces); ch20 (interventions as data; process signals as labels); ch24 (outcome labels including partial credit; annotator agreement); ch26 (data ablations; the cost and noise of real-robot evaluation); ch27 (the simulated benchmark; coded success checks; benchmark saturation); ch28 (simulation for evaluation as a first gate); ch29 (paired tests; Pearson and rank correlation; MMRV; the reverse gap); ch31 (scaling evidence and TRI's blind-tested LBM study); ch34 (resets; success detectors; progress reward models; VLM judges and FailBench); ch39 (world-model-based evaluation; whether imagined scores predict real ones); ch41 (robustness suites as venues of reported results; the failure scorecard); ch42 (confounds in head-to-heads; what a fair test holds fixed).
- **Estimated length:** about 12,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **43.01 · Why evaluating a robot policy is hard.** Derive why one honest number for "how good is this policy" is slow, costly and noisy, and set out the tiers of evaluation. *Concepts:* evaluation as estimating a probability from a limited number of trials (owned here); sources of variation between trials: starting positions, object placement, lighting, hardware wear, the person who resets the scene and the policy's own randomness (owned here; `[inference]` until sourced); the cost of a real trial, including resets (recap: resets as a cost of real-world RL, ch34); why a training or validation loss does not predict closed-loop success (recap: the loss as a stand-in for the goal, ch11; `[inference]` until sourced); the evaluation ladder from offline checks through simulation and world models to lab trials and the field (owned here; `[inference]` framing, reasoning shown); evaluation vs acceptance vs re-validation (preview: acceptance tests at a site, taught in ch46; preview: regression suites and re-validation after updates, taught in ch49).
  - **43.02 · Success rates and their uncertainty.** Teach from zero how to report a success rate honestly. *Concepts:* the trial as a pass-or-fail coin flip with an unknown bias (owned here); the success rate as successes divided by trials (owned here; equation); sampling variability, why ten trials can show 70% for a policy whose true rate is 50% (worked example with numbers marked `nofact`); the standard error of a proportion (owned here; equation); confidence intervals for a success rate (owned here; previewed in ch29; a binomial interval such as Wilson's, equation; primary source needed); why intervals near 0% and 100% need special care (`[inference]` until sourced); a reporting convention: trials, interval and what counted as success (owned here).
  - **43.03 · How many trials? Sample sizes and proving rare failures.** Show how many trials a claim needs, and why readiness against rare failures cannot be bought with trials alone. *Concepts:* sample-size planning for a target interval width (owned here; equation; numbers marked `nofact`); the zero-failure bound, how many clean trials it takes to claim a failure rate below a target (owned here; equation derived from first principles; source needed); why "no failures in 50 trials" proves less than it sounds (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); rare, severe failures as the reason readiness needs hazard analysis and safety layers as well as testing (`[inference]`; preview: taught in ch44); recap: the cost and noise of real-robot evaluation behind data ablations (ch26).
  - **43.04 · Comparing two policies: A/B tests, blinding and sequential testing.** Show how to decide fairly whether a new policy beats the old one. *Concepts:* paired comparisons under matched starting conditions (owned here); randomised trial order, so drift and wear fall on both policies alike (owned here); blind evaluation, where the person running and scoring trials does not know which policy is running (owned here); TRI's blind, randomised A/B method with sequential testing (A Careful Examination of Large Behavior Models, preprint; journal version unconfirmed); sequential tests that stop early once the evidence is clear, and why repeated peeking without one inflates false wins (owned here; equation in plain words; a source beyond TRI needed); statistical vs practical significance (`[inference]`); many comparisons at once and false discoveries (source needed); what a fair comparison holds fixed: data, compute, robot, tasks and evaluator (recap: confounds in head-to-heads, ch42).
  - **43.05 · Evaluating without the real robot: simulation, robustness suites and world models.** Place the cheap tiers on the ladder and say what each may decide. *Concepts:* recap: the simulated benchmark, coded success checks and saturation (ch27); recap: simulation as a first gate on every checkpoint (ch28); recap: paired tests, Pearson and rank correlation, MMRV and the reverse gap (ch29), with SimplerEnv and Wang et al.'s 2026 recipe as the evidence; robustness suites that perturb a benchmark's cameras, lighting, objects, wording and layout (owned here; previewed in ch41 and ch42; LIBERO-Plus and RoboTwin 2.0-Plus, seed leads to verify); recap: world-model-based evaluation and whether imagined scores predict real ones (ch39); which decisions each cheap tier may make: screen, rank or certify (owned here; `[inference]`, reasoning shown).
  - **43.06 · Writing a real-world evaluation protocol.** Show what a protocol must fix in advance so its results mean something. *Concepts:* the evaluation protocol (owned here): task definition, the distribution of starting conditions, reset procedure, success criterion, trial count, time limits and stopping rules; fixing the protocol before the first trial (pre-registration; source needed); who decides success: human raters, coded checks on process signals, and automatic judges (recap: outcome labels and annotator agreement, ch24; process signals as labels, ch20; success detectors and VLM judges, with FailBench's finding that judges lean toward "success" when unsure, ch34); recording every trial on video for audit (recap: logging, ch16; `[inference]`); strict vs lenient protocols (Figure's Helix 2.5 protocol as an attributed example of strict scoring; company claim).
  - **43.07 · Shared real-world evaluation: distributed, blind and online arenas.** Show how the field turned real-robot testing into shared infrastructure. *Concepts:* distributed evaluation across many labs and robots (owned here; RoboArena, preprint, CoRL 2025); double-blind pairwise comparison, two anonymous policies on the same task with a rater picking the better (RoboArena); ranking policies from pairwise outcomes (owned here; recap: the Bradley–Terry model, ch14; RoboArena's aggregation method to be read from the paper); online real-robot benchmarks where policies are submitted remotely (RoboChallenge and its Table30 suite, preprint); competitions with fixed task sets and difficulty tiers (ManipArena: 20 tasks, partial-credit scoring, in-domain, visual-shift and semantic out-of-distribution tiers, paired real-to-sim tasks; preprint, authors to confirm); arenas vs fixed benchmarks, breadth and realism vs repeatability (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); arena ranks quoted by labs (recap: claims to be traced to the arena's own board, ch40 and ch42); "Try it" pointer to a public leaderboard where a primary confirms one.
  - **43.08 · Generalisation axes: what is new in a test.** Teach the reader to ask in which way a test differs from training. *Concepts:* recap: generalisation and distribution shift (ch11); recap: factors of variation (ch17); generalisation axes for robot evaluation (owned here; previewed in ch11): new positions, new appearance, new objects, new scenes or sites, new instructions, new tasks and new bodies (`[inference]` taxonomy anchored in ManipArena's tiers); held-out sites vs held-out tasks (owned here); what a "zero-shot" claim means (Helix 2.5's 30 unfamiliar homes: no data from those homes, while task data came from elsewhere; company claim, scan finding); contamination, test scenes or objects that leak into training (`[inference]` until sourced).
  - **43.09 · Long-horizon tasks and partial credit.** Show how to score tasks with many steps. *Concepts:* recap: outcome labels including partial credit (ch24) and progress reward models (ch34); whole-task success vs subtask progress (owned here); why long tasks fail more often: overall success as the product of step successes when steps are independent (owned here; equation; numbers marked `nofact`); partial-credit scoring rubrics (ManipArena); strict all-or-nothing scoring (Helix 2.5: no partial credit, any safety intervention counts as failure; company claim); time metrics: completion time, cycle time and throughput (owned here; preview: takt time, taught in ch46); quality beyond done or not done: breakage, bruising, spills (`[inference]`, from the running examples' "done means" criteria).
  - **43.10 · Interventions, assists and safety events as metrics.** Show how to count human help, and why the counting rule must be set before counting. *Concepts:* recap: takeover interfaces (ch19) and interventions as data (ch20); intervention metrics (owned here): interventions per hour, mean time between interventions, assists per task (sources needed beyond Helix 2.5; `[inference]` until sourced); kinds of intervention: safety stop, rescue, nudge, remote guidance (owned here; `[inference]`); counting rules that change the headline, such as whether a remote assist counts as failure (Helix 2.5's rule; company claim); safety events and near misses as evaluation outcomes (preview: incident response, taught in ch44); preview: fleet KPIs (taught in ch47).
  - **43.11 · Reading evaluation claims, and the metric catalogue.** Turn the chapter into a checklist and a reference table. *Concepts:* how to read an evaluation claim (owned here): how many trials, which interval, blind or not, who ran it, which tier, which generalisation axis, what counted as success and as an intervention (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); (aside) worked readings of TRI's LBM study and Figure's Helix 2.5 claim, each attributed; self-reported vs independently measured results (scan finding: the statistics guidance found so far is mostly TRI's own); (aside) the evaluation metric catalogue, built to be lifted into Appendix C: each metric's definition, formula, where it is measured and typical public ranges, sourced or "not published" (owned here; ch46–ch49 add their rows).
  - **43.12 · Readiness evaluation for the running examples.** Write an evaluation plan for each task. *Concepts:* (aside) per task: metrics, protocol, generalisation axes, trials needed, and which tier may decide what (`[inference]`, reasoning shown, numbers marked `nofact`): dinner for five (zero allergen exposure cannot be shown by trials alone and needs a safety argument; each course within 10 minutes of plan; "tastes good" cannot be scored); kitchen reset (nothing broken, under an hour, at most one remote assist, unsafe moments counted as failures; the first week in a new home as a held-out site); battery station (at least 99% of packs passing the final test first time, with the torque tool's records as ground truth, and dummy modules before live packs); greenhouse (picking rate against a skilled worker, bruising and missed ripe fruit; evaluation spread across a season); preview: acceptance tests at a site (taught in ch46).
- **Running-example hooks:** all four in 43.12; battery station in 43.03 (how many packs it takes to support a 99% first-pass claim, via the zero-failure bound, numbers marked `nofact`) and 43.06 (torque and angle records as a coded success check); dinner in 43.03 (one allergen exposure is a failure, so trials alone cannot prove readiness, `[inference]`) and 43.09 (course timing as a time metric); kitchen reset in 43.08 (a never-seen home as the held-out site) and 43.10 (the one-remote-assist budget as an intervention metric); greenhouse in 43.08 (variety and season as generalisation axes) and 43.09 (picking rate and bruising as graded, not pass-or-fail, outcomes).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - RoboArena: Distributed Real-World Evaluation of Generalist Robot Policies — Atreya et al. — 2025-06-22 — arXiv 2506.18123 — preprint (CoRL 2025)
  - RoboChallenge: Large-scale Real-robot Evaluation of Embodied Policies — RoboChallenge team — 2025-10-20 — arXiv 2510.17950 — preprint
  - ManipArena — authors not confirmed — 2026-03-30 — arXiv 2603.28545 — preprint
  - A Careful Examination of Large Behavior Models — TRI LBM Team — 2025-07-07 — arXiv 2507.05331 — preprint (Science Robotics version, DOI 10.1126/scirobotics.aea6201, unconfirmed: page returned 403)
  - Statistical Thinking for Robot Policy Evaluation — TRI — undated — https://medium.com/toyotaresearch/statistical-thinking-for-robot-policy-evaluation-from-rigorous-a-b-testing-to-effective-0ae886fbd68d — company-claim (unconfirmed: page returned 403)
  - Evaluating Real-World Robot Manipulation Policies in Simulation (SimplerEnv) — Li et al. — 2024-05 — arXiv 2405.05941 — preprint
  - A Practical Recipe Towards Improving Sim-and-Real Correlation for VLA Evaluation — Wang et al. — 2026-06-09 — arXiv 2606.10366 — preprint
  - Helix 2.5: Zero-Shot 30-Home Generalization — Figure AI — 2026-09-17 — https://www.figure.ai/news/helix-2-5-zero-shot-30-home-generalization — company-claim
  - LIBERO-Plus and robustness suites; statistical guidance for robot evaluation — no date — no URL — (seed lead, verify)
- **Notes:** Core equations: success rate; standard error; a binomial confidence interval; sample size; the zero-failure bound; whole-task success as a product of step successes; interventions per hour. Gaps: independent statistics primaries (intervals, sample sizes, sequential tests), intervention-metric definitions beyond Helix 2.5, pre-registration, cost per real trial; LIBERO-Plus and world-model evaluation were not scanned (the latter is a recap of ch39). Helix 2.5's 56% and its protocol are a company claim with no independent check.

### ch44 · Safety engineering for learned robots
- **Purpose:** How a humanoid driven by learned policies is made safe enough to work near people: why learned behaviour strains classical safety arguments, how hazards are found and rated, what functional safety demands, how robots share space with people, how runtime monitors, safety filters and certified fallbacks wrap a learned policy, how semantic hazards such as knives and allergens are handled, how falls are managed, why cybersecurity and privacy are safety matters, and how incidents are handled. ch44 owns hazard analysis, functional safety concepts (SIL and PL), speed-and-separation and power-and-force limiting as safety functions, runtime monitors, safety filters and control barrier functions, certified fallbacks and the certification gap, semantic safety and robot constitutions, fall management in the safety case, cybersecurity, privacy by design in the deployed robot, and incident response. ch10 owns safety hardware; ch16 owns the safety layer as an architectural slot; ch24 owns data-protection law; ch34 owns safe exploration during training; ch39 owns world-model verification; ch45 owns the standards themselves and certification; ch47 owns operational escalation in a fleet.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch02 (safety of a heavy legged machine near people, previewed); ch04 (mass and fall energy; the wheeled, rail and soft home variants); ch05 (thermal derating); ch09 (the onboard computer; network links); ch10 (hazardous energy; hardware vs software safety layers; emergency stop and safe torque off; the fail-passive gap; the independent safety controller; force, speed and torque limits; collision detection; falling safely and SafeFall; soft covers); ch11 (distribution shift); ch12 (hallucination; VLMs); ch14 (exploration; reward hacking); ch15 (control; residual learning; balance); ch16 (the safety layer slot; watchdogs; fallback behaviours; logging; privacy of off-board processing); ch19 (remote expert modes and claimed no-go zones); ch24 (what robot data captures about people; redaction; consent; data-protection law); ch32 (embodied reasoning); ch34 (safe exploration at the training level); ch35 (falls, self-collision and infeasible commands); ch39 (verifying actions and predicting failure; uncertainty); ch41 (guardrails outside the model); ch43 (proving rare failure rates; safety events as evaluation outcomes).
- **Estimated length:** about 13,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **44.01 · Why learned robots are hard to make safe.** Set out the problem: classical safety assumes a specified, predictable machine, and a learned policy is neither. *Concepts:* recap: hazardous energy and hardware vs software safety layers (ch10); recap: the safety layer as an architectural slot (ch16); hazard, harm and risk, with risk as severity combined with likelihood (owned here; equation in plain words; source needed); why learned policies strain classical safety arguments: no written specification, behaviour learned from data, and distribution shift (owned here; `[inference]` until sourced, with Ding et al. 2026 as the closest analysis; recap: distribution shift, ch11); defence in depth, independent layers so that no single failure causes harm (owned here; `[inference]` until sourced); the safety case, a structured argument with evidence that a system is safe enough for a stated use (owned here; source needed).
  - **44.02 · Hazard analysis: HARA and FMEA.** Show how a team finds what can go wrong before it does. *Concepts:* hazard identification for a humanoid: moving mass, falls, pinch points, held tools, hot or sharp objects and the task's own hazards (owned here; recap: ch10); hazard analysis and risk assessment, HARA (owned here; previewed in ch10; source needed); failure mode and effects analysis, FMEA (owned here; source needed; a risk priority number, equation, only if sourced); the order of risk reduction: design it out, guard it, warn about it (`[inference]` until sourced); hazards that come from learned behaviour rather than broken parts (owned here; `[inference]`, reasoning shown; recap: failure modes at the brain–body seam, ch35).
  - **44.03 · Functional safety: safety functions, SIL and PL.** Explain how a safety function is rated for how reliably it must work. *Concepts:* functional safety and the safety function (owned here); the safety integrity level, SIL (owned here; previewed in ch10; IEC 61508, seed lead); the performance level, PL, with categories, redundancy and self-diagnosis (owned here; previewed in ch10; ISO 13849, seed lead); the probability of a dangerous failure per hour as the quantity behind the ratings (equation; values to be read from the standards); recap: the independent safety controller (ch10); recap: the fail-passive gap (ch10), with Ding et al.'s analysis of certified functional safety for industrial humanoids, which makes no PL e or SIL 3 claim (preprint); preview: how the standards cite these ratings (taught in ch45).
  - **44.04 · Sharing space with people: speed and separation, power and force limiting.** Show the two ways a robot may work near people without a fence. *Concepts:* recap: torque, speed and power limits in drive firmware, and collision detection (ch10); speed and separation monitoring, slowing and stopping as a person approaches (owned here; previewed in ch10; protective separation distance, equation, ISO/TS 15066, seed lead); power and force limiting, keeping any contact below limits set per body region (owned here; previewed in ch10; ISO/TS 15066, seed lead); what changes for a mobile, two-armed robot that can fall (`[inference]`, reasoning shown; recap: the fail-passive gap, ch10); "cooperatively safe" claims (Digit 5, company claim); preview: where these requirements sit in the 2025 standards (taught in ch45).
  - **44.05 · Runtime monitors and safety filters around learned policies.** Show how an independent checker watches and corrects a learned policy while it runs. *Concepts:* the runtime monitor (owned here; previewed in ch16): detecting unsafe states, unfamiliar inputs or missed deadlines; the safety filter, which passes safe actions and minimally changes unsafe ones (owned here; previewed in ch15 and ch16); control barrier functions, a mathematical test that keeps the robot inside a safe set (owned here; equation in plain words: the safety margin may shrink no faster than a set rate; recap: control, ch15); filters on VLA outputs (Park et al.'s attention-guided filter, tested on SafeLIBERO, preprint); latent safety filters and policy-library barrier functions (scan finding; primaries to locate); recap: verifying actions with a world model and uncertainty (ch39); what a filter needs, a model of the robot and a trustworthy state estimate, and where it fails (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); recap: safe exploration at the training level (ch34).
  - **44.06 · Certified fallbacks, safe stops and the certification gap.** Show what the robot falls back to, and why a learned safety layer cannot yet be certified. *Concepts:* recap: graceful degradation and fallback behaviours (ch16); the certified fallback, a simple, verified controller that takes over when the learned layer is not trusted (owned here; source needed); the safe stop for a balancing robot, choosing when and how to stop so that it stays upright (owned here; learned stoppability value, preprint, unconfirmed; recap: the fail-passive gap, ch10); the certification gap, learned monitors and filters with no recognised path to a safety rating yet (owned here; scan finding, with Ding et al. as the closest analysis); keeping the learned layers outside the certified safety function (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); preview: certification paths (taught in ch45).
  - **44.07 · Semantic safety: knives, allergens, hot surfaces and robot constitutions.** Show the hazards that depend on what things mean, not on forces. *Concepts:* semantic safety (owned here): harm that a force limit cannot see, such as a knife left at a child's height, an allergen in a dish or a pan left on a lit hob; robot constitutions, written rules for a robot's behaviour, and generated constitutions (owned here; Sermanet et al., reporting 84.3% alignment for generated constitutions, preprint); the ASIMOV benchmark for semantic safety (owned here); Gemini Robotics 1.5's ASIMOV results (tech report; company-reported); recap: hallucination (ch12) and embodied reasoning (ch32); hard constraints with no margin vs soft preferences (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); checking a plan against hard constraints outside the model (`[inference]` until sourced; recap: guardrails, ch41); manipulated instructions and adversarial inputs (gap: source needed).
  - **44.08 · Fall management in the safety case.** Show how falls are predicted, softened, recovered from and argued about. *Concepts:* recap: the physics of a fall, protective materials, learned protective falls and getting back up (ch10); recap: balance (ch15) and falls at the brain–body seam (ch35); fall management as a chain: predict, avoid, protect, recover, report (owned here; `[inference]` framing); learned fall prediction and protective control in the safety argument (SafeFall's 68.3% lower peak contact force, preprint; recap of ch10's mechanism); places where a fall is unacceptable, so the body or the site must change (beside a live pack, near a toddler; `[inference]`; recap: wheeled and rail variants, ch04).
  - **44.09 · Cybersecurity: when a hacked robot becomes a safety hazard.** Show why security is part of safety for a machine that moves. *Concepts:* security as a safety issue, since an attacker can command motion (owned here; `[inference]`, reasoning shown); the attack surface of a humanoid: wireless links, cloud services, teleoperation channels, update paths and model inputs (owned here; `[inference]` until sourced); documented humanoid flaws, static keys and telemetry on a Unitree G1 (Mayoral-Vilches, preprint); a 2026 chained Bluetooth-to-root report on the G1 EDU (press only: trace the CVEs to a primary advisory); claims about where robot data is sent, attributed to the researchers and press who made them (scan caution); secure design: unique keys, signed updates, least privilege and vulnerability disclosure (sources needed); preview: cybersecurity requirements in industrial robot standards (taught in ch45); preview: secure over-the-air updates (taught in ch47 and ch49).
  - **44.10 · Privacy by design in the deployed robot.** Show how a robot that sees inside homes and workplaces is built to reveal as little as possible. *Concepts:* recap: what robot data captures about people, where redaction runs, and consent (ch24); recap: remote expert modes and claimed no-go zones (ch19); privacy by design (owned here; previewed in ch16 and ch24): data minimisation, on-device processing, short retention, visible recording indicators and user controls (sources needed; `[inference]` until sourced); remote viewing inside a home as the hardest case (`[inference]`); storing household health information such as guests' allergies (preview: taught in ch48); recap: data-protection law (ch24); preview: what to log, and with what privacy, in a fleet (taught in ch47).
  - **44.11 · Incident response.** Show what happens after something goes wrong, and how the safety case learns from it. *Concepts:* incidents and near misses (owned here); the incident response cycle: make safe, preserve evidence, find the root cause, correct, report and share (owned here; sources needed, an explicit gap per the scan); logs and recordings as evidence (recap: logging, ch16); updating the hazard analysis and the safety case after an incident (recap: 44.01–44.02); reporting duties (preview: taught in ch45); escalation in a running fleet (preview: taught in ch47); re-validation after a fix (preview: taught in ch49).
  - **44.12 · The safety case for each running example.** Apply every layer to the four tasks. *Concepts:* (aside) per task: top hazards, which layer handles each (hardware, functional safety, runtime filter, semantic check, procedure) and the residual risk (`[inference]`, reasoning shown): dinner for five (an allergen as a hard constraint with no margin; fire, hot oil and knives); kitchen reset (a toddler near a knife; separation and force limits around children and pets); battery station (live high voltage that cannot be switched off, a fall or dropped tool beside a linked pack, and trial and error forbidden; the wheeled variant's advantage); greenhouse (cutters near workers, crop-protection chemicals, and heat stress for the robot and the people beside it, `[inference]` until sourced; recap: thermal derating, ch05).
- **Running-example hooks:** all four in 44.12; dinner in 44.07 (hidden allergens in pesto, curry paste and hummus as a semantic-safety test) and 44.10 (guests' allergies as stored health information); kitchen reset in 44.04 and 44.07 (a toddler near a chef's knife: a force limit cannot see the hazard, a semantic check can, `[inference]`); battery station in 44.03 and 44.08 (the fail-passive gap beside a live pack) and 44.09 (a compromised robot on a plant network, `[inference]`); greenhouse in 44.02 (cutters, chemicals and heat in a HARA, `[inference]`).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Generating Robot Constitutions & Benchmarks for Semantic Safety — Sermanet et al. (DeepMind) — 2025-03-11 — arXiv 2503.08663 — preprint
  - Gemini Robotics 1.5 — Google DeepMind — 2025 (date unconfirmed in the ch44 scan; ch16 records 2025-10-02) — arXiv 2510.03342 — tech-report (opened only via search)
  - Your Model Already Knows: Attention-Guided Safety Filter for VLA Models — Park et al. — 2026-06-08 — arXiv 2606.09749 — preprint
  - Toward Certified Functional Safety for Industrial Humanoid Robots — Ding et al. — 2026-08-03 — arXiv 2608.02809 — preprint
  - SafeFall: Learning Protective Control for Humanoid Robots — Meng et al. — 2025-11-23 — arXiv 2511.18509 — preprint
  - The Cybersecurity of a Humanoid Robot — Mayoral-Vilches — 2025-09-17 — arXiv 2509.14096 — preprint
  - Two Unitree G1 EDU flaws enable root RCE — The Hacker News — 2026-08 — https://thehackernews.com/2026/08/two-unitree-g1-edu-humanoid-robot-flaws.html — press (secondary; trace CVEs to a primary advisory, unconfirmed)
  - Humanoid Safe Stop via Learned Stoppability Value — authors not recorded — date not recorded — arXiv 2609.02358 — preprint (unconfirmed: search only)
  - ISO/TS 15066; ISO 13849; IEC 61508 — no date — no URL — (seed lead, verify)
- **Notes:** Core equations: risk as severity and likelihood; probability of dangerous failure per hour; protective separation distance; the control-barrier condition. Gaps: HARA, FMEA, safety-case, certified-fallback, incident-response, privacy-by-design and secure-design sources; adversarial-instruction sources; the standards are seed leads only. Learned filters are preprints, mostly on simulated suites, with no certification path: present them beside Ding et al.'s argument; the 2026 G1 flaw report is press-only.

### ch45 · Standards, certification and regulation
- **Purpose:** Which standards and laws a humanoid must meet before it can be sold and deployed, stated with their status at the build date and sourced from the issuing bodies: the vocabulary of standards and conformity, the industrial, mobile, legged and service-robot standards, the EU Machinery Regulation, the AI Act as amended by the Digital Omnibus, product liability, the position in other jurisdictions, and the certification path in practice. ch45 owns standards, certification and product regulation. ch44 owns the safety engineering concepts the standards cite (hazard analysis, SIL and PL, speed-and-separation, power-and-force limiting, cybersecurity); ch24 owns data-protection law and AI Act data governance; ch46 owns site acceptance; ch49 owns re-certification after updates.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch02 (published deployments, attributed); ch04 (the reference humanoid and its wheeled, rail and soft home variants); ch09 (battery transport certification, UN38.3); ch10 (safety hardware; the fail-passive gap; the independent safety controller); ch24 (GDPR, India's DPDP Act and Rules, AI Act data governance; the Digital Omnibus previewed); ch43 (evidence from testing; proving rare failures); ch44 (hazard analysis; functional safety, SIL and PL; speed and separation; power and force limiting; certified fallbacks and the certification gap; cybersecurity; incident response).
- **Estimated length:** about 11,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **45.01 · Why standards matter for deployment.** Explain what standards, regulations and certification are, and why a buyer cannot deploy without them. *Concepts:* standard vs regulation vs certification vs conformity assessment (owned here); who writes robot standards: ISO, IEC, ANSI/A3, UL and national bodies (owned here); harmonised standards and the presumption of conformity in the EU (owned here; source needed: EUR-Lex); how a standard matures from committee draft to published, and why status must always carry a date (owned here; ISO stage names, source needed); why buyers, insurers and site owners ask for standards (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); recap: hazard analysis, functional safety, SIL and PL (ch44).
  - **45.02 · Industrial robots and collaborative operation: ISO 10218 (2025) and ISO/TS 15066.** Teach the base standards for industrial robots and what the 2025 edition changed. *Concepts:* ISO 10218-1:2025 (the robot) and ISO 10218-2:2025 (the robot application) as Edition 3 (owned here; per ISO catalogue listings found by search, pages returned 403); collaborative applications as a property of the whole application, not the robot alone (owned here; `[inference]` until read from the standard); ISO/TS 15066 content folded into Part 1, and cybersecurity added (per the ISO listing, unconfirmed); recap: speed and separation monitoring and power and force limiting (ch44); what an industrial-robot standard does not cover for a legged, mobile humanoid (`[inference]`, reasoning shown).
  - **45.03 · Industrial mobile robots: ANSI/A3 R15.08.** Show the standards for robots that drive around a site. *Concepts:* the R15.08 series, reported complete with Part 3 in 2026 (seed lead, verify with A3); mobile robot classes and the mobile manipulator (owned here; definitions to be read from the standard); recap: the wheeled variant (ch04) and the wheeled AEON pilot in a battery hall (ch02); what transfers to a wheeled humanoid and what does not (`[inference]`).
  - **45.04 · Dynamically stable and legged robots: standards still being written.** State plainly that no published standard yet covers a balancing industrial robot. *Concepts:* why legged robots need their own rules (recap: the fail-passive gap, ch10 and ch44); ISO 25785-1 for dynamically stable industrial mobile robots, still a committee draft (ISO/CD) at the build date (ISO page via search, not opened); A3 TR R15.108 for humanoids, in development (per Agility, company claim); what can and cannot be said about a draft's content (owned here; status only until a text is public); preview: certification of learned systems as an open problem (taught in ch52).
  - **45.05 · Personal care, service and consumer robots: ISO 13482 and UL 3300.** Show the standards a home humanoid may meet. *Concepts:* ISO 13482 for personal care robots (seed lead; a revision reported near final draft, unconfirmed because the ISO page returned 403); UL 3300 for consumer, service and education robots (Edition 1, 2024; an OSHA NRTL listing dated 2025-12-31, search only, meaning to be confirmed); recap: the soft home variant (ch04) and soft covers (ch10); what these standards leave open for a home humanoid that cooks and handles knives (`[inference]`).
  - **45.06 · The EU Machinery Regulation 2023/1230.** Explain the EU law that most industrial and service humanoids will fall under. *Concepts:* from the Machinery Directive to Regulation (EU) 2023/1230, applying from 20 January 2027 (seed lead; date per Eurogip, confirm in EUR-Lex); essential health and safety requirements (owned here; source needed: EUR-Lex); conformity assessment routes, self-assessment vs a notified body, and CE marking (owned here; source needed); provisions on machines with self-evolving behaviour and on protection against corruption (to be read from EUR-Lex before use); recap: harmonised standards (45.01).
  - **45.07 · The EU AI Act and the Digital Omnibus.** Show how AI rules attach to robots, with every date as of the build date. *Concepts:* the AI Act's risk classes (owned here; previewed in ch24); high-risk through EU product-safety law (Annex I) vs high-risk listed uses (Annex III) (owned here; which product laws Annex I lists, to be confirmed in EUR-Lex); recap: AI Act data-governance duties (ch24); the Digital Omnibus on AI, Regulation (EU) 2026/1744, in force 27 July 2026 and moving high-risk dates to 2 December 2027 (Annex III) and 2 August 2028 (Annex I products) (per Eurogip; ch24 records 8 July 2026 for the EUR-Lex text; confirm both); what changes for a humanoid maker on each date (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); the "current as of" stamp and the `/refresh` re-check.
  - **45.08 · Product liability.** Explain who pays when a robot causes harm. *Concepts:* product liability vs product-safety regulation (owned here); Directive (EU) 2024/2853 on liability for defective products, with transposition due 9 December 2026 (unconfirmed; EUR-Lex needed); software and AI as products, and defects that arise from updates or missing security updates (to be read from the Directive before use); recap: incident response and evidence (ch44); liability outside the EU (gap).
  - **45.09 · Beyond the EU: United States, China, Japan, Korea and India.** Lay out what other jurisdictions require, dated, and where the book has no evidence yet. *Concepts:* the United States: consensus standards (ANSI/A3, UL), nationally recognised testing laboratories, and field evaluation (owned here; Agility's statement that Digit passed an OSHA-recognised field evaluation, company claim; OSHA primary needed); China's Humanoid Robots and Embodied Intelligence Standards System (2026), with 52 initial standards, 28 February 2026 (MIIT/TC08, reported by SESEC; MIIT original needed); Japan, Korea and India (not scanned: explicit research requests); recap: India's DPDP Rules (ch24); (aside) a dated jurisdiction table with "not yet researched" cells.
  - **45.10 · The certification path in practice.** Walk through how a humanoid goes from prototype to a certified product. *Concepts:* the conformity-assessment steps: risk assessment, applying standards, testing, technical file, declaration and marking (owned here; source needed); third-party certification vs self-declaration; notified bodies and testing laboratories (owned here); field evaluation of one installation vs listing of a product model (owned here; source needed); reading "first certified humanoid" claims (AgiBot A2 and Digit, company claims with unnamed or partial certifiers; scan caution); a robot that keeps learning, where an update may reopen the assessment (`[inference]`; preview: safety re-validation and re-certification, taught in ch49); timelines and costs (gap); recap: battery transport certification (UN38.3, ch09).
  - **45.11 · Standards and regulation for the running examples.** Map each task to the rules that apply, and their status at the build date. *Concepts:* (aside) a dated status table of every standard and law in the chapter (published, draft, in development, unconfirmed), stamped with the "current as of" date; per task (`[inference]`, reasoning shown): battery station (ISO 10218, R15.08 for the wheeled variant, the Machinery Regulation, and high-voltage work rules, source needed); kitchen reset and dinner for five (consumer and personal care standards, the AI Act, product liability, and health data, recap ch24); greenhouse (agricultural machinery and chemical-handling rules, not scanned: research request); what a buyer should ask a maker to show (preview: deployment readiness, taught in ch46).
- **Running-example hooks:** all four in 45.11; battery station in 45.02, 45.03 and 45.10 (certifying a wheeled humanoid for a car plant's high-voltage hall, `[inference]`); dinner and kitchen reset in 45.05, 45.07 and 45.08 (a home humanoid as a consumer product, its AI rules, and who is liable if a guest has an allergic reaction, `[inference]`); greenhouse in 45.09 and 45.11 (agricultural rules not yet researched: an explicit gap).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - ISO/CD 25785-1 — ISO — undated — https://www.iso.org/standard/91469.html — standard (status "under development"; via search, page not opened)
  - ISO 10218-1:2025 — ISO — 2025 — https://www.iso.org/standard/73933.html — standard (via search; page returned 403)
  - ISO 10218-2:2025 — ISO — 2025 — https://www.iso.org/standard/73934.html — standard (via search; page returned 403)
  - Humanoid Robots and Embodied Intelligence Standards System (2026) — MIIT/TC08, reported by SESEC — 2026-02-28 — https://sesec.eu/2026/04/01/chinas-first-standards-system-for-humanoid-robots-and-embodied-intelligence/ — press (secondary; MIIT original needed)
  - Machinery Regulation 2023/1230 and the AI omnibus — Eurogip — 2026 — https://eurogip.fr/en/machinery-regulation-2023-1230-what-the-digital-omnibus-on-ai-changes/ — press (cites Reg. 2026/1744; EUR-Lex needed)
  - Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) — EU Parliament and Council — 2026-07-08 — https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/reg/2026/1744/oj/eng — standard (from the ch24 scan; not opened in the ch45 scan)
  - Digit 5 announcement — Agility Robotics — 2026-09-15 — https://www.agilityrobotics.com/content/agility-unveils-digit-5-humanoid-robot-built-for-cooperatively-safe-work-at-scale — company-claim
  - UL 3300 — UL Solutions — Edition 1, 2024 — https://www.ul.com/news/ul-3300-outline-investigation-helps-advance-safety-consumer-service-and-education-robots — standard (OSHA NRTL listing 2025-12-31, search only)
  - Directive (EU) 2024/2853 (product liability) — EU — transposition 2026-12-09 — no URL — standard (unconfirmed: EUR-Lex needed)
  - Machinery Regulation (EU) 2023/1230; ISO/TS 15066; ISO 13482; ANSI/A3 R15.08 — no date — no URL — (seed lead, verify)
- **Notes:** Every standard and law is stated with its status at the build date, taken from the issuing body (ISO, IEC, A3, UL, EUR-Lex, MIIT); ISO pages returned 403, so 10218 and 25785-1 rest on catalogue listings found by search and ISO 13482's revision is unconfirmed. Gaps: EUR-Lex originals of Regulation 2023/1230, Regulation 2026/1744 and Directive 2024/2853; the MIIT original; Japan, Korea and India; OSHA's field-evaluation primary; agricultural and high-voltage work rules; certification costs and timelines. "First certified humanoid" claims stay attributed; standards content is paraphrased, never reproduced.

### Changes from seed (P9)
- ch43: split "success rates and statistics" into 43.02 (success rates and uncertainty) and 43.03 (sample sizes and proving rare failures); split "real-world protocols" into 43.04 (A/B tests, blinding and sequential testing), 43.06 (writing a protocol and who judges success) and 43.07 (distributed, blind and online arenas) — the scan finds real-world evaluation has become shared infrastructure and TRI's A/B method is the main statistics source, and readiness hinges on proving rare failures.
- ch43: merged "simulation benchmarks and how well they correlate with reality" and "world-model-based evaluation" into 43.05, recapping ch27–ch29 and ch39 and owning only robustness suites and which tier may decide what — ch29 owns correlation metrics and ch39 owns world-model evaluation, per the Part brief and the P5 and P7 change logs.
- ch43: widened "intervention metrics" into 43.10 (interventions, assists and safety events) and added 43.11 (reading evaluation claims, and the metric catalogue built for Appendix C) — the Part brief asks for metrics that lift into Appendix C, and the scan shows counting rules (Helix 2.5's) change headline results.
- ch44: added 44.01 (why learned robots are hard to make safe: risk, defence in depth and the safety case) and 44.12 (the safety case per running example); split "safety layers around learned policies" into 44.05 (runtime monitors and safety filters) and 44.06 (certified fallbacks, safe stops and the certification gap) — the scan reports a 2026 wave of filters for VLAs and no certification path for them.
- ch44: drew boundaries — safety hardware and SafeFall's mechanism stay in ch10, the safety-layer slot in ch16, safe exploration during training in ch34, world-model verification and uncertainty in ch39, data-protection law in ch24, the standards themselves in ch45, operational escalation in ch47 — teach once; those chapters already preview ch44.
- ch45: merged "industrial robots (ISO 10218-1/-2)" and "collaborative operation (ISO/TS 15066)" into 45.02 — the scan reports that the 2025 edition folds ISO/TS 15066 content into Part 1 (per ISO's listing, unconfirmed); paired ISO 13482 with UL 3300 in 45.05 — the scan adds UL 3300.
- ch45: renamed "EU AI Act (status at build date)" to 45.07 (the EU AI Act and the Digital Omnibus); grouped "US, China, Japan, Korea and India" as 45.09 with Japan, Korea and India as explicit research requests; added 45.11 (a dated status table and the running examples) — the scan finds Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 moving the high-risk dates, and Japan, Korea and India were not scanned.
- No PROPOSED chapter-level changes.

## P10 · Deployment

### ch46 · Deployment readiness
- **Purpose:** How a team decides that a humanoid, a task and a site are ready for each other, and what must be in place on day one. The chapter covers what readiness means and the stages from lab to scaled fleet; choosing and scoping the task against the site's takt time; assessing the site; connecting to the site's systems (or a household's); setting readiness criteria and acceptance tests before the pilot; designing remote assistance; planning uptime, charging and service; preparing people; and making the business case. It reads the published deployments as readiness lessons and ends with a readiness checklist for each running example. ch46 owns readiness dimensions, the deployment ladder, task scoping and takt time, site assessment, integration with site systems, readiness criteria, pilot exit criteria and site acceptance tests, remote assistance as a deployment capability, uptime and service planning (fleet sizing, service-level agreements), operator training and change management, and deployment economics (purchase, leasing, robots-as-a-service, per-hour pricing, total cost of ownership, payback). ch43 owns evaluation methodology; ch44 the safety case; ch45 certification; ch47 day-to-day operations, remote-assistance staffing and field service; ch51 the sector-by-sector landscape.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch02 (when a general-purpose robot beats a specialist; evidence from deployments so far); ch03 (the robots-as-a-service milestone at GXO); ch04 (the reference humanoid and its wheeled, rail and soft home variants); ch08 (how sites degrade sensors); ch09 (runtime vs shift length; recharging methods; networks and offline operation; ingress protection); ch10 (MTBF, MTTR and availability; design for maintainability and field-replaceable units; lessons from deployments); ch16 (fallback behaviours; tool calls as reasoner actions; remote assistance previewed); ch19 (takeover interfaces; remote expert modes and claimed no-go zones); ch20 (process signals as labels); ch24 (privacy and consent in homes); ch33 (readiness criteria previewed); ch39 (readiness gates previewed); ch43 (evaluation vs acceptance; sample sizes and the zero-failure bound; cycle time and throughput; intervention metrics; readiness evaluation plans); ch44 (speed and separation; fall management; privacy by design; cybersecurity; the safety case per running example); ch45 (standards status; the certification path; what a buyer should ask a maker to show).
- **Estimated length:** about 13,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **46.01 · What deployment readiness means.** Define readiness as a property of the robot, the task, the site, the people and the business case together, and lay out the stages from lab to scaled fleet. *Concepts:* deployment readiness as a joint property of robot, task, site, people and economics (owned here; previewed in ch01, ch02, ch03, ch10 and ch45; `[inference]` framing, reasoning shown); the readiness dimensions used through the chapter: technical performance, safety and compliance, integration, operations, people and economics (owned here; `[inference]`); the deployment ladder: lab demonstration, on-site pilot, limited production, scaled fleet, repeat orders (owned here; `[inference]` framing; ch51 reuses it as an evidence ladder); technology readiness levels as an analogue from other industries (source needed, else cut); recap: evaluation vs acceptance vs re-validation (ch43); recap: the certification path (ch45); why readiness is judged per site, not per robot model (`[inference]`, reasoning shown; the "same brain, three deployments" thread).
  - **46.02 · Choosing and scoping the task.** Show how to pick a first task a humanoid can do well enough, and draw its edges. *Concepts:* task-fit criteria: repetitive, measurable, hard on people, tolerant of a slower first version, with bounded failure consequences (owned here; `[inference]` until sourced); recap: when a general-purpose robot beats a specialist (ch02); the scope boundary: what the robot does, what it hands off, and which exceptions it handles vs escalates (owned here); "done means" criteria as the specification (recap: readiness evaluation plans, ch43); takt time, the pace that demand sets for a station, vs the robot's cycle time (owned here; previewed in ch43; equation: takt time equals available working time divided by required units; source needed; numbers marked `nofact`); recap: cycle time and throughput (ch43); published first tasks as attributed examples: sheet-metal loading at BMW Spartanburg (Figure, company claim), tote handling at GXO (Agility, company claim), production tasks at BMW Leipzig (Hexagon, company claim; tasks to be confirmed from the release).
  - **46.03 · Site assessment.** Show what to survey at a site before a robot arrives. *Concepts:* the site survey (owned here; source needed): floors, slopes, steps and stairs, aisle and doorway widths, lighting, temperature, humidity and dust; recap: how sites degrade sensors (ch08) and ingress protection (ch09); network coverage and dead zones (recap: Wi-Fi, private 5G and offline operation, ch09); where charging or battery swapping fits in the layout (recap: recharging methods, ch09); people flow, shared zones and places where a fall is unacceptable (recap: speed and separation monitoring and fall management, ch44); choosing the body variant by site, legged, wheeled or rail (recap: ch04 and ch02); the home survey in the first week: mapping rooms, stairs, pets and children, and where this household keeps things (owned here; `[inference]`: no published home-readiness material, an explicit gap).
  - **46.04 · Connecting to the site's systems.** Show how the robot takes work from, and reports results to, the systems that run a factory, a warehouse or a home. *Concepts:* manufacturing execution systems (MES), warehouse management and execution systems (WMS, WES) and enterprise resource planning (ERP), defined (owned here; sources needed); the integration contract: work orders and instructions in; completions, quality records and exceptions out (owned here; `[inference]` until sourced); traceability records such as torque and angle per bolt (recap: process signals as labels, ch20); a fleet platform that links to WMS, WES and MES (Agility Arc, per the Digit 5 release, company claim; preview: fleet management software, taught in ch47); home ecosystems: appliances, calendars and messaging (gap: sources needed); online ordering with payment approval, where the reasoner builds an order and a person approves the spend (owned here; recap: tool calls as reasoner actions, ch16; agentic grocery-checkout sources needed); recap: cybersecurity of network connections (ch44).
  - **46.05 · Readiness criteria, pilot exit criteria and acceptance tests.** Show how a team sets the pass marks before the pilot and proves them at the site. *Concepts:* readiness criteria as thresholds agreed before the pilot starts (owned here; previewed in ch33 and ch39); pilot design: duration, shifts, volume and what the pilot must show (owned here; `[inference]` until sourced); pilot exit criteria and the go, extend or stop decision (owned here); factory acceptance test at the maker vs site acceptance test at the customer (owned here; previewed in ch43 and ch45; source needed); recap: sample sizes and the zero-failure bound (ch43); recap: the safety case (ch44); off-site application centres that train and validate tasks before the line (Boston Dynamics' Robotics Metaplant Application Center, company claim); the pilot-to-production decision as published: Figure 02's run at BMW Spartanburg and BMW's move to Figure 03 (company claims with BMW confirmation; pilot length reported as 10 or 11 months, sources differ); ISO 25785-1 as a draft that future criteria may cite (status only; recap: ch45).
  - **46.06 · Remote assistance and teleoperation fallback.** Show why a deployed humanoid needs a human it can call, and what that capability must include. *Concepts:* remote assistance as a deployment capability (owned here; previewed in ch09, ch16 and ch19); three modes: approval (a person confirms a decision), guidance (a person advises while the robot acts) and takeover (a person drives the robot) (owned here; `[inference]` taxonomy until sourced); recap: takeover interfaces and remote expert modes as data (ch19); recap: fallback behaviours and graceful degradation (ch16); what remote assistance needs: network latency and coverage, a trained operator on call, and a safe hold while waiting (recap: ch09 and ch16; `[inference]`); scheduled vs on-demand assistance (1X NEO's Expert Mode, company claim); privacy and consent when a remote person sees inside a home (recap: ch24 and ch44); the assist budget as a readiness criterion (the kitchen reset's "at most one remote assist"; recap: intervention metrics, ch43); preview: staffing remote assistance at scale (taught in ch47).
  - **46.07 · Uptime, charging and service planning.** Show how to plan, before launch, the hours a robot will actually be available. *Concepts:* recap: MTBF, MTTR and availability (ch10); recap: runtime vs shift length and recharging methods (ch09); the charging plan: matching charging, swapping or fast charging to shift patterns and breaks (owned here; Digit 5's charging design, company claim); fleet sizing for coverage: how many robots keep a set number of stations staffed, given availability and charging time (owned here; equation derived from availability; numbers marked `nofact`); the service-level agreement: promised uptime, response and repair times, spares on site (owned here; source needed); recap: design for maintainability and field-replaceable units (ch10); preview: field service operations (taught in ch47).
  - **46.08 · People: operator training and change management.** Show what the people around the robot need before launch. *Concepts:* the roles a deployment creates: site lead, robot technician, remote operator, safety officer, integration engineer (owned here; sources needed; detailed in People, tools and costs); training co-workers to work beside, stop and report on the robot (owned here; sources needed); change management: communicating with staff, redesigning jobs and consulting worker representatives where required (owned here; gap: no sources yet); ergonomics as a motive, such as tasks done in cumbersome protective gear (`[inference]` until sourced from BMW or Hexagon); households as operators: onboarding, setting no-go rooms and teaching preferences (owned here; `[inference]`; recap: claimed no-go zones, ch19).
  - **46.09 · Economics: buying, leasing, robots-as-a-service and payback.** Teach the business case from first principles, and say how little is public. *Concepts:* capital vs operating expense (owned here); purchase, leasing, subscription and robots-as-a-service, where the buyer pays for use and the maker keeps the robot (owned here; recap: the RaaS milestone at GXO, ch03); per-hour and per-task pricing (owned here; the widely repeated "$25 per robot-hour" has no primary source and is not used); published prices: 1X NEO at $20,000 or $499 a month (company claim); milestone-conditional multi-year orders (Agility reports over $300M for Digit 5, company claim); total cost of ownership: price plus integration, site changes, training, service, energy, remote assistance and downtime (owned here; equation in plain words; `[inference]` until sourced); payback period (owned here; equation; numbers marked `nofact`); cost per robot-hour vs the fully loaded cost of a worker-hour (owned here; labour-cost primary needed); shift economics: more working hours per day spread fixed costs (owned here; `[inference]`, reasoning shown); the comparison with specialist machines and fixed automation (recap: ch02).
  - **46.10 · What published deployments teach about readiness.** Read each public deployment for what it shows about readiness, attributed, and say what is missing. *Concepts:* (aside) case studies as readiness lessons, each with its rung on the deployment ladder, task, published metrics and evidence tag: Figure at BMW Spartanburg (Figure 02 to Figure 03; company claims with BMW confirmation; fleet size and price not published); Hexagon AEON at BMW Leipzig (test from December 2025, production tasks from June 2026, full deployment targeted for end-2026; no metrics; company claim); Boston Dynamics Atlas with Hyundai (application centre opened 2026-09-21; the 25,000-unit and 30,000-a-year figures are plans; Hyundai's stated real deployment from 2028); Agility Digit at GXO and other sites (fleet hours and a tote milestone; company claims); Apptronik with Mercedes-Benz (no primary document: gap); 1X NEO in homes (pre-orders; no verified delivery: gap); what a buyer should ask for that no maker yet publishes: uptime, interventions per hour, cost per task (`[inference]`; recap: ch10 and ch43); preview: deployment by sector (taught in ch51).
  - **46.11 · A readiness checklist for each running example.** Turn the chapter into one checklist per task. *Concepts:* (aside) per task, a checklist across the readiness dimensions of 46.01: scope and "done means", site survey, integration, acceptance tests, remote assistance, uptime and service, people, economics, safety case and certification status (`[inference]`, reasoning shown, numbers marked `nofact`; recap: readiness evaluation plans, ch43; the safety case per running example, ch44; standards per running example, ch45): dinner for five (ordering and payment approval; the allergen hard constraint; remote assistance during an evening peak); kitchen reset (the first week in a new home; at most one remote assist; privacy of remote viewing); battery station (MES work instructions and torque records; takt time; live high voltage; acceptance on dummy modules before live packs; the operator ratio); greenhouse (shift economics against a specialist harvester; charging without blocking rows; acceptance spread across a season).
- **Running-example hooks:** all four in 46.11; battery station in 46.02 (cycle time vs takt time), 46.03 (the wheeled variant in a battery hall), 46.04 (work instructions from the MES; torque and angle traceability) and 46.05 (acceptance on unpowered dummy modules before live packs, `[inference]`); dinner for five in 46.04 (ordering with payment approval) and 46.06 (approval as the simplest mode of remote assistance); kitchen reset in 46.03 and 46.08 (the first week in a new home; household onboarding) and 46.06 (the one-assist budget; privacy of remote viewing); greenhouse in 46.07 (recharging without blocking the row; full-shift runtime) and 46.09 (shift economics against a specialist machine).

Gaps: home-readiness material, a Mercedes–Apptronik primary, any verified NEO delivery, MES/WMS, acceptance-test (FAT/SAT), takt-time, service-level-agreement, change-management and labour-cost primaries, and prices or leases beyond 1X. People, tools and costs: roles from 46.08, fleet platforms and site systems as tools, and the only public figures (1X's prices and Agility's order value, both company claims).

### ch47 · Running a fleet
- **Purpose:** How a fleet of deployed humanoids is run day to day. The chapter covers the operations loop and who runs it; fleet management software; monitoring and observability; what to record for operations, and with what privacy; staffing remote assistance as a queueing problem; incidents and escalation; how over-the-air updates physically reach robots and install; spares, repair and field service; and the operating KPIs, built so they lift into Appendix C beside ch43's evaluation metrics. ch47 owns fleet operations, fleet management software, observability, operational records and their retention and access rules, remote-assistance staffing, operational escalation, the mechanics and operations of over-the-air (OTA) updates, field service and spares logistics, and fleet KPIs. ch46 owns readiness and remote assistance as a capability; ch43 owns evaluation and intervention metrics; ch44 owns incident response as safety engineering; ch48 owns logging triggers and the flywheel; ch49 owns release decisions (regression gates, shadow mode, canaries, staged rollouts, rollback policy and re-certification).
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch05 (wear and failure signals; fleet monitoring previewed); ch09 (networks; batteries; recharging; OTA previewed); ch10 (MTBF, MTTR and availability; preventive, predictive and corrective maintenance; design for maintainability and field-replaceable units; lessons from deployments; fleet KPIs and field service previewed); ch16 (logging at the architecture level; middleware; fallback behaviours; fleet observability previewed); ch19 (takeover interfaces; remote expert modes); ch20 (fleet logs as a data source; interventions; privacy of fleet logs previewed); ch23 (storage; retention and deletion; lineage); ch24 (automated quality checks; redaction; consent; data-protection law); ch33 (the artefact bundle and model export; OTA previewed); ch43 (cycle time and throughput; intervention metrics; the metric catalogue); ch44 (the incident response cycle; signed updates and secure design; privacy by design; escalation previewed); ch45 (the ISO 25785-1 draft; product liability and updates); ch46 (readiness criteria; remote-assistance modes and the assist budget; integration with site systems; takt time; service-level agreements; fleet sizing).
- **Estimated length:** about 12,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **47.01 · What running a fleet means.** Show the daily loop that keeps deployed robots working, and who runs it. *Concepts:* the fleet, robots run by one operator across one or many sites (owned here), and fleet operations (owned here); the operations loop: monitor, assist, respond, update, repair, report (owned here; `[inference]` framing); the operations centre and on-site teams (owned here; sources needed); recap: readiness and the service-level agreement (ch46); one robot in each of many homes vs many robots at one plant, and what that changes (`[inference]`, reasoning shown).
  - **47.02 · Fleet management software.** Show the software that tracks, assigns and coordinates robots. *Concepts:* fleet management software (owned here): robot inventory and health, task assignment, maps and zones, charging schedules and links to site systems (recap: integration with MES, WMS and WES, ch46); Agility Arc as an attributed example (its original 2024 feature set, unconfirmed; the KPIs and site-system links named in the Digit 5 release, company claim); fleet managers for autonomous mobile robots (AMRs) as the mature analogue (primary source needed); mixed fleets and interoperability between makers (gap: sources needed); what a humanoid fleet adds to an AMR fleet: manipulation outcomes, falls and remote assistance (`[inference]`).
  - **47.03 · Monitoring and observability.** Show how operators know each robot's state now, and spot trouble before it stops work. *Concepts:* monitoring vs observability (owned here; source needed); three kinds of signal, metrics, events and traces (owned here; source needed); robot health telemetry: battery, joint temperatures, motor-current trends, error counts and localisation quality (recap: wear signals, ch05; logging at the architecture level, ch16); dashboards, alerts and alert fatigue (owned here; `[inference]` until sourced); fleet-wide anomaly detection by comparing a robot with its peers (`[inference]` until sourced); predictive maintenance triggered by telemetry (recap: maintenance types, ch10); recap: automated quality checks (ch24).
  - **47.04 · What to record, when, and with what privacy.** Draw the line between recording for operations and recording for learning, and set privacy rules for operations. *Concepts:* recap: fleet logs as a data source (ch20); recap: storage, retention and deletion (ch23); operational records (owned here): health metrics, task outcomes, assists, incidents, software versions and traceability records; the event recorder, a rolling buffer kept on the robot and saved when an incident occurs (owned here; `[inference]` until sourced); retention set by purpose, such as short for raw video, longer for incident evidence and longest for traceability records (owned here; `[inference]`, any durations marked `nofact`); access control: who may see which records and live views (owned here; recap: privacy by design, ch44; redaction and data-protection law, ch24); homes vs factories (`[inference]`); 1X's privacy statements for NEO (company claim; FAQ not yet read); preview: logging triggers that select data for retraining (taught in ch48).
  - **47.05 · Remote assistance at scale: operator-to-robot ratios as a queueing problem.** Derive from first principles how many remote operators a fleet needs, and say why no public ratio can be trusted yet. *Concepts:* recap: remote-assistance modes and the assist budget (ch46); recap: interventions per hour (ch43); the queueing model of an assistance centre, where requests arrive at a rate, each takes a handling time and operators serve them (owned here; source needed); offered load and operator utilisation (owned here; equation: load in operator-hours per hour equals robots times assist requests per robot-hour times handling time); Little's law, linking queue length, arrival rate and waiting time (owned here; equation; source needed); why waiting time climbs steeply as utilisation nears 100% (`[inference]` until sourced; worked numbers marked `nofact`); the operator-to-robot ratio as an output of assist rate and handling time, not a fixed number (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); demand peaks, such as many homes cooking dinner at once (`[inference]`); scheduled sessions vs on-demand help (1X Expert Mode, company claim); the evidence gap: published ratios come only from vendor blogs, which are excluded; preview: assists falling as the flywheel improves the policy (taught in ch48).
  - **47.06 · Incidents and escalation.** Show how a fleet handles problems in operation, from a stuck robot to a safety event. *Concepts:* recap: the incident response cycle, incidents and near misses (ch44); severity classes for operational events (owned here; `[inference]` until sourced); the escalation ladder: the robot recovers itself, a remote operator assists, on-site staff attend, engineering investigates, safety leads take over (owned here; previewed in ch44; `[inference]` until sourced); on-call rotas and response-time targets (owned here; recap: the service-level agreement, ch46); the fleet-wide hold, pausing every robot that shares a fault or software version (owned here; `[inference]`, reasoning shown); mean time between incidents as a fleet KPI (named in Agility's Digit 5 release, company claim; distinct from ch43's mean time between interventions); safety requirements bearing on remote operation in the ISO 25785-1 draft (status only; recap: ch45); reporting duties to authorities (recap only if ch45 covers them; otherwise a research request).
  - **47.07 · Over-the-air updates: mechanics and operations.** Show how new software and models reach robots in the field and install safely, leaving the decision of what to release, and to whom, to ch49. *Concepts:* what gets updated: firmware, operating system, the policy model and its artefact bundle, maps and configuration (owned here; recap: the artefact bundle and model export, ch33); the update pipeline: package, sign, distribute, install, verify, report (owned here; previewed in ch09 and ch44; recap: signed updates and secure design, ch44); installing only in a safe state, such as docked or between shifts (owned here; `[inference]` until sourced); dual-partition installs that fall back to the previous version if the new one fails to start (owned here; source needed); bandwidth and download windows for multi-gigabyte models (recap: networks, ch09; `[inference]`); the fleet version inventory and configuration drift, knowing exactly what runs on each robot (owned here; recap: lineage, ch23); automotive analogues for update mechanics, UN Regulation No. 156 and ISO 24089 (texts not yet opened; carrying them to humanoids is `[inference]`); updates and liability (recap: ch45); preview: regression gates, shadow mode, canaries, staged rollouts, rollback decisions and re-certification (taught in ch49).
  - **47.08 · Spares, repair and field service.** Show how broken robots get fixed fast enough to keep the uptime promise. *Concepts:* recap: design for maintainability, field-replaceable units and preventive, predictive and corrective maintenance (ch10); field service models: on-site technician, swap-and-ship to a depot, and remote diagnosis first (owned here; previewed in ch10; sources needed); spares stocking from failure rate and replenishment time (owned here; equation: expected failures during the replenishment time; numbers marked `nofact`); repair turnaround and its effect on availability (recap: MTTR and availability, ch10); battery logistics: swaps, spare packs and battery health (recap: ch09; Digit 5's battery design, company claim); learning from field failures, such as the forearm as the top failure point Figure reported at BMW (recap: ch10; company claim); feeding field failures back into design (`[inference]`).
  - **47.09 · Fleet KPIs: the operating scorecard.** Build the scorecard a fleet is run by, with rows that lift into Appendix C without repeating ch43. *Concepts:* the KPI tree, from business outcomes (cost per task, output) down to robot measures (owned here; `[inference]` framing); KPIs owned here: utilisation (hours working over hours available), assists per robot-hour across the fleet, mean time between incidents, update success rate, cost per task and cost per robot-hour (owned here; previewed in ch10 and ch43; equations; sources needed beyond Agility); KPIs recapped with a cross-reference: availability, MTBF and MTTR (ch10); task success, cycle time, throughput, interventions per hour and mean time between interventions (ch43); normalising by robot-hours, and why a fleet average can hide a failing site (owned here; `[inference]` until sourced); published fleet figures, attributed: Agility's 65,000+ operating hours, one GXO site past 100,000 totes at about 98% accuracy, and the KPIs Agility names for Arc (company claims); what no maker publishes yet, such as uptime, intervention rates and cost per task (scan finding); (aside) the fleet KPI table for Appendix C: definition, formula, where measured, public values or "not published", and the chapter that defines each.
  - **47.10 · Running the fleet for each running example.** Apply the chapter to the four tasks. *Concepts:* (aside) per task: what is monitored, who assists and how often, what escalates, how updates land and which spares are kept (`[inference]`, reasoning shown, numbers marked `nofact`): dinner for five (payment-approval requests and dinner-time assist peaks across many homes; guests' health information in records); kitchen reset (assists expected to fall over the first weeks; privacy of remote viewing); battery station (plant-system links, response times bounded by takt time, the operator ratio per line, updates in shift breaks; preview: the engineering-change rollout, taught in ch49); greenhouse (shift-long runtime, heat and humidity telemetry, harvest-season peaks in spares and staffing).
- **Running-example hooks:** all four in 47.10; dinner for five in 47.05 (evening peaks in assist requests across many homes; payment approvals as short assists) and 47.04 (guests' allergies in operational records; preview ch48); kitchen reset in 47.04 (who may view a family's kitchen) and 47.05 (the one-assist budget at fleet scale); battery station in 47.06 (a stopped robot beside a live pack escalates straight to on-site staff, `[inference]`), 47.07 (updates installed only while docked between shifts, `[inference]`) and 47.09 (cost per pack and first-pass rate on the scorecard); greenhouse in 47.03 (heat and humidity telemetry) and 47.08 (spares and service in a remote greenhouse at harvest peak).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Agility unveils Digit 5 — Agility Robotics — 2026-09-15 — https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/agility-unveils-digit-5-humanoid-robot-built-for-cooperatively-safe-work-at-scale-302878540.html — company-claim
  - Agility Arc launch — Agility Robotics via Robotics Tomorrow — 2024-03-11 (date from URL) — https://www.roboticstomorrow.com/news/2024/03/11/agility-robotics-brings-operational-visibility-to-deployment-of-digit%E2%93%87-fleets-with-the-launch-of-agility-arc/22211/ — company-claim (unconfirmed: not opened)
  - Digit Moves Over 100,000 Totes in Commercial Deployment — Agility Robotics — 2025-11-20 — https://www.agilityrobotics.com/content/digit-moves-over-100k-totes — company-claim
  - NEO Home Robot — 1X — undated — https://www.1x.tech/neo — company-claim
  - F.02 Contributed to the Production of 30,000 Cars at BMW — Figure — 2025-11-19 — https://www.figure.ai/news/production-at-bmw — company-claim

  - UN Regulation No. 156 (E/ECE/TRANS/505/Rev.3/Add.155) — UNECE — 2021-03-04 — https://unece.org/transport/documents/2021/03/standards/un-regulation-no-156-software-update-and-software-update — standard (unconfirmed: fetch 403, text not opened; from the ch49 scan)

Gaps: primary operator-to-robot ratios (vendor figures such as "1:5 to 1:10" are rejected); AMR fleet-operations, RaaS and ROI primaries; the R156 and ISO 24089 texts; observability, queueing (Little's law), field-service and spares sources; the Arc launch article; any published uptime or intervention rate for a humanoid fleet. ### Changes from seed (P10)
- ch46: added 46.01 (what readiness means: readiness dimensions and the deployment ladder) — nine earlier chapters (ch01, ch02, ch03, ch10, ch33, ch39, ch42, ch43, ch45) preview readiness, readiness criteria or acceptance tests to ch46, the 46.11 checklists need fixed dimensions, and ch51 can reuse the ladder as its evidence ladder.
- ch46: placed takt time in 46.02 (choosing and scoping the task) — ch43 previews takt time to ch46, and the battery station's "cycle time met" needs it.
- ch46: widened integration (46.04) to include online ordering with payment approval — the P10 running-example thread names it.
- ch46: renamed "readiness criteria and acceptance tests" to 46.05 and added pilot design, pilot exit criteria, factory vs site acceptance and off-site application centres — the scan suggests acceptance tests built from pilot exit criteria and reports Boston Dynamics' application centre.
- ch46: recast "uptime, charging and maintenance plans" as 46.07 (planning only: charging plans, fleet sizing, service-level agreements), recapping ch09 and ch10 — ch10 owns reliability measures and design for maintainability; field-service execution belongs to ch47.
- ch46: reframed "case studies" as 46.10 (readiness lessons, each placed on the ladder with its evidence tag) — ch51 owns the sector landscape and ch02 already summarises deployment evidence; teach once.
- ch46 vs ch47 (boundary recorded): ch46 owns remote assistance as a capability (modes, needs, the assist budget), uptime planning and service-level agreements; ch47 owns staffing remote assistance, operating to the agreement, and field service — the seed listed remote assistance and maintenance in both chapters.
- ch47: added 47.01 (what running a fleet means) and 47.10 (fleet operations for each running example) — framing and synthesis, matching earlier Parts.
- ch47: renamed "what to log, when, and with what privacy" to 47.04 and recast it as operational records — boundary with ch48 (recorded): ch47 owns recording for operations (the fleet's state now, incident reconstruction, traceability, retention and access rules); ch48 owns logging triggers that select data for learning, failure mining and the flywheel; ch20 keeps fleet logs as a data source, ch23 retention mechanics, ch24 redaction and law, ch44 privacy by design.
- ch47: recast "remote assistance at scale" as 47.05 (a queueing problem) — the scan finds no primary operator-to-robot ratios, so the chapter teaches how to derive one instead of quoting one.
- ch47: narrowed "incidents and escalation" to operational escalation (47.06), recapping ch44's incident response cycle — P9 assigned operational escalation to ch47.
- ch47: retitled "over-the-air updates" as 47.07 (mechanics and operations) — boundary with ch49 (recorded): ch47 owns how an update physically reaches and installs on a robot (packaging, signing, delivery, safe-state installation, verification, device-level fallback after a failed install, the version inventory); ch49 owns what is released and to whom (regression gates, shadow mode, canaries, staged rollouts, fleet rollback decisions, re-certification, and the update-management and approval side of UN R156); ch09, ch33 and ch44 already preview OTA to ch47 and release engineering to ch49.
- ch47: rebuilt KPIs as 47.09 (the operating scorecard), owning only fleet KPIs and cross-referencing ch10 and ch43 — the Part brief asks for rows that lift into Appendix C without duplicating ch43.
