## P11 · After deployment

### ch48 · The data flywheel
- **Purpose:** How a deployed fleet turns its own experience into better models. The chapter covers what a data flywheel is and why it can compound or stall; which moments a robot keeps for learning; how failures and rare edge cases are mined; how interventions, failed attempts and automatic labels re-enter training; how to choose what to retrain on; how to run the loop without exposing a household; how a slow, seasonal loop differs; and what the loop costs. ch48 owns the data flywheel as a closed loop, logging triggers that select data for learning, failure mining, assists per robot-hour as a flywheel health metric, hindsight relabelling, auto-labelling at the level of the loop and how label noise compounds, active data collection and retraining-data priority, privacy-preserving learning (on-device and federated), storing household health information and taste memory, the seasonal flywheel and flywheel economics. ch20 owns fleet logs, interventions, DAgger and free labels as data sources; ch24 owns VLM auto-labelling, redaction and data-protection law; ch34 owns Recap, success detectors and VLM judges; ch38 owns the world-model co-improvement loop; ch44 owns privacy by design; ch47 owns operational records; ch49 owns retraining cadence, continual learning and release.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch11 (distribution shift; embeddings); ch13 (compounding errors); ch14 (off-policy learning and the replay buffer; offline RL; learning from preferences); ch17 (cost per useful hour; diversity vs volume); ch19 (takeover interfaces; remote expert modes); ch20 (autonomous rollouts; DAgger and interventions as noisy data; fleet logs; Learning While Deploying; process signals as labels); ch21 (size per hour; metadata); ch23 (the data pipeline; indexing and embedding search; storage tiers; lineage); ch24 (automated quality checks; VLM auto-labelling; outcome labels; gold sets; redaction; consent; data-protection law and special categories); ch26 (coverage and the long tail; the marginal value of data; data budgets); ch31 (conditioning on data-quality metadata); ch33 (adapters per variety); ch34 (corrections inside RL; Recap; success detectors; VLM judges and FailBench); ch38 (the world model and the policy improving together); ch39 (uncertainty; generated data); ch41 (the failure-to-data map; the failure scorecard); ch43 (intervention metrics); ch44 (privacy by design; health information previewed); ch46 (remote-assistance modes; the assist budget); ch47 (operational records and the event recorder; fleet KPIs; falling assists previewed).
- **Estimated length:** about 12,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **48.01 · What a data flywheel is.** Show the whole loop from a deployed robot back to a better model, and why it can speed up or stall. *Concepts:* the data flywheel (owned here; previewed in ch01, ch26, ch29 and ch38): deploy, record, select, label, train, evaluate, release, deploy again; open-loop data collection vs a closed loop fed by the robot's own deployment (owned here; `[inference]` framing, reasoning shown); why the loop can compound (the robot's own mistakes are exactly the data it lacks; recap: on-policy data, ch20, and compounding errors, ch13) and why it can stall (the same failures repeat, labels are wrong, nothing ships; `[inference]`); flywheel cycle time, the time from a field failure to a deployed fix (owned here; `[inference]`; preview: retraining cadence, taught in ch49); fleet-scale loops in published work (Learning While Deploying, preprint; π*0.6, tech report, results company-reported; recap: ch20 and ch34); recap: the world model and the policy improving together (ch38); where each step is taught (recap: fleet logs, ch20; preview: release, taught in ch49).
  - **48.02 · Logging triggers: deciding what to keep.** Show why a robot cannot keep everything, and how it chooses. *Concepts:* recap: size per hour (ch21), upload bandwidth and storage tiers (ch23), and operational records and the event recorder (ch47); the logging trigger (owned here; previewed in ch47): a rule that saves a window of data around a moment worth learning from; rule-based triggers: interventions, failed or aborted tasks, safety stops, process-signal rejects such as a refused torque reading (owned here; recap: process signals as labels, ch20; `[inference]` until sourced); learned triggers: low confidence, unfamiliar inputs, disagreement between models (owned here; recap: uncertainty, ch39; sources needed); a random baseline sample so the kept data does not show only failures (owned here; `[inference]`, reasoning shown); trigger precision and recall, and the selection bias every trigger creates (`[inference]`); equation: kept hours per day equal fleet hours times trigger rate times window length (owned here; numbers marked `nofact`); Tesla's "shadow mode" triggers (only secondary pages and a patent surfaced: not used until a primary is found).
  - **48.03 · Mining failures and the long tail.** Show how a team finds the rare cases that matter among millions of hours. *Concepts:* recap: coverage and the long tail (ch26) and the failure-to-data map (ch41); failure mining (owned here; previewed in ch23, ch26 and ch41): searching fleet logs for failures and rare situations by metadata, embeddings and detectors (recap: indexing and embedding search, ch23); rarity arithmetic, the exposure needed to see an event of a given rate at least once (owned here; equation derived from first principles; numbers marked `nofact`; recap: the zero-failure bound, ch43); a labelled analogue from autonomous driving, Waymo's WOD-E2E, mining 6.4 million miles for long-tail events reported as under 0.03% (denominator to read from the paper; preprint, company research); clustering failures into categories and counting them release over release (recap: the failure scorecard, ch41; `[inference]`); turning a mined failure into targeted collection, a simulated scenario or a regression test (`[inference]`; recap: simulators, ch27; preview: regression suites, taught in ch49).
  - **48.04 · Interventions and corrections in the loop.** Show how human help during deployment becomes training data at fleet scale. *Concepts:* recap: DAgger, interventions as noisy data and ROVE's filtering (ch20); recap: corrections inside RL and Recap (ch34); recap: remote expert modes and remote-assistance modes (ch19 and ch46); the fleet correction loop (owned here at the loop level): rollouts and interventions from many robots feed one shared policy, which is redeployed (Learning While Deploying: 16 robots, 8 tasks, 95% average success, per the authors; preprint); learning from deployment logs without new trial and error (recap: offline RL, ch14; previewed there to ch48); assists per robot-hour as the flywheel's health metric, falling as the policy improves (owned here; previewed in ch47; recap: intervention metrics, ch43); conflicting corrections from different operators or households (`[inference]`, reasoning shown).
  - **48.05 · Failures as data: hindsight relabelling and failure-aware learning.** Show how a failed attempt can still teach. *Concepts:* hindsight relabelling (owned here; previewed in ch20 and ch24): a failed attempt at one instruction is a successful demonstration of whatever the robot actually did; VLM-based hindsight relabelling (Learning More from Less: RL from Hindsight, about five times the sample efficiency, per the authors; preprint; recap: VLM auto-labelling, ch24); failures turned into preferences for continual improvement (FAR: Failure-Aware Retry, preprint; recap: learning from preferences, ch14); recap: conditioning on data-quality metadata so failed episodes become usable (π0.7, ch31); limits: relabels that describe nothing useful, and failures that are unsafe to repeat (`[inference]`).
  - **48.06 · Auto-labelling at fleet scale, and how label noise compounds.** Show the labels a flywheel attaches automatically, and what their errors do. *Concepts:* recap: success detectors, VLM judges and FailBench's finding that judges lean toward "success" when unsure (ch34); recap: VLM auto-labelling (ch24) and process signals as labels (ch20); the auto-labelling pipeline in the loop: outcome, subtask and language labels on every kept episode (owned here at the loop level; `[inference]` until sourced); label noise that compounds when a model trains on its own judge's verdicts (owned here; `[inference]`, reasoning shown, anchored in FailBench); equation: expected false "success" labels equal kept episodes times the failure share times the judge's false-positive rate (owned here; numbers marked `nofact`); human audit sampling of automatic labels (owned here; recap: gold sets and audits, ch24; `[inference]`).
  - **48.07 · Choosing what to retrain on.** Show how a team decides which data the next model sees. *Concepts:* recap: the marginal value of data and data budgets (ch26) and influence-based curation (ch24); active data collection, choosing what to collect or label next by uncertainty, novelty or expected effect (owned here; previewed in ch39; robot sources for 2025–26 needed: seed research target); priority by impact: how often a failure happens, how bad it is, and whether more data can fix it (owned here; `[inference]`, reasoning shown; recap: the failure-to-data map, ch41); dropping repeated easy successes (recap: deduplication, ch24); keeping old data in the mix so new data does not erase old skills (preview: experience replay, taught in ch49); lessons that transfer between sites vs lessons that stay local (the "same brain, three deployments" thread; `[inference]`; preview: global vs site-specific models, taught in ch49).
  - **48.08 · Privacy-preserving flywheels in homes.** Show how a home robot can learn from a household without exposing it. *Concepts:* recap: what robot data captures about people, redaction, consent and data-protection law (ch24); recap: privacy by design (ch44) and access rules for operational records (ch47); the home as the richest and most private data source (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); filtering and redacting on the robot before anything is uploaded (recap: ch24); on-device learning, where the model adapts inside the robot and nothing leaves (owned here; sources needed); federated learning, where robots train locally and share only model updates that a server averages (owned here; equation in plain words: a weighted average of local updates; original source needed); federated VLA training that keeps raw data local (ForgeVLA, benchmarks only, preprint); limits: what shared updates can still reveal, compute on the robot, and thin evidence in real homes (scan finding); storing household health information and taste memory, such as guests' allergies and the host's preferences (owned here; previewed in ch24 and ch44): what to keep, where, for how long, who may see it, and consent from guests who are not the account holder (`[inference]` until sourced; recap: special categories of personal data, ch24); a memory the household can inspect, correct and delete (`[inference]`).
  - **48.09 · Seasonal flywheels on farms.** Show how the loop changes when the data arrives once a year. *Concepts:* the slow flywheel, where the crop sets the loop's cycle time and some situations recur only once a season (owned here; `[inference]`, reasoning shown; recap: once-a-year situations as the long tail, ch26); bridging the gap between seasons: pooling across greenhouses, sites and growing calendars, simulation and generated data (recap: ch20 and ch39; `[inference]`); one small adapter per variety or season (recap: ch33); pre-season checks before the first harvest (preview: seasonal retraining, taught in ch49); no agricultural flywheel source in the scan (explicit research request).
  - **48.10 · The economics of the flywheel.** Teach what a loop costs and what it must return, and how little is public. *Concepts:* recap: cost per useful hour (ch17), storage tiers (ch23) and the marginal value of data (ch26); the cost of one turn of the loop: upload, storage, labelling, compute, evaluation and release (owned here; `[inference]`, reasoning shown); the value side: fewer assists, higher success, faster cycles and new tasks (recap: fleet KPIs, ch47); diminishing returns as easy failures are fixed and the remaining ones grow rarer (owned here; `[inference]`; recap: rarity arithmetic, 48.03); equation: the payback of a flywheel turn as value gained divided by its cost (owned here; numbers marked `nofact`); "data moat" claims, that the biggest fleet wins, as untested company claims (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); the evidence gap: no public flywheel cost figures (scan finding).
  - **48.11 · A flywheel for each running example.** Design the loop for each task. *Concepts:* (aside) per task: triggers, what is mined, labels, privacy rules, cycle time and the metric that shows the loop is working (`[inference]`, reasoning shown, numbers marked `nofact`): dinner for five (taste memory and returning guests' allergies stored under strict rules; label-reading misses as mined failures; "tastes good" has no automatic judge); kitchen reset (household corrections such as "the board never goes in the dishwasher" as data; assists falling over the first weeks; nothing leaves the home without consent; preview: household preference learning, taught in ch49); battery station (the fastest loop: torque and latch records as free labels, refused torque readings as triggers, dummy modules for new data; preview: the engineering-change rollout, taught in ch49); greenhouse (the slowest loop: seasonal data pooled across sites; preview: seasonal retraining, taught in ch49).
- **Running-example hooks:** all four in 48.11; dinner for five in 48.08 (storing taste memory and returning guests' allergies as health information) and 48.06 ("tastes good" cannot be judged automatically, so outcome labels exist only for checkable sub-steps, `[inference]`); kitchen reset in 48.04 (remote-assist takeovers in the first week as correction data; assists per robot-hour falling over weeks) and 48.08 (federated or on-device learning so a family's kitchen never leaves the home, `[inference]`); battery station in 48.02 (a refused torque reading as a logging trigger, `[inference]`) and 48.03 (a cross-threaded bolt as a mined rare failure, `[inference]`); greenhouse in 48.09 (once-a-year situations and a loop that turns once a season).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - pi*0.6: a VLA That Learns From Experience — Physical Intelligence — 2025-11-18 — arXiv 2511.14759 — tech-report (results company-reported)
  - Learning While Deploying — Wang et al. (Luo) — 2026-05-01 (rev. 2026-09-16) — arXiv 2605.00416 — preprint
  - Learning More from Less: RL from Hindsight — Xu et al. (Agrawal) — 2026-07-10 — arXiv 2607.09042 — preprint
  - FAR: Failure-Aware Retry — Hao et al. — 2026-07-01 — arXiv 2607.01111 — preprint
  - FailBench — Navasardyan et al. — 2026-09-03 — arXiv 2609.03611 — preprint
  - ROVE — Xiao et al. — 2026-06-15 — arXiv 2606.17011 — preprint
  - WOD-E2E — Xu et al. (Waymo) — 2025-10-30 — arXiv 2510.26125 — preprint, company-claim
  - ForgeVLA: Federated VLA Learning without Language Annotations — Zhou et al. — 2026-05-08 — arXiv 2605.07474 — preprint
  - Active learning and fleet learning for robots (2025–26) — no authors recorded — no date — no URL — (seed lead, verify)
- **Notes:** Core equations: kept hours from a trigger rate; rarity exposure; expected false "success" labels; federated averaging in plain words; flywheel payback. Gaps: any primary on Tesla's data engine (only secondary pages and patent US 12,195,021), robot active-learning sources, logging-trigger and auto-labelling pipeline sources, federated learning's original paper, health-data storage rules, agricultural flywheels, flywheel costs. The ch48 scan saw π0.7 only via secondary pages: cite the arXiv paper recorded under ch31. Fleet results come from lab fleets, not homes, and the chapter says so. People, tools and costs: data-engine roles (data engineer, labelling lead, ML engineer, privacy officer), indexing and labelling tools, and costs that are almost entirely unpublished.

### ch49 · Continual improvement and release engineering
- **Purpose:** How a team turns a better model into a safe change across a running fleet, again and again. The chapter covers what a release is; how often to retrain; how to keep learning in deployment without forgetting; when to run one global model or many site-specific ones, including a household's preferences; the regression suites and gates a release must pass; shadow mode, canaries and staged rollouts; rollback; safety re-validation and re-certification after an update; the release record and audit trail; how to measure improvement across releases; and the running examples' later twists. ch49 owns release engineering, retraining cadence, continual learning in deployment (the stability–plasticity trade-off, experience replay and regularisation across releases), global vs site-specific models and household preference learning, regression suites and evaluation gates as release practice, shadow mode, canary releases, staged rollouts, fleet rollback decisions, change-impact analysis with re-validation and re-certification (including the update-management and approval side of UN R156 as an analogue), release records, and release-over-release tracking. ch30 owns catastrophic forgetting; ch33 owns avoiding forgetting during one fine-tune; ch34 owns RL from deployment experience as a training method; ch43 owns evaluation methodology; ch45 owns certification; ch47 owns how an update installs on a robot; ch48 owns the data flywheel.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** yes.
- **Depends on:** ch12 (in-context and few-shot learning); ch13 (compounding errors); ch14 (the replay buffer; learning from preferences); ch23 (lineage, versioning and reproducibility); ch24 (consent; withdrawal and deletion); ch26 (co-training; mixture weights; a revised connector as a new coverage cell); ch30 (training stages; catastrophic forgetting and its equation; continual learning previewed); ch32 (memory for robot policies); ch33 (avoiding forgetting during fine-tuning; LoRA; few-shot adaptation; adaptation without weight updates; the artefact bundle; release engineering previewed); ch34 (RL fine-tuning of VLAs; Recap); ch39 (world-model-based evaluation); ch41 (the failure scorecard; regression suites previewed); ch43 (the evaluation ladder; evaluation vs acceptance vs re-validation; A/B tests and sequential testing; sample sizes and the zero-failure bound); ch44 (the safety case; certification gap; incident response; re-validation after a fix previewed; signed updates); ch45 (the certification path; the Machinery Regulation; the AI Act; product liability and updates; re-certification previewed); ch46 (readiness criteria; pilot exit criteria and acceptance tests); ch47 (OTA mechanics; device-level fallback; the version inventory; the fleet-wide hold; fleet KPIs; the engineering-change rollout previewed); ch48 (the data flywheel and its cycle time; mined failures; assists per robot-hour; federated learning; household health information and taste memory; the seasonal flywheel).
- **Estimated length:** about 12,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **49.01 · What a release is.** Show the step from a trained checkpoint to a version the fleet runs, and why it needs its own discipline. *Concepts:* release engineering for robots (owned here; previewed in ch33): turning a model into a tested, traceable, reversible change to a fleet; the release as the unit of change: model, artefact bundle, configuration and safety parameters together (owned here; recap: the artefact bundle, ch33); the release pipeline: candidate, gates, limited exposure, full rollout, monitoring (owned here; `[inference]` framing); recap: OTA mechanics (ch47) and the data flywheel (ch48); recap: evaluation vs acceptance vs re-validation (ch43); why a robot release is harder than a software release: physical consequences, closed-loop behaviour and certification (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); MLOps for robotics as a field without primary sources yet (seed research target; gap).
  - **49.02 · Retraining cadence.** Show what decides how often a fleet gets a new model. *Concepts:* retraining cadence (owned here; previewed in ch48): scheduled vs triggered retraining (owned here; `[inference]` until sourced); what sets the cadence: data arrival, training and evaluation cost, the burden of re-validation, and the season (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); continual post-training with frequent redeployment across a fleet (Learning While Deploying, preprint; recap: ch48); a full retrain from an earlier stage vs an incremental fine-tune of the deployed model (owned here; recap: training stages, ch30; `[inference]`); cadences per running example (`[inference]`; numbers marked `nofact`).
  - **49.03 · Continual learning and forgetting in deployment.** Show how a deployed model keeps learning without losing what it could already do. *Concepts:* recap: catastrophic forgetting and its equation (ch30) and avoiding forgetting during one fine-tune (ch33); continual learning, learning from a stream of new data and tasks across releases (owned here; previewed in ch30 and ch33); the stability–plasticity trade-off, holding old skills vs taking in new ones (owned here; CRL-VLA's advantage regularisation, on LIBERO, preprint); experience replay across releases (owned here at the continual-learning level; recap: the replay buffer, ch14, and co-training, ch26): Zhu et al. find replay mitigates forgetting on real-world data and beats joint multi-task training (preprint); RL fine-tuning for lifelong learning (LifeLong-RFT: "+22% over SFT, 20% data" as the scan records it, read the exact meaning from the paper; preprint; recap: RL fine-tuning of VLAs, ch34); recap: plain sequential LoRA with RL forgetting little ("Simple Recipe Works", ch33); measuring forgetting release over release with a retained-skills test set (`[inference]`; preview: regression suites, 49.05); limits: mostly small benchmarks (scan finding).
  - **49.04 · Global vs site-specific models, and household preferences.** Show when one model should serve every site and when a site needs its own. *Concepts:* one global model, site-specific fine-tunes, per-site adapters, and per-site prompts or memory as four options (owned here; recap: LoRA and few-shot adaptation, ch33; adaptation without weight updates, ch33; memory, ch32); trade-offs: the number of versions to evaluate and certify, transfer across sites, and the risk that a local fix breaks elsewhere (`[inference]`, reasoning shown; the "same brain, three deployments" thread); household preference learning (owned here; previewed in ch33 and ch48): turning stored preferences such as "the board never goes in the dishwasher" into behaviour, as a remembered rule, an instruction in the prompt or a fine-tune (`[inference]` until sourced; recap: in-context learning, ch12; preference capture and storage, ch48); folding site lessons back into the global model (`[inference]`; recap: federated learning, ch48).
  - **49.05 · Regression suites and evaluation gates.** Show how a release proves it did not break what already worked. *Concepts:* the regression, a new version failing something an earlier version did well (owned here); the regression suite (owned here; previewed in ch41 and ch43): fixed tasks and scenarios, including mined field failures (recap: failure mining, ch48), retained skills (49.03) and safety-critical checks that may never get worse (`[inference]`); the evaluation gate, pass criteria a candidate must meet before each wider exposure (owned here; previewed in ch43; recap: readiness and pilot exit criteria, ch46); gate tiers: offline checks, simulation, world-model evaluation, lab or dummy-hardware trials (recap: the evaluation ladder, ch43; world-model-based evaluation, ch39); comparing a candidate with the deployed version (recap: paired A/B tests and sample sizes, ch43); recap: the failure scorecard (ch41); the gate's trade-off between speed and safety (`[inference]`); no robotics-specific primary on regression practice (scan finding: an explicit gap).
  - **49.06 · Shadow mode, canaries and staged rollouts.** Show how a new version meets the real world a little at a time. *Concepts:* shadow mode, where the new model runs alongside the deployed one and its outputs are recorded but never executed (owned here; source needed); what shadow mode cannot measure on a robot: the consequences of its own actions, because the robot never follows them (owned here; `[inference]`, reasoning shown; recap: compounding errors, ch13); the canary release, a new version on a few robots first (owned here; source needed); the staged rollout in widening rings of robots, sites or customers (owned here; source needed); choosing who goes first: low-risk sites, dummy hardware, staff-attended shifts (`[inference]`); how long a canary must run to catch a regression of a given rate (owned here; equation derived from the zero-failure bound, recap ch43; numbers marked `nofact`); halt criteria and the fleet-wide hold (recap: ch47); no robotics primary for any of the three; Tesla's shadow mode unconfirmed; software-operations blogs excluded (scan finding: present as clearly labelled analogues only once a primary is found, otherwise `[inference]`).
  - **49.07 · Rollback.** Show how to undo a release, and what cannot be undone. *Concepts:* recap: device-level fallback after a failed install and the fleet-wide hold (ch47); the rollback decision, returning the fleet or one ring to the previous version (owned here; `[inference]` until sourced); rollback vs roll-forward with a fix (owned here; `[inference]`); what a rollback cannot undo: memories and preferences learned since, data already collected, and site processes that changed around the new behaviour (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); compatibility between model versions, artefact bundles and configurations (recap: the artefact bundle, ch33); keeping the previous version ready to run (`[inference]`).
  - **49.08 · Safety re-validation and re-certification after updates.** Show how a team decides whether an update reopens the safety case or the approval. *Concepts:* recap: the safety case and the certification gap (ch44) and the certification path, where an update may reopen the assessment (ch45); change-impact analysis, deciding which parts of the safety case and approval an update touches (owned here; `[inference]` until sourced); keeping certified safety functions outside the learned model so a model update leaves them untouched (recap: ch44; `[inference]`); the automotive analogue: UN Regulation No. 156, its software update management system and its software identification number (RXSWIN), and the question of whether an update affects type approval (owned here as a labelled analogue; text not yet opened, unconfirmed); ISO 24089 software update engineering (unconfirmed: search snippet only); carrying vehicle rules to humanoids as `[inference]`; recap: the Machinery Regulation's provisions on self-evolving behaviour (ch45, to be read from EUR-Lex); EU AI Act post-market monitoring and substantial modification, and modification rules in ISO 10218 and IEC 61508 (research requests); recap: re-validation after an incident fix (ch44).
  - **49.09 · Versioning, release records and audit trails.** Show what a team must be able to prove about every version it shipped. *Concepts:* recap: lineage, versioning and reproducibility (ch23) and the fleet version inventory (ch47); the release record (owned here): model version, data snapshot, training configuration, gate results, approvals, rollout history and known limits (`[inference]` until sourced); model documentation that travels with a release (owned here; recap: dataset cards, ch26; model-card source needed); who may approve a release, as a safety and security control (`[inference]`; recap: signed updates, ch44 and ch47); audit trails for incidents and liability (recap: incident response, ch44; product liability and updates, ch45); deletion requests that reach a trained model, retraining vs removing a contributor's influence (recap: withdrawal and deletion, ch24; previewed in ch23; sources needed).
  - **49.10 · Measuring improvement across releases.** Show how a team knows the fleet is actually getting better. *Concepts:* release-over-release tracking (owned here): success, assists per robot-hour, incidents and cycle time per version (recap: fleet KPIs, ch47; intervention metrics, ch43; assists per robot-hour, ch48); confounds that fake or hide improvement: season, site changes, new tasks and changed counting rules (owned here; `[inference]`, reasoning shown; recap: counting rules, ch43); comparing versions in the field with a randomised split of robots or shifts (recap: A/B tests and sequential testing, ch43; `[inference]` for fleet use); the improvement curve and when it flattens (`[inference]`; recap: diminishing returns, ch48); what makers publish about improvement after launch (scan finding: almost nothing).
  - **49.11 · The running examples' later twists.** Take each task's twist through the whole release process. *Concepts:* (aside) each twist from data to rollback (`[inference]`, reasoning shown, numbers marked `nofact`): battery station engineering change, a revised module with a new connector and torque spec (new demonstrations on dummy modules of the new module; a fine-tune that keeps the old variant, recap ch33; a regression suite with both variants; work instructions and torque settings from the plant's systems tied to the release record; a station-by-station rollout; change-impact analysis before live packs; recap: a new coverage cell, ch26; preview from ch47 closed here); kitchen reset household preferences (memory vs adapter vs fine-tune; a per-household change that the household can see and undo); dinner for five taste and allergy memory (an update may never weaken the allergen hard constraint, so allergen checks sit in every regression suite; recap: semantic safety, ch44); greenhouse seasonal retraining (a pre-season release for a new variety, gated on last season's held-out data and checked again in the first weeks of harvest).
- **Running-example hooks:** all four in 49.11; battery station in 49.06 (a canary on one station with dummy modules before live packs, `[inference]`) and 49.08 (does a new torque spec reopen the safety case, `[inference]`); kitchen reset in 49.04 (household preference learning) and 49.07 (a rollback must not erase what the household taught, `[inference]`); dinner for five in 49.05 (allergen checks as safety-critical regression tests, `[inference]`); greenhouse in 49.02 (cadence set by the season) and 49.10 (season as a confound when comparing releases, `[inference]`).

  - R156 PDF — UNECE — no date — https://unece.org/sites/default/files/2024-03/R156e%20(2).pdf — standard (unconfirmed: not opened)

### Changes from seed (P11)
- ch48: added 48.01 (what a data flywheel is) and 48.11 (a flywheel for each running example) — framing and synthesis, matching earlier Parts; ch01, ch26, ch29 and ch38 preview the flywheel to ch48.
- ch48: recast "interventions as data" as 48.04 (interventions and corrections in the loop), recapping ch20's DAgger and ROVE and ch34's Recap, and owning assists per robot-hour as the flywheel's health metric — per the Part brief, ch20 owns interventions as a source and ch34 owns learning from deployment experience as a method; ch47 previews falling assists to ch48.
- ch48: split "hindsight labelling and auto-labelling" into 48.05 (hindsight relabelling and failure-aware learning) and 48.06 (auto-labelling at fleet scale and compounding label noise) — the scan reports VLM hindsight relabelling and failures-as-preferences as distinct 2026 methods and FailBench measures judge error; ch24 and ch34 own auto-labelling and judges, so 48.06 owns only their use in the loop.
- ch48: renamed "prioritising retraining data" to 48.07 (choosing what to retrain on) and placed active data collection there — ch39 previews active data collection to ch48.
- ch48: widened "privacy-preserving flywheels in homes" (48.08) to include federated and on-device learning and the storage of household health information and taste memory — the scan reports federated VLA training, ch24 and ch44 preview health-information storage to ch48, and the P11 running-example row names it.
- ch49: added 49.01 (what a release is) and 49.10 (measuring improvement across releases) — improving robots after deployment is half the reader's north star (BRIEF §1); ch43 and ch47 own A/B tests and KPIs, so 49.10 owns only release-over-release tracking and its confounds.
- ch49: merged household preference learning into "global vs site-specific models" (49.04) — ch33 previews preference learning to ch48 and ch49; boundary recorded: ch48 owns capturing and storing preference and health data, ch49 owns turning them into per-site behaviour and releasing it.
- ch49: kept "shadow mode, canaries, staged rollouts" as one section (49.06) marked as analogues or `[inference]`, with a derived canary-duration equation — the scan found no robotics primary, and LLM-ops blogs are excluded by the sources policy.
- ch49: renamed "safety re-validation and re-certification" to 49.08 and built it on change-impact analysis, with UN R156 and ISO 24089 as labelled automotive analogues and AI Act, ISO 10218 and IEC 61508 modification rules as research requests — scan suggestions; ch47 assigned the update-management and approval side of R156 to ch49.
- ch49: widened "versioning and audit trails" to 49.09 (release records), recapping ch23's lineage and ch47's version inventory and owning deletion requests that reach a trained model — ch23 previews lineage for audits and deletion to ch49.
- No PROPOSED chapter-level changes.

## P12 · Landscape and frontier

### ch50 · Lab-by-lab teardowns
- **Purpose:** A uniform, evidence-graded teardown of the 19 agreed labs: the body each builds or uses, the data it bets on, the model family and training stages it discloses, its world-model or WAM work, where it deploys, how open it is, and what remains unknown. Labs are grouped by kind; every lab gets the same fields, the same grading and alphabetical order within its group, and OpenAI, Tesla, Meta's humanoid effort and Neura get short, strictly evidence-bound entries that say plainly what was not found. ch50 owns the teardown template, the lab-level evidence grade, the kinds of lab, the lab comparison table, the cross-lab patterns and the disclosure gradient. It recaps and extends ch30's master lab-recipe map rather than re-teaching it, and cross-references the specialised tables: specs (ch04), architectures (ch16), data volumes (ch17), stage recipes (ch31–ch34), world-model recipes (ch38), WAMs (ch40) and deployments (ch46). ch51 owns the sector picture; ch52 owns open problems.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** no.
- **Depends on:** ch01 (evidence tags and claim badges); ch03 (milestones from RT-2 to π0, GR00T N1 and Gemini Robotics); ch04 (today's leading humanoids compared; how to read a spec sheet; published prices); ch05–ch07 (actuator, transmission and hand disclosures, including Tesla's patent-only claims); ch09 (Jetson Thor; the NVIDIA reference humanoid; battery swapping); ch10 (Digit 5's independent safety controller; no public MTBF); ch16 (published architectures compared); ch17 (units of robot data; reading a volume claim; the 2025–26 data race); ch18 (human-video and wearable capture at scale); ch19 (handheld grippers; remote expert modes); ch30 (the four stages; the master lab-recipe map; how to read a recipe claim); ch31 (pre-training recipes compared; open vs closed models); ch32 (embodied-reasoning models, including Gemini Robotics-ER and UBTech's Thinker); ch33 (adaptation without weight updates); ch34 (Recap; RL recipes compared); ch35 (learned whole-body controllers; the Atlas model; Agility's controller); ch36 (V-JEPA 2; the 1X world model; Tesla's keynote-only world-model work); ch38 (the world-model recipe table); ch39 (world-model evaluation, including Google DeepMind's Veo study); ch40 (the WAM landscape; Dyna-2; GR00T N2 as unconfirmed); ch42 (questions to ask of any robot brain); ch43 (reading evaluation claims; Helix 2.5's protocol); ch44 (Unitree G1 security findings; ASIMOV); ch45 ("first certified humanoid" claims; China's standards system); ch46 (the deployment ladder; case studies as readiness lessons); ch47 (published fleet figures); ch48 (Tesla's data engine as unconfirmed).
- **Estimated length:** about 14,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **50.01 · Method: what is compared and how evidence is graded.** Set one template and one grading scale before any lab appears, so no lab is treated differently. *Concepts:* the teardown template (owned here: body, data strategy, model family, training stages, world-model or WAM work, deployment status, openness, evidence grade, unknowns, each dated); the lab-level evidence grade (owned here: what kind of disclosure backs each field, from peer-reviewed paper or open weights down to press only or nothing, and whether anyone outside the lab has replicated it; a method choice, `[inference]`, reasoning shown); recap: evidence tags and claim badges (ch01); recap: how to read a recipe claim (ch30), a volume claim (ch17) and an evaluation claim (ch43); neutrality rules (same fields, same grade, alphabetical order within groups, company claims attributed, "not disclosed" cells kept visible); the "current as of" date on every row.
  - **50.02 · The landscape: kinds of lab and how they connect.** Show the 19 labs on one map before the teardowns, and the axes along which they differ in 2026. *Concepts:* the six groups used in this chapter (owned here; `[inference]` classification, reasoning shown): platform providers, robot foundation-model labs, humanoid makers that build their own brain, industrial humanoid makers, Chinese humanoid makers (grouped by region for their shared market, standards and evidence access) and big-tech entrants; why the groups blur (makers train models, model labs need bodies; `[inference]`); links between labs: Apptronik's Robot Park feeding Gemini Robotics (unconfirmed), Boston Dynamics with TRI, production Atlas shipped to Hyundai and Google DeepMind (scan finding), NVIDIA compute in others' robots (recap: Jetson Thor and the reference humanoid on Unitree H2 Plus, ch09); the 2026 axes of difference (owned here; scan finding): data strategy, world models and WAMs reaching products, openness and deployment rung; (aside) a map of the 19 labs by group with partnership links.
  - **50.03 · Platform providers: Google DeepMind and NVIDIA.** Tear down the two labs that supply models, simulators, reasoning models or compute for robots other companies build. *Concepts:* the platform provider (owned here; `[inference]` framing: a lab whose robot models and tools run on many makers' bodies); (aside) Google DeepMind: Gemini Robotics 1.5 (tech report) and Gemini Robotics 2's reasoner, whole-body VLA and on-device VLA (blog only, company claim; recap ch16, ch32 and ch35); Gemini Robotics-ER versions (recap ch32); Veo-based policy evaluation (recap ch39); Genie 3 (recap ch36); ASIMOV and robot constitutions (recap ch44); partner bodies, Apollo 2 and production Atlas (company claims); unknowns: no Gemini Robotics 2 technical report; (aside) NVIDIA: GR00T N1 to N1.7, an open 3B VLA with human-video pre-training (tech report; recap ch31); GR00T N2 announced as a preview for release at the end of 2026 (newsroom link unconfirmed; its reported DreamZero basis stays out, recap ch40); Cosmos world models and DreamZero (recap ch36–ch38 and ch40); data and control tools (DreamGen, ch20; EgoScale, ch18; SONIC, ch28 and ch35); compute and the reference humanoid (recap ch09); openness (recap ch31).
  - **50.04 · Robot foundation-model labs: Dyna Robotics, Generalist AI, Physical Intelligence and Skild AI.** Tear down the labs that build one brain meant to run on many bodies. *Concepts:* the body-agnostic robot foundation-model lab (owned here; `[inference]` framing, reasoning shown: what selling a brain rather than a body implies for data, adaptation and evaluation); (aside) Dyna Robotics: Dyna-2, a mixture-of-transformers world-action model with a claimed scaling law over 1M hours of egocentric video (company claim; exact dates unconfirmed; recap ch17 and ch40); reward-model claims not found (recap ch34); (aside) Generalist AI: GEN-0 (270,000 hours and a claimed model-size threshold) and GEN-1 (over 500,000 hours of wearable data and an RL stage), company claims (recap ch17, ch30, ch31 and ch34); (aside) Physical Intelligence: the π family from π0 to π0.7, with VLM initialisation, flow-matching action experts, knowledge insulation, real-time chunking, π*0.6's Recap and π0.7's context conditioning (papers and tech reports, results company-reported; recap ch13, ch16 and ch30–ch34; ch42 for subgoal images); open weights for earlier models through openpi (recap ch31); (aside) Skild AI: S1, in-context learning from one video prompt, trained on a mix of teleoperation, handheld-gripper (UMI) and simulation data (company blog; no technical report found; S1 supersedes Skild Brain; recap ch19, ch31 and ch33).
  - **50.05 · Humanoid makers building their own brain: 1X, Figure and Tesla.** Tear down the makers that design the body, the model, the data pipeline and the deployment in one company. *Concepts:* vertical integration (owned here; `[inference]` framing, reasoning shown: co-design of body and brain and data from one's own fleet, against carrying every risk alone); (aside) 1X: NEO's body and tendon hand (recap ch04, ch06 and ch07); the 1X World Model for policy evaluation, with no sizes disclosed (company claim; recap ch36 and ch39); world-model policy figures (unconfirmed; recap ch40); Expert Mode remote teleoperation (recap ch19); pre-orders and price (recap ch46); no verified home delivery (preview: ch51); (aside) Figure: Figure 03 (recap ch04); Helix, Helix 02's System 0/1/2 and learned whole-body control (recap ch16 and ch35); Helix 2.5's pre-training on Index human-behaviour video and its zero-shot 30-home claim under a strict protocol (company claim; recap ch30, ch31 and ch43); Project Go-Big and Index (recap ch17); BMW Spartanburg (recap ch46); (aside) Tesla, kept short: the Q2 2026 investor update reports Fremont lines being converted for Optimus and "Optimus Academy" data collection (company claim); Optimus V3 not unveiled at scan time (recap ch04); actuator and hand claims patent- or blog-only (recap ch05–ch07); world-model work keynote-only (recap ch36); "shadow mode" unconfirmed (recap ch48); no policy recipe disclosed (recap ch30).
  - **50.06 · Industrial humanoid makers: Agility Robotics, Apptronik, Boston Dynamics with TRI, and Neura Robotics.** Tear down the makers that start from paid industrial work and either build their own controllers or partner for a general model. *Concepts:* the industrial-first maker and the brain partnership (owned here; `[inference]` framing, reasoning shown); (aside) Agility Robotics: Digit 5 (recap ch04 and ch10); a whole-body control foundation model trained by RL in simulation, an LSTM under 1M parameters with zero-shot transfer (company claim; recap ch35); Arc fleet software and published fleet figures (recap ch47); GXO and Digit 5 orders (recap ch46); (aside) Apptronik: Apollo 2 without a published spec table (recap ch04); Robot Park, a data-collection site said to feed Gemini Robotics (search snippet only, unconfirmed); Gemini Robotics 2 success rates on Apollo 2 (company-reported; recap ch16 and ch35); the Mercedes-Benz pilot without a primary (recap ch46); (aside) Boston Dynamics with TRI: production Atlas and its supplier actuators (recap ch04 and ch05); a single language-conditioned whole-body Large Behavior Model with no paper (company claim; recap ch35); TRI's blind-tested LBM study (recap ch31 and ch43); the Hyundai application centre and timeline (recap ch46); production Atlas shipped to Hyundai and Google DeepMind (scan finding); a May 2026 heavy-lift RL result known only from press; the new four-finger hand (recap ch07); (aside) Neura Robotics, kept short: a 4NE1 datasheet the tools could not read, other claims only on aggregators (excluded), and no technical disclosure of a model or data strategy found.
  - **50.07 · Chinese humanoid makers: AgiBot, Galbot, UBTech and Unitree.** Tear down four makers whose market, standards and published evidence share a context. *Concepts:* volume hardware with open models and datasets (owned here; `[inference]` framing anchored in AgiBot World's open dataset and Unitree's open UnifoLM-WMA-0, with prices recapped from ch04); recap: China's humanoid standards system (ch45); (aside) AgiBot: AgiBot World Colosseo (recap ch17); GE-Act 2.0's 300-to-30,000-hour scaling (recap ch30 and ch31); GE-Sim 2.0, a 2B video simulator (preprint); GO-2 (2026-04-09) known only from press; the A2 "first certified" claim (recap ch45); (aside) Galbot: synthetic pre-training, real post-training and retail deployments, all from press (the GraspVLA paper not yet searched); (aside) UBTech: Walker S2 battery swapping (recap ch09); the Thinker VLM (recap ch32); deployment figures from press only; hardware primaries missing; (aside) Unitree: G1 and H2 bodies and prices (recap ch04); the G1 as the field's research workhorse (recap ch35); UnifoLM-WMA-0, an open world-model-action model on the Z1 and G1 with its data not detailed (tech report); G1 security findings (recap ch44).
  - **50.08 · Big-tech entrants: Meta and OpenAI.** Tear down the two entrants briefly, saying what can and cannot be known. *Concepts:* reading an entrant with no technical disclosure (owned here; `[inference]`, reasoning shown: what research assets, acquisitions, hiring and executives' remarks can and cannot tell a reader); (aside) Meta, kept short: research assets recapped (V-JEPA 2's 1M-hour video pre-training and 62 hours of robot data, ch36 and ch38; Sparsh and Digit 360 touch, ch08; Aria Gen 2, ch18; DINOv3, ch11); the acquisition of Assured Robot Intelligence (ARI), May 2026, from press only; no technical disclosure of a humanoid effort; (aside) OpenAI, kept short: the 2019 Rubik's Cube hand (recap ch03); a robotics division from 31 May 2026 and Altman's remark that a humanoid is coming, from press only (the Forbes page returned 403: unconfirmed); no technical disclosure.
  - **50.09 · The lab comparison table.** Put every lab on one dated page, gaps included. *Concepts:* (aside) the lab comparison table (owned here): one row per lab, with columns for hardware, data strategy, model family, training stages (pre-, mid- and post-training, RL), world-model or WAM work, deployment status (highest rung; recap the deployment ladder, ch46), openness, evidence grade and date, and "not disclosed" cells; recap: the master lab-recipe map (ch30), with rows brought up to the scan date; pointers to the specialised tables (ch04, ch16, ch17, ch31–ch34, ch38, ch40, ch46); how to read the table, and what one row cannot say about a lab (`[inference]`).
  - **50.10 · Patterns across the labs.** Draw out what the teardowns show together, without ranking labs. *Concepts:* the data-strategy split (scan finding): human egocentric video at very large claimed scale (Dyna-2, GEN-1, Figure's Index) against teleoperation and fleet data (Apptronik's Robot Park, Tesla's Optimus Academy), all company claims (recap ch17 and ch18); world models and WAMs reaching products (Dyna-2, GR00T N2 as a preview, UnifoLM-WMA-0, GE-Sim 2.0, the 1X World Model; recap ch40 and ch42); open vs closed (recap ch31); vertical integration vs brain partnerships (`[inference]`); version churn, with major model releases at many labs in 2026 (scan finding; dated rows); self-reported benchmarks everywhere and no independent replication (scan finding; recap ch43); which labs' disclosures touch each running example (`[inference]`).
  - **50.11 · What is unknown.** State plainly what the public record does not show, lab by lab and across the field. *Concepts:* the disclosure gradient (owned here: from paper with open weights, through technical report, company blog, investor filing and press, to nothing; `[inference]` framing); labs with no technical disclosure found (OpenAI, Meta's humanoid effort, Neura), Tesla limited to an investor update, and UBTech's hardware (scan finding); claims that reach the book only through press (AgiBot GO-2, Atlas's 2026 heavy-lift RL, Apptronik's Robot Park); training compute almost never disclosed (recap ch31 and ch38); deployment metrics unpublished (recap ch10 and ch47); what would make a lab's claims checkable (`[inference]`); questions to put to any lab (`[inference]`; recap the questions in ch42 and ch46).
- **Running-example hooks:** battery station in 50.05 and 50.06 (the listed labs' automotive work, Figure at BMW, Atlas at Hyundai and Tesla's own Fremont lines, is the closest any comes to the station; the battery-hall pilot itself is Hexagon's, outside the list, recap ch02 and ch46); kitchen reset in 50.05 (Helix 02's kitchen sequence and Helix 2.5's homes; 1X's Expert Mode; all company claims); dinner for five in 50.03 (Gemini Robotics' reasoner and the ASIMOV semantic-safety work as the nearest disclosed work on allergen-style hard constraints, `[inference]`); greenhouse in 50.10 (no listed lab's disclosures in the scans cover greenhouse work, `[inference]`).

### ch51 · Where deployment is happening, and where it's next
- **Purpose:** The sector-by-sector picture of humanoid deployment at the build date: how to grade a deployment claim, how many humanoids exist and where, what gates each sector (task fit, safety, economics, regulation), what has actually been deployed in automotive plants, logistics, retail and services, homes, care, agriculture and research labs, which leading indicators to watch, and an evidence-based case for where the next wave looks imminent, with confidence levels. ch51 owns the evidence ladder for deployment claims, independent market counts, the sector gate profile, the per-sector deployment record, leading indicators and the next-wave case. ch02 owns the case for and against the humanoid form; ch46 owns readiness, the deployment ladder and readiness lessons from individual case studies; ch50 owns each lab's deployment status; ch52 owns open problems.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** no.
- **Depends on:** ch02 (evidence from deployments so far; wheels, rails and specialist machines; when a general-purpose robot beats a specialist); ch03 (the first commercial deployments); ch04 (the body variants; published prices); ch09 (runtime vs shift length; sealing against heat and humidity); ch10 (no public MTBF or uptime figures); ch17 (reading a volume claim); ch19 (remote expert modes in homes); ch24 (privacy and consent in homes); ch42 (the scenario method with confidence levels); ch43 (intervention metrics; reading evaluation claims; Helix 2.5 as an evaluation); ch44 (the fail-passive gap; the certification gap; semantic safety; privacy by design); ch45 (the dated standards status table; ISO 13482 and UL 3300; the EU Machinery Regulation and AI Act dates; China's standards system); ch46 (readiness dimensions; the deployment ladder; task-fit criteria; economics, RaaS and total cost of ownership; case studies as readiness lessons); ch47 (fleet KPIs; published fleet figures); ch48 (the economics of the flywheel; "data moat" claims); ch50 (each lab's deployment status; the evidence grade; the comparison table).
- **Estimated length:** about 11,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **51.01 · How to read a deployment claim: the evidence ladder.** Give one scale for grading every claim in the chapter before any sector is surveyed. *Concepts:* recap: the deployment ladder from lab demonstration to repeat orders (ch46) and evidence tags (ch01); the evidence ladder (owned here: each claim placed by rung, demonstration, pilot, paid deployment, fleet, repeat orders, and by who makes it); maker-only vs customer-confirmed vs independently counted claims (owned here; Figure's BMW claims with BMW's own confirmation as the example, recap ch02 and ch46); announced targets vs verified fleets (owned here; the 25,000-Atlas plan, recap ch46, and a reported 10,000-NEO target to trace to a primary; scan finding that targets far exceed verified fleets); aggregator trackers and "evidence reports" that repeat unsourced numbers, some listing products that looked fabricated (scan caution: excluded); an evaluation is not a deployment (recap: Helix 2.5's 30 homes, ch43); recap: reading a volume claim (ch17).
  - **51.02 · Counting humanoids: market size, and China's volume against Western pilots.** Establish what independent counts exist and what they do and do not say. *Concepts:* independent counts as the baseline (owned here; the IFR's 2026-09-30 release on professional service robot sales, reported up 24%); the professional service robot category and where humanoids sit in it (definitions to be read from the IFR: source needed); the roughly 7,000 full-size humanoids said to have been sold in 2025, almost all in China and mostly for research or entertainment (secondary reports only; the IFR release does not state it: unconfirmed until the full report is read); China's volume vs Western pilots (owned here; `[inference]` until Chinese fleet primaries are found): many units to research, education and entertainment buyers vs few units in paid production work; Chinese deployment figures that reach the book only through press (UBTech Walker S2, Galbot retail; recap ch50); recap: China's humanoid standards system (ch45) and published prices (ch04).
  - **51.03 · What gates a sector: task fit, safety, economics and regulation.** Set the four gates every sector section applies, and the idea of a binding gate. *Concepts:* the four gates as a sector-level frame (owned here; `[inference]`, reasoning shown): task fit (recap: task-fit criteria, ch46; when a general-purpose robot beats a specialist, ch02), safety (recap: the fail-passive gap and the certification gap, ch10 and ch44; semantic safety, ch44), economics (recap: total cost of ownership, RaaS and per-hour pricing, ch46), regulation (recap: the standards status table, ch45); the binding gate, the one that stops a sector first (owned here; `[inference]`); site structure as a hidden factor: engineered factory cells, shared warehouse aisles, public spaces, private homes (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); (aside) the gate profile used in 51.04–51.10.
  - **51.04 · Automotive manufacturing.** Report what car plants have deployed, rung by rung, and why they came first. *Concepts:* why car plants came first (`[inference]`, anchored in the published deployments: repeated tasks, engineered sites, an industrial safety culture); Figure at BMW Spartanburg: an 11-month Figure 02 run loading sheet metal (10 months in some sources; recap ch46), one task only, then Figure 03 (company claims with BMW confirmation; recap ch46); Hexagon's wheeled AEON in high-voltage battery assembly at BMW Leipzig (company claim; recap ch02 and ch46); Boston Dynamics' Atlas training centre at Hyundai's Georgia plant, opened 2026-09-21, with Hyundai's stated plan of real deployment from 2028 and assembly by 2030 (company claim; the "deployed in 2026" framing conflicts, so both are shown; recap ch46); Tesla's Fremont lines converted for Optimus, an internal deployment (investor update, company claim; recap ch50); Apptronik with Mercedes-Benz and UBTech Walker S2 in car plants (no primary: gap); the gate profile for automotive.
  - **51.05 · Logistics and warehousing.** Report the deployments in moving totes and goods, and what competes with them. *Concepts:* tote handling as the archetypal humanoid logistics task (`[inference]`); Agility Digit at GXO, from the 2024 RaaS agreement to one site past 100,000 totes (company claims; recap ch03 and ch47); fleet hours and multi-year, milestone-conditional Digit 5 orders (company claims; recap ch46 and ch47); a reported Toyota Canada RaaS deal for Digit (February 2026; secondary report only, primary unconfirmed); Figure 03 in Spartanburg logistics (company claims; recap ch46); competition from wheeled mobile robots and fixed automation (recap ch02); the gate profile for logistics.
  - **51.06 · Retail, hospitality and services.** Report the thin evidence for public-facing work and what these sites demand. *Concepts:* what public-facing sites ask of a robot: crowds, varied tasks and customer contact (`[inference]`); Galbot's reported retail deployments (press; primary needed; recap ch50); hospitality and service deployments (no primary found: explicit research request); rules for robots among the public (recap: ISO 13482 and UL 3300, ch45; `[inference]` on fit); the gate profile.
  - **51.07 · Homes.** Report the state of the largest prize and the hardest site. *Concepts:* why homes are the largest prize and the hardest site (`[inference]`, recap ch02); 1X NEO: pre-orders from October 2025, a stated price, and a remote human expert as part of the service (company claims; recap ch19 and ch46); no verified customer delivery found as of July 2026 (an absence reported by aggregators: research request for a primary either way); research evaluations in homes vs deployments (recap: Helix 2.5, ch43); privacy and the remote viewer (recap ch24 and ch44); home and consumer standards (recap ch45); the gate profile for homes.
  - **51.08 · Healthcare and elder care.** Report the small trials in care settings and what blocks hospitals. *Concepts:* care as a sector short of workers (`[inference]` until sourced); Donut Robotics' humanoid patrolling a Japanese nursing home since July 2026, with the operator Charm Care investing (press; a small trial; Donut's own release needed); hospitals (no primary found: research request); safety and regulation near vulnerable people (recap ch44 and ch45; medical-device rules not scanned: research request); the gate profile for care.
  - **51.09 · Agriculture.** Show why farms test the general-purpose case hardest, and how little humanoid evidence exists. *Concepts:* recap: rail trolleys and specialist harvesters as the alternatives (ch02); specialist machines as the bar to beat (eternal.ag's Harvester, press; recap ch02); humanoid greenhouse trials as leads to verify (Daedong and Rainbow Robotics' Agroid; Harvest House with the Humanoid Application Center; from the running-examples anchors, no primary in the scans); seasonality, heat and humidity (recap ch09 and ch48); agricultural rules not yet researched (recap ch45); the gate profile for agriculture.
  - **51.10 · Research labs, data collection and other early buyers.** Show the buyers that account for most units today, and the sectors with no evidence yet. *Concepts:* research and education as today's main buyers (secondary, unconfirmed; recap 51.02); humanoids sold as research platforms (NVIDIA's reference humanoid for academic research, company claim, recap ch09; production Atlas shipped to Google DeepMind, recap ch50; the Unitree G1 as the research workhorse, recap ch35); robots deployed to collect training data as a market of their own (Apptronik's Robot Park, unconfirmed; Tesla's Optimus Academy, company claim; recap ch50; `[inference]` on the market); data centres and entertainment (no primary found: research requests).
  - **51.11 · Leading indicators.** Teach which signals arrive before scale, and build the dated indicator table. *Concepts:* leading vs lagging indicators (owned here; `[inference]` framing); the indicators (owned here; scan suggestion): repeat and follow-on orders (BMW's move from Figure 02 to Figure 03), contract structure (multi-year, milestone-conditional), hours per robot and fleet hours, intervention and assist rates, price per hour, customer-confirmed rather than maker-only claims, independent counts (the IFR), standards published (ISO 25785-1 still a draft; recap ch45), and application centres opened (Boston Dynamics at Hyundai); what each indicator can and cannot show (`[inference]`); the indicators no maker publishes yet: uptime, interventions per hour, cost per task (recap ch46 and ch47); (aside) a dated indicator table by sector with "not published" cells; preview: signals to watch across the field (taught in ch52).
  - **51.12 · Where the next wave looks imminent.** Make the evidence-based case that BRIEF §3 asks for, with confidence levels and no hype. *Concepts:* the next-wave case (owned here; `[inference]`, reasoning shown from 51.11's indicators): sectors ordered by highest verified rung, binding gate and indicator trend, each with a confidence level of low, medium or high (recap: the scenario method, ch42) and the evidence that would raise or lower it; the case for industrial sites first (car plants and warehouses hold the only customer-confirmed, follow-on and multi-year order evidence) and the counter-case (targets far exceed verified fleets; no published uptime; Atlas's 2028 date); homes and care as the furthest on current evidence; "same brain, three deployments" across sectors (`[inference]`); (aside) the four running examples placed on the sector map.
- **Running-example hooks:** all four in 51.12; battery station in 51.04 (AEON in Leipzig's high-voltage battery assembly; Hyundai Mobis's harness-connection pilot from the running-examples anchors, re-verify); greenhouse in 51.09 (the specialist bar; Agroid and Harvest House as leads) and 51.03 (economics as the likely binding gate, `[inference]`); dinner for five in 51.07 (the ordering half already exists as software while cooking demonstrations stay narrow; running-examples anchors, re-verify); kitchen reset in 51.07 (a remote expert inside a home; recap ch19 and ch46).

Atlas timing appears twice (a 2026 training centre vs real deployment from 2028): show both. The scan tags the IFR release "standard"; check the tag against `docs/SOURCES.md` at research time.

### ch52 · Open problems and where the field is heading
- **Purpose:** The cross-cutting problems that stand between today's humanoids and reliable, widespread deployment, each stated from first principles with its graded evidence, the competing positions, what would change our mind and the signals to watch; then one signals table linked to ch51's leading indicators and ch42's scenario signals, and a frontier test against the four running examples. ch52 owns each problem as an open problem, the "what would change our mind" test, the field-wide signals table and the running-examples frontier test. It teaches no new mechanism: every problem recaps the chapter that owns its concepts. ch42 owns the WAM-versus-VLA scenarios, recapped here; ch51 owns leading indicators for deployment.
- **Lifecycle chapter:** no.
- **Depends on:** ch02 (where the debate stands; Brooks's case); ch04 (design for manufacture; the supply chain; price points); ch07 (where hands stand in 2026; no independent dexterity benchmarks); ch08 (tactile sensing; weak transfer across tactile sensors); ch09 (energy, power and heat budgets; runtime; recharging); ch10 (the fail-passive gap; no public MTBF); ch13 (compounding errors); ch17 (the data pyramid; diversity vs volume; limits of the scaling evidence); ch18 (missing force and touch in human data; unreplicated human-video claims); ch29 (the sim-to-real gap); ch32 (memory for robot policies); ch35 (loco-manipulation; whole-body failure modes; a thin evidence base); ch36 (world models vs simulators); ch37 (long-horizon drift); ch39 (evidence on world-model reliability); ch41 (the failure taxonomy; the modality gap; force- and touch-aware WAMs; no dose–response study); ch42 (the scenarios and their signals); ch43 (whole-task success as a product of step successes; arenas; reading evaluation claims); ch44 (the certification gap); ch45 (ISO 25785-1 as a draft); ch46 (total cost of ownership; published prices); ch47 (no published uptime or intervention rates); ch49 (re-certification after updates); ch50 (the disclosure gradient); ch51 (leading indicators; the next-wave case).
- **Estimated length:** about 10,000 words.
- **Sections:**
  - **52.01 · What makes a problem open, and how to read the field's forecasts.** Set the method every later section follows. *Concepts:* an open problem vs an engineering backlog (owned here; `[inference]`, reasoning shown); the section pattern: why it is hard, where it stands, competing positions, what would change our mind, signals; the "what would change our mind" test (owned here; scan suggestion): naming in advance the evidence that would settle or reverse a view; the field's own lists (the Psiris et al. review's open challenges, peer-reviewed; Karcini et al.'s position that robots need data, reward and embodiment interfaces beyond VLAs and world models, preprint); recap: the scenario method and confidence levels (ch42); recap: the evidence grade and the disclosure gradient (ch50).
  - **52.02 · Dexterity and touch.** Ask whether hands will learn from more data or need new hardware and new senses. *Concepts:* recap: the hand as the hardest part and the data bottleneck (ch07); recap: tactile sensing and weak transfer across sensors (ch08); recap: missing force and touch in human data (ch18) and force- and touch-aware WAMs (ch41); the hardware-or-data question (owned here as an open problem): Brooks's position that today's humanoids will not learn dexterity from data without touch and force (as summarised in ch02) vs the data-scaling position (both attributed; recap ch02); touch in humanoid policies ("Touch Dreaming", unconfirmed, not opened); tactile and touch-aware world-action models as a fast-growing 2026 arXiv theme (scan finding); what would change our mind: an independent dexterity benchmark, of which none exists (recap ch07 and ch43).
  - **52.03 · Data: scale, diversity and grounded supervision.** Ask what kind of data, not just how much, will unlock general robots. *Concepts:* recap: the data pyramid, diversity vs volume and the limits of the scaling evidence (ch17); recap: the unreplicated claims that human video beats robot data (ch18); the grounding bottleneck (owned here as an open problem): Karcini et al.'s argument that the limit is turning unstructured behaviour data into grounded robot supervision, not scaling VLAs alone (preprint); reward and success signals as part of supervision (recap: VLM judges and FailBench, ch34); no dose–response study for world models (recap ch41); what would change our mind: independent replications at matched evaluation (`[inference]`).
  - **52.04 · Long-horizon reliability.** Ask why robots that succeed in demonstrations still fail over long tasks and long shifts. *Concepts:* recap: compounding errors (ch13), memory (ch32) and drift (ch37); recap: whole-task success as the product of step successes (ch43; illustrative numbers marked `nofact`); reliability as the gap between a demonstration and a deployment (owned here as an open problem; `[inference]`, reasoning shown); the surveys' naming of long-horizon reliability as open (Psiris et al.); the missing public numbers: uptime, MTBF and interventions per hour (recap ch10 and ch47); recap: Helix 2.5's 56% whole-task result as a company claim (ch43); what would change our mind (`[inference]`).
  - **52.05 · Whole-body control and loco-manipulation.** Ask whether a humanoid can move and manipulate at once, reliably, outside the lab. *Concepts:* recap: loco-manipulation, learned whole-body controllers and joint vs separate training (ch35); recap: falls (ch10 and ch44); the surveys' naming of whole-body humanoid control as open (Psiris et al.; scan finding); an evidence base that is mostly Unitree G1 preprints with thin failure reporting (recap ch35); the wheeled and rail variants as a way round the problem (recap ch04; `[inference]`); what would change our mind (`[inference]`).
  - **52.06 · Evaluation.** Ask how the field will measure progress it can trust. *Concepts:* recap: real-world protocols, arenas and robustness suites (ch43); recap: whether imagined scores predict real ones (ch39); evaluation without standard protocols as an open problem (Psiris et al.; scan finding); self-reported results everywhere (recap ch50); what would change our mind: shared protocols adopted across labs and independent arena results (`[inference]`).
  - **52.07 · Safety certification for learned systems.** Ask whether a learned, balancing robot can ever earn the highest safety ratings. *Concepts:* recap: the fail-passive gap (ch10) and the certification gap (ch44); Ding et al.'s analysis that cutting power makes a biped fall, which blocks the top safety levels under existing standards (preprint, tested on a Unitree G1); ISO 25785-1 for dynamically stable industrial robots still in draft (recap ch45; ISO page not opened); whether certification can ever cover a learned balancing policy (owned here as an open problem; scan controversy; both positions shown); keeping learned layers outside certified safety functions, and wheeled or rail bodies, as workarounds (recap ch04 and ch44; `[inference]`); re-certification after updates (recap ch49).
  - **52.08 · Physics in world models.** Ask whether world models can learn enough physics to be trusted for data, evaluation and control. *Concepts:* recap: the failure taxonomy, physics benchmarks and the modality gap (ch41); recap: action following (Chen et al., ch41); recap: world models vs simulators and hybrids (ch36); physics fidelity as an open problem (owned here; `[inference]`, reasoning shown); no 2026 roadmap found (scan gap: explicit research request); what would change our mind (`[inference]`).
  - **52.09 · Cost and manufacturing.** Ask whether humanoids can become cheap and reliable enough to make the economics work. *Concepts:* recap: design for manufacture and the supply chain (ch04) and actuator supply (ch05); recap: published prices (ch04 and ch46) and total cost of ownership (ch46); recap: Tesla's Fremont line conversion as a company claim (ch50); cost as an open problem (owned here; `[inference]`, cost drivers reasoned from first principles); no primary cost-curve or bill-of-materials analysis found (scan gap, as in ch02: explicit research request).
  - **52.10 · Energy and runtime.** Ask whether a humanoid can work a full shift on one body's worth of battery. *Concepts:* recap: the energy, power and heat budgets and runtime as stored energy over average power (ch09); recap: swapping, wireless and fast charging (ch09); runtime as an open problem (owned here; `[inference]`, reasoning shown, illustrative numbers marked `nofact`); no 2026 roadmap found (scan gap: explicit research request).
  - **52.11 · Signals to watch.** Pull every problem into one table of observable signals. *Concepts:* (aside) the signals table (owned here): per open problem, the signal, where it would first appear and which chapter it links to; links to ch51's leading indicators and to ch42's scenario signals (recap, not repeated); how to update a view when a signal arrives (`[inference]`, reasoning shown); the "current as of" date and the `/refresh` re-check.
  - **52.12 · The running examples as a frontier test.** Say which open problems stand between today's field and each of the four tasks. *Concepts:* (aside) per task, the blocking problems and the signal that would show each easing (`[inference]`, reasoning shown): dinner for five (long-horizon reliability, food physics in world models, semantic safety for allergens, judging quality without tasting; no published system yet does the whole task, per the running-examples anchors, re-verify); kitchen reset (dexterity with glass and wet items, home safety certification, privacy); battery station (touch and the connector click, certification beside live high voltage, cycle-time reliability); greenhouse (cost against specialist machines, a full shift of runtime in heat, plant physics, seasonal data).
- **Running-example hooks:** all four in 52.12; battery station in 52.02 (feeling the click as the touch problem in miniature, `[inference]`) and 52.07 (the fail-passive gap beside a live pack); dinner for five in 52.04 (a multi-day, multi-dish task as the long-horizon test, `[inference]`); kitchen reset in 52.02 (a wine glass and a wet sponge) and 52.06 (scoring "clean"); greenhouse in 52.09 and 52.10 (the toughest business case and a full shift in heat, `[inference]`).
- **Must-cite sources:**
  - Robots Need More than VLA and World Models — Karcini, Schwager, Hutter, Peters, Bou-Ammar et al. — 2026-06-04 — arXiv 2606.06556 — preprint
  - Toward Certified Functional Safety for Industrial Humanoid Robots — Ding, Cui, Wang, Wen — 2026-08-03 — arXiv 2608.02809 — preprint
  - Foundation Models in Robotics: A Comprehensive Review — Psiris et al. — 2026-04-16 (TMLR, July 2026) — arXiv 2604.15395 — peer-reviewed
  - ISO/CD 25785-1 — ISO — date unconfirmed — https://www.iso.org/standard/91469.html — standard (unconfirmed: 403; draft status)
  - Learning Versatile Humanoid Manipulation with Touch Dreaming — authors not recorded — 2026 — arXiv 2604.13015 — preprint (unconfirmed)
  - Why Today's Humanoids Won't Learn Dexterity — Rodney Brooks — 2025-09-26 — https://rodneybrooks.com/why-todays-humanoids-wont-learn-dexterity/ — press
  - Do Robotic World Models Really Follow Actions? — Chen et al. — 2026-08-25 — arXiv 2608.24885 — preprint
  - Data Scaling Laws in Imitation Learning for Robotic Manipulation — Lin et al. — 2024-10-24 (rev. 2026-06-26) — arXiv 2410.18647 — preprint
  - Do WAMs Generalize Better than VLAs? A Robustness Study — Zhang et al. — 2026-03-23 (rev. 2026-07-30) — arXiv 2603.22078 — preprint
- **Notes:** Gaps: no 2026 roadmap found for energy and runtime, cost curves or physics fidelity in world models; the ICML 2026 position-papers list (https://icml.cc/virtual/2026/events/2026-position-papers) is unmined; "Touch Dreaming" and ISO 25785-1 are unconfirmed. No new equation: recaps only (whole-task success as a product of step successes, ch43; runtime as stored energy over average power, ch09). Present Brooks and the data-scaling camp with equal care, and certification optimists and sceptics likewise.

### Changes from seed (P12)
- ch50: replaced "one section per lab" with six group sections (50.03–50.08), each opening with one five-layer concept for its kind of lab and holding one `.aside` teardown per lab with identical fields, in alphabetical order — 19 labs exceed the 12-section limit, and one template keeps every lab on equal terms.
- ch50: added 50.02 (the landscape: kinds of lab and how they connect) and 50.10 (patterns across the labs) — the scan reports data strategy and WAMs reaching products as the main 2026 axes, which need one place to be drawn together.
- ch50: the training-stage columns recap ch30's master lab-recipe map, and the spec, architecture, data-volume, stage-recipe, world-model, WAM and deployment tables of ch04, ch16, ch17, ch31–ch34, ch38, ch40 and ch46 are cross-referenced, not rebuilt — teach once; ch30 previews per-lab teardowns to ch50.
- ch50: OpenAI, Tesla, Meta's humanoid effort and Neura are kept short and evidence-bound, with "no technical disclosure found" stated — BRIEF §3 and the scan.
- ch51: added 51.01 (the evidence ladder, built on ch46's deployment ladder), 51.02 (counting humanoids, with China's volume vs Western pilots), 51.11 (leading indicators, widened from the seed) and 51.12 (where the next wave looks imminent) — scan suggestions, and BRIEF §3 asks ch51 to make an evidence-based case for the next wave.
- ch51: moved "what gates each sector" from the end to 51.03, so every sector section applies the same gate profile — teach the frame before using it.
- ch51: merged the seed's "retail" and "hospitality and services" into 51.06 — the scan found no primary for either, and both stay visible within the section limit.
- ch51: renamed "labs, data centres and others" to 51.10 (research labs, data collection and other early buyers) — the scans show research platforms and data-collection robots as real buyers, while data centres have no source yet.
- ch51 boundary (recorded): ch02 owns the case for and against the form; ch46 owns readiness, the deployment ladder and readiness lessons from case studies; ch50 owns each lab's deployment status; ch51 owns the sector picture, the evidence ladder, leading indicators and the next-wave case.
- ch52: added 52.01 (what makes a problem open, with the "what would change our mind" test), 52.05 (whole-body control and loco-manipulation) and 52.12 (the running examples as a frontier test) — scan suggestions, and the surveys name whole-body humanoid control as open.
- ch52: widened "data scale and diversity" to 52.03 (adding grounded supervision) — the June 2026 position paper argues the bottleneck is grounding, not scale alone.
- ch52: reordered the seed's problems into learning (52.02–52.05), proof (52.06–52.07), world models (52.08) and the body's economics (52.09–52.10) — simplest and most familiar first, and each group recaps neighbouring owners.
- ch52: "signals to watch" (52.11) links to ch51's indicators and ch42's scenario signals instead of restating them — ch42 owns the WAM-versus-VLA scenarios.
- No PROPOSED chapter-level changes.
